## Summary of Changes

The Louisiana Department of Wildlife and Fisheries (LDWF) notified the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) of the intent to comprehensively review and revise the Louisiana State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP) on 6 February 2023. This section details the major changes made during the 2025 SWAP revision, and, in conjunction with the Roadmap to the Eight Required Elements (page 12), will enable readers to quickly identify those areas that have been updated.
Prior to the official start of the SWAP revision, LDWF sent a SWAP survey to partners and tribal representatives (Appendix B). The survey was also sent to all LDWF staff and individuals from the State Wildlife Grant (SWG) distribution list. This survey was conducted in an effort to gauge how the 2015 SWAP was utilized and to acquire recommendations for the 2025 comprehensive revision. More information on the survey and results can be found in Chapter 3: Approach. The survey responses guided the decision making process for the LDWF Core Team during initial SWAP revision meetings. In an effort to reduce redundancy and make the SWAP more streamlined and easy to use, the research needs and conservation actions were combined into one list, and a table of the most commonly needed actions was added for each taxa group. In addition, the Core Team removed tiers from both the Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and Natural Communities chapters.
The Core Team and Taxa Team members (Appendix A) agreed that the designations were of limited value when prioritizing focal species for projects or management actions. However, taxa experts indicated that tiers were valuable when discussing invasive species. As such, the Invasives Team revised Tier (1 -4) definitions and added Tier 5, which serves as a watch list for species not currently documented in Louisiana, but that have the potential to invade.
As in 2015, LDWF used teams of taxa experts to review all SGCN and natural communities for the 2025 SWAP; a total of 64 animal SGCN were removed (Table 
1
) and 133 were added (Table 
2
). Taxa teams updated scientific and common names, global and sub-national ranks (G-and S-ranks), and modified research needs and conservation actions, and threats. One natural community -Ouachita Sand Prairie and Woodlandwas added, Cave was removed, and other communities were renamed or reorganized. In the 2015 SWAP, a full list of SGCN was listed under each natural community type. This resulted in substantial redundancy. In an effort to minimize redundancy in the 2025 revision, only obligate or near-obligate SGCN are listed for each natural community. A list of animal SGCN occurring in each ecological subtype is included in Appendix F and a list of animal and plant SGCN occurring in each natural community is included in Appendix G.
The most significant change in the 2025 revision was the addition of plants to the SGCN chapter. Plant SGCN, which were included in an appendix in the 2015 SWAP, are now in the body of the SGCN chapter and include the same information as animal species. Although plants are not eligible for funding under the State Wildlife Grant Program, they are eligible through other funding sources and their importance to animals and natural communities should not be overlooked. The complete list of plants can be found in Chapter 4: SGCN.
In addition to the SGCN and Natural Community revisions, LDWF worked with the USFWS Science Applications Migratory Birds Program and the Southeast Conservation Adaptation Strategy (SECAS) to redefine the 2015 Conservation Opportunity Areas (COAs). This resulted in fewer COAs (11 instead of 30). The development of the COAs were data informed and defined, in part, by specific conservation actions. The new COA boundaries and information about the COA process are located in Chapter 7.
Other changes in the 2025 SWAP include a chapter written by SECAS staff on regional and landscape conservation (Chapter 10). This chapter has information about Regional SGCN (RSGCN) and the potential impacts of climate change on SGCN and their habitats and includes a case study provided by the Southeast Climate Adaptation Science Center. SECAS also provided a new standalone Threats chapter (Chapter 8), which details Louisianaspecific threats and actions.

## Summary of Changes Introduction

## CONSERVATION AND MANAGEMENT OF WILDLIFE AND FISHERIES RESOURCES IN LOUISIANA

The Louisiana Department of Wildlife and Fisheries (LDWF) is the government agency responsible for the conservation and management of fish and wildlife resources in the state, including aquatic and terrestrial vertebrate and invertebrate species, plant Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN), and natural communities. LDWF is authorized by the State Constitution and in revised statutes under Titles 36 and 56 to execute the laws enacted for the control and supervision of programs relating to the management, protection, conservation, and replenishment of wildlife, fish, and their habitats. Responsible stewardship of the state's renewable natural resources contributes to the quality of life and the economic well-being of the state, benefiting millions of consumptive and non-consumptive users annually. LDWF incorporates the use of good science, accurate information, and cuttingedge technology in carrying out its mission and continually looks for ways to improve the management of resources to ensure their sustainability and availability for all users now and in the future.

## CONGRESSIONAL MANDATE AND GUIDANCE

The State Wildlife Grants (SWG) Program was designed and created to provide annual allocations of funding for the development and implementation of on-the-ground efforts to benefit wildlife species and their habitats. This funding is intended to supplement, not duplicate, existing fish and wildlife programs by targeting species in greatest need of conservation, species indicative of the diversity and health of the states' wildlife resources, and species with low and declining populations, as deemed appropriate by the states' fish and wildlife agencies. In order to participate in the SWG Program, Congress mandated that each state develops a Comprehensive Wildlife Conservation Strategy, now known as the State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP). SWAPs are required to undergo a comprehensive revision every 10 years to ensure that they remain a relevant and effective tool for conservation planning and implementation and must address Eight Required Elements. These Eight Required Elements provide a framework for national consistency while enabling each state to determine how best to address those elements for themselves, preserving state-level authority in conservation decision making. In response, LDWF developed the Louisiana State Wildlife Action Plan to establish conservation needs and guide the use of SWG funds over the next 10 years. A crucial aspect of the SWAP is the identification of Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN), those species most in need of conservation action as identified by the state. Coauthored by Wildlife Diversity Program staff and peer-reviewed by a diverse group of colleagues from state and federal agencies, academia, non-governmental groups, citizen scientists, and others, the SWAP is truly a collaborative effort by subject-matter experts best positioned to positively affect conservation and restoration actions in our state. The first edition of the SWAP was submitted to the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) National Advisory Acceptance Team for approval and was subsequently approved in December 2005. The current document represents the third iteration of the Louisiana SWAP, which is the roadmap for nongame conservation in Louisiana, and is a pro-active document meant to identify actions needed to conserve wildlife and their habitats before species become too rare and restoration efforts too costly. In addition to the requirement of having a SWAP, state agencies are required to provide 25% of total project costs for planning grants and 35% for implementation grants, thereby leveraging resources to bolster federal investments.
Although plants are not currently an eligible expense for SWG funding, LDWF added plant SGCN to the appendix of the 2015 SWAP. This addition highlighted the need for the conservation of at-risk plants and prepared LDWF for anticipated funding sources that would support plant-based projects. For the 2025 SWAP, the USFWS Office of Conservation Investment approved the use of SWG funds to assess and officially add plant SGCN.
As such, 552 plant species have been added and given the same consideration as the wildlife and fish SGCN.

## THE SWAP IN LOUISIANA

The purpose of the SWAP is to provide a blueprint for guiding LDWF and conservation partners in the development and implementation of management actions for Louisiana's fish, wildlife, and plant species with emphasis on SGCN and associated habitats they depend upon. The SWAP has been in place for 20 years, during which time much progress has been made, and there has been a growing interest in regional, landscape level conservation efforts. As such, LDWF performed a comprehensive review of the status of all fish, wildlife, and plant species in Louisiana and completed a comprehensive review of the SWAP. For more information on the accomplishments of the SWAP and SWG since 2005, refer to: www.wlf.louisiana.gov/ page/state-and-tribal-wildlife-grants.
Additionally, conservation needs and priorities are fluid, and many data gaps, research needs, threats, and conservation opportunities have emerged since the first SWAP was approved in 2005. Because the focus of the SWAP is conservation of SGCN and natural communities, entire chapters are devoted to these topics: Chapter 4: Species of Greatest Conservation Need and Chapter 5: Natural Communities. The 2025 SWAP will provide a clear directive for the future management of these species in Louisiana; address localized, statewide, and regional concerns and implement conservation actions aimed at preventing federal listing under the ESA; and aim to recover species already listed. The SWAP will achieve this by 
(1)
 providing updated information on the current status of fish, wildlife, and plant SGCN in the state as well as important natural communities, (2) identifying threats to SGCN and natural communities and providing ways of addressing those threats, and (3) initiating new partnerships and developing existing partnerships to conserve biodiversity in Louisiana. In addition, the SWAP has identified Conservation Opportunity Areas (COA) where conservation can have the greatest impact.
By establishing a framework to measure the effectiveness of proposed conservation actions and monitoring results, this SWAP not only fulfills the requirements set forth by Congress, it also serves as a blueprint in providing the critical directives and management objectives LDWF will use to conserve the rich biodiversity of Louisiana for future generations.

## ADDRESSING DATA GAPS -CRAYFISHES IN LOUISIANA

Lack of data or data gaps represent one of the most commonly stated challenges in nongame conservation. In many instances, especially for arthropods, it is nearly impossible to determine if a species should be included on the SGCN list given information currently available. For many arthropods, data do not exist to substantiate listing as an SGCN or to show that populations are stable. A majority of the SGCN in the 2025 SWAP have research needs and conservation actions that aim to address data gaps such as (1) determine abundance, (2) determine distribution, and (3) monitor populations. State Wildlife Grants provide an excellent opportunity to fund projects with the main goal of obtaining baseline data and filling data gaps.
In the 2015 SWAP, the Crustacean Research and Survey Needs included: 
(1)
 research of life history strategies of all crayfish SGCN (2) estimate population abundance and trends, and (3) conduct drainage-wide surveys for all crayfish SGCN. Since 2015, LDWF has funded nine SWG projects focusing on crayfishes.
Of the more than 1,000 crayfish Element Occurrence Records (EORs) in the WDP database, more than 950 were added since 2017. These data were invaluable during the 2025 SWAP revision process and resulted in S-rank changes for 11 species (five determined to be more imperiled, six determined to be less imperiled), and the removal of two species from the SGCN list. Through these grants, LDWF was able to focus on specific data gaps for species with a last observation date recorded more than 20 years ago, those with few EORs, and those for which no published life history studies exist (e.g., Pontchartrain Painted Crayfish, Faxonius hobbsi).

## Examples of filled data gaps:

h Ouachita Fencing Crayfish, Faxonella creaseri, a species thought to be endemic to Louisiana was found in Arkansas.
With this newly discovered range extent, coupled with the fact that Louisiana records were more numerous than previously expected, the species was removed from the SGCN list 
(Himes 2022
). h Vernal Crayfish, Procambarus viaeviridis, historically known from only four sites in Louisiana, was discovered at 70 new locations 
(Himes 2022
). h Sabine Fencing Crayfish, Faxonella beyeri, range extent was found to be approximately twice as large as was historically known, and the species was removed from the SGCN list 
(Himes 2022
). h Blair's Fencing Crayfish, Faxonella blairi, was only known from Arkansas, Texas, and Oklahoma until it was confirmed to occur over a large area in Louisiana 
(Himes 2022)
.
For Chapter 2: State Overview

## GEOGRAPHY

Louisiana is located in the south-central United States at the terminus of the Mississippi River. Alexandria, Baton Rouge, Lafayette, Lake Charles, Monroe, New Orleans, and Shreveport are the major cities in the state (Figure 
2
.1).
The physiographic features include forested uplands, alluvial plains, coastal marshes, prairies, and bluffs. Natural elevations range from below sea level along the coast to 535 feet in the northern uplands. Land cover in the northwestern and western part of the state consists mostly of upland, mixed evergreen/ deciduous forests. The northeast and south-central parts of the state are heavily agricultural, with fragmented forests, including Bottomland Hardwood Forest. The Florida Parishes, located in the southeastern part of the state, consists primarily of upland forest dominated by evergreen/mixed hardwoods and Longleaf Pine (Pinus palustris) flatwoods. The southern portion of this region is dominated by marshes and forested wetlands. Southwestern Louisiana is dominated by upland or wetland scrub-shrub vegetation and agriculture and improved pasture in the historical Coastal Prairie region. Freshwater, Intermediate, Brackish, and Salt Marshes and, increasingly, open water comprise coastal Louisiana 
(Hartley et al. 2000)
.
Nearly all of coastal Louisiana is retreating the advance of the Gulf of America due to the containment of the Mississippi River for navigation and flood control. The Mississippi and Atchafalaya river deltas are the only coastal areas with significant sediment accretion and delta formation. The floodplain of the Atchafalaya River, the largest distributary of the Mississippi River, holds the best and largest example of forested swamp in the country.
Geologically, 80% of Louisiana's surface area consists of Quaternary Period sediments. Holocene alluvial sediments deposited by the Mississippi, Red, Ouachita, and other rivers constitute 55% of the surface area, and 25% of the state's surface is comprised of deposits associated with Pleistocene terraces.
Tertiary Period sediments account for the other 20%, principally on the Sabine uplift (which lies in the northwest portion of the state), and in the north Louisiana salt-dome basin. Within this area, Cretaceous rocks are present in a few small exposures on the tops of salt domes that have surface expression along with wind-blown loess deposits.
During glacial episodes in the Quaternary, sea levels dropped, and shorelines moved seaward. As a result, rivers flowing into the Gulf of America would deposit their sediments farther offshore, and fluvial deposits of sand, gravel, and silt, known as valley trains, were deposited in the lower Mississippi River valley. Remnants of valley trains deposited in the late Pleistocene can be found along the western edge of the Mississippi River flood plain in northeastern Louisiana. Areas adjacent to the Mississippi River valley were covered by loess, a wind-blown silt derived from glaciofluvial deposits. Loess deposits up to several meters thick remain preserved in areas flanking the Mississippi River valley.

## GEOLOGY COASTAL ZONE

Louisiana has over 3 million acres of coastal wetlands, which constitute about 40% (USGS 2014) of the remaining coastal marsh in the Lower 48 States. Louisiana's coastal zone can be divided into two distinct regions: the Chenier Plain, extending west from Vermilion Bay to Texas; and the Deltaic Plain, which extends from Vermilion Bay east to the Pearl River Basin on the Mississippi state line. Both areas were formed by historic patterns of sedimentation and erosion from the Mississippi River and its distributaries along with influences from the Gulf of America. Over the past several thousand years, these fluvio-deltaic processes created more than 4 million acres of coastal wetlands and gave rise to one of the most productive ecosystems in the United States. The Chenier Plain contains highly productive inland lakes and wetlands behind oak-covered remnant beach ridges (Cheniers) that parallel the coast. The Deltaic Plain is characterized by a vast system of low-lying wetlands and coastal Barrier Islands 
(Benoit 1997)
. These wetland ecosystems are of regional, national, and international significance in terms of their ability to support substantive marine fisheries (commercial and recreational) and a diverse array of wildlife SGCN 
(Remsen et al. 2019)
.
As highlighted within the State's 2023 
Coastal Master Plan (CPRA 2023)
, Louisiana is in the midst of a land loss crisis. Since the 1930s, nearly 2,000 square miles (1.28 million acres) of land have been lost, and without action, the state could lose up to another 3,000 square miles square miles (1.92 million acres) under the higher environmental scenario¹ over the next 50 years. The ongoing land loss crisis has been attributed to a host of natural and anthropogenic factors. Levees and floodgates have channeled the Mississippi River and its tributaries into the Gulf of America, depriving the coastal ecosystem of the fresh water and sediment it needs to survive. Dredging canals for oil and gas exploration and pipelines have resulted in the weakening of coastal marshes and have allowed salt water to spread further inland into coastal basins. In addition, sea level rise, subsidence (i.e. sinking of the land), storms, invasive species, and oil spills (with emphasis placed on the geographically-expansive Deepwater Horizon spill, 2010) have further stressed Louisiana's coastal environment.

## CLIMATE

The climate in Louisiana is relatively mild due to the subtropical influence of the Gulf of America and cooler, drier air from the Central Plains. Summers are hot and humid with an average high temperature of 93.3°F. Winters are mild with an average low temperature of 36.2°F. The highest temperature on record of 114°F was captured in northwest Louisiana in the town of Plain Dealing in 1936. The lowest temperature on record of -16°F was also captured in ¹ The higher environmental scenario includes +2.5' sea level rise by Year 50, a 10% increase in average storm intensity over 50 years, higher rates of subsidence, and other factors 
(CPRA 2023)
.
Chapter 2: State Overview
According to the U.S. Census Bureau (2021), Louisiana experienced a 2.7% increase in its population from 2010-2020. Nationwide, there was a 7.4% population increase with a 10.2% increase occurring in the South. As of 1 July 2024, there are 4,597,740 Louisiana residents and 107.8 persons per square mile and the median annual household income is $58,229 (U.S. Census Bureau 2021). Parishes with the greatest population decline (9% decrease or higher) include Allen, Assumption, Bienville, Cameron, Catahoula, Claiborne, Concordia, Iberville, Madison, Pointe Coupee, Red River, Tensas, Webster, West Carroll, and Winn (U.S. Census Bureau 2021). Parishes with the greatest population increase (9% increase or higher) include Ascension, Bossier, Calcasieu, Lafayette, Livingston, Orleans, St. Bernard, St. Tammany, Tangipahoa, and West Baton Rouge (U.S. Census Bureau 2021). Several of these parishes occur in the East Gulf Coastal Plain ecoregion and have experienced increased residential development resulting in habitat fragmentation, degradation, and loss.

## LOUISIANA FACTS

## POPULATION LIVING IN COASTAL PARISHES

In 2020, over 2 million residents, more than 50% of the state's population, lived in Louisiana's coastal parishes (USCB 2024, NOAA 2024).

## LOUISIANA FACTS

## ECONOMIC EXPORTS

Louisiana is the number one export state in the nation. The working coast annually sends more than $120 billion in goods and services to the rest of the United States and exports $36.2 billion internationally. Louisiana represents 20% of the nation's annual waterborne commerce. (CPRA 2023, 
Barnes et al. 2017)
.
northwest Louisiana in the city of Minden in 1899 
(NOAA 2024)
. Average annual temperatures have risen by 0.5°F since the beginning of the 20th century with the warmest five-year interval occurring from 
2019
-2024
(Kunkel et al. 2013
, Franskon et al. 2022
, NASA 2024)
.
Average annual precipitation ranges from 50 inches in the northern part of the state to 70 inches in the southeastern part of the state 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
. The average number of extreme precipitation events (4 inches or greater), which has been above average for over 40 years, reached record highs between 2015 and 2020 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
, including the devastating flood of 2016. Louisiana has the second-highest annual number of thunderstorms compared to others states in the contiguous United States 
(Frankson et al. 2022
). In addition, Louisiana is vulnerable to direct impacts from hurricanes, which occur approximately every three years. The state was in the direct path of six hurricanes between 2002 and 2009, the largest number of hurricanes during a five-year period. In 2020, Louisiana was hit by three hurricanes, including Hurricane Laura, the strongest hurricane to make landfall in the state since 1856. Laura produced 150 mph winds, had a 15-foot storm surge, and resulted in massive flooding. In 2021, Louisiana was hit by Hurricane Ida, which tied Hurricane Laura in intensity as a Category 4 hurricane.

## LAND OWNERSHIP

The state of Louisiana is 33.5 million acres, with 5.9 million acres covered by water and 27.6 million acres of land (U.S. Census Bureau 2010). Approximately 10% of the state is in federal or state ownership, and roughly 90% is privately owned 
(USGS 2000)
. The high degree of private land ownership highlights the vital role private landowners can play in the conservation of the state's wildlife and fisheries resources.
Forestland covers approximately 14.9 million acres ( ~50%) of the state's land area 
(LFA 2025)
. Private, non-industrial landowners own 9.5 million acres (64%) of the state's forestland, 3.9 million acres is owned by the forest industry (26%), and the remaining 1.5 million (10%) is in general public lands 
(LFA 2022)
. Agricultural lands which include crop, pasture, rangelands, and other operational lands (e.g., Conservation Reserve Program) cover 8 million acres (over 25%) of the state's land area (USDA 2022).

## ECONOMY

In addition to contributing to the conservation landscape of the Southeast, Louisiana's lands and waters also benefit the state's economy. The Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA) provides Outdoor Recreation Satellite Accounts for each state. In 2023, the BEA estimated that the outdoor recreation economy generated $9.2 billion in value for the state's Gross Domestic Product and another $2.8 billion in wages and salaries. In addition to recreational value, natural landscapes can support working lands such as agriculture and timber, and water resources such as commercial fisheries.
According to the Louisiana Seafood Promotion and Marketing Board, it is estimated that $2.4 billion is generated annually from the production, harvest, and sale of fish, crawfish, oyster, shrimp, crab, and alligator (2025).

## POPULATION TRENDS

Data provided by the latest National Survey of 
Fishing, Hunting, and Wildlife-Associated Recreation (USDOI 2022)
 show that in 2022, 39.9 million people participated in fishing, 14.4 million in hunting, and 148.3 million in wildlife-watching activities including observing, feeding, or photography. Sportspersons and wildlife watchers across the United States spent $394.9 billion in 2022. Of that total amount, $91 billion was trip related; $179 billion was spent on equipment; and $124.9 billion was spent on items such as licenses, land leases, and property ownership. Hunters spent $45.2 billion on hunting; anglers spent $99.4 billion on fishing; and wildlife watchers spent $250.2 billion. Expenditures for wildlife watching include trip related expenses (e.g., goods, lodging, and transportation), equipment, magazine subscriptions, membership dues, contributions to conservation or wildlife-related organizations, land leasing and owning, and plantings.
The 2022 National survey divided states into nine geographic divisions. The West South Central (WSC) geographic division includes Louisiana, Arkansas, Oklahoma, and Texas. Of those who responded, 17% participated in angling (15% national average); 14% participated in hunting (6% national average); and wildlife-watching, which was split into around-the-home and away-from-home, with approximately 53% (56% national average) and 23% (28% national average), respectively. Similarly, the 2019 Addendum to the 2016 National Survey of Fishing, Hunting, and Wildlife-Associated Recreation: Birding in the United States: A Demographic and Economic Analysis divided the United States into eight regions. The South Central region, which includes Louisiana and seven other states, featured 14% of the nation's total number of birders. The South Central region also had the highest avidity for birders at 130 days per year spent bird watching versus an average of 88 days for all regions. In 2016, nationwide birding expenditures "generated nearly $96 billion in total industry output" (USDOI 2019).

## ECOLOGICAL REGIONS AND AQUATIC DRAINAGE BASINS IN THE STATE

## TERRESTRIAL SYSTEMS

Louisiana contains a highly diverse ecological landscape, and the physiographic distribution of species often corresponds to ecological boundaries. Areas that share similar ecological attributes such as vegetation, soils, geology, climate, hydrology, and wildlife can be classified as ecoregions. For species and habitats, this strategy will follow the ecoregional habitat classification developed by The Nature Conservancy (TNC), which is adapted from 
Bailey (1995)

## LOUISIANA FACTS

## FUR HARVEST

Hunting and trapping furbearers generate at least $1.75 million annually for coastal communities (LDWF 2024). Flatwoods Savanna and Woodland were once predominant natural communities. Other natural communities found in the EGCP are Acidic Bay Forest, Shortleaf Pine/Oak-Hickory Woodland, Eastern Hillside Seepage Bog, Slash Pine-Pondcypress Woodland, Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest, and Small Stream Forest.

## LOUISIANA FACTS

## MIGRATORY GAME BIRDS

The Florida Parishes have had one of the largest human population growth rates in the state. As this area continues to experience the pressures of urban expansion, challenges for balancing the needs of wildlife with those of humans will increase.

## Upper East Gulf Coastal Plain (Upper Loess Plain)

The Upper East Gulf Coastal Plain (UEGCP) ecoregion includes portions of five states from western Kentucky and Tennessee south through Mississippi and Alabama and into Louisiana in West Feliciana Parish (Figure 
2
.5). Within this small area of the state, Southern Mesophytic Hardwood Forest is the predominant natural community type that developed on loess hills with steep ravines and intermittent or spring-fed streams. Other associated community types include Hardwood Slope Forest, Bottomland Hardwood Forest, Small Stream Forest, and Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest found in low-lying areas of this ecoregion.

## Mississippi River Alluvial Plain

The Mississippi River Alluvial Plain (MRAP) ecoregion extends from the southern tip of Illinois down through southeastern Missouri, encompasses all of eastern Arkansas, the delta region of Mississippi and into northeast Louisiana (Figure 
2
.6). It then continues south along the Mississippi River, terminating where bottomland forests meet coastal marshes.
The MRAP, rich in alluvial sediments, is known primarily for Bottomland Hardwood Forest as well as associated Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest natural communities.
In addition, the northeastern portion of this ecoregion contains both Wet and Mesic Hardwood Flatwoods found on Macon Ridge.

## Upper West Gulf Coastal Plain

The Upper West Gulf Coastal Plain (UWGCP) ecoregion extends from south-central and southwestern Arkansas to the extreme southeastern portion of Oklahoma, south into eastern Texas, and east to parts of northern Louisiana (Figure 
2
.7).
The UWGCP was once recognized as the Shortleaf Pine/ Oak-Hickory Woodland region of Louisiana, existing on sandy and clayey uplands north of the range of Longleaf Pine in the West Gulf Coastal Plain 
(Newton 1972)
. Upon settlement, the majority of the Shortleaf Pine (Pinus echinata) was logged and replaced, most recently, by Loblolly Pine (Pinus taeda) plantations. However, some natural stands of Shortleaf Pine/Oak-Hickory Woodland still exist in this ecoregion. Xeric Sandhill Woodland occurs on deep sands in the UWGCP, while Hardwood Slope Forest

## LOUISIANA FACTS

## ALLIGATOR HARVEST

The annual economic contribution of Louisiana's American Alligator (Alligator mississippiensis) harvest totaled over $200 million in 2019 
(Chiessa 2021
). other major river systems including the Ouachita, Black, Calcasieu, Atchafalaya, Sabine, Pearl, Vermilion, and Mermentau, combine to incorporate more than 2,300 miles of navigable waterways.

## EAST GULF COASTAL PLAIN ECOREGION

Riparian areas, found immediately adjacent to stream banks, consist of narrow strips of land to broader bottoms that represent a transition between drier upland areas and streams. Forested riparian areas perform important ecological and environmental services. Riparian areas reduce the amount of sediment and nutrients that reach streams in surface runoff, provide wildlife habitat and wildlife corridors, and lower water temperatures by providing shade. Riparian zones also protect against stream bank erosion, reduce flood peaks by storing flood waters, provide a source of detritus and woody debris for aquatic and terrestrial organisms, and remove and store carbon from the atmosphere. These areas are, therefore, critical for maintaining healthy streams.
Man-made water bodies account for over 1 million surface acres of water. Toledo Bend Reservoir, located on the Louisiana-Texas border, is the largest man-made body of water in the South and fifth largest in surface acres in the United States. The reservoir covers 186,000 acres and has a controlled storage capacity of 4,477,000 acre-feet 
(1.4
 trillion gallons) at conservation pool (172.0 ft. MSL) level. The reservoir was formed when the Sabine River was impounded for hydroelectric purposes, water supply, and recreation.
A vast array of levees has been constructed for flood protection and to channelize water flow in the rivers for navigation. Louisiana has more than 2,000 miles of levees as well as other flood control structures along these rivers. The Sabine, Pearl, Atchafalaya, Ouachita, and Black rivers have all undergone alterations to their natural flow regime. In addition, the present conditions of the Pearl and Red rivers are heavily influenced by locks and dams constructed for navigation and to control water levels.
Chapter 2: State Overview

## Water Quality Assessments

The Louisiana Department of Environmental Quality (LDEQ) completed sampling of all 12 Louisiana watershed management basins (Figure 
2
.11) in 2024 including a total of 487 water body management subsegments. Designated use categories for the waters of Louisiana are agriculture, drinking water supply, ecological significance, fish and wildlife propagation, outstanding natural resource, oyster production, and primary and secondary contact recreation. Low dissolved oxygen, which is one of the parameters used to assess water quality for fish and wildlife propagation, is the most frequently reported cause of impairment across use categories. The second and third highest causes of water quality impairment are fecal coliform and enterococcus, respectively (LDEQ 2024).

## Louisiana's Natural and Scenic Rivers

Louisiana's Natural and Scenic River System (System) is one of the nation's largest, oldest, most diverse, and unique state river protection initiatives. It encompasses more than 60 streams or stream segments totaling over 3,000 miles in length (LDWF 2014) (Figure 
2
.10). In the early 1970s, the Louisiana Natural and Scenic River Act (Act) was passed by the Louisiana State Legislature, creating the System and setting certain requirements for a river to meet for inclusion in the program. The System was developed for the purpose of preserving, protecting, developing, reclaiming, and enhancing the wilderness qualities, scenic beauties, and ecological regimes of certain free-flowing Louisiana streams. The Act also contains a regulatory component and designated the LDWF Secretary to administer the System.
Six activities are prohibited on designated Natural and Scenic Rivers because of their detrimental ecological impacts on the streams. These prohibited uses include: 1. channelization, 2. clearing and de-snagging, 3. channel realignment, 4. reservoir construction, 5. commercial clearcutting of trees within 100 feet of the ordinary low water mark, and 6. use of a motor vehicle or other wheeled or tracked vehicle on a system stream, except for direct crossings by immediately adjacent landowners.
Scenic River Permits are required for all activities on or near System Rivers that may detrimentally impact the ecological integrity, scenic beauty, or wilderness qualities of those rivers. These permits, when granted, contain specific conditions aimed at preserving the stream's natural character and quality. Activities that must be permitted include, but are not limited to: bridge, pipeline, and power line crossings; bulkhead, pier, dock, and ramp construction; waste water discharges; and land development adjacent to the river.
For more information on Scenic Rivers: www.wlf.louisiana.gov/ page/scenic-rivers. (LEFT) The Comite River is a designated Louisiana Scenic River, known for its natural beauty and recreational opportunities. (ABOVE) Cane Bayou is also a designated Louisiana Scenic River and a popular destination for kayaking, canoeing, and bird watching.

## LOUISIANA'S NATURAL AND SCENIC RIVERS

Coastal water bodies in the southwest region are enclosed estuaries rather than the expansive open bays of the southeast. These estuaries are heavily impacted by human marsh management and hydrological and navigational changes to the landscape.
LDWF biologists in the Marine Fisheries Section focus sampling efforts on the seven Coastal Study Areas (CSAs; Figure 
2
.12).
The CSAs are delineated based on the major coastal basins.
Sampling is standardized and focuses on shrimp, crab, oyster, and coastal finfish (e.g., Red Drum [Sciaenops ocellatus] and Spotted Seatrout [Cynoscion nebulosus]).
LOUISIANA'S COASTAL STUDY AREA Chapter 3: Approach
The task of revising the State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP) was coordinated by Louisiana Department of Wildlife and Fisheries (LDWF) staff from the Office of Wildlife and the Office of Fisheries. Input was provided by LDWF taxa team experts and solicited from representatives of other state and federal agencies, universities, non-governmental and environmental organizations, corporations, industry, and the citizens of Louisiana. The revision of the SWAP would not have been possible without their feedback. This chapter presents the approach used during the 2025 revision process.

## ORGANIZATIONAL STRUCTURE

A Core Team (Appendix A) was formed to oversee the revision of the SWAP. This committee included LDWF representatives from the Office of Wildlife and the Office of Fisheries. The Core Team was responsible for reviewing each section of the 2015 SWAP to identify areas in need of update and to find ways to streamline and minimize redundancy of the 2025 SWAP.
To assist with the comprehensive revision, 10 taxa teams (Table 
3
) were formed including Mollusk, Crustacean, Non-crustacean Arthropod (i.e. arachnids and insects), Inland Fish, Marine Fish, Amphibian and Reptile, Bird, Mammal, Plant, and Natural Community. All members of the Core Team and taxa teams were LDWF employees and included staff with appropriate expertise from the Wildlife Diversity Program, Wildlife, Inland Fisheries, and Marine Fisheries (Appendix A). The taxa teams were tasked with review of the Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) list from the 2015 SWAP1, removal and addition of SGCN or natural communities, review and revision of research needs and conservation actions, review and update of global and state ranks (G-and S-ranks), reassessment of threats, and identification of outside technical experts. In addition, an Invasive Species Team was formed. This team assessed species from the 2015 SWAP and added or removed taxa as appropriate; team members also revised the tier definitions and placed species in their respective tiers. The total number of members for each SWAP comprehensive revision team is listed in Table 
3
.
In the 2015 SWAP, all research needs and conservation actions were listed in text, oftentimes repeating the same actions for multiple species. To reduce redundancy, the 2025 SWAP captures research needs and conservation actions for each taxon in tabular format (i.e. action tables), reducing space, while providing an easy-to-read snapshot of species conservation. Research needs and conservation actions relevant to the majority of taxa were used to populate the header fields of a draft action table, upon which other action tables could be based. Taxa teams then created action tables for their respective taxa, populating the leftmost column with SGCN and then marking needs or actions applicable to each species. For consistency purposes, column headings were kept the same for each taxa group as much as possible. However, to accommodate distinct differences among taxa groups, additional headings or slight changes in wording were used. In addition, each taxa team reviewed the research needs and conservation actions in the 2015 SWAP and determined if those actions were (1) still applicable, (2) successfully completed, or (3) no longer relevant. When a need or action was selected for every SGCN in a taxa group (e.g., Promote quality habitat was selected for all mollusk SGCN), that item was removed from the action table and placed in a text section for that taxa group's general research needs and conservation actions. When appropriate, guild specific or species specific actions were added as text under the action tables. Invasive Species 9

Chapter 3: Approach

## COORDINATION WITH PARTNERS AND THE PUBLIC

Prior to the initiation of this comprehensive revision, a SWAP survey, similar to those used in other states, was sent to all LDWF employees (approximately 750 individuals), over 120 partners and 140 individuals from the State Wildlife Grant (SWG) distribution list, and representatives of seven native tribes. The survey investigated how LDWF staff, partners, stakeholders, and tribal representatives utilized the 2015 SWAP, and chapters or appendices that were the most frequently referenced. In addition, it sought recommendations for the 2025 revision.
The survey was also used to identify those who wanted to be involved in the revision process.
LDWF received 102 responses2. Of those responses, 62 respondents were aware of the SWAP and used the document; 21 respondents were aware of the SWAP, but never used the document; and 17 respondents were not aware of the SWAP.
When asked how respondents had used the SWAP, responses included as a reference document for drafting SWG proposals (41) and non-SWG proposals (28), for conservation planning (33), to prioritize conservation planning and implementation (21), and other 
(10)
. The majority of respondents use the SWAP primarily to obtain information on SGCN or natural communities. Identifying specific research questions, understanding conservation strategies that can be implemented, and addressing specific management issues were ranked in the middle by respondents. The Species of Greatest Conservation Need (66), Habitat Conservation (32), Conservation Opportunity Areas (23), and Research and Monitoring (24) chapters and the SGCN List (23) were the sections of the SWAP ranked as most useful.
In addition to incorporating information and feedback received from the SWAP survey, LDWF worked with partners to refine the 2015 COAs, revise the Conservation Opportunity Areas chapter, and add two new chapters including Chapter 8: Threats and Chapter 10: Louisiana: A Regional Perspective.
The Conservation Opportunity Areas (Chapter 7) revision, led by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service's Science Applications Migratory Bird Program with assistance from the Southeast Conservation Adaptation Strategy (SECAS), began in 2020. The Louisiana: A Regional Perspective, spurred by a 2018 resolution from the Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (AFWA) to focus efforts on landscape conservation, was written by SECAS and the Southeast Climate Adaptation Science Center. This chapter, which uses the SECAS Southeast Conservation Blueprint, includes Regional Species of Greatest Conservation Need (RSGCN), Louisiana-specific climate change and climate resiliency information, a case study on a subset of Louisiana amphibians and their climate vulnerability and adaptability as well as a section on working with partners across the region.
In addition, SECAS wrote Threats (Chapter 8), which includes Louisiana-specific information based on Conservation Measures Partnership's Direct Threats Classification v 2.0 (2016).

## SPECIES OF GREATEST CONSERVATION NEED

The Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) list from the 2015 SWAP was the starting point for the 2025 SGCN list (Appendix D). LDWF subject matter experts (Appendix A) reviewed their respective SGCN lists, and species were suggested for removal or addition. Subject matter experts outside of LDWF were identified for each taxa group (Appendix C) and were asked to 
(1)
 provide input on the 2015 SGCN list, including suggestions and justifications for species' removal and addition;
(2) provide data that could be used for determining S-ranks; and (3) fill out Species Assessment Forms (identical to those used during the 2015 revision). LDWF taxa team subject matter experts examined all of the outside reviewer input, discussed the recommendations of those experts, and revised the SGCN list accordingly.
Considerations used to determine inclusion or removal of a species from the list were S-rank, Louisiana's responsibility (i.e. when a significant portion of a species' range or population is confined to Louisiana), known or potential threats, status and trends, and expert opinion. In many instances, a species was placed on the list due to lack of data. For an explanation of federal and state listing categories and ranks refer to Appendix J.
In addition to the 2015 SGCN list, taxa teams also considered lists suggested by the Southeast Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (SEAFWA) SWAP Standardization Sub-committee. This sub-committee recommended that each state in the Southeast consider for inclusion those species listed under the Endangered Species Act, those considered Regional SGCN, and species ranked as G1-G2 by NatureServe. Inherent overlap occurred among these lists.
A concerted effort was also made during this revision to fully incorporate plant SGCN into the 2025 SWAP primarily utilizing plant SGCN nomenclature from Flora of the Southeastern U.S. 
(Weakley et al. 2025)
. In 2015, plant SGCN were included in the SGCN list, but were not included in the body of the SWAP and were not given the same amount of consideration as animal SGCN and natural communities. Although plants are not eligible for funding under the SWG Program, state fish and wildlife agencies were allowed to use U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service SWG funds for the development of plant SGCN lists and the incorporation of plant SGCN into 2025 SWAPs. This was due, in part, to potential for new mechanisms that would allow funding for projects focused solely on plants.
Chapter 3: Approach

## PROCEDURE FOR 2035 SWAP COMPREHENSIVE REVISION NATURAL COMMUNITIES

As with SGCN, the list of natural communities from the 2015 SWAP was the starting point for the 2025 natural communities list (Appendix E). All natural communities were reviewed by the Plant and Natural Community taxa teams and included updates to natural community global and state ranks, status, and extent maps. The taxa teams also revised natural community associations, when needed. In addition, information on basins and marine substrates were updated by LDWF staff in the Office of Fisheries. A list of select obligate or near obligate SGCN that occur in each natural community was provided by the respective taxa teams and are featured for each natural community, where applicable. For a broader list of animal SGCN that occur in each ecological subtype, refer to Appendix F, and for a list of animal and plant SGCN that occur in each natural community, refer to Appendix G. Over the next ten years we will review available data on any SGCN not covered by Appendix G and place them in their respective natural communities.

## PUBLIC AND PARTNER INVOLVEMENT

In August 2025, a draft of the 2025 SWAP was sent to 122 external reviewers (Appendix B) for comment. This included 38 individuals from 21 state and federal government agencies, 38 individuals from 33 non-governmental entities, 33 individuals from 13 academic institutions, and six private individuals. In addition, six individuals from five Native American Tribes (four federally recognized, one state-recognized) and one Native Tribe Representative were given the opportunity to provide feedback. This list of external reviewers included those who took the SWAP pre-survey and indicated a desire to assist with draft review, past and current SWG recipients, external technical experts, entities and individuals identified by LDWF staff, scientific research and collecting permit holders, and others. In addition, at their discretion, LDWF staff forwarded the draft SWAP to individuals, partners, working groups, and listservs. The draft, accompanied by a standardized template for comments, was also made publicly available on the LDWF website. During the open comment period of 22 days, all feedback was emailed to the SWAP Coordinator and logged. A total of 17 responses was received including three from the public, nine from individuals representing three government agencies, and five from individuals representing four universities. All feedback was reviewed by the appropriate taxa team(s) and the principle authors and incorporated, as needed, prior to submittal to the USFWS. A copy of the approved 2025 SWAP will be available on the LDWF website, which will also feature opportunities for public and partner involvement.
The Louisiana Department of Wildlife and Fisheries commits to a comprehensive review and revision of the 2025 SWAP by 1 October 2035. Although it is too soon to determine the exact method for the 2035 revision, it is anticipated that an approach similar to the 2025 revision will be used. Over the next 10 years, LDWF will use the approach and monitoring framework outlined in this SWAP. During this time, if it is recognized that mid-cycle revisions are necessary, LDWF will initiate a minor revision; this will allow the SWAP to be an adaptive document that presents the best conservation measures for SGCN and their habitats. Similarly, LDWF will use the Emerging Issues process to address any new conservation issues that may arise.
Chapter 4: Species of Greatest Conservation Need
Chapter 4: Species of Greatest Conservation Need
The primary focus of the Louisiana State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP) is the recovery of Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN), those wildlife or plant species in need of conservation action within Louisiana. SGCN may be species for which population declines have been documented or suspected, or species that may be subject to population declines within the next 10 years.
Other species are included because more data are needed to accurately determine their status (data gaps). The identification of SGCN is Element # 1 of the Eight Required Elements for SWAPs. This chapter also addresses Elements # 3 (threats to SGCN and priority research and survey needs for SGCN) and # 4 (description of conservation actions necessary to conserve SGCN).
For details on the approach the Louisiana Department of Wildlife and Fisheries (LDWF) used to revise the SGCN list during the 2025 revision, refer to Chapter 3: Approach. There are a total of 428 animal and 552 plant SGCN identified in this 3rd iteration of the SWAP. A total of 64 animal SGCN were removed, and 133 were added (for details see the Summary of Changes, pages 13-16). In addition, plants were officially added to the SGCN list.
Research and conservation needs and actions and threats are described for each taxonomic group below. These items are not exhaustive and are not intended as the only conservation priorities for these species.
The 2025 SWAP captures research needs and conservation actions for each taxon in tabular format. Column headings were kept the same for each taxa group as much as possible. However, to accommodate distinct differences among taxa groups, additional headings or slight changes in wording were used. When a need or action was selected for every SGCN in a taxa group, that item was removed from the action table and placed in a text section for that taxa group's general research needs and conservation actions. When appropriate, guild-specific or species-specific actions were also added to the text sections.
Note that SGCN tables have been organized alphabetically by taxonomy.

## SGCN BY TAXONOMIC GROUP

## MOLLUSKS

Good water quality starts with healthy aquatic systems.
Freshwater mussels act as natural filters that help purify Louisiana's streams, bayous, and lakes. In fact, by some estimates, a single mussel may filter between 10 and 20 gallons of water per day 
(Kreeger et al. 2018
). In areas with dense mussel aggregations, this could equal the complete filtering of a stream's daily discharge. Mussels remove suspended sediments such as dirt and debris as well as pharmaceuticals and other chemical contaminants. In addition, mussels are a valuable food source for other species (e.g., Freshwater Drum [Aplodinotus grunniens],
North American River Otter [Lontra canadensis], and Muskrat), and hold important cultural significance (e.g., food, jewelry, and button making). Despite providing valuable ecosystem services, freshwater mussels receive disproportionately less attention than more charismatic species. Currently, North American freshwater mussels are one of the world's most imperiled taxonomic groups 
(Master et al. 2000)
. Habitat destruction and alteration, whether direct (e.g., sand and gravel mining, dam construction) or indirect (e.g., altered flow regimes), often act cumulatively and cause declines. Freshwater mussels have a unique life history, and most rely on fish to act as hosts for larval mussels, called glochidia. Some mussels parasitize specific species of host fish, while others use a variety of host species. In fact, mussels have developed elaborate adaptations to entice fish to approach, ensuring that the glochidia attach to the gills or fins of the hosts. Appropriate host fish provide nutrients to the developing mussel larvae for days to weeks prior to the mussels releasing into the substrate as juveniles. In addition, because fish are more mobile, this relationship ensures maximized dispersal of mussels. Louisiana mussel SGCN would not persist without their hosts.
Over 300 species from two families -Unionidae (298) and Margaritiferidae (5) -occur in the United States 
(FMCS 2023)
.
Of those species, more than 90 are listed as federally threatened or endangered. The southeastern U.S. contains the greatest mussel diversity with approximately 270 species 
(Neves et al. 1997)
, 63 of which are currently known to occur in Louisiana 
(Vidrine 2023)
. Of those, 33 species of freshwater mussels are listed as Louisiana SGCN (Table 
4
.1) including the federally listed Rabbitsfoot (threatened), Pink Mucket (endangered), Fat Pocketbook (endangered), Inflated Heelsplitter (threatened), and Louisiana Pearlshell (threatened). The Louisiana Pearlshell is the only federally listed mussel endemic to Louisiana. In 2023, U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) proposed to list two additional species, the Texas Heelsplitter as endangered and the Louisiana Pigtoe as threatened.
In addition to 33 freshwater mussels, one terrestrial snail is also included on the SGCN list. Five marine mollusks are included on Louisiana's SGCN list due to their dependence on highly restricted habitats. At least three of these marine mollusks are known only from Marine Submerged Aquatic Vegetation at the Chandeleur Islands. Mollusk SGCN occupy diverse habitats from small, freshwater, inland streams to the shallow waters of the Gulf of America, demonstrating the complex nature of conserving Louisiana's mollusks. 

## MOLLUSK SGCN

## Scientific

## MOLLUSK THREATS

Like many aquatic organisms, mollusks in Louisiana face a variety of threats, many of which are associated with habitat alterations and aquatic connectivity. Modifications to streams can have significant environmental impacts. These modifications can include the construction and operation of aquatic barriers like dams and weirs and the removal of snags and other woody debris for aesthetic reasons, flood prevention, or navigational purposes. Sand and gravel mining operations and improperly designed culverts are significant contributors to headcutting, a process that erodes and degrades streambeds. Additionally, excessive extraction of ground or surface water intensifies the degradation.
All of these activities have the potential to decrease habitat quality and quantity as well as alter flow, aquatic connectivity, and water chemistry. In addition, reservoirs without constant flow mandates pose a threat to areas below dams during droughts or in seasons with lower than normal rainfall.
Anthropogenic activities such as infrastructure construction and maintenance projects, the use of off-road vehicles (ORVs), and the clearing of riparian zones can cause sedimentation. The subsequent increase in suspended solids in the water column decreases food availability, interferes with filter feeding and respiration, and impedes aspects of mussel-host fish relationships. Very high levels of sedimentation can cause smothering and mortality 
(Goldsmith et al. 2020)
. The input of household, industrial, and agricultural effluents, specifically ammonia, can be lethal to mollusks, which are extremely sensitive to changes in water quality. Organic enrichment of streams due to unnatural accumulations of decaying invasive vegetation, agricultural runoff, and wild or domestic animal feces may act in tandem with other stressors. When driven across or through stream beds, off-road vehicles may be responsible for the direct destruction of mussel aggregations. Many mussels and their host fish, already living at the upper limits of their thermal tolerances 
(Khan et al. 2019)
, are susceptible to the clearing of vegetation in riparian zones and the resultant increase in water temperatures due to shade reduction 
(Sullivan and
Adam 1989, Rutherford et al. 2004
).
Non-native animal species such as Feral Hogs (Sus scrofa) cause direct destruction of mussel beds, changes in microbial community composition, and increases in organic enrichment, leading to decreases in freshwater mussel diversity and abundance 
(Kaller et al. 2007
). The Limpkin (Aramus guarauna), a marsh bird specializing in gastropod predation, has rapidly expanded its range into Louisiana, presumably capitalizing on the invasion of apple snails (Pomacea spp.). These birds have been observed feeding upon freshwater mussels in Louisiana; however, potential impacts to local populations from this novel predator are only beginning to be investigated.
Due to their unique life history characteristics, mollusks are one of the most susceptible taxa to climate change 
(Halpern and Kappel 2012
, Poff et al. 2012
, Ingram et al. 2013)
. Sea level rise may result in the loss of seagrass beds associated with Barrier Islands and can increase the salinity of coastal streams, potentially reducing habitat suitability. More frequent, high intensity tropical cyclones can decimate seagrass beds, further reducing available habitat. Predicted decreases in precipitation could result in loss of habitat and increased salinity. One of the most widespread climate related threats to freshwater mussels is elevated water temperatures. Many mussel populations are already near their thermal maxima, and additional increases in temperature could cause altered metabolic rates which would decrease growth and reproduction 
(Ganser et al. 2015)
. Droughts, also expected to increase in severity, lead to reduced streamflow, which increases the risk of desiccation, but also contributes to warming water temperatures and decreased oxygen levels. The link between mussels and their host fish means that any reduction in abundance of host fish, shifts in timing of fish presence, or alteration of their distribution, could have serious consequences. Many of the threats affecting mollusks occur simultaneously, and the negative effects are cumulative. 

## COMMON NAME SCIENTIFIC NAME

## CRUSTACEANS

Although Louisiana is well known for its substantial crawfish consumption and its associated annual $250 million annual industry (LSU AgCenter 2023), few are aware of the diversity in crawfish species that exists within its borders. Thirty-nine species of crawfish, or in scientific terms, crayfish, are known to occur in Louisiana 
(Walls 2009
, Johnson et al. 2021
, USFWS 2024
, Himes 2022)
. More than half of these taxa ( 
22
) are considered SGCN by the Wildlife Diversity Program, including at least two endemic or apparently endemic crayfishes: the Teche Painted Crayfish and the Calcasieu Creek Crayfish (Table 
4
.3). The population viability of many of these rare crayfishes is threatened because of their small ranges; habitat degradation severe enough to cause the extirpation of these species at a single site or even a few sites could also lead to their extinction 
(Taylor et al. 1996)
.
Crayfishes are considered one of the most imperiled taxonomic groups in the United States, second only to freshwater mussels 
(Master 1990
). The Slenderwrist Burrowing Crayfish, the Kisatchie Painted Crayfish, and the Calcasieu Painted Crayfish (a subspecies of the Louisiana Painted Crayfish), have been petitioned for federal listing as threatened or endangered under the ESA. Due to their popularity as a food source, Louisiana's two commercial crayfish species have been the subject of intense research. However, the remaining native species have received comparably little attention despite the fundamental role they play in nutrient cycling by facilitating detrital decomposition and by serving as prey items for aquatic and terrestrial vertebrates 
(Reynolds et al. 2013
, Taylor et al. 2019)
. In addition, crayfishes are often used as indicators of habitat or water quality, and the burrows they construct not only provide refugia for other organisms, but also assist with soil integration and aeration 
(Reynolds et al. 2013
, Taylor et al. 2019)
.
Much has been accomplished regarding the geographic distribution of several crayfish SGCN since the publication of Louisiana's 2015 SWAP. Primarily through State Wildlife Grants, various researchers conducted extensive field inventories for several crayfish SGCN. In most cases, these surveys resulted in larger ranges of crayfish SGCN than previously thought, and S-ranks improved for some of these species. In addition, during this time period, four new species were added to the list of Louisiana crayfish fauna: the Slenderwrist Burrowing Crayfish, Blair's Fencing Crayfish, and two recently described species in the genus Creaserinus, for which common names have yet to be assigned 
(Johnson et al. 2021)
. While this progress is encouraging, the discovery of several new species in the state only highlights the data gaps that remain to be addressed.

## CRUSTACEAN THREATS

Freshwater taxa are at a greater risk of extinction than many other groups, and crayfishes are no exception 
(Darwall et al. 2011, Riccicardi and
Ramsussen 1999)
. Habitat loss, habitat degradation, invasive species, climate change, severe weather, and over-harvesting all pose threats to native crayfish species 
(Reynolds et al. 2013
, Sibley et al. 2011
, Taylor et al. 2007
). In addition to these, 
Richman and colleagues (2015)
 note urban development, pollution, damming, and water management as problems in North America.
Anthropogenic activities such as infrastructure construction and maintenance projects, sand and gravel mining, the use of ORVs, and clearing of riparian zones all cause siltation. Siltation can be problematic for all crayfishes but can be especially challenging for stream dwelling species that may not be adapted to tolerate increased turbidity. The suspended particles make respiration challenging, and the resulting stress makes crayfishes more susceptible to parasites and other threats 
(Rosewarne et al. 2014)
. Clearing of vegetation, especially shade trees, along waterways can negatively impact crayfishes by increasing water temperature and reducing available dissolved oxygen; this is especially problematic for species that are adapted to cooler (<78°F) water 
(Walls 2009)
. Furthermore, application of herbicides to control vegetation in waterways and along roadsides may also threaten some crayfishes by reducing cover, food availability, and through direct mortality caused by sensitivity to herbicides 
(Walls 2009)
. Some crayfish species may use ditches as alternative habitat when higher quality, more preferred habitat, such as ephemeral wetlands, is limited.
When crayfish colonize anthropogenic habitats such as ditches, maintenance of these waterways, especially sediment removal, may cause significant negative effects. In addition, improperly designed culverts can produce higher velocities benefiting certain non-native species better adapted to traveling upstream in fast-flowing environments than slower moving native crayfishes. Limitations to dispersal of native crayfishes could limit population size, reduce migration between populations, decrease gene flow, and increase competition with non-natives 
(Foster and Keller 2011)
.
Invasive species such as Feral Hogs can directly prey upon native crayfishes and indirectly affect them by modifying habitat 
(Wood and Roark 1980)
. Many researchers consider non-native crayfishes to be the greatest threat to native crayfishes via direct competition and through their influence on the abundance of native vegetation 
(Lodge et al. 2000
, Walls 2009
). Introduction of non-native crayfishes could occur through the pet-trade, bait-bucket dumping, and hitchhiking on fish-hatchery shipments. Potential non-native invaders include the Rusty Crayfish (Faxonius rusticus), Virile Crayfish (Faxonius virilis), Calico Crayfish (Faxonius immunis), and Australian Red Claw Crayfish (Cherax quadricarinatus) 
(Walls 2009
, Pedraza-Lara et al. 2023)
. In addition to non-native crayfishes, native crayfishes introduced from a neighboring drainage could have catastrophic results for the naturally occurring crayfish species in that drainage 
(Walls 2009)
.
A large number of crayfishes in the southeastern United States are vulnerable to climate change 
(Hossain et al. 2018
).
Many species are well adapted to survive infrequent rainfall or minor droughts; however, prolonged periods of drought or consecutive yearly droughts have the potential to greatly reduce reproductive output and locally extirpate species 
(Walls 2009)
. In addition, crayfish species occurring near the coast have likely evolved adaptations to handle infrequent, temporary salinity increases caused by tropical cyclones. Combined with the persistent threat of saltwater intrusion, as tropical cyclones becomes more frequent, it is possible that the near-coastal species will reach the limit of what they are biologically able to tolerate. Unlike near-coastal species, no evidence exists that non-coastal species can tolerate even the slightest increase in salinity 
(Walls 2009)
. 
Richman and colleagues (2015)
 found that these and other threats to crayfishes are likely to increase in both magnitude and extent, emphasizing a need to develop crayfish conservation plans. 

## Crustacean General Research Needs and

## NON-CRUSTACEAN ARTHROPODS

Despite their substantial contributions to food webs and ecosystem services, non-crustacean arthropods remain poorly known 
(Noriega et al. 2018)
. In fact, Louisiana's list of non-crustacean arthropod SGCN, in part, reflects this lack of information, as several species have yet to be assigned an S-rank. Louisiana's non-crustacean arthropod SGCN include one species of scorpion and >80 species of insects ( 
(Bar-On et al. 2018
, Eggleton 2020
, Rosenberg et al. 2023)
.
Whether from lack of funding, disdain as pests, or through general apathy, insect conservation has only recently been recognized as a critical need. Indeed, the proportion of refereed journal articles published on the decline of insects vs. simply insects has dramatically increased since the publication of Louisiana's 2015 SWAP 
(Eggleton 2020)
. Reports of the magnitude of declines have varied widely from those suggesting impending global collapse to those of more moderate and measured concern (Hallman 
et al. 2017
, Cardoso et al. 2019
, Seibold et al. 2019
, Wagner 2020
, Daskalova et al. 2021
, Sanchez-Bayo and Wyckhuys 2021)
. However, little doubt remains that many insects and other noncrustacean arthropods are experiencing observable population declines. Determining the scope and severity of those declines is exceptionally challenging 
(Eggleton 2020
, Jactel et al. 2020
, Wagner 2020)
, particularly given the complexity and diversity of arthropods and the lack of even the most basic information.
Regardless of the existing data gaps, conservationists agree that proactive measures should be taken immediately to help minimize the loss of non-crustacean arthropods 
(Forister et al. 2019
, Jactel et al. 2020
, Wagner et al. 2021
). Otherwise, negative impacts from the loss of these species -already linked to population declines of insectivorous birds 
(Manning and
Sullivan 2021, Tallamy and
Shriver 2021)
 and shifts in plant abundance and phenology 
(Ulrich et al. 2020
) -will likely extend to that of human civilization. The ecosystem services provided by native insects, as pollinators, predators, decomposers, and more, amount to >$55 billion annually in the United States 
(Losey and Vaughan 2006)
. 

## NON-CRUSTACEAN ARTHROPOD SGCN

## NON-CRUSTACEAN ARTHROPOD THREATS

Louisiana's non-crustacean arthropods are threatened by a variety of elements, most of which are directly related to human activities. Unlike most other SGCN, this taxa group contains aquatic and terrestrial organisms, and some species are obligates to both. Of the 86 at-risk species of noncrustacean arthropods in this plan, 50 species are terrestrial (e.g., most beetles, bees, butterflies, and others), and 36 are aquatic for at least a portion of their lives (e.g., dragonflies, damselflies, stoneflies, caddisflies, and others). The great diversity and complexity of their life histories magnify the level of conservation challenges. Likely, as is shared with most SGCN, the greatest threat to these species is an increase in urban and agricultural development, which reduces the quality and quantity of available habitat. An increase in agricultural intensification brings more opportunity to reduce plant diversity and introduce pesticides and other pollutants into the environment. Chemical waste and sedimentation runoff from agricultural, industrial, and residential developments decreases water quality. Light pollution negatively affects insects' abilities to navigate and communicate 
(Cardoso et al. 2020
) and can lead to collision mortality. Introduction of exotic animal species such as Red Imported Fire Ant (Solenopsis invicta) or those released as biocontrol agents can depredate, outcompete, and displace other species 
(Wagner and Driesche 2010)
. When exotic plants become established, some insects (e.g., butterflies, moths, etc.) may lose appropriate host plants for their larvae or food plants for adults. The movement to essentially sanitize neighborhoods by promotion of lawns and exotic plants has been extremely detrimental; even a small patch of native plants can make a positive measurable difference 
(Eggleton 2020
, Harvey et al. 2022
, Donkersley et al. 2023)
.
Predicted alterations to ecosystems from climate change -increased weather extremes (e.g., increased rainfall and increased drought), increased magnitude and duration of maximum temperatures, and other effects -will alter the distribution, richness, and abundance of non-crustacean arthropods. Impacts may include desiccation and stressors to physiological systems, changes in plant distribution and flowering phenology, altered stream flows, expansion of invasive species, and more 
(Harvey et al. 2020
, Ulrich et al. 2020
, Harvey et al. 2022
, Prather et al. 2023)
. Finally, another emerging threat may be development of renewable energy; one study suggests that a single wind turbine, for example, may kill 40 million insects each year 
(Trieb 2018
, Voigt 2020)
. The loss of Louisiana's non-crustacean arthropods will require targeted research and conservation actions for effective solutions. 

## Non-crustacean Arthropod General Research Needs and

## INLAND FISHES

Louisiana's great diversity of inland fishes is largely attributed to the state's dynamic biogeographical landscape, which encompasses the East and West Gulf Coastal Plains, the Mississippi Basin, and a variety of aquatic habitats. These habitats range from small streams, bayous, oxbows, and backwater areas to coastal rivers, large river systems, and estuaries. The substrates in these environments vary from gravels to fine silts, with a few notable rock formations. Most of the state averages 50 to 65 inches of rain annually but is prone to drought or extremely high levels of rainfall in any given year. Drought and precipitation across the Mississippi Basin directly impacts Louisiana's water levels, resulting in variable and often harsh natural conditions for fish, especially when coupled with high average and extreme temperatures. Many spring-fed systems, however, offer stability in volume and temperature during periods of low rainfall.
Globally, freshwater ecosystems make up only 0.009% of Earth's water but hold 43% of fish biodiversity 
(Nelson 2006
, Helfman 2007)
. 
Jelks and colleagues (2008)
 reported that North America is home to 700 imperiled freshwater and diadromous (fish that migrate between fresh and salt water at various stages of their life cycle to spawn) species of fishes, a number that is likely significantly higher today due to taxonomic changes and the general negative trend in the status of freshwater species.
Freshwater migratory fishes are estimated to have declined worldwide by 81% between 1970 and 2020. However, based on a 1970 baseline, North American populations seem relatively stable 
(Deinet et al. 2025)
. This is likely due to most North American dam construction taking place from the 1930s to the 1950s, and an already-shifted baseline having been achieved by 1970. It can be inferred that similar declines have happened in North America, just earlier than less developed areas that are currently in a dam-building phase.
The diversity of fishes in Louisiana is rich with 196 primarily freshwater, euryhaline (fish that can tolerate a wide range of salinity), and diadromous fish species recorded in Louisiana 
(Doosey et al. 2021
). These species range from the smallest fish in North America, the Least Killifish (Heterandria formosa), to the giant Alligator Gar and Gulf Sturgeon. Fifty-five species are considered SGCN (Table 
4
.5), with 19 added and three removed in this update. Inland fish SGCN are found in all freshwater habitats of the state, with many specializing in big river and headwater stream habitats.
Fish are excellent indicators of aquatic health, which directly influences human health and quality of life. However, the life histories and ecological niches of many fish SGCN remain poorly understood. To improve their ecosystem management and conservation, more research is needed to better understand the complex habits, needs, and interactions of freshwater fishes in Louisiana. This understanding is essential for maintaining both biodiversity and the health of aquatic environments on which humans depend. 

## INLAND FISH SGCN

## INLAND FISH THREATS

Louisiana has the aquatic habitat diversity and productivity to support many abundant and resilient populations of inland fishes. Hot climate, combined with sluggish waters, extreme rainfall events, periods of extended flooding, periodic droughts, and high nutrient and organic loading, lead to low dissolved oxygen being a seasonal issue in much of the state. The state's aquatic systems, when healthy, are resilient to these periodic stressors, and rely on them, to an extent, to remain diverse and abundant. However, anthropogenic impacts can alter systems in such a way that they become out of balance and less resilient to the state's natural conditions.
Like many other freshwater organisms, inland fishes in Louisiana face a variety of anthropogenic threats including sand and gravel mining, increased sedimentation and nutrient loading from agriculture, transportation infrastructure (e.g., undersized culverts), ORV use in streams, stream modification, desnagging and clearing of riparian cover, decreased lateral and longitudinal connectivity (e.g., dams and flood control levees, respectively), unnaturally high connectivity (e.g., man-made canals), water withdrawals and diversions, invasive species, abandoned fishing gear, pollution, and compounding impacts from extreme weather events and climate change. Magnitude and timing of threats can cause varying impacts, and threats are often compounded by natural and man-made conditions.
A notable feature of Louisiana is its susceptibility to flooding due to a number of natural conditions including a high potential for extreme rainfall, a low-relief landscape, and its location at the mouth of the Mississippi Basin. Fish populations in the state have evolved to not only rebound from, but depend on, periodic high water events and seasonal fluctuations. However, humans have altered the landscape to reduce flood risk, resulting in many intended and unintended consequences. Many of the ecological consequences of past flood control measures, such as declining species and shifting aquatic communities, have become apparent in the 20th and 21st centuries. With advancing technologies and methods, Louisiana has the opportunity to not only mediate past watershed alterations, but to plan for more sustainable and ecologically beneficial methods such as naturebased solutions and green infrastructure through organizations like the Louisiana Watershed Initiative. Watershed-scale planning and implementation of beneficial projects could greatly relieve stresses and threats to many of our fish SGCN.

## MARINE FISHES

Marine fishes occur in a wide range of habitats from Louisiana's marshes and estuaries to open-ocean pelagic environments. Due to the productivity of coastal wetlands and bays, most of Louisiana's recreational and commercial harvest comes from species that are estuarine-dependent for some portion of their life cycle. That being said, the vast majority of the species present in Louisiana's coastal and estuarine environments are not recreationally or commercially exploited, and three species of marine fishes are federally listed in Louisiana: Giant Manta Ray (threatened), Oceanic Whitetip Shark (threatened), and Smalltooth Sawfish (endangered). Forty-six species of marine fishes have been identified as SGCN (Table 
4
.7) and play crucial roles in balancing the overall health and biodiversity of marine habitats. Due to a lack of targeted sampling effort and limited funding for marine SGCN, there are insufficient data on the life history, abundance, and habitat ranges and requirements for many of these species. Further understanding the spatial distribution and abundance of these under-studied species could be achieved through additional surveys. Data from commercial bycatch, targeted sampling regimes, and possible citizen science collaboration could provide valuable information on these poorly studied marine fishes. For many of these species, the collection of baseline data and life history information is high priority.
