## MARINE FISH THREATS

Many marine fish species use Louisiana's diverse estuarine habitats throughout their life cycles. In fact, estuaries, transition zones where freshwater rivers meet the sea, are among the most productive of all habitats. The associated freshwater marshes (utilized by marine species such as Southern Flounder), as well as brackish and saline marshes, comprise a substantial portion of Louisiana's coastal environments. Unfortunately, despite their critical roles in providing storm buffers and nursery habitat for aquatic life, Louisiana's coastal marshes are becoming increasingly fragmented and degraded due to human activity and natural processes. Human activities like the creation of canals, levees, spillways, locks and dams, and other aquatic barriers can have significant negative impacts to natural hydrology, limiting movement of organisms, and, potentially, causing increases in salinity in previously freshwater-dominated systems. Increases in salinity can kill less salt-tolerant vegetation, exacerbating marsh loss. Man-made levees and locks and dams restrict the natural flow of sediments to the Gulf of America and its coastal marshes. Historically, seasonal flood pulses would deposit nutrient-laden sediment in these habitats, overtopping natural levees and nourishing and rebuilding Louisiana's marshes. Instead, much of this sediment is functionally "lost" offshore. The inappropriate diversion of sediments and the loss of sediment-trapping vegetation further magnify habitat loss caused by coastal subsidence, which may occur naturally or through anthropogenic means. Whether natural or manmade, changes in Louisiana's hydrology patterns influence the quality, quantity, and distribution of marine habitats, altering the availability of suitable habitat important to the survival and reproduction of marine species.
Predicted alterations to ecosystems from climate change such as sea level rise, an increase in intensity and frequency of severe weather events, ocean acidification, alterations to ocean currents, and changes in environmental conditions can directly influence habitat availability 
(Pershing et al. 2018
). When combined with subsidence and sea level rise, the impacts of tropical cyclones on coastal habitats can be immediate, severe, and long term. Alterations to ocean temperatures and currents can lead to changes in the distribution of marine life, greatly altering food webs.
Negative impacts from the run-off of pollution such as pesticides, herbicides, pharmaceutical and industrial contaminants, and micro-plastics may directly and indirectly affect water quality and the health of marine fishes. Nutrient run-off also increases the occurrence of hypoxic events along the coast and have been shown to increase the size of the Gulf of America Dead Zone, an annually forming area of hypoxic water off the coast of Louisiana. Hypoxia can lead to mass mortality events as well as alterations in fish distribution.
The rise of economic and cultural globalization has increased the potential spread of non-native species across the globe. In Louisiana, non-native species threaten marine fishes on several fronts. Each year, herbivory by Nutria (Myocastor coypus) further decreases the acreage of coastal marshes. Non-native plants may outcompete natives and change the structure of habitats and the resources within. Non-native fishes can Carcharhinus longimanus Oceanic Whitetip Shark* GNR S3
Carcharhinus obscurus Dusky Shark G3 S3

## AMPHIBIANS AND REPTILES

Amphibians and reptiles, also referred to as herpetofauna, are integral components of terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems and provide beneficial services that directly and indirectly affect human well-being 
(Valencia-Aguilar et al. 2013)
. Many species consume rodents and invertebrates, several of which are pests or disease vectors, and help to control populations of these prey species and may prevent outbreaks. Several snake species feed on fish, particularly injured or diseased fish, which helps keep fish populations healthy. All life stages of amphibians and reptiles serve as prey for other species such as fishes, birds, and mammals, including humans. In fact, the American Bullfrog (Lithobates catesbeianus), Pig Frog (L. grylio), snapping turtles (Chelydra serpentina and Macrochelys temminckii), softshell turtles (Apalone spp.), Red-eared Slider (Trachemys scripta elegans), and others have been sought as food sources by Louisianans for centuries. In addition, amphibians and reptiles or their derivatives (e.g., snake venom) have been instrumental in the development of medicinal treatments for various human ailments including asthma, skin conditions, cardiovascular diseases such as high blood pressure and congestive heart failure, and more 
(Valencia-Aguilar et al. 2013
, Hocking and Babbitt 2014
, Li et al. 2018
, Offor and Piater 2023)
.
Some beneficial services may not be as apparent but are, nevertheless, critical to ecosystem function and resilience. For example, the Gopher Tortoise not only serves as a seed disperser of native groundcover plants, it is also a keystone species, providing shelter for over 360 vertebrate and invertebrate species in its extensive subterranean burrows 
(Eisenberg 1983)
.
In addition to acting as ecosystem engineers, some species can be part of a comprehensive approach to environmental contaminant surveillance. Amphibians function as biological indicators of ecosystem health due to their permeable eggs, gills, and skin and ensuing sensitivity to environmental changes 
(Vitt et al. 1990
, Stebbins and Cohen 1995
, Oloo 2024)
. Monitoring the health of aquatic amphibians can provide valuable insight into overall water quality 
(Gibbons et al. 2000
, Rouse et al. 1999)
. Similarly, increases in pollution may be indicated by declines in the health of terrestrial amphibians and reptiles 
(Brühl et al. 2011
, French et al. 2018)
.
More than 150 native species and subspecies of amphibians and reptiles inhabit Louisiana's diverse landscape 
(Boundy and Carr 2017
, Means et al. 2017
, Pyron and Beamer 2023)
, with the greatest species richness in the Florida Parishes.
Louisiana is unique among the states with high herpetofaunal diversity in that it has no endemic species. Twenty-five species and subspecies of native amphibians (15 salamanders and 10 frogs) and 47 species and subspecies of native reptiles (24 turtles, 5 lizards, and 18 snakes) are SGCN in Louisiana (Table 
4
.9). The Dusky Gopher Frog, Ornate Chorus Frog, Ornate Box Turtle, Eastern Pinesnake, Harlequin Coralsnake, and Eastern Diamond-backed Rattlesnake may be extirpated from the state; recent surveys have been unable to detect these species at historical sites or new sites with suitable habitat 
(Siegel and Doody 1992
, Thomas 1996
, Leonard et al. 2003
, Pechmann et al. 2006
, Buhlmann 2007
, Lejeune et al. 2021)
. In fact, most of these species have not been observed in the state for >40 years.

## AMPHIBIAN AND REPTILE THREATS

Louisiana's native amphibian and reptile species face a variety of challenges that affect long-term viability and persistence in a rapidly changing human-impacted environment. Habitat loss and habitat degradation from urban development, transportation corridors, unsustainable forestry and agricultural practices, and coastal land loss are the primary threats affecting Louisiana's amphibian and reptile populations. Other threats include sand and gravel mining, pollution, road mortality, invasive species, diseases, unsustainable and illegal collection, and climate change.
In addition to complete loss of habitats, urbanization can cause habitat fragmentation, which reduces the amount of contiguous habitat available. This may bisect and isolate populations, reducing species' population size, which may lead to inbreeding 
(Schlaepfer et al. 2018)
. The increased necessity for installation of transportation corridors in response to urban growth also leads to increased habitat fragmentation and further isolation of ephemeral wetlands, which some species require for successful reproduction and recruitment 
(Gibbons et al. 2000, Mitchell and
Gibbons 2010)
. Fragmentation disrupts a species' ability to access resources for survival, reproduction, and migration, and increases the risk of vehicular strikes. Louisiana's East Gulf Coastal Plain Ecoregion, which holds the highest herpetofaunal diversity in the state, has experienced rapid human population growth in recent years and, thus, an increase in urban development. Like the need for transportation corridors, the requirement of energy can increase with urbanization, which can also affect native wildlife and their habitats. Large-scale energy development, such as renewable energy, is rapidly expanding in the state creating the potential for multiple taxa, including threatened and endangered species and SGCN, to suffer significant habitat loss, disruption, and declines in populations 
(Lovich and Ennen 2011)
.
Land conversion and inappropriate habitat management practices serve as major stressors to many amphibian and reptile species. Along with development, upland habitats are often targeted for silviculture. Roller-chopping and bedding, used for site preparation of pine plantations, are detrimental to upland species like the federally threatened Gopher Tortoise and species that rely on ephemeral ponds like the imperiled Eastern Tiger Salamander. Fire suppression in fire-adapted communities changes the vegetation structure and causes a build-up of woody shrubs and ground fuels, which significantly degrades the habitat and increases the risk of severe wildfire (USDA 2018). Additional anthropogenic impacts include filling for development, dredging, ditching, draining, and installation of levees and water control structures.
Chemical and physical alterations of streams and associated riparian habitat also negatively affect amphibians and reptiles 
(Koehnken et al. 2020)
. Chemical waste and sedimentation runoff from agricultural, industrial, and residential developments pollute streams, removing oxygen and increasing ammonia. The resulting decrease in water quality negatively impacts herpetofauna directly and can be particularly detrimental to turtles that feed on mollusks, which are sensitive to poor water quality. Sand and gravel mining degrades aquatic systems through siltation, increased turbidity, and surface water pollution 
(Bailey 2006)
. Riverine turtle species appear to be most impacted by sand and gravel mining, likely because of their dependence on these habitats as well as their biological constraints around nesting (e.g., nesting requires sandbars; 
Lovich et al. 2009, Selman and
Qualls 2009)
. In addition, nesting success of sandbar-nesting riverine turtles can be substantially reduced by other human disturbance, particularly ORV usage on sandbars. The removal of woody debris and neglecting Best Management Practices (BMPs) for riparian areas are detrimental to freshwater turtle species that utilize the woody debris for basking and shelter and to aquatic salamanders that rely on the debris for nesting and shelter 
(Bailey et al. 2006
, Glorioso et al. 2021)
.
Invasive non-native species and problematic native species can greatly impact populations of amphibians and reptiles. As human development expands, populations of predators such as Raccoon (Procyon lotor), Opossum (Didelphis virginiana), foxes (Vulpes spp.), skunks (Mephitis spp., Spilogale spp.), North American River Otter, and crows (Corvus spp.) also increase in some areas 
(Bailey et al. 2006)
. Raccoons and crows are a significant source of mortality for various life stages of some herpetofaunal SGCN, particularly turtle eggs and hatchlings 
(Buhlmann 2017)
. The Red Imported Fire Ant (Solenopsis invicta) is a serious threat to many adult terrestrial amphibians and reptiles as well as to the eggs and hatchlings of aquatic and terrestrial turtles 
(Allen et al. 2004
). Feral Hogs destroy sensitive habitats and prey upon amphibians and reptiles. Outdoor cats (Felis catus) are estimated to kill approximately 350 million to more than 1 billion amphibians and reptiles per year in the United States 
(Loss et al. 2013)
. Non-native amphibian and reptile species can also affect native species through the transmission of diseases via anthropogenic movement 
(Daszak et al. 2000)
. Ranaviruses have been associated with mass 57
Chapter 4: Species of Greatest Conservation Need mortality events of amphibians and turtles and can occur asymptomatically within individuals in the same population 
(Johnson et al. 2008
, Currylow et al. 2014)
.
Many of Louisiana's SGCN will be impacted by climate change, a long-term threat that is not easily addressed. The predicted increases in the magnitude and frequency of tropical cyclones, the increase in sea level rise, and the exacerbation of salt water intrusion will be particularly injurious to coastal species, especially when those impacts are combined with erosion, subsidence, and overwash. However, climate change will not only impact coastal species but also herpetofauna across the state. Climate change is expected to be detrimental to amphibians and reptiles by reducing habitat availability, altering precipitation patterns, and causing increases and shifts in temperatures. Turtles may be particularly vulnerable; long generation times, low hatchling survival, low dispersal rates, and temperature-dependent sex determination make them ill-adapted to climate change 
(Gibbons et al. 2000
, Ihlow et al. 2012
, Butler 2019)
. In fact, even minor temperature changes can greatly affect sex ratios of nestling turtles 
(Janzen 1994
, Wright et al. 2012
, Olson and Saenz 2013)
. Warming temperatures resulting from climate change may facilitate the spread and establishment of non-native species, which can increase competition with native species 
(Hitt et al. 2016
). In addition, climate change has the potential to reduce the effectiveness of traditional management strategies, and land managers must remain vigilant and adaptive in their approach to conservation.
In Louisiana, recreational and commercial collection of native amphibians and reptiles has been largely unregulated with minimal reporting requirements. Lack of monitoring data and regulatory oversight heightens the potential for unsustainable legal collection and illegal poaching. North American turtles are particularly vulnerable, as there is great interest and increasing demand for them for use as food, as pets, and in traditional medicines 
(Gibbons et al. 2000
, Mali et al. 2015)
.
Unsustainable and illegal collection combined with the myriad of aforementioned threats could result in serious impacts to amphibian and reptile populations statewide. Wanton killing of snakes may be a significant source of mortality for some imperiled species such as Eastern Diamond-backed Rattlesnake. However, take from the wild does not have to be intentional for populations to be impacted; "ghost traps" (e.g., derelict crab traps) are a source of mortality for terrapins in some locations, and sea turtles are vulnerable as bycatch in the absence of turtle-excluder devices (TEDs) in trawling gear. With very limited collection information on native amphibians and reptiles, it is difficult to determine how populations are affected, which emphasizes the importance of monitoring collection and harvest levels, addressing illegal poaching, and minimizing adverse impacts that take could have on native herpetofaunal populations.
Herpetofaunal species are among the most imperiled taxa in the world due to the multitude of threats challenging their persistence. The unique life history traits of this group make them particularly vulnerable to anthropogenic and environmental changes. Thus, it is imperative that threats to reptile and amphibian populations be minimized to the greatest extent possible to prevent further population declines. 
2001, 2019a, 2019b, 2020)
. Importantly, Louisiana's coast represents the most significant nesting area in the northern Gulf of America for a long list of SGCN (Table 
4
.11), including Clapper Rail, Sandwich and Forster's terns, and Seaside Sparrow 
(Remsen et al. 2019)
.

## COMMON NAME SCIENTIFIC NAME

Unlike many taxonomic groups, distribution and population status and trends are relatively well understood for North American birds 
(Fink et al. 2023
, Sauer et al. 2019)
.
Unfortunately, these data resources are revealing potentially catastrophic declines in North American breeding bird populations 
(Rosenberg et al. 2019)
(Loss et al. 2013
(Loss et al. , 2015))
, followed by collisions with buildings, particularly glass windows, which result in over 1 billion bird deaths per year in the United States 
(Kornreich et al. 2024)
. Unlike habitat loss and other forms of anthropogenic mortality, straightforward practices readily available to the general public may be implemented to reduce these impacts (e.g., keeping cats indoors, covering windows with screens or applying treatments to reduce reflectance). Artificial light at night further increases risk of collision with lighted infrastructure by nocturnal migrants (the majority of migratory songbirds migrate at night and may be disoriented by or attracted to artificial light). Nocturnal collisions may be reduced by turning off unnecessary interior and exterior lights during migration, utilizing motiondetecting security lighting, and favoring downward-pointing exterior lights. Nocturnal collisions with communication towers and other structures may be reduced by using flashing lights rather than steady-burning lights. In addition, offshore wind energy development poses complex risks to several guilds of birds, including risk of wind turbine collision by migratory birds and other species as well as displacement and avoidance of foraging habitat for coastal colonial waterbirds. Information gaps currently limit our understanding of these risks and how to implement offshore wind energy while minimizing its potential negative impacts. Finally, human disturbance of nesting birds, especially of colonial nesting species, may significantly reduce nest success and productivity. Stewardship at beach-nesting bird colonies such as symbolic fencing and education and outreach efforts can be extremely effective in minimizing human-related disturbance. In addition, the full extent of intentional, illegal destruction of birds is unknown but represents a conspicuous source of mortality for some high profile species.
Additional threats to birds include contaminants, invasive species, and disease. Despite improvements in regulating some sources of pollution, harmful pesticides and pollution of various types remain threats to bird SGCN. Inland, nearshore, and offshore oil spills pose serious direct threats to both inland species and, particularly, coastal species, which frequently nest in high densities in spill-prone areas. In addition, such contaminant spills can reduce quantity and quality of food resources and can kill vegetation, further exacerbating coastal habitat loss (e.g., a reduction of nesting habitats). Trash, including fishing gear and other marine debris, can cause entanglements resulting in mortality and may be used as nesting material with potentially harmful consequences (e.g., ingestion of plastics, exposure to toxins, and entanglement). Neonicotinoid insecticides have pervasive negative direct and indirect effects on birds, with population-scale implications, yet their use remains widespread and inadequately regulated. Invasive species threaten native birds through predation, competition for nest sites or other resources, or habitat-related interactions. Lastly, emerging avian diseases (e.g., highly pathogenic avian influenza), which may be spread or otherwise exacerbated by human-wildlife interactions, may negatively impact bird populations already stressed by aforementioned threats.

## MAMMALS

Mammals are some of the most charismatic and recognizable animals with 83 species occurring in Louisiana and its adjacent waters. Mammals play many integral roles in ecosystems, including pollination, seed dispersal, nutrient cycling, and natural disturbance of habitat through burrowing, tunneling, herbivory, and other means 
(Lacher et al. 2019)
. Relative to ectothermic taxa groups, mammals require vast amounts of energy to sustain themselves, having profound impacts on food webs through consumption of natural resources. For instance, a shrew may consume up to three times its body weight per day to meet its energetic needs 
(Wilson and Ruff 1999)
, and an individual Long-tailed Weasel can eat up to three rodents per night 
(Svendson 2003
). In addition, many rodents consume large quantities of seeds or cache them for later consumption.
The act of caching unintentionally results in seed dispersal and is overall beneficial for plant communities 
(Lacher et al. 2019)
, accounting for >95% of plant biomass in some systems 
(Vander Wall 2008)
. Furthermore, rodents and other mammals serve as key prey items for numerous taxa groups 
(Lowery 1974)
.
The value of mammals is not limited to the benefits they provide to ecosystems. Many species benefit humans by consuming vast quantities of pest insects. 
Ammerman and colleagues (2012)
 estimated that Brazilian Free-tailed Bats (Tadarida brasiliensis), which preferentially prey upon crop insects, consume up to 4 billion Corn Earworm Moths (Helicoverpa zea) each night in Texas. 
Whitby and colleagues (2020)
 found agricultural pests in more than 90% of Eastern Red Bat (Lasiurus borealis) and Big Brown Bat guano samples examined. Estimates suggest that the annual value of bats to United States agriculture could be as high as $53 billion 
(Boyles et al. 2011)
. Additionally, species like the Tricolored Bat eat large quantities of biting insects such as mosquitos and midges each night 
(Ammerman et al. 2012)
.
Historically, attention has focused on mammalian game species that directly benefit humans through subsistence uses (e.g., meat and clothing; 
Feldhamer et al. 2003)
 or financial gains (e.g., fur bearers). Only within the past few decades have mesocarnivores such as the Plains Spotted Skunk and Long-tailed Weasel received more focused attention making management agencies aware of their steep declines 
(Gompper and
Hackett 2005, Jachowski et al. 2021)
. Other nongame mammals have received even less consideration, and unfortunately, many native species, such as mice and moles, are considered pests; however, the most problematic are not native and include species such as the House Mouse (Mus musculus), Norway Rat (Rattus norvegicus), Black Rat (Rattus rattus), and Nutria 
(Whitaker and Hamilton 1998)
. Similarly, bats are often viewed with superstition or as direct threats to human health and safety. Fortunately, public opinion of bats has recently shifted in a more positive direction 
(Fagan et al. 2018
) as a result of increased education and outreach on the beneficial ecosystem services they provide. These benefits, along with those provided by other mammal species, are diverse and their loss would have cascading impacts felt across ecosystems and, ultimately, human civilization. A list of the mammal Species of Greatest Conservation Need can be found in Table 
4
.13. 

## MAMMAL SGCN

## MAMMAL THREATS

Much like other taxa, the main threat to mammals is habitat loss and modification, affecting 40% of mammals worldwide 
(Visconti et al. 2011
). Louisiana's largest native terrestrial species, the Louisiana Black Bear, requires substantial tracts of land to persist, and Black Bear home ranges can vary considerably between 1,705 to 78,579 acres 
(Feldhamer et al. 2003)
. Home ranges of female Louisiana Black Bears have been estimated at 2,965 acres for the Tensas basin population in Louisiana 
(Benson and Chamberlain 2007)
. Because of habitat loss, Black Bear have trouble finding resources (e.g., food, mates, denning sites, etc.) within their home range and may be forced into urban areas, often resulting in conflicts with humans. Small mammals can also require large spaces to survive; home range size for the Alleghanian Spotted Skunk can be up to 2,139 acres 
(Lesmeister et al. 2009)
. Used by bats for roosting and foraging, forests are essential for bats' survival but are often overlooked and subjected to logging and urban development 
(Mickleburgh et al. 2002
, Frick et al. 2020)
.
Additionally, maintaining connectivity between territories and populations will continue to be a challenge as habitats become increasingly fragmented by roads and urban development.
Direct and indirect mortality from anthropogenic activities is a leading cause of population decline in mammals 
(Avila et al. 2018)
. Some SGCN, including large predators, rodents, and bats, are subject to varying degrees of human persecution that arise from human-wildlife conflicts. An additional concern for many bat species is the replacement of "bat-friendly" bridges with designs that are of lower value to these animals 
(Wilson et al. 2023)
. For other mammalian terrestrial species, roads are a prominent feature across the landscape and often fragment habitats. Roads inhibit habitat connectivity and limit animal movement through avoidance 
(McGregor et al. 2008)
 or by vehicle collision that can cause mortality 
(Kreling et al. 2019)
. Similar to roadways, in waterways, boat collisions, are an overwhelming threat to manatees 
(Bonde et al. 2004
). In addition, marine mammals can become entangled in fishing gear or other marine debris causing unintended mortality.
Non-native, invasive species can have significant impacts to native mammals and their habitats. For example, Nutria likely outcompete Muskrat, while simultaneously destroying their critical habitat and limiting their dispersal. Non-native diseases also pose a serious threat to many mammal SGCN. Although disease outbreaks are rarely the sole cause of large-scale declines in mammals, they can further tax already imperiled populations 
(Smith et al. 2006)
. Additionally, the movement of diseases between wild and domesticated mammals and humans is of growing concern 
(Choisy and Rohani 2006)
. For example, Canine distemper (Morbillivirus canis) is native to South America but now occurs throughout Louisiana. This virus is known to cause mortality in many mustelids, including spotted skunks 
(Harris et al. 2021)
. The effects of White-nose Syndrome (WNS) in bats is well studied, and research suggests it is one of the greatest threats to many bat species 
(Frick et al. 2020)
. The fungus Pseudogymnoascus destructans (Pd), which causes WNS, was first documented in Louisiana, in 2020. However, while the fungus is present, no cases of WNS have been observed in the state. The disease has been documented in neighboring states.
Energy development, industrial and residential waste, and other pollutants are additional threats to mammals. Marine mammals are at risk from noise pollution, oil spills, and other toxic releases that may result in mortality or decreased fitness 
(Avila et al. 2018)
. Brevetoxins produced by the red tide (Karenia brevis) have caused mass mortality events in manatees 
(Bonde et al. 2004
). In terrestrial habitats, discarded bottles can cause mortality in small mammals like shrews by acting as passive traps 
(Brannon and Bargelt 2013)
. Rodenticides, used to control the non-native House Mouse or Norway Rat, can lead to primary exposure through incidental consumption by native rodent species. Secondary exposure to rodenticides has been documented in carnivores with diets high in rodents 
(Smith and
Mengak 2022, Erickson and
Urban 2004)
. As with birds, wind farms pose a potential threat to many bats through collisions, and the construction of wind and solar farms can exacerbate habitat loss 
(Frick et al. 2020)
.

## PLANTS

As humans have transitioned from a traditional huntergatherer existence to a technology driven lifestyle, a variety of connections have been lost with the natural world. Whether it is the ability to navigate using the constellations or the ability to locate valuable resources by reading the landscape and weather, vast segments of the human population can no longer conduct these basic tasks that were once essential to human existence. This is nowhere more evident than in our relationship with plants. 
Wandersee and Schussler (1998)
 While not all are visually striking, charismatic species, their ecological significance is undeniable and deserves to be preserved and celebrated.
Due to their ability to transform solar energy into the carbonbased fuel essential for life, plants are a fundamental part of the food chain. As primary producers, plants provide sustenance for herbivores, which in turn supports higher trophic levels. In many cases, these relationships are mutually beneficial, such as the symbiotic bond between plants and pollinators. In these relationships, plants offer vital resources to pollinators such as nectar and pollen, while pollinators facilitate plant reproduction and seed dispersal, which ultimately aids in maintaining and creating healthy ecosystems 
(Nicolson and Wright 2017)
. From hollow trees to aquatic root systems, plants also serve as shelter and nesting sites for various taxa. For example, foliar cover from trees and shrubs can provide many organisms with protection from predators, excessive heat, and harsh weather conditions.
Plants also provide a multitude of ecosystem services that are important to mitigating the impacts of climate change. Through photosynthesis, plants are able to reduce the presence of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere by sequestering carbon and storing it as biomass 
(Kumar et al. 2006
). Many plants have intricate root systems that slow down the movement of surface water, facilitate the penetration of groundwater, and anchor soil, improving soil structure which reduces flooding and erosion leading to cleaner water in natural and urban systems 
(Phillips and Marden 2006)
. Plants enhance substrate stability, particularly in areas with highly erodible soils like coastal marshes, southern mesophytic forests, and xeric sandhills, increasing the resilience and health of these natural communities across the state. Plants can also be used to assess habitat quality as certain species may persist exclusively in exemplary natural communities. Plants found regularly in particular habitats, but absent elsewhere, can also aid in identifying existing or historical natural communities. For example, the presence of White-Leaf Prairie Hibiscus (Hibiscus leucophyllus) along a transportation corridor may indicate the presence of a remnant coastal prairie.
It is for these reasons that state agencies have begun to incorporate plants into State Wildlife Action Plans. This not only elevates the profiles of rare, threatened, and endangered plant species but also highlights the critical roles plants play as primary producers and providers of ecosystem services to wildlife and humans. Whether it is the importance plants play in preventing the loss of coastal habitats, sequestering carbon, or providing critical habitat, maintaining healthy plant populations is vital to the conservation of all species.

## PLANT THREATS

Protection of Louisiana's native plant species is essential for preserving the integrity of natural communities that support habitats and resources for all living things. Plant SGCN are perpetually threatened by anthropogenic factors both directly and indirectly.
Habitat loss, destruction, and alteration are the greatest threats to plant SGCN. Conversion of natural areas into urban and suburban developments continues to reduce or eliminate populations of imperiled species. Agricultural practices can also reduce persistence of rare plants through eutrophication (i.e. increased nutrient loads), non-selective herbicide use, and overgrazing. In addition, some commercial forestry practices (e.g., site conversion to pine plantation) and site preparation practices (e.g., bedding and herbicide) can reduce herbaceous undergrowth resulting in decreased biodiversity. Restoration of agricultural land to a natural community resembling its historical state is often very difficult due to land-leveling and tilling, which alter the natural hydrology and topography, cause soil degradation, and cause the loss of microbiota as well as the seedbank 
(Kraut-Cohen et al. 2020)
. Several plant SGCN occur in communities that are dependent on fire. However, with European settlement came fire suppression, which disrupted natural burn regimes and, consequently, reduced grassland and other open habitats and plant populations 
(Fowler and Konopik 2007)
. Efforts are underway to return fire to the landscape, but inadequate burning and inappropriate restoration practices (e.g., use of incorrect ecotypes) and out-of-kind compensatory mitigation can hinder recovery of imperiled fire-driven systems (e.g., prairies, savannas, woodlands), which covered nearly half of Louisiana prior to European settlement.
Climate change exacerbates existing threats and increases the vulnerability of plant SGCN and their habitats. An expected increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events will likely destroy habitats, displacing or severely impacting sensitive species. In addition, these events can contribute to erosion, sedimentation, and salinity changes which can negatively affect plants and natural communities 
(Fagherazzi 2019)
. As the climate changes, organisms with mutualistic relationships with native species may decline as well, impacting pollination and seed dispersal for many plant species 
(Fontúrbel et al. 2021)
. A warming climate will also likely increase potential for invasive species to colonize new areas and to reach saturation levels in existing infestation, rendering these species impossible to manage and likely causing a significant loss of biodiversity and natural community function.
Louisiana is particularly susceptible to invasive species due to the state's subtropical climate with abundant rainfall and mild winters, favorable conditions for many nonnative plants. in a loss of diversity. The movement of firewood and other plant materials (e.g., nursery stock) can also spread pests such as the Emerald Ash Borer (EAB; Agrilus planipennis), an exotic beetle that destroys ash trees (Fraxinus spp.), and the Redbay Ambrosia Beetle (Xyleborus glabratus), a prolific vector of Laurel Wilt disease (Raffaelea lauricola). The transportation of hay has also led to the spread of invasive plants such as Cogon Grass (Imperata cylindrica) and Johnson Grass (Sorghum halepense). Invasive mammals such as Nutria and Feral Hogs are also injurious to sensitive plant populations and can decimate natural communities.
The inability to recognize the ecological value of plants results in fewer efforts for plant conservation. For example, charismatic organisms are often prioritized, and, consequently, plants, especially those lacking noticeable aesthetic forms, are often neglected. Those plant species that are not neglected may suffer from over-collection in the wild by well-intentioned members of the public. Such action, in an effort to "conserve" imperiled plants, will likely result in failure due to a lack of fully understanding the specific conditions needed for these species to survive. In addition, the overharvesting of native plants can cause deleterious impacts to sensitive populations, and unsustainable foraging practices should also be discouraged. Development of informational materials and outreach programs that address the crucial and influential roles native plants have on natural community health and ecological functions is critically important for the conservation of plant species. In addition, promoting responsible conservation practices and encouraging cooperation with partners and the public are essential to ensure the survival and persistence of plant SGCN.

Chapter 5: Natural Communities
This chapter provides an overview of the natural communities of Louisiana based on the United States National Vegetation Classification (USNVC) system (USNVC 2025). The version of the natural community classification presented here is a simplified representation and may not fully align with the USNVC system.
The finest classification level in the USNVC system is the Ecological Association. In some cases, natural communities presented here are equivalent to a single Ecological Association in the USNVC system. However, most natural communities in this State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP) are slightly broader in concept and capture several ecological associations or broader classification units within the USNVC. Appendix I contains a crossreference of natural communities discussed in this chapter to the Groups and Macrogroups within the USNVC system. Groups and Macrogroups are intermediate classification units within the USNVC system hierarchy and are defined by criteria pertaining to physiognomy, biogeography, and floristics (USNVC 2025). In addition to vegetated natural communities, this chapter also addresses unvegetated and anthropogenic habitats. 101
Chapter 5: Natural Communities to reach the ground, supporting a rich and diverse herbaceous understory dominated by grasses and other light-loving plants.
These ecosystems exist along a continuum of canopy cover, with savannas typically featuring 5% to 50% canopy closure, while woodlands are somewhat more densely wooded, generally exhibiting 50% to 75% canopy cover. The exact structure varies depending on the specific natural community characteristics such as type, age, fire frequency, fire intensity, and edaphic characteristics. These landscapes support high levels of plant and animal biodiversity and are shaped by a suite of natural processes, with fire playing a particularly crucial role. Historically, frequent fires helped maintain an open structure, limit encroachment by dense woody vegetation, and promote the persistence of fire-adapted species. The dynamic interplay between canopy openness, fire, and herbaceous diversity makes savannas and woodlands some of the most ecologically rich and visually distinctive habitats in the region. In Louisiana, savannas and woodlands are primarily found inland across the Interior Southern Plains and includes Eastern Gulf Coastal Plain Savanna and Woodland, Western Gulf Coastal Plain Savanna and Woodland, and Interior Southern Plains Savanna and Woodland subtypes.
Non-forested Grasslands and Open Terrain refer to habitats that are naturally treeless with a rich diversity of herbaceous vegetation or terrain that is naturally unvegetated. Natural communities of this archetype are grouped into three subtypes: (1) Prairies, (2) Marshes, and (3) Barrier Islands, Beaches, and Sandbars. Prairies are dominated by grasses, grass-like plants, and forbs, forming extensive vegetative cover that supports a diverse array of plant and wildlife species. Fire and edaphic characteristics play a particularly crucial role in shaping prairie natural communities. Marshes, though often wet or seasonally inundated, share this non-forested characteristic and are populated by herbaceous wetland species adapted to saturated conditions. Barrier islands, beaches, and sandbars, found along coastlines and river systems, also fall within this category when they support sparse vegetation, including specialized grasses and shrubs adapted to shifting, sandy substrates. In some areas, particularly in coastal dune systems, low-growing shrubs may become more prominent, adding structural complexity while maintaining the open character of the landscape. Collectively, these habitats are crucial for biodiversity, water filtration, erosion control, and as transitional zones between aquatic and upland ecosystems.
Inland and Marine Waters encompass a variety of water bodies and vegetated habitats, which can be broadly categorized into three subtypes: (1) Lentic Water Bodies, (2) Lotic Water Bodies, and (3) Marine and Estuarine Water Bodies. Lentic Water Bodies refer to still or slow-moving systems such as natural lakes (e.g., oxbows), reservoirs, and both natural and man-made ponds. These areas often support submerged aquatic vegetation (SAV). Lotic Water Bodies, in contrast, are characterized by flowing water and include river systems and deltas. These dynamic environments also support freshwater SAV, contributing to ecological diversity and aquatic habitat complexity. Marine waters include estuarine and marine environments (e.g., Estuarine and Marine Submerged Aquatic Vegetations) and other coastal systems. Habitats in this system are often classified based on substrate type (e.g., sand, mud, or shell hash), which influences the distribution and composition of marine organisms. Together, these aquatic systems play critical roles in biodiversity, water quality, and habitat provisioning.
Anthropogenic Habitats are environments that have elements of nature but do not constitute a natural community. These habitats have been significantly altered or created directly or indirectly by human activities. Agriculture and Improved Pasture, Rice Agriculture and Aquaculture, and Pine Plantation and Contemporary Forest are included in this anthropogenic category. These landscapes often consist of degraded habitats, largely fire-suppressed habitats, or second-growth forests that have regenerated with a preponderance of weedy or ruderal species and often contain an abundance of invasive species or invasive species cover. Unlike natural, old-growth forests, savannas, woodlands, or grasslands, these anthropogenic habitats typically have simplified structures, reduced biodiversity, and altered ecological processes. Pine plantations, for example, are usually composed of single-species stands, typically, Loblolly Pine (Pinus taeda) or Slash Pine (P. elliottii) planted in rows and harvested on short rotations. Both pine plantations and contemporary forests are markedly different from the diverse and dynamic ecosystems they replaced. While they provide some economic value and habitat for certain types of wildlife, these forests represent a departure from the complex, high functioning, mixed-species habitats that once dominated the region.
Unnatural pastures are often seeded with non-native grasses and are regularly manipulated through mowing, fertilization, and grazing regimes to support livestock, resulting in reduced plant diversity and altered soil and hydrological conditions. Rice fields and aquaculture ponds, many of which are integrated into rice farming systems, may resemble natural wetlands during parts of the year, but they are flooded, drained, and cultivated in cycles that are artificial. While these habitats provide economic value and can support certain wildlife species, especially waterbirds, they differ significantly from the native ecosystems they replaced, offering only a subset of the ecological functions and biodiversity of natural systems.
For each natural community, the name, state (S-rank) and global (G-rank) conservation ranks, and synonym(s) are given. Ecological systems are defined as groups of associations (communities) that tend to co-occur in similar ecological settings and were defined to allow habitat mapping 
(Comer et al. 2003)
 Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest is forested, alluvial swamp occurring on intermittently exposed soils, most commonly along rivers and streams but also in backchannel depressions and swales. The soils are inundated or saturated by surface water or groundwater on a nearly permanent basis throughout the growing season, except during periods of drought.
Even deep-water swamps with almost continuous flooding experience seasonal fluctuations in water levels 
(LNHP 2009)
. Baldcypress Swamp generally occurs on mucks and clays but also on silts and sands with underlying clay layers 
(Conner and Buford 1998)
. Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest has relatively low floristic diversity. The composition of associate species may vary widely from site to site. Undergrowth is often sparse because of low light intensity and a long hydroperiod. Neither Baldcypress (Taxodium distichum) nor tupelo seeds germinate underwater, nor can young seedlings of these trees survive long submergence. Seedling recruitment can only occur during draw-down periods.
This probably explains why these species tend to occur in evenaged stands since the environmental conditions favorable for germination and establishment of saplings occur infrequently.
Near-permanent impoundment of Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest is a major threat affecting sustainability of these forests.
Those areas dominated by tupelo are also alluvial but may occur on higher topographic positions than Baldcypress-dominated swamps.

## Extent and Status

Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest may be found throughout Louisiana. Of the original 2 million to 4 million acres, 25% to 50% (approximately 500,000 to 1 million acres) remain.
Although old individual Baldcypress trees are not that difficult to find, old-growth examples of Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest are very rare 
(Smith 1993
, TNC 2004)
. The Atchafalaya Basin Floodway contains the greatest remaining contiguous acreage in the United States with an estimated 595,000 acres of collective Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest and Bottomland Hardwood Forest. Large tracts can also be found in the East Gulf Coastal Plain (EGCP) in areas of the Amite, Tickfaw, and lower Tangipahoa rivers and lands surrounding Lakes Pontchartrain and Maurepas (Governor's Science Working Group on Coastal Wetland Forest Conservation and Use 2005).

## BATTURE FOREST

Rarity Rank: G4G5/S4 Synonyms: Riverfront Pioneer Forest, Cottonwood-Willow Forest Batture Forest develops on the slope between the natural or man-made levee crest and major streams/rivers. Batture areas are periodically scoured when river levels rise, and depending on sediment particle size, new sediment may be deposited when river levels fall. Historically, meandering rivers naturally shifted laterally (a process now inhibited by man-made levees and water control structures) via sediment erosion. As a river shifted course, the distance between the Batture and river channel increased, allowing the Batture Forest to undergo succession into other Bottomland Hardwood Forest associations. In large rivers such as the Mississippi, the area between the man-made levee and the river channel remains unstable and thus supports a Batture Forest containing early successional plant species.

## Extent and Status

Batture Forest occurs primarily along the Mississippi River but also along the Atchafalaya, Red, Ouachita, Pearl, and other large rivers. The acreage and number of intact sites are unknown.
Portions of the Atchafalaya Basin may support forest of this type.
Batture Forest along the Mississippi River, West Feliciana Parish

## EXTENT OF BATTURE FOREST

## Threats

Batture Forests occurring along large rivers are restricted to narrow corridors by operation of man-made levees (natural system modification). This habitat is threatened by humanrelated disturbance from several sources and by invasive plants and animals.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Conduct inventory of this habitat type, especially in the Atchafalaya Basin.

## Work with partners to promote development of Batture

Forest reserves to retain natural qualities and to provide education on the importance of this habitat for both resident and migratory fish and wildlife. 3. Work with partners to fill data gaps concerning ecological system processes and water quality/discharge impacts on this habitat. 4. Work with partners to minimize impacts of dredging and water discharges in Batture Forest. 5. Promote the maintenance and restoration of natural hydrologic regimes. Forests are characterized and maintained by a natural hydrologic regime of alternating wet and dry periods generally following seasonal flooding events. They are important natural communities for maintenance of water quality, providing a productive habitat for a variety of fish and wildlife species, and regulating flooding and stream recharge 
(LNHP 2009)
. Unlike many coastal stopover sites, Neotropical migratory birds utilize Bottomland Hardwood Forests as "full-service hotels," which provide food, water, and shelter during their perilous journey 
(Mehlman et al. 2005)
. In general, forested floodplain habitats are mixtures of broadleaf deciduous, needleleaf deciduous, and broadleaf evergreen trees and shrubs. Bottomland Hardwood Forests contain a number of species which can be aggregated into specific associations based on environmental factors such as physiography, topography, soils, and moisture regime 
(Allen 1997
, TNC 2004
).

## Sweetgum-Water Oak Bottomland Hardwood Forest

Often occurring on low ridges, this Bottomland Hardwood Forest association has the least intense hydric conditions. Plant diversity generally increases with shorter hydroperiod, so this type is also the richest in plant species of the Bottomland Hardwood Forest types.

## Extent and Status

Bottomland Hardwood Forest is a significant habitat in Louisiana, having historically occupied an estimated 6 million to 8 million acres 
(Smith 1993)
. Substantial acreage of this habitat type remains on the landscape today; however, much that remains has been hydrologically altered. and food. Giant Cane was used extensively by Native Americans for building materials and as a food source. Native Americans also managed Canebrake with fire and increased cane extent when their abandoned agricultural fields reverted to cane. This anthropogenic influence is believed to have accounted for the largest and most extensive Canebrake habitat 
(Noss 2013, Brantley and
Platt 2001)
. It is hypothesized that the extinct Passenger Pigeon (Ectopistes migratorius) also contributed to the establishment and expansion of Canebrake. Huge flocks of Passenger Pigeons disturbed forests by breaking tree limbs and creating canopy openings. These sunny openings, plus large amounts of nutrient-rich excrement expelled by the birds, created the fertile conditions suitable for Giant Cane 
(Noss 2013)
. Canebrake began to decline rapidly after European settlement and, by the early 1900s, had nearly disappeared throughout the southeastern United States. The extinction of the Passenger Pigeon, excessive grazing, altered burning regimes, agricultural land clearing, and flood control projects all contributed to the disappearance of the Canebrake ecosystem 
(Brantley and Platt 2001)
.

## Extent and Status

Canebrake likely occurred statewide on rich alluvial soil in large and small floodplains and was probably most extensive in the Mississippi and Red river valleys. This habitat is now extirpated in Louisiana.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Identify historical occurrences of Canebrake using General Land Office land survey records and plat maps; concentrate search within the MRAP in areas that are currently captured by conservation lands. 2. Initiate an experimental habitat restoration project on at least one site on an existing conservation area known to have been a Canebrake based on historical evidence and where Giant Cane is still present. 

## HARDWOOD FLATWOODS FOREST

Rarity Ranks: Mesic: G1G2?/S2S3 Wet: G2G3/S2S3 Prairie Terrace Loess Forest: G2/S1 Synonyms: Willow Oak Flat, Pin Oak Flat Wet Hardwood Flatwoods Forest and Mesic Hardwood Flatwoods Forest are sometimes described as two distinct communities but are combined here. Also included in this habitat are Pleistocene Terrace Flatwoods and Prairie Terrace Loess Forest, a mesic flatwoods type restricted to East Baton Rouge Parish.
Wet Hardwood Flatwoods occur on flat, poorly drained settings on older (Pleistocene) landscapes. Mesic Hardwood Flatwoods Forest and Prairie Terrace Loess Forest, also a mesic type, occur on slightly higher and better drained sites. While species composition may overlap substantially with various types of Bottomland Hardwood Forest, Hardwood Flatwoods do not occupy floodplains. Hardwood Flatwoods are also found on sodic (alkali) soils.

## EXTENT OF HARDWOOD FLATWOODS FOREST

## FOREST SYSTEMS

## Extent and Status

Most known occurrences of Hardwood Flatwoods Forest are on the Macon Ridge in northeast Louisiana and on the Prairie Terrace in the northwest part of the state. A small amount of this habitat is captured by Bodcau WMA in Bossier Parish. The Louisiana Army Ammunition Plant in Bossier and Webster parishes supports high-quality Hardwood Flatwoods Forest 
(McInnis and Martin 1995)
. In addition to East Baton Rouge, Prairie Terrace Loess Forest may have been present in the adjacent parishes of East Feliciana and Livingston. The historical extent of all types of Hardwood Flatwoods Forest is not known. Prairie Terrace Loess Forest is estimated to have occupied 500,000 to 1 million acres historically, with 1% to 5% remaining today 
(Smith 1993)
. Ecology of Hardwood Flatwoods Forest is a major knowledge gap in Louisiana.

## Threats

This habitat faces potential residential and commercial development and conversion to anthropogenic habitat types. Forestry practices, particularly site preparation methods such as bedding, negatively impact the hydrology, soil, and vegetation composition of Hardwood Flatwoods Forest. Disturbance associated with increased human interface and invasive plants and animals also threaten this habitat.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions 1. Implement research on ecology, classification, and extent

of this habitat type.

Pleistocene terraces. This can be done to some degree with high-resolution LiDAR. 3. Designate this habitat as a high priority for inventory. 4. Seek habitat protection opportunities through conservation easements and land acquisition.

## EXTENT OF MACON RIDGE GREEN ASH POND

Hardwood Flatwoods and, possibly, other forest types on higher elevations of the Macon Ridge. On today's landscape, they are often surrounded by agricultural fields. General Land Office survey records sometimes referred to Macon Ridge Green Ash Ponds as spicewood ponds or spicewood brakes, a possible reference to the aromatic shrub Pondberry, which is federally endangered.

## Extent and Status

Macon Ridge Green Ash Pond was apparently abundant in pre-settlement times. Many ponds were lost to agriculture; faint outlines of these extirpated ponds in cultivated fields are sometimes evident on aerial imagery. However, many ponds were likely spared, because they were too wet to farm. Some of these extant ponds are now embedded in agricultural fields.

## Threats

Macon Ridge Green Ash Ponds are threatened by invasive species (particularly EAB), basin alteration and disturbance, and input of agricultural chemicals from adjacent fields. Grand Isle is Louisiana's only Barrier Island that supports a forested community. This forest is restricted to interior portions of Grand Isle, where it is sufficiently buffered from the harsh shoreline environment. Trees in Barrier Island Live Oak Forests can exhibit the effects of saltwater spray and wind, having a stunted appearance and leaning away from the prevailing wind 
(West 1938
, Brown 1930
). This community is impacted by development, invasive species, vehicle traffic, clearing of understory vegetation, and habitat fragmentation. Conservation of this system is imperative to the survival of Neotropical migratory birds, which use this habitat for stopover during migration.

## Extent and Status

## Importance to Neotropical Migratory Birds

Coastal Live Oak-Hackberry Forest is extremely important as stopover habitat for Neotropical migratory birds during spring and fall migration. The majority of migrants fly nonstop for more than 600 miles to cross the Gulf of America each spring. At least 82 species of migratory birds regularly use these wooded habitats to replenish energy reserves necessary to successfully complete their migration. During fall migration, Coastal Live Oak-Hackberry Forest provides important corridors and staging areas for both trans-Gulf and circum-Gulf migrants, which move along the coast through Texas and around the Gulf of America on their journey to Central and South America.

## SALT DOME HARDWOOD FOREST

Rarity Rank: G1/S1 Synonyms: None
In the Mississippi Interior Salt Basin, salt domes occur where large, underground salt deposits (deposited by evaporating seas in the Jurassic Period) have risen to or near to the surface 
(Stern et al. 2011)
. Louisiana contains approximately 425 salt domes on the mainland and offshore, varying in depth from the Earth's surface 
(Beckman and Williamson 1990)
. In cases such as coastal Louisiana's "Five Islands" (Jefferson Island, Avery Island, Weeks Island, Cote Blanche Island, and Belle Isle), the salt domes have raised the surface, creating ridges that rise up from the surrounding marsh habitat. Soils covering most of the islands are very fertile and loess-derived. The hardwood forests of these islands are hilly with deep, shaded ravines, up to 60 feet deep in some places. Ravines are dominated by ferns, and, in many areas, the canopy supports lianas (woody vine species that utilize trees for support and as a means to reach the canopy), giving these forests a tropical appearance 
(Reese and Thieret 1966)
. Typically, the herbaceous layer is sparse and consists of several Caric sedges (Carex spp.) and other shade loving herbs. 

## Extent and Status

## Extent and Status

This community is very restricted in Louisiana, known only from St. Tammany Parish along the northshore of Lake Pontchartrain.
The few extant examples of this natural community appear to have an association with fire, as many of the older trees including Live Oak exhibit significant fire scars. The historical extent and status of this natural community are largely unknown. Small tracts of this habitat are protected at Big Branch Marsh NWR, Fontainebleau State Park, and Northlake Nature Center.

## Threats

This habitat occurs in a rapidly developing part of the state and is threatened by this development and disturbance associated with increased human interface. Potential increased tropical storm frequency and severity associated with climate change also threatens this habitat.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Support and conduct inventory and research to identify general ecological characteristics and processes of this habitat. 2. Support invasive species control in this habitat. Baldcypress is not a typical component of this habitat elsewhere in the state. Several ferns and living peat moss (Sphagnum spp.) are often conspicuous in the understories of Acidic Bay Forest. Landscape position can vary from broad depressions or small stream bottoms in flatwoods to narrow stream valleys in hilly terrain, sometimes even occurring on upper slopes. Acidic Bay Forest was historically shaped by natural fire and is typically flanked by fire-dependent pine systems. Acidic Bay Forest receives frequent fire along its edges and only occasionally burns through; therefore, it often serves as a natural fire break. The up-slope edges of Acidic Bay Forest historically experienced fire and likely support species to which a fire-frequent edge is important. These forests naturally vary from a few acres to more than 100 acres in size 
(Brooks et al. 1993
, Smith 1996)

Pondcypress-Swamp Tupelo Forest is threatened by fire suppression and introduction of excessive nutrients, which alters species composition of this acidic and oligotrophic habitat.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

Discourage introduction of partially treated municipal waste water into Pondcypress-Swamp Tupelo Forest; this swamp type is acidic and oligotrophic and can be degraded by nutrient input. (Rummer 2004
). Spruce Pine is a common associate in the Florida Parishes, and Baldcypress and Loblolly Pine are occasional associates statewide. Historically, this system was intermingled with Canebrake. ## Extent and Status

Small Stream Forest is widely distributed in broad uplands.
An estimated 25% to 50% of Louisiana's original Small Stream Forest, which is estimated to have been 1 million to 2 million acres, is thought to remain intact 
(Smith 1993)
. A number of high-quality examples occur on KNF, Fort Polk-Vernon WMA, and Fort Polk North WMA.

## Threats

The most impactful threat to this habitat are invasive species, gravel mining, impoundment of streams for reservoirs, as well as natural system modification.

## Extent and Status

Spruce Pine-Hardwood Flatwoods Forest is restricted to Louisiana, occupying a narrow range in Livingston, East Baton Rouge, East Feliciana, and Ascension parishes. Pre-settlement acreage is estimated at 50,000 to 100,000 acres, with 10% to 25% currently remaining 
(Smith 1993)
. Protected occurrences of this habitat occur on Tickfaw State Park and on BREC's Frenchtown Conservation Area and Palomino Park.

## Threats

The predominant threat to this habitat type is conversion to commercial and residential developments due to the rapid expansion of urbanization along the Interstate 12 corridor in the Florida Parishes. Other major factors threatening this association include conversion to commercial pine plantations and hydrological alterations. Invasive species further threaten this habitat. and nutrient-poor substrates of fine sandy loams or loamy fine sands with relatively high organic matter content

. This habitat type is extremely sensitive to surrounding land management activities and is easily degraded or destroyed by activities that alter natural hydrologic regimes.

## Extent and Status

Eastern Hillside Seepage Bog is a habitat type that is naturally small in size. Pre-settlement extent of seepage bogs in the EGCP of Louisiana was estimated to be less than 2,000 acres with only 10% to 25% remaining in St. Tammany and Washington parishes 
(Smith 1993)
 Fire, soil conditions, and a seasonally high water table work in concert to regulate community structure in Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna; however, fire is considered the critical element in its maintenance. All of the species indigenous to pine savannas have evolved over millennia within a regime of frequent (once every one to three years) surface fires, and most depend on fire for perpetuation. Fire stimulates flowering and fruit/seed production of savanna herbs and shrubs, deters invasion by fire-intolerant woody vegetation, and exposes mineral soil for herb and Longleaf Pine seedlings to become established. In the absence of frequent burning, pine savannas quickly succeed into shrub/tree thickets, and sun-loving herbs are reduced and eventually eliminated 
(Smith 1996)
. 

## Extent and Status

Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna remnants are limited in size compared to the broad expanses that once existed. Historically, the eastern Florida Parishes of Louisiana were dominated by extensive stands of this habitat; barely 1% of the original estimated 100,000 to 500,000 acres of Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna remains 
(Smith 1993)
. Habitat conversion, development, and timber production were initial factors in this habitat loss. Today, there are a few thousand acres in small blocks scattered across this area. TNC protects and manages Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna on portions of their Abita Creek Flatwoods, Lake Ramsay Savannah, and Talisheek Pine Wetlands preserves. LDWF owns and manages Lake Ramsay Savannah WMA (796 acres of savanna) which is adjacent to TNC's Lake Ramsay Preserve. Big Branch NWR, Bogue Chitto NWR, and Pearl River WMA contain "pine flatwoods" with remnants of herbaceous savanna flora, and some of these sites are in the process of being restored to Longleaf Pine systems. Wetland mitigation banking has become a valuable tool for restoring Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savannas. Several mitigation banks located in close proximity to TNC preserves are protecting and restoring this habitat.

## Threats

This habitat occurs in a rapidly developing part of the state and is threatened by residential and commercial development and disturbance from human interface. This habitat is firedependent and is threatened by fire exclusion and inadequate fire. Invasive species also pose a threat.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Prioritize this habitat type for inventory to determine extent and condition with a focus on identifying the surrounding landscape context (e.g., residential developments, etc.) that might be affected by prescribed burning.
2. Carry out habitat assessments and botanical and zoological surveys on mitigation banks supporting this habitat; work with USACE and mitigation bank sponsors to maximize ecological value of this habitat on mitigation banks. 3. Educate landowners, adjacent residents, developers, parishes, and the general public about the crucial role of prescribed burning in the management of Longleaf Pine systems and promote the advantages of growing Longleaf Pine and associated herbaceous ground cover. 4. Work with partners to incorporate strategies for Longleaf Pine management and restoration into current restoration efforts. 5. Target this habitat for acquisition from willing sellers, protection (e.g., servitudes), and stewardship implementation. This includes pursuing tracts that are degraded but restorable with timber harvesting and prescribed fire. 6. Develop and support a Prescribed Burn Association for this region. ## SLASH PINE-PONDCYPRESS WOODLAND

Rarity Rank: G2?/S1 Synonyms: Slash Pine Flatwoods, Wet pine-Pondcypress Savanna
This wetland habitat is restricted to the wet, acidic flatwoods on the far eastern Pleistocene Prairie Terrace of Louisiana's EGCP. Slash Pine-Pondcypress Woodland is situated in wetter depressions of Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna. This community is characterized by a diverse understory, similar in species composition to those found in wet Longleaf Pine savannas, though it is more closely associated with more hydric site conditions. It features an open to semi-open canopy savanna to woodland structure with a species-rich ground layer and is a fire dependent system. Its structure and species composition can vary significantly from site to site, influenced by subtle differences in topography, soil characteristics, hydrology, and fire history 
(Teague et al. 1995)
.
Soils of the Slash Pine-Pondcypress Woodland are hydric, strongly acidic, and nutrient poor silt loams and fine sandy loams. Surface soils are typically saturated for much of the year, and shallow water may be present in the late fall, winter, and early spring, and after rains during the growing season.
Slash Pine-Pondcypress Woodland evolved with recurrent lightning-season ground fires, and regular, low intensity, surface fire appears critical in maintaining this community. Both Slash Pine and Pondcypress (Taxodium ascendens) are fire-adapted species and can survive fires once they attain a certain size; however, neither is as fire resistant as Longleaf Pine. The natural fire return interval of this community is short (two to three years) and similar to adjacent Longleaf Pine savannas, though fires in this community were likely less intense and less effective on the wetter expressions of this community type. This allowed for uneven encroachment and regeneration of Slash Pine and Pondcypress, at times, as well as precluded complete dominance of the site by Slash Pine, Pondcypress, and hardwoods.

## Extent and Status

In the EGCP of Louisiana, Slash Pine-Pondcypress Woodland is primarily associated with flatwoods ponds, Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna, and Acidic Bay Forest. Protected examples occur on TNC's Talisheek Pine Wetlands and Abita Creek Flatwoods preserves. In Louisiana, this system is restricted to southern portions of St. Tammany Parish and possibly a portion of southern Tangipahoa Parish.

## Threats

Conversion to anthropogenic habitats has affected this habitat and is expected to continue, along with fire exclusion and disturbance from human activities.
Chapter 5: Natural Communities . Some examples of this community are prairie-like with little to no canopy and dense herbaceous understories, while others appear to take on brushy woodland characteristics. Notably, Pondberry has been reported along edges of a Lowland Sand Prairie within the Coffee Prairie Natural Area in southern Arkansas and may be associated with Ouachita Sand Prairie and Woodland habitat edges in northern Louisiana.

## Extent and Status

Additional field survey work is needed in order to accurately determine the status and extent of Ouachita Sand Prairie and Woodland.

## EXTENT OF OUACHITA SAND PRAIRIE AND WOODLAND

## Threats

Examples of Ouachita Sand Prairie and Woodland have been mostly found in an impaired or degraded state. In some of these areas, this may be due to failed conversions to Loblolly Pine plantations, which are now mostly covered with Chinese Tallow Tree and excessive brush. The historical role fire played in this community is yet to be determined, but fire suppression may be a threat.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Prioritize surveys for Ouachita Sand Prairie and Woodland to determine history, extent, and status. 2. Create a management plan to control brush and invasive species (particularly Chinese Tallow Tree) while considering the historical role of fire in this community. 

## Extent and Status

Sandstone Glades are thought to have occupied less than 2,000 acres in pre-settlement times with an estimated 50-75% remaining today 
(Smith 1993)
. Most known occurrences are on the Kisatchie District of KNF in southern Natchitoches Parish.
There are a number of Sandstone Glades on private lands in Sabine Parish.

## Threats

This habitat is threatened by disturbance and the resulting soil erosion. Glades with deeper soil are fire-dependent and are degraded by woody encroachment without adequate fire.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Continue surveys to determine the current extent and condition of this habitat type and support research on the community classification of Sandstone Glade/Barren. 2. Encourage and fund the use of prescribed fire and chemical and mechanical brush control as management tools. 3. Work with timber companies to ensure they can recognize this plant community and apply appropriate management techniques and buffers when warranted. Little is known about this habitat in Louisiana.

## SPARTA SAND POND

## Extent and Status

In Louisiana, this habitat is only known from the xeric, sandy, upland Longleaf Pine Woodland on the Sparta Formation in Bienville Parish and likely occupies less than 100 acres.
Chapter 5: Natural Communities . It occurs commonly on mid-to lower slopes on saturated, strongly acidic (pH ca. 4.5 to 5.5), and nutrient-poor substrates of fine sandy loams or loamy fine sands with relatively high organic matter content 
(Smith 1996)
. Soil series names have generally not been assigned to bogs due to the naturally limited acreage in the state 
(Smith 1996)
. Soils of this bog type are generally persistently wet from seepage and are saturated at or near the surface for most or all of the year. This bog type is variable in size being most often less than one acre, but rarely exceeding 10 acres. 
(Barker 1980)
.
The degree to which a bog remains wet throughout the year depends on the size of the watershed, the soil infiltration rate upslope, the rate of saturated flow in the soil, the topographic position of the bog, the bog's water storage capacity, and the rate of water leaving the bog from evapotranspiration and through surface and subsurface flow. In general, the greater the infiltration rate of the watershed soils and the water holding capacity of bog soils, the smaller the recharge area needed to maintain seepage throughout dry periods of the year. Therefore, bogs are extremely sensitive to surrounding land management activities and are easily degraded or destroyed by activities that alter natural hydrology of the surface or subsurface, especially actions such as timber harvesting, bedding, and fire break installations.
Western Hillside Seepage Bog, Fort Polk, Vernon Parish

## Extent and Status

This habitat occurs as small patches and is historically thought to have occupied less than 2,000 acres with an estimated 50% to 75% remaining today. In the WGCP, Hillside Seepage Bog is found from Calcasieu north to Natchitoches and Winn parishes.
Most known occurrences are in Vernon and Natchitoches parishes on KNF and Fort Polk-Vernon WMA, and there are possibly many more bogs within this geography (e.g., few biological inventories have occurred in Beauregard Parish).
