## SAVANNAS AND WOODLANDS

## Threats

Fire exclusion or inadequate fire and invasive species (especially Feral Hogs), and hydrology alterations are the main threats to this habitat.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Continue surveys to determine the extent and condition of this habitat type. 2. Work with staff of KNF and Ft. Polk to implement appropriate management, including optimal fire timing and frequency. 3. Encourage landowners to include this community type in prescribed burning plans and discourage the placement of firebreaks or bedding in and around bogs. 4. Provide additional cost-share funds for landowners to reduce or eliminate the costs associated with conducting prescribed burns on their property. 5. Develop and support a Prescribed Burn Association throughout the range of this natural community. 6. Support control of Feral Hogs within and near this habitat type.
Western Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna -Acidic

## WESTERN LONGLEAF PINE FLATWOODS SAVANNA

Rarity Rank: Acidic: G2G3/S2 Saline: G1/S1 Synonyms: Open Savanna, Pine Savanna, Pine Flatwoods, Grass-Sedge Bog, Pitcher-Plant Prairie, Coastal Meadow, Pine Meadow, Pine Barren, Pitcher-Plant Meadow, Pitcher-Plant Bog, Herbaceous Bog, Flatwoods Bog Western Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savannas are herb-dominated wetlands that are naturally sparsely stocked with Longleaf Pine. This community is most often dominated by numerous grasses and sedges in the understory and is noted for very high plant diversity, including insectivorous plants, showy orchids, lilies, and many other types of wildflowers. Club-mosses (Lycopodium spp.) and peat mosses are often conspicuous. One essential difference between Western and Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savannas is that the western expression of this system is typically associated with pimple-mounded topography, generating a complex matrix of upland and wetland plant communities. The only known extant Louisiana occurrences of American Chaffseed (Schwalbea americana), which is federally endangered, are found on pimple mounds in Western Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna in Allen and Beauregard parishes. Pine savannas historically dominated the Gulf Coastal Plain flatwoods regions of southeast and southwest Louisiana 
(Smith 1996)
.
Two types of Western Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna are recognized: Acidic (S2) and Saline (sodic/alkali; S1). Acidic Western Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna occurs on a variety of soils, while Saline Western Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna is restricted to mainly Brimstone Silt Loam and possibly other sodic or alkali soil types.
Wet savannas in the WGCP occupy the poorly drained and seasonally saturated/flooded depressional areas and low flats, whereas the non-wetland flatwoods occupy better drained, low ridges. Pine savannas experience a highly fluctuating water table, ranging from surface saturation/shallow flooding in late fall, winter, and early spring to growing season drought. Soils are hydric, very strongly acidic, nutrient poor, fine sandy loams and silt loams, and are low in organic matter. The surface soils for both western and eastern types may be underlain by slowly permeable subsoil through which air and water move slowly.
Frequent fire is a major factor controlling species occurrence and community structure. All of the species indigenous to pine savannas have evolved over millennia within a regime of frequent (once every one to three years) surface fires, and most depend on fire for perpetuation. Fire stimulates flowering and fruit/seed production of savanna herbs and shrubs, deters invasion by fire-intolerant woody vegetation, and exposes mineral soil for herb and Longleaf Pine seedlings to become established.Without frequent fire (particularly growing season burns, which more accurately mimic historical fire regimes), shrubs and trees, especially Loblolly and Slash pines, will gain dominance and eventually eliminate the herbaceous flora.

## Extent and Status

Western Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna and embedded habitats are highly threatened and much reduced from their original extent. This habitat is estimated to have occupied 1 million to 2 million acres in pre-settlement times with an estimated 1% to 5% remaining 
(Smith 1993)
.
Most extant Western Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna occur on private land, although one of the largest tracts occurs on the southwestern portion of KNF Vernon Unit. A combination of factors has favored their continued existence during the last 100 years, including their utilization as rangeland (involving frequent burning). Several examples are captured by the newly acquired LDWF Flatwoods Savanna WMA and conservation lands owned by TNC. When science-based criteria and standards are employed, wetland mitigation banking can be a key tool for conservation of this habitat. Recovery potential in degraded examples varies depending on the site history. In some cases, much of the diverse herbaceous ground cover has returned with the reintroduction of fire.

## Threats

Threats include conversion to Slash or Loblolly pine plantations, residential and commercial development, fire exclusion or inappropriate fire regime, hydrological alterations, contamination by chemicals (herbicides, fertilizers), and physical damage from timber harvesting and planting activities 
(Smith 1996)
, severe weather events, and climate change. Invasive species also threaten this habitat. 

## Research Needs and

## Threats

Most of the historical acreage of this habitat now supports anthropogenic forests. Due to rarity and limited opportunity, additional habitat conversion is expected to be infrequent but to have severe consequences where it does occur. This habitat is mainly threatened by inadequate fire. Climate change, with its projected increase in severe weather, threatens this habitat.
Several sources of human disturbance also degrade this habitat.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Continue surveys to determine the extent and condition of this habitat. 2. Educate landowners, adjacent residents, developers, and the general public about the crucial role of prescribed burning in the management of Longleaf Pine. 3. Continue to promote advantages of growing Longleaf Pine and associated herbaceous ground cover in cooperation with the Longleaf Alliance and incorporate their strategies for restoration into new and ongoing restoration efforts. 4. Promote value-added products produced from Longleaf Pine to encourage landowners to replant Longleaf Pine instead of off-site pine species. 5. Support and provide cost-share opportunities to offset costs to landowners for management activities such as prescribed burning, brush control, and invasive species control in this habitat. 6. Target this habitat for acquisition, protection (e.g., conservation servitudes), and stewardship implementation. This includes pursuing tracts that are degraded but restorable with timber harvesting and prescribed fire. Trees, often appearing stunted, may be present in deeper, more frequently flooded and, therefore, less fire-exposed, areas of ponds. The hydrologic regime of these ponds is characterized by a seasonally fluctuating water level -dry in summer and fall and flooded to various depths in winter and early spring. This water level fluctuation causes distinct vegetation zones with species occurring according to their relative tolerance or competitive adaptations to flooding and saturated soil conditions. The Flatwoods Pond natural communities were historically maintained by frequent lightning-generated fires that swept the Longleaf Pine flatwoods savannas every few years. Such fires burned into the ponds during the late spring/summer dry season, killing back encroaching shrubs and trees and rejuvenating the herbaceous ground cover. These communities provide important breeding habitat for many amphibians, including several SGCN. 
Smith (1993)
 estimated that Flatwoods Pond habitat occupied 2,000 to 10,000 acres historically. 
Bridges (1988)
 surveyed the WGCP of Louisiana extensively for this community and estimated that less than 100 Flatwoods Ponds remain and determined that less than 20 were still of high-quality. Based on recent surveys by LDWF staff, it is estimated that less than 80 Flatwoods Ponds remain in Louisiana and that approximately 12 to 15 remain in a high-quality state. It is not clear if Smith's estimate is for all Flatwoods Pond habitat or simply those in the WGCP. However, these features are significantly more prolific in the WGCP than the EGCP. Examples of high quality Flatwoods Ponds can be observed at the newly acquired LDWF Flatwoods Savanna WMA in Allen Parish. 

## Extent and Status

## SHORTLEAF PINE/OAK-HICKORY WOODLAND

Rarity Rank: G2G3/S1 Synonyms: Shortleaf Pine-Oak Woodland, Oak-Hickory Forest
The Shortleaf Pine/Oak-Hickory Woodland community occurs on dry hills, principally in north-central and northwestern Louisiana as well as in the Florida Parishes. In the UWGCP, this was the most prevalent habitat on the landscape (i.e. it was the matrix community). Small pockets of this community type also occur in the Longleaf Pine region of the WGCP. The overstory is composed of a combination of Shortleaf Pine (Pinus echinata) and various dry-sited hardwood species, predominantly oaks and hickories.
The herbaceous component of this community is just as much, if not more, important than the canopy component. The ground cover was historically grassy and similar to that of Longleaf Pine systems. However, the ground cover in Shortleaf Pine/ Oak-Hickory Woodland was likely variable and possessed some shaded areas with associated shade-loving plants, in contrast to large continuous stands of sun-loving plants found in Longleaf Pine grasslands. Some expressions of this community have a calcareous component and, thus, are similar to calcareous woodland and forest types. Fire is an important process in this community, and historical fire frequency is thought to have been 5 to 15 years 
(Martin and Smith 1993)
 or more frequently but with very low intensities and restricted to the duff layer, especially for Shortleaf Pine/Oak-Hickory Woodland embedded within other pyrogenic communities.

## Extent and Status

There was an estimated 4 million to 6 million acres of Shortleaf Pine/Oak-Hickory Woodland in Louisiana 
(Smith 1993
) and, of this original extent, less than 5% is thought to remain today. Most 

## Extent and Status

Xeric Sandhill Woodlands are more frequent west of the Mississippi River. A few examples of this habitat are known from stream terraces (e.g., along Pushepatappa Creek). Pre-settlement extent of Xeric Sandhill Woodland habitat west of the Mississippi River is estimated to have been 50,000 to 100,000 acres, with 10-25% remaining today 
(Smith 1993)

## Threats

The main threats to this habitat are destruction by residential and commercial development and conversion of land for energy production and anthropogenic forests, as well as disturbance from several sources including mineral extraction and other human activities. Inadequate fire is also a threat to occurrences that are situated in a position on the landscape where fire was historically important in shaping the habitat. Brackish Marsh is usually found between Salt Marsh and Intermediate Marsh, although it may occasionally lie adjacent to the Gulf of America. This community is irregularly tidally flooded and is dominated by salt-tolerant graminoids. Small pools or ponds may be scattered throughout. Plant diversity and soil organic matter content are higher in Brackish Marsh than in Salt Marsh. Brackish Marsh is typically dominated by Marshhay Cord Grass (Spartina patens). Two other major autotrophic groups in Brackish Marsh are epiphytic algae and benthic algae. Vertebrate species population levels are generally higher in Brackish Marsh compared to Salt Marsh. Brackish Marsh is of very high value to estuarine larval forms of marine organisms such as shrimp, crabs, and Menhaden (Brevoortia tyrannus). Brackish Marsh salinity averages about 8 ppt; however, this community may transition to other marsh types by shifts in salinity. Intrusion of salt water from the Gulf via numerous waterways exerts a major influence in the configuration of the various marsh types.

## Research Needs and

High Marsh is a unique form of coastal marsh that occurs on slightly higher elevation sites and is thus flooded infrequently, typically only during extreme tides, spring tides, and storm surge events. management of these sites is largely aimed at preserving and improving wintering waterfowl habitat. This involves the use of water control structures to regulate water levels and salinity input, water and sediment diversions to abate marsh deterioration, and prescribed burning to improve habitat and food quality for wildlife.

## Threats

The main threats to this habitat include sediment supply reduction due to development, levees, canal dredging, subsidence, SLR, incompatible fire and livestock management practices, and the effects of potential increases in storm frequency and intensity associated with climate change. 

## Research Needs and

## COASTAL MANGROVE-MARSH AND SHRUBLAND

## EXTENT OF COASTAL MANGROVE-MARSH AND SHRUBLAND

several important ecological functions: the extensive root systems stabilize the shoreline and reduce erosion; the cover and food they provide create an excellent nursery area for fish and shellfish; the community improves surrounding water quality by filtering nutrients and suspended sediments; and many colonial waterbirds use mangroves for nesting.

## Extent and Status

Coastal Mangrove-Marsh and Shrubland in Louisiana is found along the fringes of the Deltaic Plain coastal marshes, most commonly flanking large bays and on the leeward side of barrier islands. Estimations of areal coverage by this habitat have varied widely. The limitations of past and present aerial photography technology and difficulties associated with ground-truthing can make estimating acreage problematic. Research Needs and Conservation Actions 1. Promote the continued planting of mangrove as a soil stabilizer in habitat restoration projects. 2. Support partner efforts for shoreline stabilization and habitat restoration. 3. Work with partners to support coastal restoration projects, specifically targeting habitat and habitat features important for SGCN.

## FRESHWATER FLOATING MARSH

Rarity Rank: GNR/S2 Synonyms: Flotant, Peat Marsh, Prairie Tremblant
Freshwater Floating Marsh is an emergent marsh that, along with peat (decomposing organic matter), composes a freefloating mat that rises and falls with water levels. The flotant described herein has a 2-3 ft. thick mat that typically supports the weight of a person. The grass Maidencane (Hymenachne hemitoma) is the dominant plant in this community and is apparently the best species for forming buoyant floating mats due to its prolific root production. Evidence suggests that this Freshwater Floating Marsh developed from detachment of a rooted marsh following formation of a peat zone 
(Swarzenski et al. 1991
, Sasser et al. 1995
, Sasser et al. 1996)
. Buoyancy of the floating mat is affected by the capacity of the vegetation to float (internal air spaces, vegetative characteristics), capacity of the substrate to retain metabolic gases, and low bulk density of the substrate 
(Swarzenski et al. 1991
, Sasser et al. 1995
, Sasser et al. 1996)
. The Maidencane-dominated Freshwater Floating Marsh is buoyant year-round, whereas thick-mat Freshwater Floating Marsh dominated by Bulltongue (Sagittaria lancifolia) is only seasonally buoyant 
(Swarzenski et al. 1991)
. Freshwater Floating Marsh of the type described here is typically rainfall, rather than floodwater-driven 
(Swarzenski et al. 2005)
, and the pH is usually acidic (C. Swarzenski, personal communication).
Peat mosses (Sphagnum spp.) are often conspicuous. This habitat supports a number of plants that otherwise occur in acidic seepage wetlands in interior Louisiana, including several showy orchids. As with interior prairies and pine grasslands, Freshwater Floating Marsh is readily colonized by the shrub Wax-myrtle. Fire is required to prevent conversion of emergent herbaceous marsh to shrub thicket.
The Maidencane Freshwater Floating Marsh is restricted to freshwater environments. State transitions can occur with environmental changes such as increases in salinity, sediment input, and nutrient input. With increasing salinity, the plant community may transition to a Bulltongue-dominated community 
(Sasser et al. 1996
, Swarzenski et al. 1991)
. Key to the sustainability of the Freshwater Floating Marsh is a thick healthy mat. Nutrients and sulfate introduced by seawater can weaken the floating mat by accelerating decomposition of the peat. More than half of the Freshwater Floating Marshes in the Terrebonne
Basin have converted to thin, unstable mats and open water over the past 50 years 
(Visser et al. 1996)
. Concurrently, the source of fresh water in the Terrebonne Basin has shifted from rain water to river water 
(Swarzenski et al. 2008)
. Eutrophication by introduction of Mississippi River water via diversions may destabilize intact floating marshes 
(Swarzenski et al. 2008)
. Salinity pulses, if increasing in persistence and duration, could also destabilize thick mat Freshwater Floating Marsh.
Colonization of stands of free-floating plants by emergent marsh vegetation can happen. For example, free-floating invasives such as Water Hyacinth (Eichhornia crassipes) and Common and Giant Salvinia (Salvinia minima and S. molesta, respectively) can recruit emergent aquatic and wetland plants, eventually forming a well-developed floating mat 
(Penfound and Earle 1948)
. Such floating mats are outside of the concept of the Freshwater Floating Marsh discussed here, despite the broad and general application of the term "flotant."

## Extent and Status

The majority of floating marsh in Louisiana is associated with the Deltaic Plain. However, a unique form of freshwater floating marsh was described by O'Neal (1949) from the eastern portion of the Chenier Plain. Floating marshes of all types are estimated to occupy 375,000 acres 
(Evers et al. 1996
, Sasser et al. 1996)
 Chapter 5: Natural Communities

## Threats

Freshwater Floating Marsh is threatened by input of nutrients and salinity, which is hastened by human activities associated with placement of canals and diversions as well as other habitat alteration to the marsh landscape. Inadequate fire is also an issue for some occurrences, which allows shrub dominance. Because this marsh type is highly buoyant, it has some resilience to subsidence; however, increases in salinity associated with subsidence of surrounding rooted marshes poses a serious threat to this habitat. Aerial overspray from herbicide applications or excessive herbicide use may have prolonged negative impacts and cause floating mash collapse.

## EXTENT OF FRESHWATER FLOATING MARSH

## FRESHWATER MARSH

Rarity Rank: G3G4/S2 Synonyms: Fresh Marsh, Paille Fine (pronounced "pie feen") Marsh
Freshwater Marsh is normally located adjacent to Intermediate Marsh along the northern most extent of the coastal marshes, although it may occur beside coastal bays where freshwater enters (e.g., Atchafalaya Bay). Small pools or ponds may be scattered. The floristic composition of these sites is quite heterogeneous and variable from site to site. Frequency and duration of flooding, which are intimately related to microtopography, seem to be the primary factors governing plant species distributions. Substrate, current flow, salinity, competition, and allelopathy are also important in determining species distribution patterns. Freshwater Marsh has the greatest plant diversity and highest soil organic matter content of any marsh type. 
Chabreck (1972)
 As with the other marsh types, Freshwater Marsh acts as an important nursery area for the young of many marine species such as Atlantic Croaker (Micropogonias undulates), Spotted Seatrout (Cynoscion nebulosus), Black Drum (Pogonias cromis), and flounder (Paralichthys spp.). This community may change to a more saline marsh type due to saltwater intrusion or may become open water due to subsidence.

## Extent and Status

Coastal marshes are highly dynamic systems; thus, the acreage of each marsh type has varied greatly across time. Presettlement, Louisiana coastal marshes covered approximately 4.5 million acres 
(Smith 1993)
. Based on surveys conducted in 2021, the remaining total coastal marsh extent in Louisiana is 3.62 million acres 
(Nyman et al. 2022)
, which is approximately 80% of the estimate pre-settlement extent. Statewide coastal marsh surveys conducted in 2021, estimated that approximately 992,000 acres are Freshwater Marsh 
(Nyman et al. 2022)
.
Freshwater Marsh has undergone the largest reduction in acreage of any of the marsh types over the past 30 years, due mainly to saltwater intrusion; canal dredging; and commercial, industrial and residential development. The largest contiguous tracts of Freshwater Marsh occur in Terrebonne, St. Mary, Vermilion, Cameron, Lafourche, and St. Charles parishes 
(Hartley et al. 2000)
. In the Chenier Plain of southwestern Louisiana, federal lands containing Freshwater Marsh include Sabine, Cameron Prairie, and Lacassine NWRs. White Lake Wetlands Conservation Area captures a substantial amount of Freshwater Marsh on state land. In the Deltaic Plain of southeastern Louisiana, LDWF lands with Freshwater Marsh include the Atchafalaya Delta, Salvador, Timken, Pass-a-Loutre, and Pearl River WMAs, and to a lesser extent, Joyce and Maurepas Swamp WMAs. Federal lands with Freshwater Marsh in the Deltaic Plain include Delta, Bayou Sauvage, Big Branch Marsh, and Mandalay NWRs.

## NON-FORESTED GRASSLANDS AND OPEN TERRAIN

## Threats

Threats to this habitat include sediment and freshwater supply reduction due to levees, canal dredging, subsidence, SLR, salinity input, and invasive species (especially Feral Hogs and Nutria). Increased storm frequency and intensity associated with climate change would subject Freshwater Marsh to a greater disturbance and potentially result in higher incidences of saltwater intrusion in concert with SLR. 

## Research Needs and

## INTERMEDIATE MARSH

Rarity Rank: G4?/S3 Synonyms: Oligohaline Marsh Intermediate Marsh is fresh most of the time but is occasionally affected by saltwater inputs associated with tropical storm surges. This marsh type typically lies between Brackish Marsh and Freshwater Marsh and in estuaries, although it infrequently may be adjacent to the Gulf of America. Intermediate Marsh has an irregular tidal regime and is oligohaline (salinity of 3-10 ppt). Small pools or ponds may be scattered throughout the marsh. Plant diversity and soil organic matter content is higher than in Brackish Marsh. This marsh is characterized by a diversity of species, many of which are found in Freshwater Marsh and some of which are found in Brackish Marsh. 
Chabreck (1972)
 Intermediate Marsh, St. Tammany Parish 

## SALT MARSH

Rarity Rank: G5/S4 Synonyms: Smooth Cord Grass Marsh, Saltgrass Marsh, Saline Marsh Typically, Salt Marsh is the marsh area closest to the beach rim of the Gulf of America and, in general, varies from 1 to 15 miles in width. This marsh is a regularly tidally flooded, flat, polyhaline Small pools or ponds may be scattered. Salt Marsh has the lowest plant diversity and soil organic matter content of any marsh type. This community is strongly dominated by Smooth Cord Grass (Spartina alterniflora). Two other major groups of autotrophs found in Salt Marsh are microscopic algae located on the surface of the vascular plants and benthic algae (usually diatoms) living on or in marsh sediment. Soil and water conditions regulate plant growth, and salinity appears to be the primary factor determining species composition. The mean salinity of Salt Marsh is about 16 ppt. The area of Salt Marsh is increasing, apparently due to saltwater intrusion resulting in shifts in marsh salinity levels. Salt Marsh provides nursery areas for larval forms of many species such as shrimp, crabs, Red Drum, Spotted Seatrout, and Menhaden, and greatly enhances the production of such marine organisms due to the enormous primary productivity of the Salt Marsh vegetation. Factors that promote the growth of Salt Marsh plants include: (1) a long growing season, (2) abundant rainfall, (3) presence of soil nutrients, (4) low tide differential, and (5) tidally transported nutrients. Factors negatively impacting Salt Marsh include prolonged periods of inundation, subsidence, and erosion.

## EXTENT OF SALT MARSH

Salt Marsh also functions as a nitrogen and phosphorus sink (at least seasonally), thereby improving the quality of water that passes through it. In addition, Salt Marsh can alleviate the effects of storms and flooding farther inland by acting as a buffer and providing storage for large amounts of water. Although Salt Marsh is known for low species diversity overall, a few species are wholly dependent on this habitat. For example, Seaside Sparrow (Ammospiza maritima) is endemic to Salt Marsh, one of only five such Salt Marsh-obligate vertebrate species on Earth 
(Greenberg et al. 2006)
.

## Extent and Status

Coastal marshes are highly dynamic systems; thus, the acreage of each marsh type has varied greatly across time. Presettlement, Louisiana coastal marshes covered approximately 4.5 million acres 
(Smith 1993)
. Based on surveys conducted in 2021, the remaining total coastal marsh extent in Louisiana is 3.62 million acres 
(Nyman et al. 2022)
, which is approximately 80% of the estimate pre-settlement extent. Statewide coastal marsh surveys conducted in 2021, estimated that 805,650 acres are Salt Marsh 
(Nyman et al. 2022
). Salt Marsh is most extensive on the Deltaic Plain of southeastern Louisiana. The area of Salt Marsh is currently increasing, likely due to saltwater intrusion resulting in shifts in marsh salinity levels. 

## NON-FORESTED GRASSLANDS AND OPEN TERRAIN

## VEGETATED PIONEER EMERGING DELTA

Rarity Rank: G3G4/S3 Synonyms: Delta Flats, Emergent Islands Vegetated Pioneer Emerging Delta is a dynamic community forming primarily within the actively building delta region at the mouth of the Atchafalaya and Mississippi rivers. Substrates contain a greater percentage of sand and less moisture than do marsh soils. The pioneer ridge vegetation is similar to the Sandbars and delta of the Mississippi River, whereas the pioneer marsh vegetation is similar to that of Freshwater Marsh. This community can be floristically diverse, containing many species also found on Sandbars. Rapid invasion by the invasive Torpedo Grass (Panicum repens) reduces plant species richness, particularly on higher sandy deposits. The pioneer community is successional in nature and changes rapidly with time. The new delta community's ecological functions are similar in nature to marsh and mudflat systems.

## Extent and Status

According to 
Smith (1993)
, there was an estimated 2,000 to 10,000 acres of Vegetated Pioneer Emerging Delta in presettlement times; an estimated 75% to 100% of this amount is present today. Two areas of the Louisiana coast support this habitat: the actively forming Atchafalaya Delta and the current mouth of the Mississippi River. In the case of the former area, newly accreted delta land is incorporated into Atchafalaya Delta WMA. Pass-A-Loutre WMA, near the mouth of the Mississippi River, contains natural and constructed crevasses which promote the expansion of this habitat type.

## Threats

The greatest threat to this habitat is invasive plant and animal species, primarily Feral Hogs and Nutria, which denude newly created delta habitat via rooting and wallowing behavior and direct herbivory, respectively. 

## Research Needs and

## Extent and Status

Since deltaic processes have been altered due to the leveeing of the Mississippi River, no new barrier islands are expected to form. Major efforts are underway to rebuild and preserve remaining islands. These efforts include using breakwaters to buffer wave action and retain sediment, pumping sand onto beaches and dunes, creating back barrier marsh platforms, and the use of sand fencing and vegetative planting to anchor sand and stabilize the substrate. 

## Threats

Barrier Islands are threatened by habitat destruction and disturbance by humans, trash and marine debris accumulation, subsidence, SLR, hydrologic alterations, inadequate sand supply, and increased frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones. Calcareous Prairie is typically a small, naturally treeless area occurring on calcareous substrata in the uplands of central, western, and northwestern Louisiana. Prairie communities can range in size from less than one acre to 100 or more acres and occur in a mosaic with Calcareous Forests 
(Smith et al. 1989)
.

## Extent and Status

## COASTAL PRAIRIE

Rarity Rank: G2Q/S1 Synonyms: Great Southwest Prairie, Eastern Coastal Prairie, Gulf Cordgrass Prairie, Cajun Prairie
Coastal Prairie is an extension of the tall-grass prairie of the eastern Great Plains and is characterized by a diverse flora consisting of tall grasses and forbs. A combination of historical dry climate intervals, clay-pan soils (which accentuate the effects of drought), and frequent fire are thought to account for the presence of tall-grass prairie in humid Louisiana. Studies of remnant prairies suggest there are three prairie types, based on moisture: wet (marsh-fringing) prairie, wet-mesic prairie, and dry-mesic prairie. Small, circular, soil mounds known as pimple mounds can be found in Coastal Prairie. These mounds were formed by eolian deposition during historical harsh droughts (Siefert et al. 2009) and, possibly, fluvial deposition during periods with higher than normal precipitation 
(Wilcox et al. 2011)
. Pimple mounds and embedded marshes and ponds (e.g., Prairie Pothole) add to the habitat diversity of the Coastal Prairie landscape.

## Extent and Status

Coastal Prairie historically occupied about 2.22 million acres in southwest Louisiana 
(Grace et al. 2000)
. About 0.02% (4,000 to 5,000 acres) of the original Coastal Prairie remains today 
(Early et al. 2024
). The marsh-fringing prairie type is represented by several remnants and totals about 1,015 acres. White Lake Wetlands Conservation Area supports the largest and best examples of this wet prairie type, while small highly degraded examples are captured by Sabine NWR and Cameron Prairie NWR. Wet-mesic prairie occupies approximately 3,015 acres.
All of these remnants are on private lands in Calcasieu and Cameron parishes. LDWF is currently working with owners of most of these sites to implement stewardship. The dry-mesic prairie type, which historically accounted for most of the prairie acreage, is now known only along railroads and within pimplemounded areas imbedded in wet-mesic sites. Less than 300 acres of dry-mesic prairie likely remain. All the railroad remnants are in various states of degradation due to woody encroachment, soil disturbance, and prolonged fire suppression. 

## MISSISSIPPI TERRACE PRAIRIE

Rarity Ranks: G2/SX Synonyms: Macon Ridge Prairie Mississippi Terrace Prairie was a tall-grass prairie type of northeastern Louisiana found on Pleistocene terraces within the MRAP floodplain. This prairie may have been similar to the Grand Prairie of eastern Arkansas. Frequent fire and soil type would have prevented invasion of woody species. Topographic maps indicate some historical occurrences of Mississippi Terrace Prairie in northeastern Louisiana such as the Boeuf Prairie in Franklin Parish. This habitat is now considered extirpated in Louisiana.

## Extent and Status

Mississippi Terrace Prairie historically occupied less than 2,000 acres in the MRAP ecoregion in northeast Louisiana 
(Smith 1993)
. This community was converted primarily to agriculture, but some areas were also converted for housing development.
No known examples remain today. However, several small remnants of a calcareous or other unknown prairie type occur in the region, which appear to be similar to Mississippi Terrace Prairie.
Chapter 5: Natural Communities

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Conduct field surveys within and near areas that historically supported Mississippi Terrace Prairie for grasslands which retain some prairie plants, such as pastures and neglected agricultural land ("go-back" lands). 2. Promote management (e.g., prescribed fire) on prairie-like grasslands in areas where this habitat occurred historically. Upland Sodic/Alkali Barren soils have high levels of exchangeable sodium and sometimes magnesium in the subsoil and near the surface horizons, which create extreme conditions for plant growth. Such conditions include relatively high alkalinity, very poor movement of water and air in the soil, resistance to wetting that can induce droughty conditions, resistance to drying once saturated, and a sodic horizon in the subsoil which acts much like a dense claypan that is resistant to root penetration. The principal soils associated with Sodic/Alkali Barren natural communities are Bonn, Brimstone, and Lafe soil series 
(Reid et al. 2010
). However, Guyton soils or soil map units containing the Guyton soils series are the primary mapped soil units at some Sodic/Alkali Barrens 
(Reid et al. 2010)
et al. 2009
et al. , Reid et al. 2010))
. Wet Sodic/Alkali Barren occurs on lower landscape positions than upland prairie and is seasonally flooded. The flora of wet Sodic/Alkali Barren is entirely different from that of upland prairie and includes several plants that also occur in coastal saline habitats. Wet expressions of this system also feature large barren patches and are in need of more detailed floristic edaphic studies.

## SODIC/ALKALI BARREN (FORMERLY SALINE PRAIRIE)

Rarity Rank: G1G2/S1 Synonyms: Alkali Flat, Barrens, Salt Barren, Slick, Saline Prairie Sodic/Alkali Barren is a small-scale grassland, often occurring within various natural community types. Sodic/Alkali Barren habitat ranges from less than one acre to about 30 acres in size. There are two types of Sodic/Alkali Barren classified by hydrology and landscape position: dry-mesic (upland) and wet. Dry-mesic Sodic/Alkali Barren occurs on fluvial terraces adjacent to active small stream floodplains, and its soils formed in Pleistocene alluvium. Wet Sodic/Alkali Barren occurs in stream valleys subject to regular flooding. Wet expressions of this community sometimes transition upslope into dry-mesic Sodic/ Alkali Barren. Some areas of these systems contain exposed or sparsely vegetated soil known as "slicks," which support "cryptogamic lips" dominated by algae, mosses, lichens, and very small flowering plants 
(Pittman 1988, Reid et

## NON-FORESTED GRASSLANDS AND OPEN TERRAIN

## Extent and Status

Sodic/Alkali Barren was estimated to have occupied less than 2,000 acres historically 
(Smith 1993)
. It is unclear whether this estimate includes both upland and wet variants. An effort was made from 2006 to 2008 to locate Sodic/Alkali Barrens in northwestern Louisiana. This work was very successful, revealing about 10 new records and expanding the range of the Sodic/Alkali Barren to include three additional parishes 
(Reid et al. 2010)
. Sodic/Alkali Barren is likely extirpated in East Baton Rouge and Livingston parishes; however, small remnants in these parishes may persist in utility corridors that intersect sodic/alkali soils.

## Threats

Sodic/Alkali Barren is threatened by disturbance associated with mineral extraction and roads and service corridors as well as afforestation attempts. Several recently rediscovered Sodic/ Alkali Barrens are under threat of commercial and residential development as they are not protected. Feral Hogs pose a serious threat to Sodic/Alkali Barren communities.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Continue surveys to monitor the current extent and condition of this habitat type.

## LENTIC WATER BODIES

## COASTAL FRESHWATER SUBMERGED AQUATIC VEGETATION

Rarity Ranks: G4/S3S4 Synonyms: none
Well-developed Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation (SAV) beds often occur in lentic water bodies within the coastal plain. Typically, this natural community consists primarily of freshwater but may include pulses or short durations of higher salinities ranging from 0 to 5 ppt salinity. Species found within the Coastal Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation natural community can overlap those found in other SAV types such as Interior Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation and Estuarine Submerged Aquatic Vegetation communities.
Floating-leaved aquatics such as water-lilies (Nymphaea spp.) are often conspicuous in Coastal Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation. This habitat occurs throughout coastal Freshwater Marsh and the interface of Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest. This SAV type develops in natural and man-made water bodies.

## Extent and Status

Exemplary occurrences of this habitat can be found in Lacassine Pool on Lacassine NWR, White Lake Wetlands Conservation Area, and Salvador WMA. Other public lands that support this community include Pass-a-Loutre, Atchafalaya Delta, and Lake Boeuf WMAs and Delta NWR. The historical extent is unknown, and current acreage has not been estimated. 

## INTERIOR FRESHWATER SUBMERGED AQUATIC VEGETATION

Rarity Ranks: G4/S5 Synonyms: none
Interior Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation is highly variable and can occupy swamps, lakes (e.g., oxbows), reservoirs (especially upper ends), sluggish bayous, Beaver ponds, and small farm ponds. Water turbidity, temperature, substrate, wave action, nutrient loads, and light penetration are key factors in determining the composition of the flora and fauna of SAV beds. The benefits of SAV include oxygenation of water, habitat structure for all forms of aquatic life (e.g., shade for fish), and a basis for aquatic food webs that benefits all wildlife. The magnitude of benefits may vary based on the quantity and quality of habitat 

## Extent and Status

This habitat occurs statewide in a variety of water bodies. Areal extent, degree of development, and plant species richness vary widely.

## Threats

Interior Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation is threatened by residential and commercial development; contamination by agricultural, municipal, and industrial effluents; eutrophication; trash dumping; aging infrastructure; and invasive species such as Water Hyacinth, Giant Salvinia, and Hydrilla. Climate change poses an additional threat, especially if there are reductions in precipitation.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Continue surveys to document and describe exemplary occurrences of this SAV type. 2. Produce literature presenting information on the identification of aquatic plants and explaining the benefits of SAV.

## Produce and distribute a poster series highlighting Interior

Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation habitat and associated native and exotic aquatic plants. 4. Continue to invest in cleaning stations at boat ramps to limit the spread of invasive aquatic plants. 5. Work with partners to minimize human impacts and develop preventative measure to avoid or minimize impacts from nutrient loading, sedimentation, and other pollution affecting this natural community type.

## LAKES AND RESERVOIRS

Rarity Rank: S5 Synonyms: none
Lakes are larger and usually deeper than ponds, but no strict size or depth criteria exist for designating a particular water body as a lake. Natural lakes in Louisiana include oxbows and other floodplain lakes occupying abandoned river channels.
Oxbow lakes form when a river meander is cut off and left as a free-standing water body; as a result, oxbows are typically U-shaped. Other natural lakes occur in coastal Barataria, Calcasieu, Mermentau, and Terrebonne basins. Natural lakes provide valuable fish and wildlife habitat.
Reservoirs are man-made lakes created by impounding streams and can be relatively small or up to thousands of acres (e.g., Toledo Bend). The Red River Raft lakes (Lake Bistineau, Caddo Lake, and Cross Lake) were formed by damming of the Red River by the "Great Raft," a massive log jam that persisted for centuries. Following clearing of the Great Raft in the 19th century, water levels in these lakes fluctuated greatly until control structures were installed to maintain these lakes as reservoirs. Although reservoirs can provide habitat that is utilized by native species, including some SGCN, it is always desirable to remove impoundments and restore natural hydrology and habitat connectivity.
Interior Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation, Sabine Parish

## INLAND AND MARINE

## Extent and Status

River Delta Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation beds are associated with the Mississippi and Atchafalaya river deltas. This community can be found on Atchafalaya Delta and Pass-a-Loutre WMAs as well as Delta NWR.

## Threats

River Delta Freshwater Submerged Aquatic Vegetation is threatened by disturbance associated with mineral extraction, canals, utility corridors, and damage from outboard motors. Invasive species and the possible increase in frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones associated with climate change may impact this habitat. Increased salinity due to altered hydrology, marsh loss, and SLR also threaten this community.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Conduct studies to determine the areal extent of this SAV type and address basic ecological questions regarding its development and maintenance. 2. Reduce human disturbance of this habitat type on public and private lands and waterways.

## RIVERS, STREAMS, CREEKS, AND BAYOUS

Rarity Rank: GNR/SNR Synonyms: None River, stream, and creek are interchangeable terms for flowing water, with no technical definition (USGS 2018). In general, rivers are typically considered the largest of the three, with streams intermediate in size and creeks the smallest. These water bodies are formed when water moves from an area of higher elevation to an area of lower elevation due to gravity. Water comes from direct precipitation but also as runoff from the surrounding land surface. Land surfaces that drain water into rivers, streams, and creeks are known as basins; for more information on the basins in Louisiana, see pages 167-181.
Bayous are slow-moving, typically shallow, water bodies, found in flat areas where water pools. They can consist of fresh, brackish, or salt water and often have abundant vegetation. Some bayous, like Bayou Bartholomew, have high biological diversity and are critical habitat for mussel SGCN. 

## INLAND AND MARINE

## Extent and Status

Louisiana contains over 120,000 miles of rivers, streams, creeks, and bayous throughout the state 
(XU 2004)
. The Mississippi River, the second longest river in the United States at approximately 2,300 miles long, is a major transporation artery that empties into the Gulf of America (NPS 2025). Bayou Bartholomew, the longest bayou in the world, flows for approximately 364 miles through Arkansas and Louisiana, before converging with the Ouachita River (LDEQ 2025).

## Threats

Rivers are threatened by residential and commercial development; commerce and transportation; contamination by agricultural, municipal, and industrial effluents; loss of riparian habitat; instream and gravel mining; loss of instream and lateral connectivity; flow alteration; and invasive species such as bigheaded carps (Xenocyprididae) and Giant Salvinia. Streams, creeks, and bayous face many of the same challenges as rivers but on a more localized scale.

Research Needs and Conservation Actions 1. Investigate the impacts of impediments to aquatic organism passage and mitigate impacts with fish ladders, bypasses, conservation locking, and barrier removal, where appropriate. 2. Utilize green infrastructure, BMPs, and habitat restoration to minimize pollution and sedimentation inputs. 3. Restore natural flows and connections to floodplains to encourage resilient and healthy ecosystems. 4. Partner with LDEQ to promote practices such as requiring updated sewage systems in communities around lakes. 5. ## MARINE AND ESTUARINE WATER BODIES

## ESTUARINE SUBMERGED AQUATIC VEGETATION

Rarity Rank: G4?/S1 Synonyms: none
The Estuarine Submerged Aquatic Vegetation brackish water community consists of submerged, rooted vascular plants growing in shallow, protected waters with low turbidity. Water temperature, salinity (5 to 10 ppt), substrate, wave action, nutrient loads, and light penetration are key factors in determining the composition of the flora and fauna of SAV beds.
The substrate is often predominantly sand, but other substrates such as other minerals or Soft Mud Bottom can occur. In lower salinity regimes, these bottoms may be vegetated by water milfoils (Myriophyllum spp.), Widgeon-Grass (Ruppia maritima), Southern Naiad (Najas guadalupensis), and other species. The presence of SAV provides additional structure, shelter, and food to the animals dependent upon these habitats. Estuarine Submerged Aquatic Vegetation is more likely to be abundant in smaller, sheltered areas of Soft Mud Bottom and less likely to be present or abundant in areas where wave action or other factors causing turbulence and turbidity are persistent. Small scattered beds occur in relative abundance in brackish water ponds throughout coastal Louisiana. More extensive beds are found in the Lake Pontchartrain and Barataria basins. Although a small component of the larger estuarine ecosystem, these beds play an important ecological role. The beds support a diverse invertebrate and epiphytic population and serve as nursery grounds and shelter for many species of fishes and shellfish. In addition, these beds are extremely productive and release detritus and nutrients to surrounding waters. These beds lack widespread distribution due to the general turbidity of most of the estuaries in Louisiana.

## Extent and Status

Historical extent of this SAV type is unknown 
(Smith 1993)
. This SAV occurs in waters subject to occasional salinity pulses like those in Lake Pontchartrain and several lakes in the Barataria Basin such as Lake Salvador.

## INLAND AND MARINE

## Threats

Estuarine Submerged Aquatic Vegetation faces threats from various sources of human disturbance, including damage from outboard motors. Activities that increase the turbidity in the waters surrounding SAV beds threaten the viability of this community. Alteration to salinity levels due to marsh loss, tropical cyclones, and SLR also threaten this habitat.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Continue surveys to determine the extent and condition of this habitat type; revisit and evaluate existing occurrences in the Wildlife Diversity Program database. 2. Recommend a maximum vessel speed, vessel draft, and engine draft when boating in particularly sensitive areas such as shallow SAV beds and provide education about methods for boaters to reduce negative, unintentional impacts. 3. Protect this SAV type from damage resulting from boat traffic and development of oil and gas infrastructure. 4. Generate greater public awareness of the importance of SAV. Chapter 5: Natural Communities bottoms. Wave action, currents, temperature, salinity, substrate characteristics, and light penetration affected by turbidity determine species assemblage. Violent storms may drastically disrupt or alter community structure. Although this grass bed is a relatively small part of the ecosystem in coastal Louisiana, it is believed they play an extremely important role. The actual ecological value of this benthic grass community is poorly understood and may be underestimated. This is an extremely productive community often as productive as Salt Marsh. It provides food for a number of animals and acts as a nursery area and refuge for the young of many fishes and invertebrates. It can support a diverse epiphytic biota, including algae, fungi, bacteria, protozoans, bryozoans, and hydrozoans, thus creating a unique environment that allows for the existence of indigenous grass bed species. It supplies detrital material and nutrients to the water, adds oxygen via photosynthesis, and stabilizes bottom sediments by increasing deposition of suspended particulate matter.

## MARINE SUBMERGED AQUATIC VEGETATION

## Extent and Status

This habitat is restricted to the Chandeleur Islands, where it is extensive in the clear shallows on the leeward side of the islands.

## Threats

Long-term survival of the Marine Submerged Aquatic Vegetation natural community depends on having adequate protection from the Chandeleur Islands, which have degraded in recent decades. Impacts to seagrass beds by tropical cyclones, SLR, and outboard motors threaten this community.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Determine areal extent of, and map Marine Submerged Aquatic Vegetation at Chandeleur Islands. 2. Conduct inventory and monitoring to determine changes in condition and extent over time and to identify emerging threats.

measures to nourish the Chandeleur Islands, such as augmenting sand supply.

## MARINE SUBSTRATE HABITATS

Rarity Rank: SNA Synonyms: None
The following marine habitats are all submerged, primarily nonvegetated habitats and are described based on characteristics such as seafloor composition and the presence of seagrass beds. Although listed as "marine" habitats, the following substrates, except State Territorial Open Water, can be found adjacent to all marsh types and across all salinity regimes. Fishery managers in the Gulf of America suspect that estuarine hard bottoms support more diverse, complex communities than adjacent soft bottoms, prompting an increase in low profile artificial reefs.

## Threats

Shell and Shell Hash Bottom habitats are affected by variability in temperature and salinity levels that are a result of climate change, coastal restoration projects, and management practices that disrupt natural hydrology. These hydrological fluctuations, combined with overharvesting, nutrient loading, erosion, and invasive species, can alter ecosystem functions and undermine the resilience of mollusk populations 
(Banks et al. 2016)
.

(Rabalais et al. 2002)
. Further habitat disturbances arise from dredging, fishing activities, pollution, and invasive species

## Salinity in State Territorial

Open Water can vary widely by location and season. Near-freshwater conditions may be found near the mouths of the major rivers in high-water conditions, especially during the spring. Salinity above 30 ppt may regularly be found in the waters along the Chandeleur and Timbalier islands. Other areas of the state may have a similarly high salinity during years with drier conditions.

## Threats

Louisiana's State Territorial Open Water habitat is under significant threat from coastal erosion and hydrological alterations 
(CPRA 2023)
. Activities such as dredging and pollution, coupled with the impacts from changes in climate, are intensifying habitat degradation 
(Reed et al. 2012)
. Threats to biological communities stem from direct fishing pressures, bycatch mortality, and the presence of invasive species 
(Gallaway et al. 2000)
.

## AGRICULTURE AND IMPROVED PASTURE (EXCLUDING RICE)

Rarity Rank: SNA Synonyms: None Agriculture and Improved Pasture encompasses diverse land cover and land use features of altered habitats resulting from human activity. These areas typically are dominated by nonnative species and occur in every ecoregion throughout the state. The land cover types may include all or some of the following: h Scattered woody and herbaceous vegetation such as orchards (e.g., pecan and citrus), vineyards, experimental plots, and plant nurseries h Row and cover crops (e.g., grain, cotton, sweet potatoes, soybeans, and sugarcane) h Tilled or untilled fields containing exposed or partially exposed soil h Fallow fields or areas that have been left idle during the growing season h Pastures dominated by turf grasses used for ungulate grazing, hay fields, or sod farms h Rangelands on previously plowed land receiving minimal management inputs and supporting a variable mix of grasses and forbs, usually "low-end" forage grasses such as Old Field Broomsedge (Andropogon virginicus) and Smut Grass (Sporobolus indicus) Some species of wildlife benefit from agricultural production. Historically, agricultural practices and the type of crops produced were highly varied, and this provided habitat diversity for numerous species. As this habitat became less diverse with changing agricultural practices (i.e. "clean" agricultural practices) and larger tracts were put in agricultural production, the habitat quality on the landscape declined. Negative effects of this shift are particularly apparent for grassland bird species such as Northern Bobwhite, Loggerhead Shrike, and many species of sparrows. Broad-spectrum pesticides that are systemic in plant tissues (e.g., Neonicotinoids) have been implicated in negatively impacting native insects that utilize agricultural lands, including important pollinators such as bees and butterflies as well as other insects that are major constituents of food webs that support SGCN.
Within these habitat types, there may be patches of "natural" habitat such as vegetated streamsides, embedded wetlands, and small blocks of forest which can serve as important breeding, dispersal, and travel corridors for various wildlife species. Farm Bill programs such as Conservation Reserve Program and Wetlands Reserve Program have paid landowners to set aside or retire portions of farmlands from active production. Lands susceptible to erosion or farmed wetlands (lands on which yields are variable or unreliable) are typically enrolled and are usually planted in native vegetation. Young, replanted Bottomland Hardwood Forest (early successional) is heavily used by grassland and scrub-shrub Neotropical migratory birds and American Woodcock. Although no SGCN are fully dependent upon these habitats for survival, these systems often support high concentrations of resident and migratory species and will likely become increasingly important for these animals as changes in climate and urbanization claim otherwise suitable habitat.
Grain crops can support SGCN such as Northern Bobwhite and wintering sparrows when appropriate field borders are incorporated into farming operations. Rain-flooded (harvested or unharvested) grain fields also provide valuable foraging habitat for wintering waterfowl. Post-harvested or tilled grain fields, where flooded shallowly, are valuable habitats for a variety of shorebirds. Dry harvested fields are primary feeding areas for wintering geese and are also used by several SGCN.

## Soybean field

Cotton field, Rapides Parish

## Sugarcane

In fragmented habitats, conservation features on agricultural and pastoral lands may serve to connect patches of natural habitat. Irrigation ditches are heavily used by birds, mammals, and crustaceans. Fencerows serve as breeding sites for some birds and as impaling substrates for Loggerhead Shrike. Wooded drainages can serve as travel corridors for birds and mammals, especially highly mobile species such as Neotropical migratory birds and Louisiana Black Bear.

## Extent and Status

There are approximately 8 million acres of farm land in Louisiana (USDA NASS 2025). Working agricultural landscapes can be greatly enhanced for fish and wildlife value with proper planning. The USDA administered Farm Bill programs offer some of the greatest opportunities for these enhancements to occur, because of the sheer magnitude of funding associated with these programs. Programs such as CRP, WRP, and the Environmental Quality Incentives Program (EQIP) provide costshare, incentive payments, or both to qualified participants.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Encourage planting of native species along field borders and filter strips to create habitat and improve connectivity for wildlife species. 2. Encourage the development of "soft or feathered" edges on the agricultural landscape through natural succession and the planting of native grasses, legumes and forbs, and small shrubs (e.g., plum thickets and blackberry), and promote management to maintain these habitats. 3. Encourage management of fallow fields to maintain early successional habitat and to prevent invasion of woody vegetation and invasive species.
4. Target permanently fallowed agricultural fields for habitat stewardship opportunities to maintain grassland habitat and prevent dominance by woody vegetation. 5. Encourage management for and planting of native grasses and forbs and proper timing of mowing and haying to prevent destruction of burrows and nests in grasslands. 6. Encourage use of more pest-specific pesticides and pesticides that are not systemic in plant tissues. 7. Support and encourage prescribed burning as a routine rangeland management tool. 8. Work with partners and legislators to develop a comprehensive statewide water rights/use plan. 9. Provide landowners with information on federal and state incentive programs to promote BMPs on working lands. 10. Continue to coordinate with NRCS on the development of practices via the Farm Bill that are beneficial for SGCN. 11. Promote BMPs to minimize soil erosion and runoff.

## PINE PLANTATION AND CONTEMPORARY FOREST

Rarity Rank: SNA Synonyms: Loblolly Pine Plantation, Slash Pine Plantation Pine Plantation is a general category encompassing single species or homogenous plantings typically for the purposes of commercial timber production. In Louisiana, both Loblolly Pine and Slash Pine (Pinus elliotii) plantations are common, depending on geographic location. Loblolly Pine is most often planted for timber production by industrial and non-industrial private landowners because of its productivity and adaptability to a wide range of site conditions found throughout the West Gulf Coastal Plain and East Gulf Coastal Plain. Timber production for Slash Pine, which was introduced to southwest Louisiana, typically occurs in this region. Most pine plantations are managed similarly for production of various wood products, including many types of paper and packing products, fuel wood pellets, utility poles and pilings, structural lumber, and engineered wood products. Demand for these products over the last several decades has driven the expansion of pine plantations to replace many other habitat types on private lands across the state. Pine Plantation management generally includes intensive site preparation, high planting densities, one or more herbicide treatments, and multiple thinnings. Stands are usually regenerated by clearcutting and replanting at a rotation age of 25 to 30 years.
Although some species of wildlife utilize Pine Plantation, overall, this habitat type is of significantly lower quality when compared to natural habitat types in the Gulf Coastal Plain such as more open, grassy Longleaf Pine and Shortleaf Pine savannas and woodlands that historically dominated the landscape. Often, these and other higher quality community types are replaced by Pine Plantation. Pine Plantation has less plant species diversity in both the forest canopy and understory as a result of single species planting, high stocking (i.e. dense shading), more intensive use of herbicides, and exclusion of prescribed fire. However, habitat quality can be improved greatly with a few modifications to management regimes. Implementing site specific herbicide prescriptions for site preparation and mid-rotation treatments can help maintain structure and plant diversity for wildlife, while decreasing competition and controlling invasive species. ## EXTENT OF AGRICULTURE AND IMPROVED PASTURE

## ANTHROPOGENIC HABITATS

Thinning at regular intervals and implementing prescribed burning programs on many of these sites will provide improved understory conditions for many wildlife species.
Currently, a significant portion of forest on the Louisiana landscape is classified as an anthropogenically driven Contemporary Forest. This forest type has been cut and is two or more growth cycles removed from an old-growth stand. This regrown stand will typically have an abundance of weedy, fast-growing species and will be frequently overrun by invasive plants. Unlike the region's original old-growth forests, savannas, woodlands, or grasslands, this modern forest generally features simpler structures, lower biodiversity, and disrupted ecological processes. As a result, it is a stark contrast to the rich, dynamic ecosystem it has replaced. Although it still offers some economic benefits and serves as habitat for certain wildlife, Contemporary Forest is a poor replacement for the complex, ecologically rich, mixed-species habitat that once occupied the area. Afforestation (e.g., creation of a Contemporary Forest), especially through out-of-kind mitigation, is one of the major threats to grassland systems in southwest Louisiana 
(Early 2025)
.

## Extent and Status

Pine Plantation can be found throughout the Gulf Coastal Plain of Louisiana. Although properly managed Pine Plantation can provide limited benefits to wildlife, many plantations, particularly those that are densely planted, are of little tangible wildlife value. Over the years, Farm Bill programs such as the CRP have incentivized the establishment of Pine Plantations as a soil conservation measure. This habitat type is also preferred by forest industry and non-industrial private landowners as a means to maximize commercial timber production and derive revenue from their lands. Pine Plantation covers approximately 3.5 million acres of Louisiana's landscape 
(Zhang and Polyakov 2010)
. Contemporary Forest is found throughout the state.
Incentives have led to a proliferation of these anthropogenic habitats in lieu of natural communities.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Promote multi-use management (wildlife and timber) within this habitat type. 2. Provide education and outreach opportunities to landowners on the benefits and methods needed to manage these habitat types for wildlife.
