## RICE AGRICULTURE AND AQUACULTURE

Rarity Rank: SNA Synonyms: None Rice Agriculture and Aquaculture habitats encompass rice fields, crawfish ponds, and catfish and baitfish ponds. Rice fields are fields of annual grasses and forbs, shallowly flooded for substantial portions of the year, and drawn down during periods of active rice cultivation and harvest. Both before and during spring rice planting, bare fields and mudflats provide foraging grounds for numerous species of wading birds, shorebirds, and other waterbirds. These birds feed on aquatic invertebrates, rice and weed seeds, and green shoots. Fields with growing rice are then flooded, which can provide nesting and brood rearing cover for resident waterfowl (e.g., Mottled Duck, Blackbellied Whistling-Duck [Dendrocygna autumnalis], and Fulvous Whistling-Duck [Dendrocygna bicolor]) and secretive marsh birds (e.g., King Rail and Least Bittern). Flooded rice fields and crawfish ponds are extremely important to shorebirds, wading birds, and waterfowl and are integral components of the LMVJV and Gulf Coast Joint Venture plans for meeting the present and future nutritional needs of these avian guilds. The fields are drained in summer for harvest, at which point they are left fallow, burned, rolled, or disked and sometimes flooded in late fall to suppress weed growth. These flooded fields are also regularly used for waterfowl hunting. Alternatively, after the first harvest, fields in the southern regions may be flooded again to grow a second "ratoon" crop. Preparation for this ratoon crop, most often mid-July to early August, including manipulation of harvested stubble and re-flooding, provides valuable habitat for waterbirds, as most other water is removed from the landscape. Ratoon crops also increase food available for wintering waterfowl by as much as 15% compared to fields that are not ratooned. Rice is often cultivated in rotation with soybeans or sorghum or left fallow. Rice can also be rotated with crawfish. For crawfish production, a forage crop is grown during the summer (often rice, sorghum, or volunteer wetland vegetation). Rather than a shallow flood, crawfish production requires deeper water (up to 24 in.) during the winter. These fields are used extensively by wading birds, waterfowl, and other waterbirds. Bald Eagle, Peregrine Falcon (Falco peregrinus), and other raptors are often associated with rice agriculture and crawfish aquaculture due to the abundance of potential prey. Crawfish ponds typically retain water until harvest ends in June, at which point, water is drawn down for summer management and planting. The resulting mudflats are used extensively by resident and migratory shorebirds. These summer drawdowns concentrate aquatic prey into shallow pools that persist due to elevational differences, and waterbirds including Roseate Spoonbill, Wood Stork, and other species of wading birds exploit this foraging opportunity. The expansion of sugarcane into the rice region of southwest Louisiana, formerly dominated by Coastal Prairie, has greatly reduced the agricultural lands' benefits for wildlife, particularly waterbirds.

## Extent and Status

In 2024, Louisiana had 473,000 acres of rice (USDA NASS 2025). Louisiana has approximately 359,000 acres of crawfish ponds currently in operation (Ag MRC 2025).

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Encourage planting of native prairie species along field borders and filter strips to create habitat and improve connectivity for wildlife species. 

## ANTHROPOGENIC HABITATS

2. Encourage management of ditches and canals associated with Rice Agriculture and Aquaculture that favors development of emergent aquatic and wetland plants, as opposed to herbiciding ditches and canals to bare mud. 3. Encourage use of more pest-specific pesticides and pesticides that are not systemic in plant tissues. 4. Pursue acquisition of large areas of rice agriculture and crawfish aquaculture from willing sellers within the Coastal Prairie region, including abandoned or unproductive lands, for re-establishment of native grassland and wetland complexes. 5. Assist rice and crawfish producers in replacement of degraded infrastructure (e.g., levee and water control structures) to ensure working wetlands persist, as opposed to being converted to dry land row crops (e.g., sugarcane). 6. Encourage provision of mudflat habitat and aquaculture farms for migrating shorebirds. 7. Work with partners to promote the continued presence of rice acreage on the landscape to benefit native birds, including the use of incentive programs. 8. Promote the use of traditional rice production methods over dry-seeding techniques and implement research exploring how these practices impact native flora and fauna. 9. Promote BMPs to minimize soil erosion and runoff.
Louisiana has over 120,000 miles of rivers and streams (Figure 
5
.54), which support diverse assemblages of crustaceans, mussels, fishes, turtles, and other wildlife, including many SGCN. Many of Louisiana's most diverse and productive wildlife habitats are associated with, or maintained by, these water bodies. Rivers and streams are also important for agriculture, transportation, and stormwater drainage. Many streams are used for recreation including fishing, hunting, paddling, boating, swimming, wildlife watching, and wildlife photography.
Unfortunately, according to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), many of Louisiana's rivers and streams are impaired, meaning that the streams are of poor water quality and do not support all of their intended uses. Designated uses may include recreation (e.g., swimming and boating), fish and wildlife propagation (e.g., fishing and fish consumption), drinking water supply, and irrigation. Furthermore, impaired water quality is a significant threat to fish and wildlife populations and their supporting habitats.
Similar factors impact most basins across the state, but vary by scale and magnitude due to the source of the threats and the diverse geography of the state. The main threats to aquatic systems across Louisiana include residential and commercial development, agriculture and aquaculture, energy production and mining, transportation and service corridors, biological resource use, human intrusion and disturbance, water withdrawals and diversions, natural system modification, invasive and problematic species, pollution, geological events (subsidence), and changes in climate and severe weather. One factor that is unique to coastal drainages is saltwater intrusion from the Gulf of America. Notable threats to each basin will be highlighted below.

## ATCHAFALAYA BASIN

The Atchafalaya Basin, at approximately 1 million acres, is the nation's largest river-swamp system 
(Demas et al. 2001)
, consisting primarily of Bottomland Hardwood Forest and Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest (Figure 
5
.55). Located in south-central Louisiana, the system stretches from the river's origin near Simmesport to its terminus at the Atchafalaya Bay. This basin is contained on its east and west borders by flood protection levees. Water flow into the Atchafalaya Basin is controlled at the Old River Control Structure, which diverts 30% of the combined flow of the Red and Mississippi rivers through the Atchafalaya Basin 
(LDEQ 1993)
. A unique feature of the Atchafalaya Basin system is that it supplies sediment to the Atchafalaya and Wax Lake deltas, which currently have the most significant accretion of land on the Louisiana coast 
(LCWCRTF 1993)
. Also contributing to land formation is the use of dredge material resulting from the maintenance of navigation channels. Much of this newly created land is contained within the 136,200 acre Atchafalaya Delta WMA, which consists primarily of tidal riverine Freshwater Marsh.

## MAJOR WATER BODIES AND RIVER BASINS OF LOUISIANA

## BASINS BASINS FIGURE 5.54

The Atchafalaya Basin has many commercial uses including fishing, trapping, logging, oil and gas exploration and production, ecotourism, and limited shipping. Recreational activities include fishing, hunting, camping, bird watching, swimming, and boating. The Atchafalaya Basin contains a diversity of habitats, from Bottomland Hardwood Forest in the upper basin to coastal marshes, oyster reefs, and Vegetated Pioneer Emerging Delta in the lower portions of the basin. Much of the Atchafalaya Basin consists of public lands, including Sherburne WMA, Atchafalaya NWR, and multiple USACE properties (e.g., Bayou des Ourses and Indian Bayou).
There are approximately 100 species of freshwater fishes (W. Kelso, personal communication), 19 species of mussels 
(Vidrine 1993
(Vidrine , 2019))
, and nine species of crayfishes 
(Walls 2009)
 found within the Atchafalaya Basin. For more information about this basin, see the LDWF Inland Fisheries Management plan for the Atchafalaya Basin (www.wlf.louisiana.gov/resources/category/ freshwater-inland-fish/inland-waterbody-management-plans).

## Water Quality

The 2024 Water Quality Inventory Report (LDEQ 2024) indicates that only 50% of the 12 waterbody subsegments within the basin fully supported their designated use for fish and wildlife propagation. The suspected causes of poor water quality in the subsegments include fecal coliform bacteria, suspended solids, mercury, turbidity, non-native aquatic invasive plants, and low concentration of dissolved oxygen. The suspected sources of poor water quality include home sewage systems, agriculture, and stormwater runoff.

## Threats

The primary threat in the Atchafalaya Basin is natural system modification, which is largely related to changes in natural hydrology and floodplain connectivity (CWPPRA 2025). In addition, invasive plants such as the non-native Water Hyacinth and Giant Salvinia and invasive fishes such as non-native carp have negative impacts to habitat and wildlife in this basin. Agricultural runoff also impacts the basin with increased sediment and nutrient loads. 

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

## BARATARIA BASIN

The upper Barataria Basin was formed approximately 3,500 to 4,000 years ago as part of the Lafourche Delta complex.
Encompassing approximately 1,565,000 acres, this basin is bordered on the north and east by the levees of the Mississippi River, which were constructed after the Great Mississippi Flood of 1927 (CWPPRA 2025) (Figure 
5
.56). The basin is bordered on the west by Bayou Lafourche and on the south by the Gulf of America. The basin is primarily comprised of the following terrestrial habitat types: agriculture/improved pasture (mostly sugarcane), Bottomland Hardwood Forest, Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest, and all coastal marsh types ranging from freshwater to saltwater. Freshwater Floating Marsh is known from this basin and may occupy a substantial area. Approximately 60 species of freshwater fishes (W. Kelso, personal communication) and nine species of crayfishes 
(Walls 2009
) are found within the Barataria Basin. The basin supports many commercial activities ranging from sugarcane production and aquaculture to commercial fishing, trapping, logging, and oil and gas production. This basin is one of the most productive areas in the state for commercial shrimp and oyster harvest.
Recreational activities include fishing, hunting, bird watching, swimming, and boating.
For more information about this basin, see the LDWF Inland Fisheries management plan for the Barataria Basin (www. wlf.louisiana.gov/resources/category/freshwater-inland-fish/ inland-waterbody-management-plans).

## Water Quality

The 2024 Water Quality Inventory Report (LDEQ 2024) indicates that 56% of the 27 waterbody subsegments within this basin fully supported their designated use for fish and wildlife propagation.
Causes of poor water quality include nitrates and nitrites, non-native aquatic invasive plants, fecal coliform bacteria, low concentration of dissolved oxygen, dissolved and suspended solids, and turbidity. The suspected sources of poor water quality include crop production, pastureland, urban runoff, septic tanks, spills, minor industrial point sources, petroleum activities, highway runoff, hydromodification, and dredging.

## Threats

The primary threat to this basin includes the alteration of the natural hydrology of the system (CWPPRA 2025). The loss of historic connectivity to the Mississippi Basin, along with current efforts to reconnect the basins, presents unique challenges for the Barataria Basin. Invasive plants including Water Hyacinth and Giant Salvinia have substantial impacts on this system. Due to the location and nature of this basin, severe weather and other climate related impacts are significant. Subsidence, which leads to increased saltwater intrusion, is also a threat to this basin. 

## Research Needs and

## CALCASIEU BASIN

The Calcasieu Basin, located in southwest Louisiana, comprises approximately 4,400 square miles (Calcasieu Parish 2025) of drainage area and represents 8% of the area of the state (Figure 
5
.57). Headwaters of the Calcasieu River are found in the hills west of Alexandria, Louisiana, and flow in a southerly direction for about 215 miles. From the upland hills where elevations range from 260 feet to 400 feet above mean sea level, the river flows through the Coastal Prairie and coastal marshes, which have an elevation ranging from 1 foot to 2 feet above mean sea level. The floodplains of this basin are extremely flat, averaging 2 feet to 3 feet above mean sea level. The lower Calcasieu River flows through Prien Lake, Moss Lake, and Calcasieu Lake (all natural lakes) before emptying into the Gulf of America approximately 30 miles east of the Louisiana-Texas state line. Dominant features of this basin include oxbow lakes, natural levees, and the surrounding Pleistocene Uplands 
(Weston 1974)
. The middle and upper Calcasieu Basin are sparsely populated. The city of Lake Charles lies in the southern portion of the basin and is an area that has been heavily industrialized by petro-chemical plants.
The Calcasieu River varies from a small, fast flowing stream in the headwaters to a broad, sluggish estuary from the latitude of Lake Charles to its entrance into the Gulf of America. Flows in the upper basin can range from a high of 180,000 cubic feet per second in downstream portions to zero cubic feet per second in the uppermost portions during the summer and fall. The lower portion of the river is subject to tidal variation. A semidiurnal tide extends 65 miles upstream and has mean tidal ranges of 1.7 feet at the mouth of the river and 0.7 foot at Lake Charles.
The upper Calcasieu River is largely unaltered and free-flowing. An existing saltwater barrier across the Calcasieu River at Lake Charles divides the basin into an upper and lower basin and prevents saltwater intrusion from degrading this major source of irrigation for rice production. Navigation improvements have also modified the river from its mouth to approximately 52.6 river miles inland 
(Weston 1974)
.
Approximately 90 species of freshwater fishes 
(Maxwell 2012, LDWF unpublished data, B. Reed, personal communication)
, 29 species of mussels 
(Vidrine 1993
(Vidrine , 2019))
, and 17 species of crayfishes 
(Walls 2009
) are found within the Calcasieu Basin.
At the southern terminus, Calcasieu Lake supports a small but viable commercial fishing industry, which includes the harvest of crabs, shrimp, and oysters. For more information about this basin, see the LDWF Inland Fisheries management plan for the Calcasieu River (www.wlf.louisiana.gov/resources/category/ freshwater-inland-fish/inland-waterbody-management-plans).

## Water Quality

The 2024 Water Quality Inventory Report (LDEQ 2024) indicates that only 24% of the 40 waterbody subsegments within this basin fully supported their designated use for fish and wildlife propagation. The suspected causes of poor water quality include metals, nutrients, fecal coliform bacteria, low concentration of dissolved oxygen, dissolved and suspended solids, turbidity, elevated levels of mercury, elevated water temperatures, and low pH. The suspected sources of poor water quality include home sewage systems, agriculture, silviculture, urban stormwater runoff, and dredging.

## Threats

The Calcasieu Basin is largely delineated by the saltwater barrier north of Lake Charles, with differing impacts above and below it. Primary threats to the upper basin include natural system modification, agricultural and silvicultural runoff, and the removal of woody debris from waterways. The lower basin faces a larger number and greater magnitude of threats than the upper basin. Dominant threats to the lower basin include natural system modification, pollution, transportation corridors, impacts from industrial developments, and severe weather and other climate related events (CWPPRA 2025). Invasive species such as Feral Hog (Sus scrofa) are significant threats to water quality, habitat, and SGCN of the region.
Saltwater intrusion and coastal land loss are also significant threats to the southern portion of this basin, most notably the Brackish Marsh surrounding Calcasieu Lake. The straightening and dredging of the Calcasieu Ship Channel is the likely source behind a general increase in salinity over the last half-century.
Numerous water control structures have been constructed on bayous that connect Calcasieu Lake with surrounding marshes for salinity control, thereby decreasing ingress and egress opportunities for marine species which spend critical portions of their life history in coastal marshes. A variety of hydrologic restoration projects have been proposed for this area by the coastal restoration community in an attempt to address this threat 
(CPRA 2023)
.
Research Needs and Conservation Actions 1. Support current initiatives, develop new programs, and encourage BMPs that help reduce siltation and sedimentation throughout the Calcasieu Basin. 2. Determine impacts of weirs on fish passage and mussel distributions. 3. Support practical initiatives that will help address saltwater intrusion into and erosion of coastal marshes surrounding Calcasieu Lake while also allowing for adequate connectivity between the lake and marsh habitats. 

## MERMENTAU BASIN

The Mermentau Basin is in the southwestern part of the state and drains an area of approximately 6,730 square miles 
(Figure 5.58)
. This basin, located between the Vermilion-Teche and Calcasieu basins, comprises a controlled system for the drainage of the Mermentau River and its tributaries. Catfish Point and Schooner Bayou control structures and Calcasieu and Leland Bowman locks control the impoundment of winter runoff for irrigation use in the summertime and function to restrict inflow of water from surrounding estuarine waters and the Gulf of America (USACE 1998).
The basin is composed of three different and distinctive land forms which are arranged in broad bands from north to south. The upper part of the basin is a flatwoods area which gives way to an undulating landscape extending northward into the Calcasieu and Red basins. To the south of the flatwoods area lies the Coastal Prairie region which extends from west of Vinton, Louisiana (located in the Calcasieu Basin), east to Bayou Teche. This region is characterized by large expanses of flat grassland dissected by numerous tributaries narrowly flanked by gallery forests north of the coastal marsh. The Coastal Prairie region, which is extensively cultivated, gives way to a band of marsh, which extends along Louisiana's entire coastline. This marsh region is further subdivided into Freshwater, Intermediate, and Brackish marshes, and, finally, Salt Marsh, which forms the coastline adjacent to the Gulf of America and its bays 
(Domingue, Szabo & Assoc. Inc. 1975)
.
The lower portion of the basin is bound on the east by Freshwater Bayou Channel, on the south by the Gulf of America, on the west by Louisiana Highway 27, and on the north by the Gulf Intracoastal Waterway. In 1932, the coastal portion of the Mermentau Basin was made up of 2,482 square miles of wetlands. By 2016, the basin had 1,994 square miles of wetlands remaining, after experiencing a conversion of 20% of the area's land to open water 
(Couvillion et al. 2017)
.
Approximately 75 species of freshwater fishes (Tulane 2008, LDWF unpublished data), 19 species of mussels 
(Vidrine 1993
(Vidrine , 2019))
, and 14 species of crayfishes 
(Walls 2009
) are found within the Mermentau Basin.

## Water Quality

The 2024 Water Quality Inventory Report 
(LDEQ 2024)
 indicates that only 6% of the 18 waterbody subsegments within this basin fully supported their designated use for fish and wildlife propagation. Causes of poor water quality include metals, nutrients, fecal coliform bacteria, low concentration of dissolved oxygen, pesticides, dissolved and suspended solids, sedimentation and siltation, and turbidity. The suspected sources of poor water quality include home sewage systems, agriculture, silviculture, urban stormwater runoff, and dredging. Chapter 5: Natural Communities

## Threats

The Mermentau Basin faces some of the most impactful threats in the state. The most significant threats include agriculture and natural system modification (CWPPRA 2025), as the natural landscape of this watershed is almost completely altered.
Pollution poses a threat from toxins and heavy metals left by historical industry and from runoff including nutrient loading and suspended solids. Invasive species, primarily Water Hyacinth and Giant Salvinia, also pose threats to the system. 

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

## MISSISSIPPI BASIN

The portion of the Mississippi River that occurs in Louisiana is part of the Lower Mississippi Drainage Basin, which extends from Cairo, Illinois, to Head-of-Passes at the Gulf of America (Figure 
5
.59). Within Louisiana, the Mississippi Basin is composed of the Mississippi River, West Feliciana Parish, portions of East Feliciana Parish east of Redwood Creek, portions of East Baton Rouge Parish east of the Comite River and the city of Baton Rouge, and the Mississippi River Delta. The river is completely leveed on its western side from the Arkansas line to Venice, Louisiana, and on its eastern side from Baton Rouge to Venice.
The primary habitat types within the basin are Batture, Bottomland Hardwood Forest, and Sandbar. This basin also contains nearly all of the Southern Mesophytic Hardwood Forest found in Louisiana, much of which occurs in Tunica Hills WMA.
The delta is characterized by river channels with attendant channel banks, natural bayous, and man-made canals which are interspersed with Intermediate and Freshwater marshes.
The Mississippi Basin contains approximately 375 species of freshwater fishes which comprise 45-50% of all freshwater fish species in North America 
(Brosse et al. 2013)
. Approximately 50 species of freshwater fishes (W. Kelso, personal communication), 34 species of mussels 
(Vidrine 1993
, 2019
, Jones et al. 2019)
, and nine species of crayfishes 
(Walls 2009
) are found within the Mississippi Basin in Louisiana.

## Water Quality

The 2024 Water Quality Inventory Report 
(LDEQ 2024)
 indicates that only 43% of the 14 waterbody subsegments within this basin fully supported their designated use for fish and wildlife propagation. Causes of poor water quality include metals, nutrients, polychlorinated biphenyls (PCBs), hexachlorobenzene, fecal coliform bacteria, organic enrichment and low concentration of dissolved oxygen, oil and grease, nonnative aquatic plants, and turbidity. The suspected sources of poor water quality include home sewage systems, agriculture, silviculture, urban stormwater runoff, and dredging.

## EXTENT OF THE MISSISSIPPI BASIN

EXTENT OF THE MERMENTAU BASIN

. Primary threats to this basin include natural system modification, agriculture, industrialization, and pollution (CWPPRA 2025). The Mississippi River and its tributaries act as major transportation corridors. Due to the size of the basin, changes in climate and severe weather directly impacting states as far away as Montana, Minnesota, or Pennsylvania can pose threats to this highly altered system in Louisiana.
The compounded threats and disturbances in this basin also provide ample opportunities for invasive species to thrive, further threatening the wildlife and habitats of the region.

## Basin Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Develop a comprehensive biological survey methodology for the Mississippi River and its tributaries. 2. Explore the possibility of reconnecting the Mississippi River with portions of its floodplain through the use of controlled diversions in order to restore the natural hydrology of forested wetlands. 3. Enhance connectivity and habitat in batture to support floodplain-dependent species. 4. Encourage the use of nature-based solutions and other environmentally beneficial approaches in floodwater mitigation projects. River, downstream from the lock and dam. The control structure is used to regulate the flow entering the diversion channel from the lake. The closure dam is located on Little River. These features allow for regulation of stages in the lake to permit its continued use as a resting and feeding area for migratory waterfowl and shorebirds 
(USACE 1998)
.

## OUACHITA BASIN

Approximately 120 species of freshwater fishes (W. Kelso, personal communication), 45 species of mussels 
(Vidrine 1993
(Vidrine , 2019))
, and 20 species of crayfishes 
(Walls 2009
) are found within the Ouachita Basin.

## Water Quality

The 2024 Water Quality Inventory Report 
(LDEQ 2024)
 indicates that only 15% of the 61 waterbody subsegments within this basin fully supported their designated use for fish and wildlife propagation. Causes of poor water quality include metals, pesticides, nutrients, fecal coliform bacteria, organic enrichment and low concentration of dissolved oxygen, oil and grease, non-native aquatic plants, sedimentation and siltation, and turbidity. The suspected sources of poor water quality include home sewage systems, agriculture, silviculture, urban stormwater runoff, surface mining, and dredging.

## Threats

Primary threats to the Ouachita Basin include natural system modification, agricultural water withdrawals and runoff, and Chapter 5: Natural Communities industrial pollution. The natural system modifications that most affect this basin include deforestation and channelization. The impact of invasive species, including several species of carp and Giant and Common Salvinia, is one of the most pressing threats to this basin. Protection of the state's most diverse mussel assemblage will require amelioration of these threats in the region.

Although this basin is not as threatened as many other systems in Louisiana, there are still threats that need to be addressed. One of the primary threats to the Pearl Basin is modification of the natural flow regime described above. Sedimentation associated with transportation infrastructure construction has also impacted water quality within this basin. Invasive species like Feral Hog are significant threats to water quality, habitat, and SGCN of the region. 

## Research Needs and

## PONTCHARTRAIN BASIN

The Pontchartrain Basin is a 4,700 square mile watershed in southeast Louisiana and southwest Mississippi (Figure 
5
.62).
The topography of the basin ranges from more than 300 feet above sea level in the rolling hills along the Louisiana-Mississippi state line to sea level throughout the coastal wetlands to more than 10 feet below sea level in some areas of New Orleans.
The northern half of the basin contains all or portions of seven of the eight Florida parishes: East Baton Rouge, East Feliciana, Livingston, St. Helena, St. Tammany, Tangipahoa, Land use within this basin is varied, ranging from high density urban areas that drain metropolitan Baton Rouge and New Orleans to rural pastures in the Florida Parishes. In 1995, the Lake Pontchartrain Basin Foundation released a comprehensive management plan for the basin that details management strategies to address sewage and agricultural runoff, stormwater runoff, saltwater intrusion, and wetland loss. Additionally, numerous coastal restoration projects, including marsh creation and shoreline protection, have been proposed for this basin to address coastal wetland loss 
(CPRA 2023)
.

## EXTENT OF THE PEARL BASIN

The Pontchartrain Basin contains some of the greatest aquatic species diversity in the state. Approximately 100 species of freshwater fishes (W. Kelso, personal communication), 36 species of mussels 
(Vidrine 1993
(Vidrine , 2019))
, and 15 species of crayfishes 
(Walls 2009)

## Threats

Primary threats to the Pontchartrain Basin include natural system modification, urbanization, energy production and mining, and changes in climate and severe weather. Flood control tactics (e.g., channelization, levees, and removal of woody debris) that do not maximize the use of nature-based solutions pose major threats to drainages in the Pontchartrain Basin. Invasive species, particularly Water Hyacinth, are threats to this system. With the intense urbanization of the basin, various associated threats will likely increase in the future. Approximately 100 species of freshwater fishes (W. Kelso, personal communication), 35 species of mussels 
(Vidrine 1993
(Vidrine , 2019))
, and 22 species of crayfishes 
(Walls 2009
) are found within the Red Basin.

Chapter 5: Natural Communities

## Threats

The primary threats to the Red Basin include extensive agriculture and silviculture leading to sedimentation and runoff, natural system modification, transportation and service corridors, energy production, and mining. The locks and dams pose threats to fish passage and lead to increased siltation.
Hydropower is proposed on dams on the Red River and, if constructed, would add a new threat to the system. Invasive species, including extensive Giant Salvinia infestations, also threaten many water bodies within the system.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Develop a comprehensive biological stream survey methodology for the Red Basin.

The 

## EXTENT OF THE SABINE BASIN

## Threats

The primary threats to the lower Sabine Basin in Louisiana are natural system modification and the related energy production by the Toledo Bend Dam. Historically, there have been negative impacts from operation of the dam, but recent changes in flow requirements may reduce those impacts. Toledo Bend Dam is also the most significant barrier to fish passage in the state. Silvicultural runoff and related pollution are substantial threats to the lower Sabine. As with many other systems in Louisiana, invasive species and changes to the natural hydrology pose great threats within this basin.

## Research Needs and Conservation Actions

1. Support initiatives and programs that help reduce siltation and sedimentation throughout the Sabine Basin. 2. Continue to provide input to the Sabine River Authority in regard to reducing impacts of power generation on fish and wildlife above and below Toledo Bend Dam. 3. Restore aquatic connectivity to enhance aquatic organism passage throughout the basin. 4. Encourage the use of nature-based solutions and other environmentally beneficial approaches in floodwater mitigation projects.

## TERREBONNE BASIN

The Terrebonne Basin covers approximately 1,712,500 acres in south-central Louisiana 
(LCWRCTF 1993)
, bordered by Bayou Lafourche to the east, the Atchafalaya Basin floodway to the west, the Mississippi River to the north, and the Gulf of America to the south (Figure 
5
 In addition to these islands providing vital habitat for colonial nesting waterbirds, the islands buffer mainland marshes from the tidal processes of the Gulf and help protect the coastline from tropical cyclones. The southeastern coastal marshes are isolated from any type of riverine input, and with high rates of subsidence, show the highest incidence of wetland loss within the basin.

) are found within the Terrebonne Basin. For more information on this basin, see the LDWF Inland Fisheries management plan for water bodies within Terrebonne Basin (www.wlf.louisiana.gov/resources/category/ freshwater-inland-fish/inland-waterbody-management-plans).

## Water Quality

The 2024 Water Quality Inventory Report (LDEQ 2024) indicates that only 48% of the 56 waterbody subsegments within this basin fully supported their designated use for fish and wildlife propagation. Causes of poor water quality include metals, nutrients, fecal coliform bacteria, non-native aquatic plants, organic enrichment and low concentration of dissolved oxygen, dissolved and suspended solids, low pH levels, sedimentation and siltation, and turbidity. The suspected sources of poor water quality include non-irrigated crop production, pastureland, urban runoff, hydromodification, combined sewers and unsewered areas, surface runoff, and chemical spills.

## Threats

Subsidence, SLR, and the potential increase in frequency and magnitude of tropical cyclones are major threats to this basin and are of particular concern for the islands contained within Terrebonne Bay. As with many other basins, changes in natural hydrology and negative impacts of invasive plants are also among the primary threats. 

## Research Needs and

## VERMILION-TECHE BASIN

The Vermilion-Teche Basin's drainage area covers approximately 4,047 square miles (Figure 
5
.66). Land cover within the basin ranges from upland pine woodlands northwest of Alexandria and corn and soybean agriculture in the northern portion of the basin to rice and sugarcane in the central and southern portions of the basin. The coastal zone is primarily Freshwater Marsh from Bayou Cypremort east to Louisiana Hwy. 317. Intermediate and Brackish marshes occupy all of the coastal zone west of Bayou Cypremort with small areas of Salt Marsh on Marsh Island WMA and National Audubon Society's Paul J. Rainey Wildlife Sanctuary.
Authorized by the Flood Control Act of 1966, supplemental freshwater from the Atchafalaya River upstream of Krotz Springs is diverted to the head of Bayou Teche at Port Barre.
The supplemental fresh water is distributed among Bayou Teche, the Vermilion River, and the west side borrow pit along the Atchafalaya Basin protection levee for municipal, industrial, irrigation, and water-quality control uses (USACE 1998). Approximately 60 species of freshwater fishes (W. Kelso, personal communication), 28 species of mussels 
(Vidrine 1993
(Vidrine , 2019))
, and 14 species of crayfishes 
(Walls 2009)
 are found within the Vermilion-Teche Basin. Many marine fish species exist within the southern portion of the basin supporting both commercial and recreational fishing industries. Commercial harvest of crabs, shrimp, and oysters occurs within the coastal bay system and in waters offshore of Marsh Island.

## Water Quality

The 2024 Water Quality Inventory Report (LDEQ 2024) indicates that only 15% of the 42 waterbody subsegments within the basin fully supported their designated use for fish and wildlife propagation. Causes of poor water quality include metals, pesticides, nutrients, fecal coliform bacteria, non-native aquatic plants, organic enrichment and low concentration of dissolved oxygen, dissolved and suspended solids, sedimentation and siltation, and turbidity. The suspected sources of poor water quality include crop production, aquaculture, urban runoff, petroleum activities, hydromodification, surface mining, construction, and dredging.

## Threats

Coastal land loss is a significant threat, most notably on Marsh Island, and numerous projects have been proposed to address this issue 
(CPRA 2023)
. Additional primary threats to the Vermilion-Teche Basin, which are similar to those of neighboring basins, include natural system modifications, invasive plants such as Hydrilla and Common Salvinia, and sedimentation from agricultural lands.

## EXTENT OF THE VERMILION-TECHE BASIN

Intermediate Marsh in the Vermilion-Teche Basin Invasive species are one of the most widespread and serious threats to Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and their habitats in Louisiana. The scope of this threat continues to expand, and it may be one of the few threats that private landowners and managers can actively mitigate through concerted efforts. This chapter provides a list of invasive species that are known to occur in Louisiana or have the potential to occur within the next decade (Table 
6
.1).
Important distinctions must be made between two closely related and oftentimes confusing terms in invasion biology, namely nonnative and invasive. These terms are discussed in detail in 
Mack and colleagues (2000)
 but will be defined in general terms here.
Non-native species are sometimes referred to as alien, introduced, exotic, or naturalized species. These terms all have different connotations and meanings across various disciplines, but are encapsulated in this section under the term non-native. Nonnative species are those species transferred to a new geographic location previously unoccupied by that species. Invasive¹ species are non-native species that have become established in an area, causing negative impacts to native species and their environment.
Invasive species often have life history traits that allow them to outcompete native species. Such traits may include betteradapted root structures, faster growth rates, more efficient seed dispersal methods, a marked preference for disturbance, and higher fecundity rates 
(Mack et al. 2000)
. Additionally, invasive species frequently thrive in new habitats because they lack natural controls (e.g., reproductive limitations, predators, diseases, and competitors) that would normally regulate their populations in their native range, further enhancing their ability to spread, consume resources, alter habitats, and dominate other species. Louisiana's humid subtropical climate makes it especially vulnerable to the introduction and establishment of invasive species. Long, hot summers and short, mild winters, along with high precipitation levels, provide favorable conditions for a plethora of invasive species to survive year-round. The negative impact of these species on native wildlife and natural communities is profound, often resulting in diminished food availability, reduced habitat quality, decreased species diversity, increased habitat fragmentation, and weakened ecosystem resilience. For SGCN, the threat of invasive species is a perpetual and escalating challenge that compounds pressures already faced by vulnerable native species and natural communities.
In addition to ecological impacts, invasive species also have far reaching negative economic consequences to industrial, agricultural, commercial, and private business sectors.
Thousands of invasive species have become established in the United States. Although the precise economic costs are difficult to quantify, 
Pimentel and colleagues (2000)
 estimated damages caused by invasive species to be as much as $137 billion (or $257 billion in 2025, adjusted for inflation) annually. To limit the spread of invasive species, federal and state governments have passed laws regulating the transport and possession of invasive species and have created legal consequences for violators. Whereas monetary figures of economic damages are difficult to comprehend, the greatest damages may come in the unknown extent of degradation to our habitats as well as the decline of our native wildlife.
Efforts to control invasive species often rely on methods such as trapping, shooting, and utilization of herbicides and pesticides. However, the rate at which invasive species spread is frequently faster than the rate at which these control or removal techniques can be effectively implemented. This lag in response time is in part due to insufficient invasive species removal resources for land managers and state agencies. If more substantial efforts are not made to seriously and proactively address the spread of invasive species, they will continue to pose a major threat to our nation's resources and natural heritage. To minimize and mitigate these injurious impacts, it is essential to enhance funding for more effective invasive species management programs and for addressing gaps in invasive species legislation. In addition, education plays a crucial role in ensuring individuals understand the risks of invasive species on the environment and the impacts to the economy. Teaching the public to be mindful of intentional or unintentional transport or release of invasive species may be the best chance of preventing further introductions. In addition, discouraging the sale of invasive plant species by plant nurseries is critical. The combination of robust management (including restoration of natural processes such as fire and seasonal flows), legislative action, and public awareness will be critical for minimizing the damage caused by invasive species and protecting Louisiana's native wildlife and habitats.
¹ In this document, invasive is reserved for non-native species only; we recognize that problematic native species may also be referred to as invasive by some authorities. 

## LOUISIANA INVASIVE SPECIES LIST

The following list includes definitions of tier categories used to classify threat levels of non-native species currently known to occur in Louisiana (Tiers 1-4) and Watchlist species (Tier 5) that pose a reasonable threat of invasion in the near future.
h TIER 1
This tier is reserved for species currently having severe or widespread negative impacts on native wildlife or natural communities in Louisiana. This tier may include species that have a limited distribution in the state but that have severe impacts where found.
h TIER 2
This tier is reserved for species currently having moderately negative impacts on native wildlife or natural communities in Louisiana. This tier may include species exhibiting severe impacts in other states but that have not reached Tier 1 status in Louisiana.
h TIER 3
This tier is reserved for species currently having limited negative impacts or likely to have limited negative impacts on native wildlife or natural communities in Louisiana. This tier may also include species for which impacts to native wildlife or natural communities are poorly known as well as species for which negative impacts to native wildlife or natural communities might quickly escalate due to ongoing range expansion or population increases.
h TIER 4
This tier is reserved for species currently having very limited or minimal negative impacts on native wildlife or natural communities in Louisiana. This tier includes well established non-native species that are unlikely to ever have measurable negative impacts in Louisiana.

## h TIER 5 (WATCHLIST)

This tier is reserved for species not currently documented in Louisiana but that have the potential to invade in the next 10 years, some with potentially severe negative impacts.

## GENERAL INVASIVE SPECIES MANAGEMENT ACTIONS

The following management actions apply to many or all invasive species and should be considered priority conservation strategies. Implementation of these actions will benefit multiple natural communities and SGCN. Although not exhaustive, their implementation will support broader ecological health and resilience.
h Encourage the development and implementation of an Early Detection Rapid Response (EDRR) protocol to prevent the establishment of non-native species. h Promote existing occurrence databases for emerging invasives (e.g., iMapInvasives, USGS Nonindigenous Aquatic Species, etc.), including rigorous documentation of newly discovered individuals or populations of such species. h Document current range extent and expansion of invasive species, enabling more effective landscape-level management and targeted interventions. h Educate and encourage the public to report sightings of invasives to the appropriate source, fostering and utilizing community involvement for early detection and response. h Work with partners to verify and eradicate newly detected non-native wildlife and plant species. h Promote education on the identification of invasive species, highlighting their negative impacts on natural communities, and methods for control or eradication. h Restore natural flow regimes and connectivity in aquatic systems to encourage seasonal cycles and health of native plants and animals, supporting ecosystem resilience. h Promote the utilization of federal cost-share programs (e.g., Natural Resource Conservation Service Farm Bill programs) to help landowners and land managers address invasive species problems on private lands.
h Provide technical assistance and encourage landowners to control invasive species to benefit SGCN and natural communities. h Control invasive species 
(Nutria, Feral Hogs, Giant Salvinia, etc.)
 as appropriate, particularly when the species is documented to have negative impacts on SGCN or natural communities. h Pursue the creation, implementation, and enforcement of laws and regulations prohibiting the possession and commercial sale of invasive and potentially invasive plants and animals that are not currently covered by existing regulations.
h Increase awareness about the potential risk of using non-native plant species that are commonly used as ornamentals and promote the use of native species for landscaping. h Educate the public on preventative measures to curb the spread of invasive plants such as cleaning protocols for equipment, vehicles, and clothing; mowing and/or hand removing invasive plants before seed production; targeting invasive plants along roadsides to prevent spread; and providing wash stations at trailheads, boat launches, and parking lots in parks and recreation areas. 

## CONSERVATION OPPORTUNITY AREAS NEED AND OVERVIEW

There are 428 animals and 552 plants that have been identified as Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN)¹ in Louisiana. Given the finite time and resources available for the conservation of SGCN and their habitats, a growing need exists to focus conservation actions where they can have the greatest impact. For that reason, the 2025 State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP) defined priority conservation areas for Louisiana's SGCN. These areas were primarily developed using SGCN richness and habitat condition and are known as Conservation Opportunity Areas.
Conservation Opportunity Areas (COAs) are geographies within Louisiana that have been identified as uniquely important for the conservation of SGCN. Conservation actions within these COAs will benefit many SGCN, often across taxa and habitats. The COAs presented in this chapter represent those areas where, all else being equal, conservation funding and other resources should be allocated to have the most significant impact on SGCN. COAs are an addition to, rather than a replacement of, other landscape-scale conservation planning efforts such as those undertaken by bird habitat Joint Ventures. COAs vary greatly in size and landscape composition, and each may have specific threats, conservation needs, and conservation actions.
The COAs identified in this SWAP afford the Louisiana Department of Wildlife and Fisheries (LDWF) and partners some of the best opportunities for SGCN conservation, research, and monitoring. However, conservation actions carried out in other areas of the state are not devalued by this methodology. In fact, not all SGCN and associated habitats are represented by these COAs, and for those that are, actions within COA boundaries alone may not be sufficient to ensure their persistence. Therefore, to fully meet the objectives of the Louisiana SWAP, a degree of flexibility and opportunism must be retained, along with a willingness to modify priorities as new threats, issues, and opportunities arise. No regulatory aspect exists for COAs, and all conservation actions by private landowners or other partners are strictly voluntary. Land use or other activities will not be altered or restricted as a result of COA designation.

## IDENTIFYING COAs

For the 2015 SWAP, LDWF staff used a multi-tiered Geographic Information System (GIS) based approach in which multiple data layers were overlaid to determine SGCN hot spots. These data included layers representing the predicted distribution of all S1, S2, and S3 SGCN, predicted distribution of S1, S2, and S3 natural communities, the location of existing conservation lands and Louisiana Natural and Scenic Rivers, and the location of stream segments that are of very high quality for fish and wildlife propagation. This process resulted in 38 potential COAs, which were then refined to a final set of 30. However, ¹ These numbers represent the 2025 SGCN list, but all analyses conducted throughout this chapter used the SGCN and natural communities in the 2015 SWAP. All global ranks, state ranks, and natural community names are reflective of the 2015 SWAP.
Chapter 7: Conservation Opportunity Areas due to the large number of COAs and the number of acres encompassed within each COA, LDWF still found it difficult to target conservation resources and actions.
The 2015 SWAP recognized the need for future iterations of COA development to narrow the broad focus towards more targeted conservation opportunities. In 2020, LDWF partnered with U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) staff supporting the Southeast Conservation Adaptation Strategy to better target acquisition and management projects. This occurred by refining the 2015 approach and redefining COA boundaries. The methodologies selected to analyze and define new COAs, incorporated the SE Conservation Blueprint (SECAS 2021), LDWF Biotics datasets, and others. These COAs were mostly informed by living datasets, some of which have known deficiencies (e.g., 
https://secassoutheast. org/blueprint-known-issues
). The resulting methodology refined the original approach by focusing on information needs to support select actions in the SWAP, and data available within the SE Conservation Blueprint (SECAS 2021) could help address these information needs. This process produced data-informed priority areas that can be replicated and defended. Further, it achieved the LDWF objectives of reducing the number of COAs (11 instead of 30), and creating focal areas defined by a specific set of priority actions. Details of the methods can be found in the final project report available on USGS ScienceBase (www. sciencebase.gov/catalog/item/650efc46d34e823a0275500f).
Briefly, LDWF and USFWS staff (hereafter, the COA Team) developed a six step, data-informed process to identify COAs for Louisiana. 

## STEP 1: IDENTIFYING FOCAL PRIORITY ACTIONS

The COA Team compiled a full list of priority actions outlined in the 2015 SWAP and compared them to Blueprint indicators. Throughout the SWAP, priority actions were identified by habitats or groups of habitats (e.g., forests). This approach often resulted in specific priority actions occurring in a subset of the state's six primary ecoregions. To simplify the analysis, the COA Team defined focal "ecosystems" as either a habitat group or an important sub-group. The final targeted list of ecosystems included (1) Freshwater Streams and Rivers, (2) Pine Forests and Woodlands, and (3) Floodplain Forests and Woody Wetlands.
For the Freshwater Streams and Rivers ecosystem, the COA Team focused on actions that would improve linear and lateral connectivity. Other potential actions were deferred for a later assessment due to a lack of adequate ecosystem condition data (e.g., water quality) or because LDWF does not have significant and direct influence over the priority action (e.g., sandbar creation and management in big rivers). For the Pine Forests and Woodlands ecosystem and the Floodplain Forests and Woody Wetlands ecosystem, the COA Team focused on restoration and protection. Priority actions related to the application of fire in upland forest ecosystems were considered a necessary component of both restoration and protection and, therefore, were not analyzed separately. Marine ecosystems (i.e. Island and Seagrass) were not included in this analysis. The relevant indicators in the 2019-2021 versions of the Southeast Conservation Blueprint did not provide adequate coverage for Louisiana. Similarly, Inland Prairies/Grasslands, Coastal Prairies, and Chenier ecosystems were not considered due to the lack of adequate ecosystem condition information. The Chenier ecosystem also lacks a specific set of focal species and, thus, did not fit well in this analysis framework. The COA Team retained the original five COAs targeting these ecosystems (i.e. Calcasieu Prairie, Sabine Prairie, Chenier, Barataria-Terrebonne, and the Chandeleurs) without further refinement.

## STEP 2: IDENTIFYING SPECIES PRIORITY AREAS

The COA Team identified areas within the state with high concentrations of SGCN that could benefit from the focal priority action. The methodology used to determine SGCN distribution in this revision is the same subbasin-level distribution predictions that were used for the 2015 SWAP. For the restoration and protection action items in the Pine Forests and Woodlands and the Floodplain Forests and Woody Wetlands ecosystems, SGCN data were first filtered to those species dependent on the relevant ecosystem for the priority action. Unfortunately, species-specific distributions were not available for aquatics species. Thus, summary ranking data for fishes, crustaceans, and mollusks were used to identify hotspots for Freshwater Streams and Rivers ecosystem actions. For all three ecosystems, the pool of relevant subbasins was ranked based on a SGCN Index score (i.e. predicted presence of each species, weighted by S-ranks) and categorized into three bins: top 20%, top 20-50%, and bottom 50%. These categories approximate the categories used by the Southeast Conservation Blueprint to define High, Moderate, and Low priority for conservation actions. Further, earlier prototypes that defined the top category using a 5% threshold were deemed too restrictive by LDWF staff. For the Pine Forests and Woodlands ecosystem, subbasins were only ranked in the coastal plain ecoregions where that ecosystem occurs. For the Freshwater Streams and Rivers and Floodplain Forests and Woody Wetlands ecosystems, all subbasins in the state were included in the analysis, because the ecosystems are distributed statewide.

## STEP 3: IDENTIFYING ACTION OPPORTUNITY AREAS (AOAs)

For purposes of this project, AOAs are defined as those areas within species priority areas (identified in Step 2) that also have abundant and leverageable ecological opportunities to enact focal priority actions. Spatial data characterizing the amount and distribution of ecological opportunity for a particular action were based on relevant measured or modeled ecosystem condition indicators in the Blueprint and were presented to LDWF staff as map overlays within the prioritized subbasins (identified in Step 2). LDWF staff provided expert judgment, (e.g., history of efforts in an area, social opportunities for the focal action, and emerging trends), prioritizing target actions in a particular area by selecting or creating polygons. These polygons were created around concentrations of the action opportunity depicted by the Blueprint.

## STEP 4: PRIORITIZING ACTION OPPORTUNITY AREAS

The COA Team identified a total of 76 potential AOAs across the three sets of focal priority actions (Pine Protection and Restoration = 8; Forested Wetland Protection and Restoration = 26; Freshwater Aquatic Connectivity Restoration = 42). These AOAs were ranked using a suite of values or desired conditions that helped define Conservation Opportunity Areas (COAs) for the Louisiana SWAP, including ancillary data on known SGCN and priority natural community locations, the protection status of the focal ecosystem, partner leverage opportunities, and overlap with AOAs for other priority actions. Expert judgement by LDWF staff determined that AOAs ranked as the Top 20% within each of the three sets of actions (Pine Protection and Restoration, Forested Wetland Protection and Restoration, and Freshwater Aquatic Connectivity Restoration) should move forward to Step 5. Additionally, LDWF staff elevated the priority of subbasins encompassing Bayou Bartholomew in northeast Louisiana due to its importance as a stronghold of imperiled aquatic biodiversity and, therefore, a priority landscape for restoration efforts.

## STEP 5: IDENTIFYING CONSERVATION OPPORTUNITY AREAS

Once priority AOAs were identified (Step 3) and ranked (Step 4), LDWF staff identified six draft COA boundaries by examining the spatial overlap of highly ranked AOAs for each of the three sets of actions (Pine Protection and Restoration, Forested Wetland Protection and Restoration, and Freshwater Aquatic Connectivity Restoration). For most ecoregions of the state (Figure 
2
.2), there was minimal overlap of the sets of actions, and most of the boundaries closely conformed to watershed boundaries. In the East Gulf Coastal Plain, however, there was considerable overlap of Pine, Forested Wetland, and Aquatic AOAs and less congruency with watershed boundaries. In this ecoregion, LDWF staff elected to identify two draft COAs based on HUC 10 boundaries, including areas across the border in Mississippi. These boundaries include areas not highlighted as important by the six step process, but actions in those areas are likely to impact sustainability of aquatic organisms downstream.

## STEP 6: REVIEWING AND REFINING CONSERVATION OPPORTUNITY AREAS

The COA Team summarized a variety of data on known SGCN and priority natural community locations to identify critical gaps in the draft COA network (e.g., whether or not small-patch grassland communities like saline prairie were captured or represented in the network). As expected, the draft COAs showed great variability in coverage of locations for individual SGCN species and priority natural communities, ranging from 0% to 100% of known locations. In a few cases, LDWF staff adjusted draft COA boundaries as needed, but largely accepted the six draft boundaries. These six COAs, plus the five 2015 COAs encompassing ecosystems not covered by this analysis (i.e. Calcasieu Prairie, Sabine Prairie, Chenier, Barataria-Terrebonne, and the Chandeleurs), represent the final COAs for 2025 
(Figure 7.1)
.

## LOUISIANA COAs

The desired outcome of the COA update effort was to catalyze implementation of the Louisiana SWAP by better targeting research, management, and acquisition. The process used here promotes those outcomes by ensuring COAs focus on priority ecosystems and the highest diversity areas within the distribution of those ecosystems. Further, by using standardized and best available information on the relative condition of ecosystems from Blueprint Indicators, this process will support setting and achieving conservation targets within COAs and promote collaborations within Louisiana and with neighboring states. Figure 
7
.2 illustrates the COA boundaries overlaid on the ecoregions, and Figure 
7
.3 illustrates the COA boundaries along with other lands managed for conservation in the state. For a list of managed areas by ecoregion, see Appendix H.
The COA Team identified 11 COAs through this process that covers a broad spectrum of SGCN and priority natural communities (Table 
7
.1), thus, reducing the overall number of COAs and the percent of the state the COAs cover. However, this process did not appreciably reduce the range of sizes of the COAs, largely due to the size of the subbasins used to represent species distributions in both the original and update processes. Subsetting the species information by ecosystem helped better target COA placement for maintenance and protection of upland pine and floodplain forest ecosystems. Further improvements in the resolution and reliability of species distribution (e.g., occupancy) and population status (e.g., demographics) information will help further target actions and refine overall objectives. Despite the coarse resolution of the species data, this iteration of COAs is targeted to areas for specific actions that will more explicitly focus on the health, function, and connectivity of ecosystems. As such, COAs provide a laboratory for linking response of SGCN to suites of conservation actions at landscape scales, information that will in turn help refine mapping and better target actions.
In the sections that follow, a very brief overview of each COA is provided. Additional information about each COA and associated SGCN can be found in Blueprint Reports and the Landscape Assessment Report provided in the supplemental materials on ScienceBase (www.sciencebase.gov/catalog/item/ 650efc46d34e823a0275500f). TABLE 7.1. Overview of 2025 Conservation Opportunity Areas. Opportunities for restoration (R) and protection (P) are noted when an ecosystem was a primary driver of the COA boundary. A small 'x' indicates that COA encompasses highly-ranked ecological opportunity for the action and, thus, could be considered a secondary focus of the COA.

The Little River COA was primarily delineated to capture priority areas for protection and restoration actions in the Floodplain Forests and Woody Wetlands ecosystem as well as restoration of linear and lateral connectivity (Table 
7.7

7.8

The Saline Bayou COA was primarily delineated to capture priority areas for protection and restoration actions in the Floodplain Forests and Woody Wetlands ecosystem as well as protection of existing lateral connectivity (Table 
7

The Barataria-Terrebonne COA was delineated in 2015 to capture priority areas for protection and restoration actions of seagrass and barrier island ecosystems (Table 
7
.11). This COA was included in the 2025 set without further analysis. However, the 2023 Blueprint Report (see supplemental materials) identified that only 17% of seagrass and barrier island ecosystems in this part of the state scored in fair to good or better coastal condition. Further, the assessment found about 50% of the area to be important for sea turtles and 41% of the acreage to be important for marine mammals, ranking both above the 70th percentile. However, NOAA sea level rise inundation data indicates nearly all the island acres evaluated would be affected by a 2.7' sea level rise by 2060.

## INTRODUCTION

Louisiana is home to a multitude of unique ecosystems with habitats that support a rich diversity of species, some of which can be found nowhere else in the world. Many of the species that Louisiana has identified as Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) depend on these ecosystems to exist. It is important to recognize potential threats to these ecological communities to better manage and conserve habitat to preserve these key species.
Much of Louisiana has been altered from its original state by human expansion and development resulting in the reduction and impairment of suitable habitat for SGCN. State Wildlife Action Plans are required to identify threats that may adversely affect SGCN and the habitats that support them. This chapter focuses on 12 threat categories with applicable subcomponents, which are mostly based on the Conservation Measures Partnership's Direct Threats Classification, 2.0 (CMP 2016). In many instances, the definitions associated with each threat have been pulled directly from the original source document. The exception is Data Gaps, which LDWF included as an additional threat to those officially listed in the threats classification document.

## RESIDENTIAL AND COMMERCIAL DEVELOPMENT

Threats posed by human settlements and other nonagricultural land uses with a substantial footprint can be further broken down into three subcategories: residential, commercial, and associated development. Despite much of the landscape remaining relatively sparsely populated, development is a primary threat to SGCN.

## HOUSING AND URBAN AREAS

According to the 2020 U.S. Census Bureau, Louisiana is the 33rd largest state by area but the 25th most densely populated state, with over 4.6 million residents occupying roughly 52,419 square miles or 33.5 million acres, of which 9,168 square miles or 5.9 million acres is water.
Intensive development destroys natural habitats and can alter local hydrology and facilitates the introduction of invasive species (whether intentional or inadvertent) and cause considerable disruption to the environment. Urbanization, road construction, and forest fragmentation can also increase impacts to the environment by hindering the dispersal and migration of forest plants and animals.
Cities and towns, including non-housing development associated with housing, occupy an increasingly large area of former wildlife habitat. Louisiana's overall recent population growth has not been dramatic, with an overall increase of 2.7% from the 2010 to the 2020 census (Figure 
8
.1). Chapter 8: Threats
Generally, human expansion focuses development around areas of higher populations (i.e. urban sprawl). The amount of land conversion associated with urban growth varies across the state but is correlated with these population centers. 

## COMMERCIAL AND INDUSTRIAL AREAS

In addition to housing and population expansion, it is important to consider other aspects of development that can have a large impact to habitat through land fragmentation and conversion.
Commercial development consumes large amounts of space, providing retail opportunities, distribution centers, business centers, and entertainment opportunities that attract supporting economies and often results in population growth. These areas change the surrounding landscape and can drastically alter local ecology.
h Action Strategy: Work with the legislature to develop tax incentives for landowners to encourage conservation of priority natural communities. h Action Strategy: Provide educational information on natural communities and the importance of SGCN to landowners and managers through participation in outreach events, presentations, workshops, and online resources. h Action Strategy: Use citizen science programs to monitor natural communities and the impacts of commercial developments. 

## TOURISM AND RECREATION AREAS

## AGRICULTURE AND AQUACULTURE

Agricultural and aquacultural threats can be subdivided into multiple categories, each with its own unique set of challenges that can affect the surrounding landscape and natural communities.

## ANNUAL AND PERENNIAL NON-TIMBER CROPS

Non-timber crops are cultivated in farms, plantations, orchards, vineyards, and mixed agroforestry systems for purposes such as food, fodder, fiber, fuel, and other uses. Agricultural production and development serve as vital economic engines, significantly influencing communities, local and national food supplies, and individual livelihoods. The USDA\NASS 2024 State Agriculture Overview for Louisiana identifies soybeans, rice, and corn as the three leading annual crop commodities, with harvested areas measuring 1,060,000 acres for soybeans, 459,000 acres for rice, and 443,000 acres for corn. However, these activities can also negatively affect habitats, species ecology, and habitat continuity.
h Action Strategy: Encourage the use of farming practices and plant varieties that provide ecosystem services and minimize the impacts to native wildlife. h Action Strategy: Promote the establishment of native plant buffer strips along agricultural field edges and waterways to capture excess nutrients, reduce surface water runnoff, and provide wildlife corridors. approximately 8 million acres of that land was in private, nonindustrial ownership (LDAF 2025). Tracts planted as monocultures such as most pine plantations provide little tangible habitat and resources for wildlife. However, proper management of these areas can, in some cases, provide limited benefits.

## WOOD AND PULP PLANTATIONS

h Action Strategy: Encourage silvicultural practices that promote natural processes and ecosystem dynamics. 

## LIVESTOCK FARMING AND RANCHING

Livestock operations, if not managed responsibly, can have unintended consequences on the landscape. Activities related to cattle ranches, dairies, hog farms, and poultry operations can contribute to increased nutrient runoff and erosion, both of which are known to degrade water quality and damage habitats.
h Action Strategy: Work with landowners to prevent over grazing of sensitive habitat and to minimize livestock access to flowing waters. h Action Strategy: Work with landowners to conduct operations aimed at minimizing runoff of animal waste through responsible drain design and buffers. 

## MARINE AND FRESHWATER AQUACULTURE

Aquaculture in Louisiana is a multimillion dollar industry with over 800 operations contributing to the state's agricultural economy (USDA 2024). Water quality, operational controls, and site containment strategies are key in supporting these types of operations and minimizing their potential ecological impacts.
h Action Strategy: Discourage the use of non-native species in aquaculture operations. h Action Strategy: Implement and promote the use of BMPs and SMZs in appropriate habitat and promote the creation of Hazard Analysis Control Point (HACP) plans for potential aquaculture releases.

## ENERGY PRODUCTION AND MINING

Threats from production of non-biological resources can be direct and indirect. This section discusses the potential issues surrounding energy production and mineral extraction.

## OIL AND GAS DRILLING

The exploration, development, and production of petroleum and other hydrocarbons in the form of oil and gas wells can negatively affect SGCN and their habitats. According to the U.S. Energy Information Administration, Louisiana currently ranks 6th in the country for energy production. Louisiana accounts for approximately 10% of the nation's natural gas production (Figure 
8
.2) and is among the top 10 states for crude oil reserves and production (Figure 
8
.3). Construction of access roads to these sites impacts species and further contributes to habitat fragmentation (Figure 
8
.4). In addition, catastrophic oil releases at well sites can cause acute and chronic impacts to wildlife and their habitats.
h Action Strategy: Implement and promote the use of BMPs during exploration, production, and de-commissioning of energy production infrastructure.
LOUISIANA'S OIL WELLS LOUISIANA'S NATURAL GAS WELLS (LEFT) Breton Island after an oil spill. (BELOW LEFT) A coastal marsh after an oil spill. (BELOW RIGHT) A Brown Pelican (Pelecanus occidentalis) at Queen Bess Island covered in oil after an oil spill.
h Action Strategy: Provide information to oil, gas, and seismic companies and encourage resource survey and mining techniques that avoid or minimize impacts to SGCN and their habitats.

## MINING AND QUARRYING

The exploration, development, and production of minerals and rocks in the form of mines, quarries, and sand and gravel pits can negatively affect SGCN and their habitats. These activities can include those that not only alter habitat but can also result in terraforming that converts the entire area from its natural state. These operations can result in additional land fracturing, chemical seepage, and sediment loss, which can further degrade habitat over time.
h Action Strategy: Encourage protection of natural sediments of sand and gravel bars by working with the LDWF Scenic Rivers Program and partners.

## RENEWABLE ENERGY

The exploration, development, and production of renewable energy through solar and wind farms can pose risks to SGCN and their habitats. Often such projects require large footprints, destroying habitat and creating barriers to migration corridors, thus displacing or isolating wildlife populations. Birds and bats face direct mortality from collisions with wind turbines, and Louisiana's location within the Mississippi Flyway and the trans-Gulf migration corridor may heighten the collision risks for migratory birds. In addition to collisions, wind energy development may displace birds from otherwise high quality habitat, leading to functional habitat loss. It is essential to take precautions during all project phases to minimize or mitigate negative impacts. Careful consideration of the timing, location, and scale of these projects can help minimize their environmental effects.
In addition, large-scale solar farms covering extensive contiguous areas can permanently displace species like the Gopher Tortoise. If these projects are not properly designed and maintained, they may also lead to further issues related to erosion, habitat fragmentation, and establishment of invasive species. 

## TRANSPORTATION AND SERVICE CORRIDORS

Threats from long, narrow transportation and service corridors include habitat destruction and fragmentation and wildlife mortality from vehicular strikes.

## ROADS AND RAILROADS

Surface transport on roadways and dedicated tracks includes highways, secondary roads, logging roads, bridges and causeways, and railroads. Fencing associated with roads is included with this threat. Additional roadways and improvements to heavily trafficked areas can provide additional access and opportunities for local human populations. However, these travel corridors can be barriers to local wildlife if not properly sited and constructed and can promote further establishment of invasive plant species. Although pollution from roadways is a concern for local biodiversity, it is addressed in the Pollution section of this chapter.
h Action Strategy: Work with partners to promote the use of bridges and culverts designed for wildlife and fish passage such as the installation of oversized culverts below grade.
In addition, minimize impacts of road, bridge, and culvert construction and replacement on stream quality. 

## UTILITY AND SERVICE LINES

Transport of energy and resources includes electrical and phone wires, aqueducts, and oil and gas pipelines. Fragmentation of habitat occurs when new rights-of-way (ROW) are cleared.
Transmission lines and pipelines can often act as corridors for invasive plant species, and chemical releases from pipelines can cause acute and chronic impacts to wildlife and their habitats.
In addition, overhead power lines can act as barriers for bats and birds, increasing the likelihood of collisions. Although much of the infrastructure is already in place, proper management practices for these areas and additional consideration to minimize impact to SGCN during the planning phase of future projects can aid in minimizing and mitigating some of the unintended impacts. 

## SHIPPING LANES

In order to maintain shipping lanes for transport in freshwater and coastal waterways, dredging of stream bottoms is necessary. However, dredging degrades habitat for many fish and mollusk SGCN. Wakes from cargo ships and barges damage shorelines, which can further degrade habitat and cause a variety of issues affecting sedimentation and water quality. In addition, commercial and recreational boats may strike and critically injure or kill wildlife.
h Action Strategy: Encourage and maximize the beneficial use of dredge material. h Action Strategy: Promote no wake zones in areas critical for wildlife. h Action Strategy: Post signage in work areas and at boat launches of water bodies where manatee may occur to reduce strike risk.

## FLIGHT PATHS

Air transport may result in noise disturbance and bird strikes.
Most bird-aircraft strikes take place at or near airports, generally occurring during take-off or landing. This potential threat is not unique to Louisiana, and these strikes account for a relatively small amount of bird deaths per year (USFWS 2024). This can, however, compromise air travel safety; therefore, bird movements should be considered by airports and air crew.
h Action Strategy: Work with partners to incorporate language into airport hazard mitigation plans that benefits wildlife.
Threats associated with the consumptive use of "wild" biological resources encompass both intentional and unintentional consequences of harvesting. These impacts can affect both target and nontarget species as well as their habitats. This includes the persecution and control of specific species as well as nontargets that may be injured as bycatch.

## HUNTING AND COLLECTING TERRESTRIAL ANIMALS

This subcategory includes the killing or trapping of terrestrial wild animals for commercial or recreational use, subsistence, research, cultural purposes, or for predator control/persecution reasons. It also includes accidental mortality of nontarget species.
The Louisiana Wildlife and Fisheries Commission is tasked with the control and oversight of all regulations of terrestrial wildlife in the state. It receives and reviews biological, socioeconomic, and other technical data as well as management recommendations from LDWF staff. Combining these data with public input, the Commission votes on actions for long term management and sustainability of the resource. These regulations include, but are not limited to, hunting seasons and harvest limits.
h Action Strategy: Ensure management plans include considerations for SGCN.

## LOGGING AND WOOD HARVESTING

Forest harvesting activities are typically associated with the removal of trees for timber, wood pulp, fiber, or energy. 

## FISHING AND HARVESTING AQUATIC RESOURCES

Like with terrestrial wildlife, the Louisiana Wildlife and Fisheries Commission is tasked with the control and oversight of all aquatic wildlife in the state. These regulations include, but are not limited to, fishing seasons, creel limits, and the collection and sale of amphibians and reptiles.
The harvest of aquatic wild animals for commercial or recreational use, subsistence, research, cultural purposes, or for control/persecution reasons may include accidental mortality and trawling bycatch. Abandoned traps and fishing gear allowed to remain in place while not in use (e.g., derelict crab traps) can result in the continued catch and kill of species and pose a strike hazard to watercraft.
h Action Strategy: Remove passive fishing gear that are not in use, including derelict crab traps, in order to minimize mortality of wildlife and fish species. h Action Strategy: Promote harvest of invasive species (e.g., Silver Carp [Hypophthalmichthys molitrix]) in place of native species for bait and other purposes.

## HUMAN INTRUSION AND DISTURBANCE

Human activities related to various non-consumptive uses can modify and disturb habitats. While these threats typically do not result in permanent habitat destruction, the potential effects from human disturbance can be significant to many SGCN and their habitats, with certain species being especially vulnerable. Non-consumptive use refers to activities where the resource remains intact and can be enjoyed by multiple user groups.

## RECREATIONAL ACTIVITIES

People spending time in nature or traveling in vehicles outside of established transportation corridors for recreational purposes may affect SGCN. This category includes off-road vehicles, motorboats, personal watercraft, mountain bikes, hikers, birders, pets in recreation areas, and temporary campsites, among others. This section does not include permanent recreation facilities or permanent transport corridors, which have been previously covered in this chapter.

## BIOLOGICAL RESOURCE USE

LDWF manages over 1.6 million acres of land and waterways designated as wildlife management areas, refuges, and conservation areas. These spaces are vital for numerous species that rely on diverse habitats. However, these lands are not just protecting Louisiana's natural resources, they are also offering various outdoor recreational opportunities to the public.
