Camping sites, if not properly kept, can result in habitat disturbance that can disrupt and harm vulnerable species. The accumulation of human rubbish in an area, when not properly disposed of, can be an attractant to nuisance species, which may also prey on at-risk species in the area. Pets, particularly dogs, can also harass local wildlife, leading to unnecessary mortality.
The use of all-terrain vehicles is a popular pastime, but without proper knowledge and care, these activities can result in negative impacts to sensitive habitats such as wetlands, sandbars, beaches, and more. These activities can not only disrupt or destroy wildlife nesting areas, they can cause direct mortality of adults and active nests. Off-roading can also result in large ruts that can destabilize stream banks, leading to additional habitat degradation.
To ensure the long-term sustainability of these areas, Louisiana is dedicated to collaborating with conservation partners and the public. This includes helping to identify sensitive regions and their habitats, raising awareness, and minimizing the negative effects that recreational activities may have on these environments.
h Action Strategy: Work with partners to minimize the impacts of recreational activities, including ORV use, in sensitive areas. h Action Strategy: Promote conservation compatible land use. h Action Strategy: Use signs, stewards, and symbolic fencing to protect nesting and other sensitive areas.
h Action Strategy: Support outreach and education aimed at minimizing human impacts to SGCN and priority habitats. h Action Strategy: Educate the public on the impacts of domestic and feral animals (e.g., dogs, cats, and hogs). h Action Strategy: Promote legislation to prevent the intentional release of helium-filled balloons.

## MILITARY ACTIVITIES

Military exercises and training activities have the potential to impact SGCN and their habitats. Beyond the facilities and bases, the military also conducts training exercises that covers a large physical footprint across the state.
An important means of collaboration between Louisiana and the U.S. Department of Defense (DoD) is through Integrated Natural Resource Management Plans (INRMPs). INRMPs serve as management plans for the highly diverse habitats for which the DoD is responsible.
These plans are installation-specific and designed to balance conservation and military activities to satisfy environmental legal requirements without compromising the capability of installation lands to support the military's mission. INRMPs are mandatory for all installations, including state-owned National Guard installations, that have significant natural resources.
INRMPs must be prepared in cooperation with the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) and appropriate state fish and wildlife agencies and are intended to reflect the mutual agreement of the agencies concerning the conservation, protection, and management of fish and wildlife resources.
h Action Strategy: Continue to work with military installations to minimize the impacts of activities, training exercises, and ranges.

## NATURAL SYSTEM MODIFICATIONS

This category is defined as "threats from actions that convert or degrade habitat in service of 'managing' natural or seminatural systems, often to improve human welfare" (CMP 2016). Modifications are frequently implemented in an attempt to address or mitigate effects of disturbances and natural processes. However, these management efforts have the potential to adversely impact or eliminate natural processes essential to ecological communities if not planned carefully.

## FIRE AND FIRE SUPPRESSION

In the southeastern United States, low level fires play a crucial role in maintaining various ecosystems. Fire-dependent communities such as pine savannas, woodlands, and prairies have evolved with frequent low intensity fires and provide critical habitat for many SGCN.
Various factors such as habitat fragmentation and fire suppression can lead to a reduction of fire on these landscapes. If left without fire, these systems will begin to transition into a ruderal or contemporary forest community. This shift will reduce ground level forest fuels and create a positive feedback loop that further suppresses fire 
(Loudermilk et al. 2011
) while also reducing fire resiliency.
Fire caused by lightning strike is a natural and necessary part of fire-dependent ecosystems. However, on the contemporary, fragmented landscape, allowing fuels to accumulate while awaiting this ignition source can result in larger, high-intensity fires (wildfires). These larger fires have greater potential to cause damage to property and loss of life than more frequent low-intensity fires in the same area. The regular use of fire is an efficient management tool that can reduce competition and fuel levels to maintain the overall health of these systems and minimize the risk of catastrophic wildfires.
Chapter 8: Threats h Action Strategy: Support use of prescribed fire to maintain appropriate habitat. h Action Strategy: Reintroduce fire to fire-dependent remnant prairie, marsh, and savanna habitats. h Action Strategy: Establish and promote Prescribed Burn
Associations in all parishes throughout the state that have fire-dependent ecosystems.

## DAMS AND WATER MANAGEMENT/USE

Altering water flow patterns beyond their natural range of variation, whether intentionally or unintentionally, can have substantial impacts.
Human activities have significantly disrupted the natural hydrology of Louisiana. Actions such as dam construction and operation; sediment management; alterations to saltwater regimes, saltwater diversion levee, dike, and weir construction; groundwater extraction; channelization; and the creation of artificial lakes have drastically changed natural water flow in the state. These changes frequently reduce the quality and quantity of habitats available to SGCN. Hydrological obstructions, for example, can also lead to increased saltwater intrusion during times of drought by restricting natural flows. Increased saltwater can damage important habitat and affect drinking water. The alteration of flow patterns from human activities should be managed, where possible.
The timing, frequency, magnitude and duration of flows and connectivity are vital components of aquatic systems and provide critical habitat needed for species to survive. In addition, a well-connected network of water allows species to access additional areas, promoting a healthy and resilient ecosystem with migration opportunities.
h Action Strategy: Work with partners to review materials (e.g., as fill or bank stabilization) permitted as beneficial use in streams. h Action Strategy: Promote aquatic species passage by establishment and conservation of connectivity through the removal of aquatic barriers and discourage creation of new aquatic barriers.
h Action Strategy: Promote instream flows at levels that will support populations of SGCN. h Action Strategy: Minimize and mitigate the impacts and occurrence of saltwater intrusion.

## OTHER ECOSYSTEM MODIFICATIONS

Other actions that convert or degrade habitat in service of "managing" natural systems to improve human welfare include land reclamation projects, abandonment of managed lands, mowing, bush-hogging, beach construction, the removal of snags from streams, and the addition of riprap on shorelines.
h Action Strategy: Retain emergent and submerged woody debris (subsidize if warranted) and provide education and outreach regarding the importance of this habitat component. h Action Strategy: Create or restore bird nesting islands when and where feasible and explore potential partnerships and funding mechanisms to support such construction. h Action Strategy: Promote the use of BMPs for mowing. h Action Strategy: Retain overhanging vegetation in waterways to provide thermal refugia and to minimize adverse temperature fluctuations.

## REMOVING/REDUCING HUMAN MAINTENANCE

This threat includes the absence or reduction of current or historical maintenance regimes (e.g., supplemental feeding, mowing, and controlled burns) for important key ecological attributes. These regimes are typically maintained by protected area staff, farmers and ranchers, indigenous peoples, private landowners, and other resource managers.
h Action Strategy: Support use of prescribed fire to maintain appropriate habitat. h Action Strategy: Promote conservation grazing in historical prairie and savanna regions. h Action Strategy: Reconnect hydrology and reintroduce natural flow regimes to reduce invasive species and promote native flora and fauna. h Action Strategy: Promote planting of resilient native flora with local genetics.
LEFT: LDWF biologists conduct a prescribed burn during a prescribed burn workshop. RIGHT: Prescribed fire on a Coastal Prairie.

## INVASIVE AND PROBLEMATIC SPECIES, PATHOGENS, AND GENES

This threat includes non-native and problematic native plants, animals, pathogens and microbes, and genetic materials that have (or are predicted to have) harmful effects on biodiversity following their introduction, spread, or increase in abundance or virulence. This section gives a brief overview on the potential ecological impacts that these elements may pose and suggests some potential mitigation strategies. Additional information on invasive species can be found in Chapter 6.

## INVASIVE NON-NATIVE/ALIEN PLANTS AND ANIMALS

Harmful plants, animals, pathogens, and other microbes introduced and spread by human activities into areas in which they were not originally found can negatively impact native habitat and species.
The subtropical environment of Louisiana provides suitable habitat for species from many parts of the world. Non-native organisms present a major threat to SGCN. Once introduced, they can rapidly multiply and spread, exploiting habitat and food resources vital for native species. Impacts to habitat can be particularly noticeable in plant communities; where non-native species take over, plant communities can be reshaped, leading to a decline in native diversity and the degradation of habitat quality over time. Invasive terrestrial animals also destroy sensitive habitats as well as agricultural lands. According to the LSU AgCenter, Feral Hogs cause an estimated economic loss of $91.1 million annually in Louisiana 
(Salassi et al. 2022)
.
Numerous invasive and non-native aquatic plants and animals have been documented in Louisiana waters. Aquatic invasions, though occasionally less detectable than those on land, are no less impactful to habitat and wildlife. In addition to posing a threat to SGCN, they can also impact recreational activities by replacing or reducing populations of local sport fish or by clogging waterways with unabated, vegetative growth.
h Action Strategy: Promote compliance with lists of banned, invasive, and potentially invasive species of plants and animals. 

## PROBLEMATIC NATIVE PLANTS AND ANIMALS

Native plants and animals, when their populations become "out of balance," usually through human influence, can become problematic species. Examples include overabundant deer populations, excessive vegetation due to the loss of foraging species, improper stocking rates, and more.
h Action Strategy: Promote BMPs and control measures for problematic native species.

## INTRODUCED GENETIC MATERIAL

Human-altered organisms or genes include pesticide-resistant crops, genetically modified insects for biocontrol, genetically modified fishes, and genetically modified trees and crops. Modified introduced species have the potential to hybridize with local populations and pass their genetic material to local communities.
Genetically engineered crops may also pose a risk to nontarget species. In addition, consistent pesticide application on crops that are herbicide resistant may facilitate the development of resistant weeds and plants, which could lead to a cycle of increased pesticide use.
Chapter 8: Threats ¹ The following paragraph was provided by Dr. Ellen Hayes, SEAFWA Regional Wildlife Health Coordinator.
h Action Strategy: Use integrated pest management for the control of invasive and problematic native plants and animals. h Action Strategy: Maintain unique genetic trajectories and genetic diversity of local native populations.

## PATHOGENS AND MICROBES

Harmful native and non-native agents including bacteria, viruses, prions, fungi, and other microorganisms can cause illness to a host. These agents can be detrimental to SGCN by affecting the species directly or indirectly and can spread rapidly if left unchecked.
Wildlife health¹ is a complex, dynamic topic that can be difficult to define and measure. It is more than just the presence or absence of disease caused by pathogens or contaminants; rather, it can be defined by a population's ability to withstand challenges, which may include climate change, habitat loss, or the introduction of a disease-causing agent. Thus, health is the result of interacting biological, social, and environmental determinants 
(Stephen 2014)
. Diseases can have significant impacts on wildlife populations. The development of disease depends on factors related to the host organism, its environment, and the disease-causing agent. Pathogens (e.g., bacteria, viruses, parasites, and prions) and contaminants are normally present in wildlife populations, with disease typically occurring at low levels, but becoming more prevalent when hosts are stressed. An emerging disease is defined as "one that has recently been discovered; has recently increased in incidence, geography, or host range; or is newly evolved" 
(Rachowicz et al. 2005)
. Two hypotheses can apply to an emerging disease: the novel pathogen hypothesis states that the disease has recently spread into new geographic areas, whereas the endemic pathogen hypothesis suggests that it has been present in the environment but recently has affected new hosts or increased in its ability to cause disease 
(Rachowicz et al. 2005)
. For example, in North America, chytridiomycosis, caused by the fungus Batrachochytrium dendrobatidis, and whitenose syndrome, caused by the fungus Pseudogymnoascus destructans, have emerged following pathogen introduction and have had devastating impacts on amphibian and bat populations, respectively. Disease can have direct impacts, such as mortality, or can have indirect impacts, such as reproductive failure; both types of impacts can lead to population declines.
As some species disappear from the landscape, others can proliferate, causing an imbalance in predator-prey relationships or overuse of resources. Sometimes these diseases are zoonotic and threaten human health as well. According to the World Health Organization, 70% to 80% of emerging infectious diseases are zoonotic in origin. Wildlife health is a critical component of One Heath, which is the intersection of human health, animal health, and environmental health. Therefore, discussions of wildlife health must consider connections with humans, domestic animals, and ecosystems. Such connections may include game species as a food source for humans, pathogens that can be transmitted between wildlife and humans or domestic animals, and the habitats needed to support wildlife populations. In addition, healthy ecosystems provide a variety of ecosystem services for humans, including agricultural production and recreation.
h Action Strategy: Monitor disease levels for emerging pathogens. h Action Strategy: Promote BMPs for feeding stations to reduce pathogen transmission. h Action Strategy: Work with partners to address emerging diseases through surveillance and research. h Action Strategy: Promote communication and collaboration among partners to address emerging fish and wildlife health issues more effectively. h Action Strategy: Promote BMPs and protocols for disinfection of field equipment and gear to reduce pathogen transmission.

## POLLUTION

Introduction of exotic and excess materials or energy from point and nonpoint sources can have dramatic effects on local species, habitats, and the environment. Damage is generally manifested by reduced air, soil, and water quality, which can directly affect plant, wildlife, fish, and human populations.
Waste and effluents originating from urban populations, municipalities, households, and industry can significantly degrade environmental conditions, affecting SGCN and their habitats. Pollutants can dramatically change aquatic systems by altering water chemistry and can damage important terrestrial systems through contamination of soil and water. It is important to consider these potential effects and the actions required to minimize these impacts.

## HOUSEHOLD SEWAGE AND URBAN WASTE WATER

This threat includes water-borne sewage and nonpoint runoff of nutrients, sediments, and toxic chemicals (including pharmaceutical contaminants) from housing and urban areas. This threat also includes discharge from municipal waste treatment, leaking septic systems, untreated sewage, outhouses, oil and sediment from roads as well as fertilizers and pesticides from lawns and golf courses.
h Action Strategy: Protect aquatic species from anthropogenic threats including release of treated and untreated waste water, nonpoint surface runoff, and water withdrawals for public and industrial water supplies. Balloons on a beach.

## INDUSTRIAL AND MILITARY EFFLUENTS

This threat includes water-borne pollution from industrial and military sources such as mining, energy production, and resource extraction industries. These contaminants are typically in the form of nutrients, sediments, and toxic chemicals resulting from illegal dumping of chemicals, mine tailings (waste material created from mining activities), leakage from fuel tanks, oil spills from pipelines, and polychlorinated biphenyls (PCBs) in river sediments. Pollution can cause negative impacts even when the source is not located near the affected aquatic system. Some pollutants may only impact these systems once bioaccumulation occurs in the food chain.
The Clean Water Act requires the Louisiana Department of Environmental Quality (LDEQ) to protect waters of the state as it relates to aquatic life, ecological condition, and human health concerns. LDEQ assesses watersheds within the state on a fouryear rotation, releasing an updated integrated report every two years. This report details support or non-support for designated uses in each subsegment based on water quality. Figure 
8
.5 displays data based on the results of the 2020 integrated report for assessed waters' ability to support fish and wildlife propagation.
According to the Environmental Protection Agency's (EPA) 303(d) 2024 list status, Louisiana has a disproportionately high number of freshwater streams that are categorized as impaired in terms of supporting fish and wildlife propagation. Out of 9,712 miles of streams and rivers assessed, 6,375 (66%) miles were found to have some degree of impairment for this designated use. The primary causes for this impairment include low oxygen, mercury, murky water (also known as turbidity), salts, and pesticides.
The EPA's evaluation of lakes and reservoirs in Louisiana indicated that of 581,446 acres assessed, 517,018 acres (89%) were deemed as impaired for fish and wildlife propagation, with the same top five causes contributing to this impaired status as streams and rivers.
In their wetland assessment, which evaluated 941,389 acres, the EPA found that only 75,219 acres (8%) were considered in good condition (i.e. they fully supported fish and wildlife propagation). The primary reasons for impairments are listed as low oxygen, mercury, murky water, and salts.

## GEOLOGICAL EVENTS

This IUCN category was not addressed when considering threats and action strategies, because landslide and seismic activity is negligible in Louisiana, and there is no volcanic activity.
Lastly, the EPA's assessment of coastal waters within Louisiana found that 3,353 square miles (56%) of the 6,015 square miles were in good condition to support fish and wildlife propagation.
The leading causes of impairment in these coastal areas include mercury, low oxygen, pesticides, PCBs, and dioxins.
h Action Strategy: Minimize the occurrence and impacts of oil spills and other chemical releases.

## AGRICULTURAL AND FORESTRY EFFLUENTS

This threat includes water-borne pollutants from agricultural, silvicultural, and aquacultural systems that include nutrients, toxic chemicals, and sediments. The effects of these contaminants can be seen on and off the sites where they were applied, generated, or stored. Improper handling or application often results in increased discharge of fertilizer and pesticide through runoff, and soil erosion also contributes to off-site pollution.
h Action Strategy: Encourage the proper application of pesticides. h Action Strategy: Encourage the use of sediment barriers and reduce soil erosion.

## GARBAGE AND SOLID WASTE

Rubbish and other solid materials can entangle or otherwise harm wildlife. This material includes municipal waste, litter from cars and boats, construction and demolition debris, and waste that can trap wildlife such as open bottles, discarded fishing lines, and six-pack rings.
Wildlife entanglement is not the only threat associated with these items. The accumulation of harmful chemicals that can naturally leach out of these items poses an additional risk to ecological systems.
h Action Strategy: Ensure alternative garbage and solid waste use policies consider negative impacts to ecosystems. h Action Strategy: Promote recycling of used fishing line. h Action Strategy: Investigate and minimize the impacts of microplastics.

## AIRBORNE POLLUTANTS

Atmospheric pollutants from point and nonpoint sources include acid rain, smog from vehicle emissions, excess smoke, excess nitrogen deposition, radioactive fallout, wind-dispersed pollutants from farm fields, and airborne particulate matter that can cause significant damage to organisms and their environments. These types of pollutants do not often stay in the atmosphere and, after a time, will condense and precipitate or deposit into other systems causing additional impacts over a larger area.
h Action Strategy: Promote use of no-till farming practices and other BMPs to reduce airborne particulates and pollutants.

## EXCESS ENERGY

Inputs of heat, sound, and light that may disrupt wildlife or ecosystems include noise from highways or aircraft, heated water from power plants, floodlights and other outdoor lighting, and atmospheric radiation resulting from ozone gaps. Manatees have been lured to heated water discharge from power or chemical plants; such conditions may cause these individuals to attempt to overwinter, causing mortality of this threatened species. Attraction to lights can greatly impact insect behavior, possibly affecting reproductive events. Lighting also disorients migratory animals and may increase likelihood of collisions with buildings and communication towers. In addition, improperly sited lights near beaches can cause hatchling sea turtles to travel inland instead of to the water.
h Action Strategy: Promote education and outreach to reduce the impacts of light pollution. h Action Strategy: Promote wildlife-friendly lighting to reduce collision risk. 

## RESILIENT COASTAL SITES CLIMATE CHANGE

Changes in climate patterns and weather events that occur outside of the natural range of variation can be devastating to vulnerable species or habitats. Severe weather events may be increasing in frequency and intensity and expanding outside their historical range due to human influences. It is critical to take actions that minimize and mitigate the effects of these threats. See Chapter 10: Louisiana: A Regional Perspective for a more in depth consideration of this topic.

## ECOSYSTEM ENCROACHMENT

The impacts of displaced ecosystems encroaching upon the habitats of other species can have far-reaching consequences.
The migration of an ecosystem is regarded as a form of stress to the ecosystem and its component species. This movement also creates additional pressure on neighboring systems and the species that inhabit them. Consequently, this may pose a threat to those adjacent systems, and mitigation should be considered.
Resilient sites offer diversity in microhabitat and niche types to support biodiversity while remaining connected to other sites to facilitate migration opportunities. Typically, these sites feature minimal barriers to migration and limited habitat fragmentation, which further enhances species diversity and overall ecosystem function. Coastal areas of Louisiana are particularly vulnerable to sea level rise, which can disproportionately affect lowland coastal systems and their ability to adapt and provide ecosystem services. Figure 
8
.7 depicts a map of coastal resiliency for southern Louisiana using the Southeast Conservation Blueprint's Resilient Coastal Sites indicator. This indicator originates from TNC's Resilient Coastal Sites project and depicts the capacity for coastal habitats to migrate to adjacent lowlands and sustain biodiversity and natural services under increasing inundation from sea level rise. It is based on the characteristics of current tidal complexes, their predicted migration space, and surrounding buffer areas. These characteristics include marsh complex size, shared edge with migration space, sediment balance, water quality, natural land cover, landform diversity, and more. 

## FRESHWATER RESILIENCE AND CONNECTIVITY

h Action Strategy: Establish and protect habitat corridors to provide critical habitat for SGCN to mitigate impacts from climactic shifts. h Action Strategy: Acquire property to increase and support critical habitat to mitigate future impacts from climate change. h Action Strategy: Promote restoration of natural barriers to minimize storm impacts including restoration of barrier islands, mainland beaches, and coastal marshes. h Action Strategy: Protect upslope environments to provide critical habitat for SGCN to mitigate impacts from sea level rise.

## CHANGES IN GEOCHEMICAL REGIMES

The overall shift in the chemical composition in a geologic environment can be brought on by multiple causes which can be natural or human-induced and can have differing effects on habitats and systems depending on the severity of the shift.
Changes in geochemical conditions can result in changes to atmospheric CO², sedimentation, nutrient and metal content, and fluctuations in pH. These changes may have broad-scale effects to sensitive species or degraded ecosystems that may already be at increased risk and may not be able to cope with these shifts. In addition, subsidence (i.e. sinking of the land) can act in combination with sea level rise, decreasing available land in low lying areas.
h Action Strategy: Restore hydrology of the major rivers to allow sediment recharge into coastal environments. h Action Strategy: Promote restoration of natural barriers to minimize subsidence including restoration of barrier islands, mainland beaches, and coastal marshes.

## CHANGES IN TEMPERATURE REGIMES

Widespread variation in normal temperatures (i.e. based on historical observations) can impact species and habitats across regions. These are typically observed as heatwaves, cold spells, and changes in oceanic temperature. Deviations from established temperature norms can result in atypical weather conditions. These fluctuations can impact an area's ability to support ecosystem functions, thereby influencing species distributions and overall biodiversity. 

## CHANGES IN PRECIPITATION AND HYDROLOGICAL REGIMES

According to projections from the South-Central and Southeast Climate Adaptation Centers, annual precipitation levels are anticipated to remain stable. However, there will likely be changes in the timing and intensity of major rainfall events. An increase in the severity of major rainfall events is expected to heighten flood risks, particularly in low lying areas, which can adversely affect aquatic systems and pose threats to human life and property.
In parallel to major precipitation events that may increase in intensity, the length of time between rainfall events is also projected to increase, potentially leading to drought-like conditions. Longer periods without precipitation will disrupt normal hydrological and terrestrial systems and persistent dry conditions will result in decreased plant productivity and increased wildfire risk.
Diminished rainfall and hydrological flow will likely reduce water availability. These conditions may lead to small permanent streams becoming ephemeral, which will reduce availability of sustained habitat. In addition, saltwater intrusion from the Gulf of America may be exacerbated, potentially causing further harm to freshwater systems. Mitigative steps to reduce the impact of these scenarios would benefit system resiliency and increase habitat adaptability.
h Action Strategy: Reestablish natural hydrology to stabilize sensitive ecosystems and minimize impacts of saltwater intrusion. h Action Strategy: Increase use of prescribed burns to minimize wildfire risks. h Action Strategy: Encourage the use of nature-based solutions and other environmentally beneficial approaches in floodwater mitigation projects. h Action Strategy: Encourage the use of green infrastructure to build resiliency in urban environments.

## SEVERE AND EXTREME WEATHER EVENTS

Extreme weather events in Louisiana are generally seen in the form of tornadoes, hurricanes, and tropical storms. These extreme weather events can be very destructive, having the potential to produce high winds, storm surge, and heavy rainfall over a short period of time. Even though these events occur naturally in Louisiana, they are predicted to increase in frequency and intensity, weakening viable ecosystems or devastating already vulnerable systems such as low lying areas in the coastal zone (e.g., beaches, barrier and coastal islands, and marshes). In addition, extreme weather has been documented to cause high mortality events.
h Action Strategy: Monitor fish kills, wildlife impacts, and habitat impacts due to extreme weather events. h Action Strategy: Promote genetic diversity and local ecotypes to promote population resiliency to extreme weather events. h Action Strategy: Promote use of living shorelines and other natural shorelines to protect against the impacts of tropical cyclones.

## DATA GAPS

Understanding SGCN, their habitats, and their interactions to external stimuli is an evolving process. Currently, there are limited occurrence and distribution data for many species. In addition, limited information is available on the response of species to changing environments and to the impacts of these changes.
Data can be procured from a variety of sources including public, private, commercial, and academic sources. Engaging and collaborating with all willing partners is crucial for enhancing our knowledge and incorporating landscape scale conservation efforts in the state. With vetting by professional biologists, crowdsourced information from community science platforms like iNaturalist has immense potential to inform occurrence and distribution data gaps and should be encouraged and utilized. These efforts could also significantly contribute to conservation management systems designed to protect important species and their habitats.
Addressing these information gaps can be achieved through research, analysis, and monitoring and provides the opportunity to obtain new insights and develop tools that will aid in deepening our understanding of these dynamics.
h Action Strategy: Engage the public in documenting and reporting species and habitat occurrences through citizen science initiatives. h Action Strategy: Work with partners to acquire new or emerging data and tools to aid in monitoring and management efforts.

## Chapter 9: Monitoring

Monitoring is one of the Eight Required Elements of a State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP). Within each SWAP, a state must describe how it will monitor Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and their habitats, identify how it will gauge the effectiveness of conservation actions, and provide an outline of adaptive management strategies that may be incorporated as conditions change and as new information and technologies emerge. Monitoring is critical to ensure that the goals of the SWAP are met, to demonstrate the success of the SWAP and the State Wildlife Grants (SWG) Program, and to provide vital information to inform management efforts to conserve SGCN and their habitats.
Monitoring provides crucial data for documenting the status of habitats and species and evaluating the efficacy of conservation actions. Consistent, repeated monitoring provides the necessary data for adaptive management, which refines management actions based on the evolving needs of the target resource and its progress towards conservation goals 
(Ringold et al. 1996)
.
Monitoring can also provide the foresight necessary to identify emerging threats so that proactive intervention can take place 
(Beever 2006)
.
Monitoring programs should be developed based on quantifiable indicators that can assess the implementation of management actions towards desired conservation goals 
(Dalton et al. 2023)
.
To be most effective, monitoring programs should also address how ecological processes may impact the conservation target.
Techniques for monitoring vary considerably across and within all taxa groups, but all methods should be reliable, standardized, and repeatable so results can be compared over time and across regions. Species monitoring typically involves the collection of population metrics including species richness, abundance, and health 
(Dalton et al. 2023)

## MONITORING SPECIES OF GREATEST CONSERVATION NEED AND NATURAL COMMUNITIES

## DATABASE NEEDS

Sound biological data are the building blocks of all conservation planning efforts. Data can inform when and where conservation actions are most warranted and, with proper study design, can suggest successful management strategies. These data can also be used to review plans for construction activities and development projects, enabling staff to provide recommendations to minimize or eliminate threats to SGCN and natural communities.
Maximizing the utility of data relies on standardized protocols utilizing robust techniques, but it also requires that data be accessible, ideally stored within a secure, centralized database.
Given the exceptionally complicated and voluminous information collected by field biologists during projects, including SWG projects¹, such a database -one that captures raw data, ¹ For a list of Louisiana's SWG projects, visit: www.wlf.louisiana.gov/page/completed-and-ongoing-louisiana-swg-projects ² Only "tracked" species of SGCN are entered into Biotics 5; those SGCN that are not tracked are typically watch list species. 

## MOLLUSKS AND CRUSTACEANS

Although far from complete, researchers have expended considerable effort in recent years on aquatic mollusk and crustacean inventories in order to better understand their distribution and status. As regulatory agencies identify significant areas for SGCN conservation, it is important that these populations are subject to regular monitoring, both for detection of declines and validation of conservation actions that have been implemented.
In recent years, environmental DNA (eDNA) projects have assisted with aquatic mollusk and crustacean inventories in Louisiana. This technique involves searching for DNA discarded by the targeted species in water samples collected from various locations within a water body. This can be especially useful for confirming the presence of rare or cryptic species and guiding focused survey efforts.

## MOLLUSKS

Detailed information on study design and standard methods for sampling freshwater mussels can be found in 
Strayer and Smith (2003)
, and many of these can be used for marine mollusks as well.
Below are examples of recommended sampling techniques that researchers have successfully used in Louisiana.

## Time-Constrained Tactile Search

This is a type of qualitative survey and generally accepted to be the most efficient method for detecting rare species. For this technique, a surveyor moves their hands across and through substrate for a specified amount of time, collecting any mussels located during the process 
(Dunn 2000)
. The surveyor may employ a snorkel or SCUBA to access deeper water. The total amount of time spent in a given area is typically 1 to 1.5 personhours 
(Dinkins et al. 2022
, Pezold et al. 2002
, Brown and Banks 2001
, Brown et al. 2010)
; however, these time intervals are likely too short to detect the majority of rare species present 
(Garig 2023
, Metcalfe-Smith et al. 2000)
.

## Transect Tactile Search

This is a type of quantitative survey that is useful for covering large areas systematically. Transects are run from bank to bank, perpendicular to flow. Surveyors tactilely search a specified area along the transect, usually one meter wide, at a rate usually 0.5 min/m² for homogeneous substrates and 0.75 min/m² for heterogeneous substrates 
(USFWS and TPWD 2023)
.
(ABOVE AND BELOW) Visual Search: LDWF biologists survey for Louisiana Pearlshell using a transparent-bottomed bucket.
Traps: Traps used for capturing crayfish.

## Sediment Collection and Sieving

Although time consuming, this methodology is recommended when accurate mussel densities or juvenile data are required. Surveyors excavate randomly distributed quadrats, usually 0.25 m², to a depth of 10 -15 cm and strain collected material through a wire mesh. Researchers often couple this technique with tactile searches in order to obtain a more complete picture of the mussel community 
(Dinkins et al. 2022
, Dunn 2000)
. 

## Visual Search

## CRUSTACEANS

Techniques for sampling crustaceans vary widely with habitat type and targeted species. Researchers have used various trap designs, electroshocking, seines, dip nets, and burrow excavations to study the distribution of these organisms, primarily crayfish, in Louisiana.
The following information provides additional details on these techniques.

## Traps

Minnow traps, pillow traps, and pyramid traps can be effective for sampling crayfishes in Louisiana, especially in habitats where other techniques are not feasible. Multi-day trap-soak times are generally required to achieve sufficient capture rates. Traps tend to disproportionately capture large males 
(Dorn et al. 2005)
, arguably a benefit, as large males are sometimes required for species identification. Researchers can add various baits to traps to lure crayfishes; however, information on the efficacy of this is mostly limited to commercial species and baiting may not affect capture rates of non-commercial species.

## Backpack Electrofishing

This technique is an effective means of sampling for crayfishes, especially when researchers desire high capture rates, but is limited to relatively clear, shallow water with sufficient conductivity. A crew of two to three individuals is preferred; one crew member is needed to wear the backpack and move the anode across areas likely to contain crayfishes, and one or two crew members are needed as dip net operators to capture stunned crayfishes. However, it is possible for a single individual to conduct surveys using this technique 
(Budnick et al. 2018)
. Surveyors should receive safety and technique training for this gear type, which is available free of cost from the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) (www. fws.gov/course/principles-and-techniques-electrofishing-online).
Chapter 9: Monitoring

## Seine

This tool consists of a net extended between two poles that users pull through water while keeping the lower edge of the net in contact with the substrate. For crayfish sampling, a net length of 10 feet or less is recommended and works best in areas devoid of underwater obstructions. In streams with a swift current, users can hold seines stationary while surveyors upstream operate a backpack electrofishing unit. In addition, surveyors can disturb the substrate or vegetation using their feet, flushing hidden individuals into the seine net downstream.

## D-frame Net

A robust version of a dip net with at least one flat side, this tool is used for skimming streambed substrates to capture crayfishes. Net bags are typically canvas with mesh in the bottom and are often attached to the frame via hog rings. This configuration is especially useful for sampling streams, because crayfishes in these habitats are often located among roots and under rocks and woody debris. In addition, these nets are effective for sampling ephemeral pools and ditches.
When compared to backpack electrofishing, this cost effective and widely used technique typically results in fewer individual crayfishes captured per unit of time but yields similar species richness estimates 
(Budnick et al. 2018)
.

## Burrow Excavation

This is one of a limited number of options researchers have to sample crayfishes that spend most of the year in burrows (i.e. primary burrowers). Using a shovel or hand trowel, surveyors excavate a burrow featuring a recently constructed chimney. Inserting a piece of flexible tubing down the burrow beforehand will help surveyors determine the burrow path during excavation. Surveyors should search any side tunnels encountered, as the target crayfish may be in one of them.
Further details on crustacean sampling techniques can be found in 
Walls (2009)
 and 
Zale et al. (2012)
.

## NON-CRUSTACEAN ARTHROPODS

The techniques for sampling non-crustacean arthropods (e.g., insects, arachnids, etc.) are as diverse as the groups themselves, and, as such, the techniques utilized are dependent upon the target organism(s). In fact, as insects may undergo complete metamorphosis, survey techniques can also vary by life stage, adding further complexity to monitoring. Active techniques include visual encounter surveys, aerial netting, sweep netting, aquatic netting, collection by beat sheets, and more. Passive techniques can include pan traps, pitfall traps, flight intercept traps, emergence traps, and others. Depending on the target species, the effectiveness of traps can be improved by adding baits, pheromones, or light sources to lure individuals to the traps. Those species less likely to be trapped (e.g., cryptic, soil-dwelling invertebrates) can often be procured using a Berlese-Tullgren funnel.
Regardless of sampling technique employed, monitoring requires repeated surveys in order to gauge changes in abundance, richness, etc. Standardization of these techniques is key to making appropriate comparisons across studies. 
Montgomery and colleagues (2021)
 present an excellent synopsis of several insect sampling methods, the suggested metadata to be collected with each, and what methods are most appropriate for several taxa. 
Ferro and Summerlin (2019)
 provide language to define collection methodology. Coordination of insect data collection and analysis, as well as identification and amelioration of threats to insects, are goals of the Status of Insects: An International Research Coordination Network (
https://statusofinsects.github.io/
).
Despite forward progress in standardization, significant challenges remain, and standardization is still lacking for most survey techniques and taxa. Although surveyors may readily learn proper setting and collection of traps and active sampling and collection methods, identification of most arthropods, especially to family, genus, or species, is time consuming and requires special expertise, and often, specialized equipment. In fact, for these reasons, researchers often do not identify many arthropods to species level during projects. Because of the scarcity of these data, even baseline information on arthropod distribution is lacking, which often hamstrings conservation efforts of SGCN in Louisiana. To address this knowledge gap, LDWF collects data on arthropod SGCN both through in-house efforts as well as partnering with experts. Surveys of imperiled species are vital to determine the distribution and abundance of these species so that management or conservation actions can be successfully implemented. Due to their high fecundity and short generation times, arthropods often respond rapidly to habitat manipulation and may be early indicators of habitat management effectiveness.
Examples of recommended sampling techniques for noncrustacean arthropods include:

## ACTIVE TECHNIQUES

## Aspirators

Aspirators are invaluable tools used to capture terrestrial arthropods, most often using a vacuum created by the user's own lungs. Aspirators are usually made with a vial, a rubber stopper with two short pieces of metal tubing embedded in it, one short length of rubber tubing, and a piece of very fine mesh.
The mesh attaches to one end of a piece of metal tube on the vial side of the rubber stopper (i.e. inside of the vial); the rubber tubing attaches to the opposite end (i.e. outside of the vial) of the metal tube that has the mesh attached. The other metal tube, the collection tube, is often gently bent (<90°) outside the vial to assist in reach. When users inhale (ideally, only into the mouth) through the rubber tubing, the arthropod is quickly sucked into the vial via the collection tube and prevented from entering the user's mouth via the mesh. A small, plastic, in-line gasoline filter attached to the rubber tubing adds protection 
(Singh et al. 2024)
. Size of target arthropods is limited by the diameter of the collection tube.

## Aerial Nets

Aerial nets are used specifically for capturing flying insects like mayflies, dragonflies, flies, bees, wasps, butterflies, and moths. An aerial net typically consists of a soft, fragile mesh bag attached to a wire loop affixed to a long handle.

## Sweep Nets

Sweep nets are used for capturing terrestrial arthropods typically found on live or dead vegetation. Unlike aerial nets, sweep nets are more robust with short, strong handles and with hoops adorned with very fine mesh bags reinforced with strong fabric or with full canvas bags. Sweep nets can be swept through vegetation to dislodge and capture virtually all sizes of insects, most of which can then be quickly collected via aspirator. Sweep net effort "can be 'quantified' by maintaining a constant area swept per sweep and a consistent number of sweeps per sample" 
(Singh et al. 2024)
.

## Aquatic Nets

Aquatic nets, like sweep nets, are robust and made to sustain the rigors of use, in this case, striking underwater hazards like submerged debris. Aquatic nets have sturdy handles and hoops with at least one flat side (e.g., D-shaped) meant to skim streambed substrates (e.g., submerged leaf litter) to capture aquatic or semi-aquatic arthropods. Net bags are typically canvas with mesh in the bottom and are often attached to the hoops via hog rings.

## Hess Samplers

Hess samplers can be used to actively collect aquatic invertebrates in shallow, lotic waters. A Hess sampler is made from an open, upright, metal cylinder with a mesh screen on one side (upstream) to allow water flow and a collection net on the opposite side (downstream). Once the cylinder is positioned upright on the substrate, the user agitates the substrate to dislodge invertebrates, which water flow carries into the collection net. Having a fixed sampling area, Hess samplers can be used for quantitative studies. See Gibbs and colleagues for a comparison of Hess samplers with other methods 
(Gibbs et al. 2023)
.

## Beat Sheets

Beat sheets are similar to sweep nets in that they are efficient for collecting terrestrial arthropods from live or dead vegetation.
A beat sheet is typically a canvas sheet reinforced with two crisscrossed dowel rods with their ends inserted into pockets in the four corners of the sheet (i.e. similar to a homemade kite). When the beat sheet is held by the dowels, the canvas sags into a very shallow "net." With the beat sheet held under vegetation, the user then vigorously strikes the vegetation with a stick; insects are dislodged and fall into the beat sheet where they are collected, usually via aspirator.

## Visual Encounter Surveys (VES)

The use of VES in arthropod monitoring is limited to especially showy groups of day-flying insects like dragonflies and butterflies that can be readily identified in the field using binoculars. One rare example of nighttime VES includes the use of longwave ultraviolet (UV) flashlights for the detection of scorpions; the WDP documented almost 500 Southern Unstriped Scorpions along transects through Southern Mesophytic Hardwood Forest in just five nights using UV flashlights. VES may be combined with other techniques such as aerial netting to assist in species identification 
(Montgomery et al. 2021)
.
Light Traps: Researcher setting up a black light trap to capture non-crustacean arthropods (LEFT), and an example of a light trap used to trap insects (RIGHT).

## PASSIVE TECHNIQUES

## Pan Traps

Pan traps can be created using brightly colored, plastic plates or shallow bowls filled with soapy water. Pan traps target small bees and wasps, which are attracted by the bright colors and become trapped in the soapy water. Like other techniques, recording the number of active traps and the amount of time the traps are active is vital for comparison studies. This type of trap should be checked at least once per day 
(Singh et al. 2024)
.

## Pitfall Traps

Pitfall traps are often made by nesting two containers of similar size and shape in a hole in the ground with the lip of the inner container flush with the ground surface. 
(2016)
 propose pitfall trap standardization methods.

## Flight-Intercept Traps

Flight-intercept traps block the flight-path of insects, forcing insects to either fly over the barrier, typically a vertical wall of very fine mesh, to escape or drop into a liquid-filled collection tray beneath the barrier. Collection trays, often trough-shaped, are partly filled with killing/preserving agents, the type of which will determine how often traps must be checked. Plastic tarps are frequently hung over the tops of these traps to prevent inundation of collection trays by rain 
(Singh et al. 2024)
.

## Malaise Traps

Malaise traps are also used for capturing insects in flight, particularly those species that fly upward when confronted with an obstacle. The open, tent-shaped Malaise trap uses a vertical wall of very fine mesh to funnel flying insects into a collection container at the top (ceiling) of the tent. These traps, which are modified flight-intercept traps, can also be combined with the traditional flight-intercept trap design by adding collection trays beneath 
(Montgomery et al. 2021)
.

## Light Traps

Light traps capitalize on the positive phototactic (i.e. being attracted to light) behavior of many insects. Trap designs are extremely variable from a light suspended in front of a white bed sheet to specialized traps for aquatic insects. Passive light traps typically include a collection container affixed below a funnel and/or radiating vanes situated below a light source 
(Montgomery et al. 2021)
. Light sources also vary with lightemitting diodes (LED) and Mercury vapor, UV, black light, and metal halide bulbs as options 
(Ferro and Summerlin 2019)
.

## Emergence Traps

Emergence traps are used to capture insects as they transition from immature stages to adults. These traps are often openbottomed, tent-shaped structures composed of fine mesh or other material and can be floated atop water for aquatic insect capture or placed over soil for terrestrial species. Modified versions may be built in-situ around vegetation and tree trunks 
(Grootaert et al. 2010)
. Most types also include a collection container situated at the top of the trap.

## Hester-Dendy Samplers

Hester-Dendy samplers are aquatic samplers composed of several stacked plates with stacked washers to vary the spacing between plates. These samplers may be deployed at different depths including lying on the streambed. Typically, samplers are suspended in the water column by floats and anchors for a few to several weeks to allow colonization by aquatic invertebrates.

## INLAND FISHES

Although freshwater fish SGCN research and monitoring in Louisiana have made great strides in recent years, there are still data gaps in occurrence, distribution, abundance, habitat preference, habitat availability, movement patterns, and life history. In addition to focused research projects, regular monitoring is necessary, as aquatic habitats face many disturbances and threats, with significant impacts from changes in hydrology, land use, connectivity, and climate patterns (see the Inland Fish Threats section). Hypoxia, amplified by heat, drought, and storms, is prevalent in south Louisiana, and monitoring the impacts to and recovery of fish populations is required for effective management. Up-to-date freshwater fish SGCN data are necessary for targeted conservation and restoration efforts, determining impacts of proposed infrastructure projects, aiding in Species Status Assessments for federally petitioned species, and other applications. In addition to fish data, water quality and other physical data of waters across the state must be monitored to inform the health of aquatic systems.
Patterns in threats to aquatic habitats and fish SGCN can be found across the state; however, there are differences in magnitude and scale in different types of water bodies.
Research is especially needed in large river systems to determine impacts of impoundments, navigation, and flood control projects. Headwater streams are sensitive habitats, and monitoring the effectiveness of conservation actions and impacts of flood control projects is needed. Coastal basins face unique threats from saltwater intrusion and diversions, and monitoring changes in species composition over time is critical in these dynamic environments. Researching the impacts of fish passage barriers across all habitats, with the aid of the Southeast Aquatic Resources Partnership's online support tools, is necessary to reconnect habitats and populations for more resilient aquatic communities.
LDWF conducts regular sport fish and water quality monitoring across the state, and SGCN data are recorded as they are caught, either during community sampling or as bycatch. Inland
Upon retrieval, users collect the entire sampler to ensure minimal loss of invertebrates. Later, the trap is rinsed into a collection container to isolate invertebrates from the sampler. The Florida Department of Environmental Protection produced the Methods of 
Hester-Dendy Multiplate Sampler Deployment (2006)
, which provides helpful suggestions on sampler design and deployment.

## Burlese-Tullgren Funnels

Burlese-Tullgren funnels provide an efficient method for extracting small arthropods from soil/leaf litter samples. Each sample should be stored in the field in separate breathable bags. The sample can be sieved to remove coarse debris such as leaves, sticks, and rocks during field collection or later. The sample is then placed on a screen at the bottom of a funnel which leads to a collection container filled with alcohol. A heat-producing light source placed above the funnel creates a temperature and desiccation gradient in the sample, which drives soil invertebrates from the sample into the collection container. Junod and colleagues 
(2023)
 offer an interesting alternative pitfall design that may outperform Burlese-Tullgren funnels in some instances.

## Acoustic Monitoring

Acoustic monitoring has a limited use in arthropod surveys. Grasshoppers, crickets, and cicadas, which have loud speciesspecific mating calls, are the most appropriate arthropod targets for acoustic monitoring 
(Montgomery et al. 2021)
. Use of autonomous recording units for data collection can minimize field time for personnel, but identification of species from recordings is extremely time-consuming. Increased efficacy may be obtained through machine learning but requires reference audio for training 
(Montgomery et al. 2021)
.

## Metabarcoding

Metabarcoding of eDNA shows significant promise in biodiversity monitoring of insects and other arthropods 
(Ruppert et al. 2019
, Van Klink et al. 2022
, Leandro et al. 2024
, van Klink et al. 2024)
.
Environmental DNA can be collected from samples of water, air, soil, vegetation, and several other substrates 
(Thomsen and Sigsgaard 2019
, Uchida et al. 2020
, Campbell et al. 2022
, Roger et al. 2022)
. Once extracted, the resulting DNA is "amplified, sequenced, and categorized based on its sequence" 
(Ruppert et al. 2019)
. Such analyses can yield identifications of taxa based on libraries of known DNA barcodes; species representation in these libraries varies widely among taxa and remains a challenge in metabarcoding studies 
(Leandro et al. 2024
). In addition, metabarcoding, although useful for determining species presence, is not especially helpful for determining species abundance, nor can it provide sex ratios or body condition 
(Ruppert et al. 2019)
. Nevertheless, metabarcoding remains a valuable tool for arthropod monitoring, particularly for "detecting the first occurrences of invasive species, the continued presence of native species thought to be extinct or otherwise threatened, and other elusive species occurring in low densities that would be difficult to detect by traditional means" 
(Ruppert et al. 2019)
. Metabarcoding is one type of eDNA method that can be used for non-crustacean arthropods. Other methods are available and may be used depending on research questions and data needs. When using eDNA techniques, users should consider the various factors that can degrade eDNA, cause contamination, or affect abundance in the environment such as ultraviolet radiation, temperature, turbidity, microbial activity, eDNA shed rate, and water chemistries 
(Eichmiller et al. 2016
, Jo et al. 2020
, Seymour et al. 2018)
.
Chapter 9: Monitoring Hoop Nets: LDWF staff use lead nets to survey for inland fishes.
Fisheries districts are encouraged to pursue independent research projects, which may focus on SGCN; however, research needs for freshwater fish SGCN are vast and can only be addressed with the combined efforts of state and federal agencies, universities, river authorities, and other research institutions. In addition to collaborating with the these entities, LDWF will continue to participate in partnerships such as the Mississippi Interstate Cooperative Resource Association, the Lower Mississippi River Conservation Committee, the Southeast Aquatic Resources Partnership, and the Louisiana Watershed Initiative to better research and monitor freshwater fish SGCN.
Staff members are also involved with the American Fisheries Society at all levels, which aids in collaboration and awareness of the leading edge of fisheries science.
Fisheries science is ever-improving, but has a suite of welldeveloped field methods for sampling and monitoring with origins going back to the Paleolithic Era, originally in the pursuit of food. Modern materials and technologies have expanded and improved on many of these techniques, but many gear types have changed relatively little in hundreds or thousands of years.
Because of the diversity of aquatic habitats and fish morphology and habits, many techniques require modifications to best catch target species such as using a backpack electrofisher combined with a riffle kick to capture fish in a seine set downstream or adding wings and leads to a hoop net.
Recent improvements in many techniques developed in the twentieth century have greatly expanded opportunities in freshwater fish research. Electrofishing has become more precise, with improved abilities to set power and digitally track various aspects of a sampling run. Fish movement monitoring has made great strides from radio telemetry to acoustic telemetry, which allows for greater geographic coverage, detection, precision, and data sharing. With digital data storage and sharing, fisheries data from museums, agencies, and universities from across the world can be easily accessed from a personal computer.
Genetic studies are now largely performed by analyzing single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs), leading to much higher resolution genetic detail than was previously available. In addition to advances in genetic techniques used on individual fish and populations, genetic metabarcoding techniques can aid in determining stomach contents and species presence in a water sample using eDNA. Genetic metabarcoding techniques are strong tools, but are not foolproof, and should be used in conjunction with, or to guide, more traditional fisheries sampling techniques.
Most fisheries methods are versatile and can be used for a wide variety of target species and habitats. Knowledge of species' habits, habitats, gear options, intended uses, and inherent biases are necessary for effective fish sampling. Detailed fisheries sampling methods can be found in 
Zale and colleagues (2012)
 and 
Bonar and colleagues (2024)
. A few methods commonly used in Louisiana are listed below.

## Seine Nets

Seine nets have a number of applications, based on target species, habitat features, size of net, and technique, and are intended to be used in wadeable waters. They are an active gear type and generally range in size from 10 to 50 feet long, with various mesh sizes, with or without a "bag," and come untreated or treated with net coating. Seine nets are typically pulled with the current but can be set downstream of riffles to perform a "riffle kick" or used as a block net while fish are flushed from holes or cut-banks. A seine net is one of the oldest and most versatile pieces of fishing equipment.

## Gill Nets

Gill nets are passive gear used to entangle fish from a set position. Modern gill nets are usually made of nylon monofilament, which comes in various strengths and can be twisted with multiple strands for added strength. Nylon multifilament (similar to twine) nets, also known as "gar nets," provide the most strength but are not suitable for most species. Gill nets can be set for an extended period (e.g., overnight), checked periodically (e.g., every two hours), or run as strike nets that are picked up quickly after herding fish into them with a boat. Trammel nets are gill nets with extra layers of webbing, adding increased opportunity for entanglement. Gill nets come in a variety of lengths, depths, webbing sizes, and float and weight configurations. The proper gill nets should be selected based on habitat, target species, and sampling methods.

## Hoop Nets

Hoop nets are a passive gear type consisting of a series of hoops, often made of fiberglass or steel, wrapped in a synthetic or metal net with a throat or series of throats at one end. They are fished horizontally pointing downstream, facing open water in lentic environments, or can be fished vertically in calm waters. Hoop nets can be fished with or without bait. This gear type is typically used to target catfish, but it is effective on a number of other types of benthic fishes, including suckers. There are also small hoop nets to target minnows and other small fish. Hoop nets can be modified with leads and walls to make lead nets and fyke nets, which can increase effectiveness.
Chapter 9: Monitoring 

## Electrofishing

Electrofishing is an active technique that usually targets small to medium sized fish, typically using direct current (DC), with a current flowing between a cathode (metal boat or "rat tail" on a backpack) and an anode (prod pole with metal ring or booms) to stun fish for capture. Modern electrofishing equipment offers multiple settings options to maximize effectiveness in different water conductivities on different types of fish. Most electrofishing in Louisiana is conducted from a boat-mounted or backpack unit, but other methods of electrofishing include shore shocking, tow barges, and prepositioned grids. Safety and technique training for this gear type is recommended and available free of cost from the USFWS (www. fws.gov/course/principles-and-techniques-electrofishing-online).

## Hook and Line

Hook and line sampling can be active or passive and is an acceptable method in certain circumstances. Hook and line sampling can take place in the form of rod and reel, set line, trotline, or yo-yo. Successful hook and line sampling requires a fish to find and attempt to consume bait. This method can be less effective than nets, traps, and electricity, but in certain circumstances, like catching sturgeon in deep flowing water, it may be the best option.

## Traps

There are also a number of traps designed for different species such as eel pots, minnow traps, and bream traps that can be used in a number of applications.

## Trawling

Trawling is an active netting method conducted from a boat. Trawls are only recommended in large inland waters where the possibility of snagging on submerged debris is minimal (e.g., in the Mississippi River). This can be an effective method of catching fish in fresh waters that are difficult to sample with the above methods.

## Collection of Habitat Data

Habitat data are critical to fisheries research and monitoring. Water quality, physical habitat, flow, and other habitat data are vital for understanding biotic conditions and should accompany biological research and monitoring. There are numerous approaches to taking habitat data including in-person readings, continuous monitoring gauges and probes, satellite data, and land use data that may be appropriate depending on the focus of the research.

## MARINE FISHES

The status of marine SGCN is closely related to habitat threats in the coastal ecosystem, especially marsh loss and degradation, and, therefore, these species may be some of the first to exhibit population declines. Habitat threats are at a critical level in the coastal zone. In response, LDWF Office of Fisheries prioritizes these habitat and has a habitat focused approach rather than a species focused approach. Data collected through this process provides indices of community structure within and across habitats and can show trends in population abundances by habitat type.
Fixed-location stations, stratified by habitat type, are established in each Coastal Study Area (CSA), and fishing gear appropriate to that station is used to collect physical, chemical, and biological data, as appropriate. Sampling gear is deployed and data are collected and recorded according to standard protocols.
The basic framework for marine/estuarine monitoring in Louisiana was established in 1968 with the Gulf-wide Cooperative Gulf of Mexico Estuarine Inventory (GMEI) and Study 
(Perret 1971
, Perret et al. 1971)
. The GMEI was further
Chapter 9: Monitoring refined with the implementation of the watershed-based CSA management system for penaeid shrimp 
(White and Boudreaux 1977)
, which was adapted for finfish monitoring in 1985. The general system for data collection established in 1968 has been used continuously since that time. Full details on the sampling protocol used and the updated inventory are available in the Louisiana Estuarine, Coastal, and Offshore Marine Species Inventory 
(Fischer et al. 2023)
. Projects rely on sampling with standardized gear over a range of habitats to characterize biological and environmental conditions. The focus of the GMEI and CSA projects is primarily to document and monitor the importance of Louisiana's estuaries as contributors to Gulf of America recreational and commercial fisheries. As a result, these projects have established long-term data sets for the various habitats and fish and invertebrate species in Louisiana's coastal habitats.
Many marine and estuarine species are understudied, and longterm trends in their abundance are seldom known. It will be necessary to identify methods to monitor and verify the status of cryptic species by documenting presence, habitat use, and life history characteristics. This type of monitoring must be in addition to and linked to standard monitoring used for more well-known species to validate trends of cryptic species.
Many conservation efforts are underway to protect, enhance, or modify coastal wetlands. These efforts will also affect the aquatic habitats and fauna associated with these coastal habitats, sometimes in ways that are not predicted or fully understood. Special purpose assessment and monitoring projects must be developed and maintained to assess the impact of these conservation efforts on both the terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems impacted.
Areas may be identified for habitat conservation or restoration through a variety of assessment procedures. Selection criteria may include species diversity (current or potential), unique nature of the habitat in the state or region, and areas recognized by previous national or state prioritization processes.

## AMPHIBIANS AND REPTILES

Various survey methodologies exist for studying amphibian and reptile populations. It is important to select the sampling design and techniques that are best suited for the target species and can address the desired question, while considering cost, equipment needs, seasonal activity, weather patterns, habitat characteristics, and other variables 
(Graeter 2013a)
. Regardless of the design, surveys should be standardized throughout the project duration and should be repeated as feasible.
Repeated surveys can aid in determining species' life history characteristics and can help address the variability in detections caused by seasonality, time of day, and weather conditions. By utilizing survey constraints and standardization guidelines, survey results can be compared both within and among projects utilizing a similar design 
(Graeter 2013a
).
The methods listed below are recommended, standard practices for monitoring amphibian and reptile populations.

## SAMPLING DESIGN

## Time-Constrained Designs

Time-constrained designs standardize the duration of a survey, typically in the form of surveying for a predetermined amount of time 
(Todd 2013a)
. Survey effort can be calculated by multiplying the predetermined amount of time by the number of surveyors. The number of species or the number of individuals per species can then be divided by the calculated survey effort; this will provide the number of species and/or relative abundance per person-hours. The survey effort should only include search time; any time taken to process individuals (e.g., photographing and measuring specimens) should be excluded from calculations. Time-constrained designs, however, may limit the thoroughness in which an area is surveyed; it can require a significant amount of time to adequately survey an area, and there is a potential for observer bias when using certain sampling methods. It is also important to note that in order to effectively compare survey results, the same techniques must be utilized in all subsequent surveys 
(Todd 2013a)
.

## Area-Constrained Designs

Area-constrained designs standardize the size of the survey area; surveys conclude when the entire area has been surveyed completely 
(Todd 2013b)
. The predetermined size of the survey area is primarily dependent on the habitat and the focal species. Survey areas can be designed to fit a desired time commitment but should be standardized throughout a particular project and between habitat types. The area-constrained design allows for estimations of species' densities and relative abundances per unit area as well as species' presence or absence. Similar to time-constrained designs, abiotic factors can greatly influence the results of area-constrained designs, and survey replication is required to provide the most valuable data. In addition, surveys should be conducted at multiple locations of the same habitat type within the project area. This helps ensure that survey results are representative of that habitat type. The area-constrained design also has limitations in that the survey areas must be searched thoroughly and with equal effort across multiple surveys 
(Todd 2013b)
.

## Transect Designs

Transect designs, which can be viewed as a type of areaconstraint, standardize a specific survey path 
(Fedewa 2013)
.
Transects can be conducted via straight lines or adapted, as necessary, to follow suitable habitat of target species (e.g., river shoreline). Surveyors are restricted to a specified path and are expected to search a standardized buffer zone that extends from the centerline of the transect path. This design can determine species' relative presence/absence, abundance, and densities across habitat types. Transect designs are particularly useful in assessing differences between habitat types (e.g., comparing upland to bottomland habitats) and determining the parameters that influence species' occupancy 
(Fedewa 2013)
.

## ACTIVE SAMPLING TECHNIQUES

Active sampling involves actively searching for target species and may be aided by tools that increase detection success. 

## Visual Encounter Survey

Visual Encounter Surveys (VES) involve visually searching for species via randomized-walk, quadrat, or transect methods and can be time-constrained and/or area-constrained 
(Glaudas 2013a)
. Efficacy varies depending on the target species and its associated habitat type. Species that regularly elude capture by passive techniques may be detected more readily with VES; however, for species that utilize dense vegetation or areas that make detection more difficult, other techniques may be needed to adequately assess their status. VES are used to determine species richness and abundance, but comparisons between VES can be challenging, which make survey replication and collection of abiotic information critical.

## Environmental DNA Surveys

Environmental DNA (eDNA) sampling is a time-efficient method to gather presence data on rare, threatened, and endangered species, specifically species that are difficult to detect with traditional techniques. These surveys are also useful in the early detection of invasive and non-native species and facilitation of invasive species management (NISC 2022). This sampling tool has been used primarily for aquatic species, including herpetofauna, to examine biodiversity in aquatic habitats 
(Takahashi et al. 2023)
. In recent years, this technique has been applied to species inhabiting terrestrial habitats, particularly for rare upland snakes 
(Katz et al. 2020)
. Sampling should occur during the active season for target species due to the seasonal fluctuations in eDNA release 
(Huang et al. 2022)
.

## Dip-Netting

Dip-nets can be used during VES to enhance a surveyor's ability to catch species in aquatic habitats 
(Todd 2013c)
. This tool may be used opportunistically or as part of a structured survey of habitats. 
(Tesche and Hodges 2015)
. To improve detection and capture success of some species, surveyors may conduct dipnetting at night, with a spotlight. When sampling similar sites, researchers should keep a tally of the number of net sweeps so that effort can be compared to other areas sampled in a similar manner 
(Lagler 1943)
.

## Basking Surveys and Basking Traps

Basking surveys are conducted using optical equipment to scan the environment for turtles that regularly use basking structures 
(Lindeman 1996
, Buhlmann 2013)
. Spotting scopes provide a greater viewing distance than binoculars, allowing for identification with less risk of flushing basking turtles back into the water. Basking surveys can be conducted on foot or by canoe or other suitable watercraft. This technique is particularly useful to assess species presence in water bodies where it may not be feasible to employ other survey techniques due to water flow, water depth, limited site accessibility, or life history traits incompatible with other techniques. Data on turtle species, number of turtles, age class (adult, juvenile), and sex can be recorded. If turtles have been previously marked using a highly visible method (e.g., waterproof spray paint), density estimates can also be calculated.
Basking traps are useful in areas where the water column is too deep for other trapping methods and for basking turtles that cannot be captured by baited traps. These traps are opentopped and constructed of mesh material (e.g., crayfish trap wire) in the shape of a small, medium, or large basket to fit various sizes of basking logs. Traps can be attached to basking logs with nails and twine or fitted with floats along the top portion of the basket and then attached to basking logs. Traps are then placed just under the water surface and adjacent to or underneath known or potential basking structures. Surveyors return to the site after a few minutes of settling time to prompt basking turtles to retreat into the traps; surveyors can then collect trapped turtles 
(Buhlmann 2013)
.
Presence of basking sites is necessary to perform either survey technique; however, it is important to note that although a lack of basking sites may be detrimental for some species, it is not an indicator of the absence of those species 
(Lindeman 1999)
.

## Detection Dogs

Detection dogs have assisted with wildlife research and management for centuries; there is renewed interest in the use of these dogs, especially for cryptic amphibians and reptiles.
Limitations to this technique include length of time to train, lifespan of the dog, personality of the dog, ability to secure an appropriate number of scent samples for training, lack of standardized protocols, and lack of understanding regarding degradation of scent in the wild. Despite the difficulties of training detection dogs, this technique can be vastly superior to some more traditional techniques. 
Kapfer and colleagues (2012)
 detected more individuals of Eastern Box Turtle (Terrapene carolina carolina) in nine hours with detection dogs than hundreds of hours of VES. Although there may be uncertainty concerning the detection of subterranean species, trained dogs have been shown to detect Eastern Indigo Snake (Drymarchon couperi) deep within Gopher Tortoise burrows in Georgia and Florida 
(Stevenson et al. 2010)
. 
Cablk and Heaton (2006)
 found that trained dogs were effective at detecting Desert Tortoise (Gopherus agassizii) and were also able to detect much smaller tortoises compared to human observers in similar studies. This technique shows great promise at detecting difficult-to-trap species and age classes. Mark-resight surveys have been used to estimate abundance and population size of basking freshwater turtles. Captured turtles are marked via a non-toxic, tree-marking spray paint on the 2nd and 3rd vertebral scutes of the carapace and released at the point of capture 
(Selman 2024)
. Basking resight surveys are conducted within two weeks of marking turtles 
(Selman 2024)
. Surveyors must capture an adequate number of turtles to use this survey method. Both mark-recapture and markresight surveys may be repeated to gather population data and assess trends in population size and demographics.

## Mark

## Radio Tracking

Radio tracking can provide data on movement patterns and survival over a longer timeframe than typical survey-based projects 
(Mullican 1988)
. For turtles, transmitters may be attached to the carapace via marine epoxy or bolted to the edge of the carapace. Harnesses can be used to attach transmitters to frogs, toads, and lizards. Some transmitters may be surgically implanted in other herpetofaunal species such as snakes.
Using a receiver and antenna, the location of the transmitter, and thus the individual, can be determined.
