The closer the receiver is to the transmitter, the louder the "chirp" detected by the receiver. Certain implanted transmitter types can record body temperature of individuals, with the temperature of the transmitter reflected by the frequency of "chirps" 
(White and
Garrott 1990, Carter et al. 1999)
.
When using tracking techniques, the potential impact of the attached tracking device on the individual should be considered.
If the attachment weighs too much or greatly increases visibility, the device may impede normal movement or behavior, both of Automated Recording Units: An automated recording unit (ARU) used to record calling frogs.
which can increase the likelihood of predation and bias data collected. In addition, it is also possible for an attachment to become entangled with environmental objects or impact the ability of individuals to reproduce 
(Graeter 2013b)
.

## Road Cruising Surveys

Road cruising involves surveying in a transect design where target species are known or presumed to occur 
(Andrews 2013)
. Detected individuals are often reported as Alive on Road (AOR) or Dead on Road (DOR; 
Smith and Dodd 2003)
. Habitat type, time of year, weather conditions, and other variables influence the species detected during these surveys 
(Andrews 2013)
. Therefore, it is beneficial to review available natural history information for the target species and baseline site data prior to conducting a road cruising survey 
(Tsaliagos 2013
). These surveys are most effective at detecting target species during peak activity times such as breeding season. Number of observers present and distance traveled should be recorded to determine effort and compare survey results with other similar surveys. This technique has several limitations 
(Case 1978)
.
Larger roads can present more of an obstacle to species and may reduce the likelihood that crossing attempts will be made.
In addition, traffic and road presence can alter the behavior of target species by attracting them for thermoregulation or deterring them because of noise 
(Boarman and
Sazaki 1996, Langen et al. 2015)
. This technique is not adequate for population sampling; therefore, it is best suited for determining species presence 
(Andrews 2013)
.

## Aerial Surveys

Aerial surveys that utilize Unmanned Aircraft Systems (UAS) such as drones to document basking and nesting sites in or near aquatic habitats is a relatively novel technique. This method is most applicable in areas that are visually open such as grasslands, sandbars, and Longleaf Pine savannas 
(Glaudas 2013b)
. Transects are the most appropriate survey design for this technique with flight altitude, speed, and width of area sampled (including field of view of camera) remaining constant to allow for comparability 
(Caughley 1974)
. UAS should remain in view of the observers at all times. Observer bias may have a large impact on detection probability, and although this technique is inadequate at estimating population size, it can assist in determining the need for further sampling using more standard techniques 
(Caughley 1974
, Glaudas 2013b
).
In addition, the use of UAS is heavily regulated by the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA). Those wishing to use this technique should review current FAA regulations.
Fixed-wing aerial surveys are useful for locating evidence of nesting sea turtles, particularly for remote beaches and barrier islands that are inaccessible by foot or land vehicle 
(Schroeder and Murphy 1999)
. This survey method can be standardized to evaluate trends in nesting activity, which would provide insight for management and coastal restoration activities 
(Lamont et al. 2023)
. A seaplane flies the length of the survey area at a low altitude to allow surveyors to detect sea turtle crawls as well as capture photographs to subsequently confirm species identification and nesting activity (e.g., presence of body pits).
If feasible, the seaplane lands nearshore to allow surveyor(s) to gather measurements for each crawl and body pit as well as distance to high tide line and vegetation 
(Lamont et al. 2023)
. Sea turtles typically nest during nighttime hours, therefore, surveys are conducted during early morning while crawls are still intact and less likely affected by weather events.

## PASSIVE SAMPLING TECHNIQUES

Monitoring methods such as the active techniques above are effective means of evaluating population status but require considerable effort to implement. With passive technology, it is feasible to gather quality data that will inform the status and trends of various SGCN in Louisiana while reducing the demand on staff. Passive sampling involves techniques that trap or passively detect amphibians and reptiles. Some techniques collect and store data that can be processed at a time more convenient for the researcher. Passive sampling techniques can be easily standardized, minimizing potential bias, and are ideal for long-term monitoring. Results can yield species' relative abundance and, when paired with mark-recapture studies, can inform population demographics 
(Willson 2013)
. Steps should be taken to minimize negative impacts to bycatch, or nontarget animals.

## Automated Recording Units

Automated Recording Units (ARUs) are commonly used to determine the presence and distribution of vocal taxa such as frogs. ARUs are installed within or on the periphery of wetlands to capture the calls of breeding amphibians. Surveys are conducted during the breeding season when individuals can be identified by their unique vocalizations. Data are analyzed by manually listening to audio files to identify species or via bioacoustics software. This software uses machine learning to assist in identification of species by their unique vocalizations.
Chapter 9: Monitoring Aquatic Funnel Traps: Hoop nets used to survey for aquatic turtles.

## PVC Pipe Surveys

A polyvinyl chloride (PVC) pipe can be placed vertically in the ground or attached to standing structures (e.g., trees) to act as artificial refugia for frogs and lizards. Researchers can later remove and process individuals that seek refuge within the pipes. This inexpensive and easily implemented technique is useful for determining the presence and distribution of target species in a particular area. Capture probability between individuals using the artificial refugia (PVC) compared to natural refugia should be considered for studies evaluating abundance 
(Glorioso and Waddle 2014)
.

## Artificial Cover

Artificial cover may be used to detect species' presence/ absence. Cover objects include, but are not limited to, plywood, carpet sections, and sheet metal placed in systematic arrays to increase species detection 
(Mills et al. 2013)
. During sampling, surveyors can quickly lift one side of the object to look for species underneath before escape. Artificial cover objects can be used in conjunction with VES.

## Drift Fences

The use of drift fences, which act as barriers to the movement of species, is not a stand-alone technique; instead, drift fences are used to improve the capture or detection success of other passive techniques. Species attempting to circumnavigate the fence are directed into a trap such as a pitfall or funnel trap. Researchers can later remove and process individuals from the traps. When using PIT tag readers or camera traps, the individuals are directed into a detection field 
(Enge 1997)
 where data are passively collected to be reviewed and analyzed at a later date. There are numerous ways to arrange traps along drift fences. Often, fences are arranged in a "Y" or "+" shape with traps at the central point, fence ends, or along the fence. Placement will largely be determined by trap type, trap availability, and target species. Aquatic drift fences can have weights along the bottom of the fence and floats along the top to create a barrier in the entire water column; however, this may not be feasible in deeper water bodies. Aquatic barriers are typically made of woven, nylon mesh or similar material, but more options are available for terrestrial barriers such as silt-fencing, hardware cloth, and aluminum flashing (Metts and Graeter 2013). Transparent materials, particularly when used as terrestrial fences, should be avoided. These transparent fences may cause species to repeatedly attempt to penetrate the barrier, risking injury 
(Douglas et al. 1994
, Willson 2013
). Due to the climbing and digging abilities of some species, terrestrial fences should be buried deeply enough and extend high enough to deter target species from easily circumnavigating these fences 
(Jackson et al. 2015)
.

## Aquatic Funnel Traps

Aquatic funnel traps have a conical or flat opening that leads into the body of the trap. Inside the trap, the funnel is positioned to reduce escape; usually the funnel is raised from the floor of the trap or extends away from the edges of the trap towards the center. Many additional modifications exist to reduce escape (e.g., flaps and additional funnels). Aquatic funnel traps include, but are not limited to, hoop-nets, crab traps, minnow traps, collapsible nylon traps, pyramid traps, and crayfish traps. When using aquatic traps, a portion of the trap must be exposed above water so that captured individuals are able to surface for air. The potential for drowning makes regular trap checks imperative 
(Willson 2013)
. Replicate surveys should use traps of similar size and mesh and use the same bait for data comparison.

## Terrestrial Traps and Pitfalls

Numerous designs for terrestrial traps exist. Many designs employ funnels that lead into a holding area that is usually in the shape of a box consisting of wire mesh (e.g., hardware cloth) and wood (e.g., plywood). When paired with drift fences, this technique is excellent at detecting many snake and lizard species. Treadle traps are useful for surveying species like Gopher Tortoise, mainly utilized at tortoise burrow entrances to catch the resident tortoise 
(Enge et al. 2012)
. A hinged door acts as the entry point, and when the weight of the tortoise is placed on a treadle in the back portion of the trap, the door closes. The basic design of a pitfall trap involves burying a container to its rim in the substrate, often at the ends of drift fences (Metts and Graeter 2013). As animals attempt to circumnavigate the fence, they fall into the container and are captured for processing. The size of the container can be adjusted to target specific species and age classes. Care should be taken when utilizing any of the aforementioned traps, as individuals can be subjected to dangerous temperatures, predation, inundation, and other hazardous conditions. Researchers can provide a source of water and a form of shelter (e.g., shade cloth on traps) to reduce stress.
In the case of pitfalls, containers can have perforated bottoms to allow draining or be closed off during periods of inundation. In addition, all traps should be checked at appropriate intervals to prevent mortality 
(Burke 1989
, Enge 2001)
.

## Camera Traps

Camera traps can reduce time-consuming field work but still gather quality data that can inform the presence and distribution of SGCN in Louisiana 
(Adams et al. 2017)
. Currently, three types of cameras are available for monitoring wildlife: time-lapse, active infrared, and passive infrared. Time-lapse cameras take photos at specified time intervals. An active infrared camera emits an infrared beam, which functions like a trip-wire, photographing the animal as it breaks the beam.
A passive infrared camera detects temperature changes in the sensor's field of view; temperature changes trigger the camera to take photographs. In order for passive infrared cameras to be effective for ectothermic species such as amphibians and reptiles, researchers need to make sure that detection areas have a uniform temperature gradient to allow sensors to differentiate between animals and the background 
(Welbourne 2013)
. Animals can be directed into the camera's field of view by drift fences 
(Swann and Perkins 2014
, Hobbs and Brehme 2017
, Martin et al. 2017
, Richardson et al. 2017)
. Time-lapse cameras take many photographs and require researchers to spend significant amounts of time sorting through photographs that may or may not actually capture an animal; however, advancements in machine learning may help address this challenge. Infrared cameras take significantly fewer photographs but can potentially miss species' detections by triggering too quickly, too slowly, or not triggering at all. All cameras require maintenance, but, often, one visit every two months to replace batteries and memory cards may suffice.

## BIRDS

Bird monitoring techniques vary widely based on the monitoring objectives, species or guild involved, and the dependent variable of interest. For abundance, density, and similar endpoints, point counts (fixed-or unlimited-radius), transects (line or strip), area searches, and spot-mapping are among the most commonly employed survey techniques. Many of these countbased survey techniques may be modified (e.g., using distance sampling) to allow estimation of detectability, which may be used statistically to increase confidence in measurements.
In addition, passive monitoring techniques, such as passive acoustic monitoring, various forms of telemetry, and additional remote sensing techniques offer increasingly sophisticated tools as technology advances. For demographic variables such as nesting productivity, recruitment, or survival, constant-effort mist-netting or nest monitoring techniques represent standard approaches. Species-(or guild-) specific survey techniques have been developed in special cases, typically based on one of the aforementioned survey concepts, to increase detectability based on unique life history or behavior attributes (e.g., for nocturnal or secretive species). Note that all monitoring techniques have limitations and inherent strengths and weaknesses and that any given technique should be selected within the framework of specific objectives, sampling design, funding availability, and other project-specific factors. Summarizing the details, pros and cons, and technical aspects of the many bird monitoring techniques available is beyond the scope of this document, but a rich literature is available for guidance (e.g., 
Gregory et al. 2004
).
Birds represent the most widely studied animal group in North America; they are engaging, embraced by the public, widespread, and, generally, easy to detect, identify, and count (at least relative to other taxonomic groups). Because bird populations also respond to ecological and environmental changes, birds serve as ideal ecological indicators, and, as a result, a number of standardized, long-term bird monitoring programs exist. Some of these programs are continental in scale and have been in place for many years, while others are smaller in scale (e.g., state-or region-specific) or are specific to a particular species or guild.
Below is an overview of select monitoring programs for which LDWF is a partner or that are otherwise recognized as important in the conservation and management of Louisiana birds. 

## North

## Colonial Waterbird Surveys

LDWF has used a number of methods to survey colonial waterbirds over the years but has typically relied on aerial surveys using list frame sampling. In this approach, observers survey known colonies and new colonies as they are discovered while flying between known colonies, estimating the number of nests (or pairs) for each species at each colony. These and R.G. Ford Consulting Company (Colibri) to conduct colonial waterbird surveys using aerial nest photography. These surveys provide high-resolution and georeferenced photographs of all nests in a colony, with incubating adults identified (manually marked, or "dotted," on the digital images by human experts) and enumerated by species, which provides a high level of accuracy and repeatability in both the number of nesting birds and the localities of their nests. Nest photographic surveys are conducted periodically both coast-wide, using a list of known and newly discovered colonies, as described previously, and at individual colonies for restoration project-specific performance monitoring. See: 
https://avianmonitoring.com
.

## Motus Wildlife Tracking System (Motus)

Motus is a collaborator-driven, automated radio telemetry network of passive-monitoring receiving stations that allows for tracking the movements of migratory birds carrying miniature radio tags ("nanotags"). Coordinated by Birds Canada, Motus processes, archives, and makes available detection data collected by the network's receiving stations. Because nanotags are digitally coded, many individual birds can be tracked simultaneously on the same radio frequency and thus be detected by receiving stations maintained by collaborators anywhere in the network. As a result, Motus is revolutionizing the tracking of small, flying animals, including insects, bats, and small-bodied migratory birds, allowing researchers to address key challenges in full annual cycle biology such as identification of important stopover sites, migration routes, post-breeding dispersal, and overwinter survival 
(Taylor et al. 2017)
. LDWF maintains a network of Motus stations across Louisiana. See: 
https://motus.org
.

## Global Positioning System (GPS)/Global System for Mobile Communications (GSM) Telemetry

This technology may be utilized to study movement ecology of birds ranging in size from Blue-winged Teal (Spatula discors) to Greater White-fronted Geese (Anser albifrons). These lightweight (3-38 g), solar-powered devices collect highly accurate GPS locations (e.g., 3-5 m accuracy) at intervals as frequently as 15 minutes and transmit the data twice daily thru GSM/cellular networks. When not in service, data are stored on-board and later transmitted when birds reenter cellular service areas. These devices are also capable of collecting tri-axial accelerometer data, which can be used to determine a bird's behavior at the specific location (e.g., walking, flying [altitude and ground speed are also recorded], and sleeping). These data provide unprecedented insight into all life stages of ecology including nest initiation, incubation, brood rearing, molting, migration, winter and breeding site fidelity, use of habitats, survival, daily movements, energy landscapes, cross-seasonal effects, and others. These devices continue to miniaturize as technology improves, which provides exciting opportunities for smaller birds.

## Autonomous Recording Units (ARU)

The conspicuous vocal behavior of many bird species maximizes their detectability, particularly during the breeding season, making them ideal for large-scale monitoring programs (e.g., BBS). Vocal behavior, however, often varies dramatically with season, time of day, weather conditions, and other factors, which affects birds' detectability and thus the most appropriate survey protocol. Further, species that are rare, occur at low density, are secretive in nature, or occur in remote areas or inaccessible habitats tend to be difficult to detect by traditional avian monitoring protocols and are, thus, typically under-represented in widely available monitoring data sets. Recent advances in battery life, digital storage capacity, and programmable scheduling of ARUs are revolutionizing the ability to detect those aforementioned species via passive acoustic monitoring. This approach involves deployment of ARUs to "listen" for target species over long time periods or during repeated survey periods, at any time of day or night, and then allows the biologist to download and analyze sound files at a later date.
eBird eBird is a community science platform and online database managed by the Cornell Lab of Ornithology that documents bird distribution, abundance, and trends. The platform's popularity with the birding public (>100 million bird sightings contributed annually) ensures a constant supply of data with the ability for users to track crowdsourced species' occurrences in near real time. Protocols that maximize data quality on the front end, combined with statistical tools to minimize bias (e.g., spatial, temporal, observer) on the back end, result in scientifically valuable data products representing heretofore unprecedented resources for monitoring, conservation, and management. eBird data, for example, are increasingly used as components of largescale monitoring efforts (e.g., USFWS 2020) and population status and trend analysis (e.g., 
Rosenberg et al. 2019)
. LDWF frequently uses eBird observation data and eBird data science products to inform monitoring priorities, conservation and management needs, and public outreach. See: 
https://ebird.org
.

## Other Surveys

In addition to the ongoing and routine survey efforts described above, LDWF and partners conduct additional types of monitoring on a less frequent or as-needed basis, including Bald Eagle nest productivity surveys, Swallow-tailed Kite roost surveys, International Piping Plover Census, and beach-nesting bird surveys, among others.

## MAMMALS

Methods of detecting each of the mammal SGCN vary greatly because of the huge breadth of ecological niches that mammals occupy. Monitoring should be reliable, standardized, and easily repeatable so that results are comparable over time and across regions; unfortunately, for many mammals, established protocols are lacking. Working groups such as those for Eastern Spotted Skunk, weasels, and others are attempting to address some of these challenges. For many weasel species, targeted surveys are relatively new with most historical detections coming from trapping records or incidental sightings. Therefore, much of the current research is focused on testing methods to maximize detection. Once such methods are determined, they can be utilized to help address key data gaps.
Trapping is required for projects that mark animals, affix VHF or GPS collars, assess health or condition of the animal, or collect tissue samples. If trapping, researchers should check traps at an appropriate frequency to limit stress and reduce the risk of mortality. In addition, traps should be in locations protected from the sun, weather exposure, flooding, and potential predators, whenever possible 
(Silvy et al. 2020a)
. Researchers handling mammals should consider being up-to-date on rabies vaccinations.
Baits and lures have been used throughout history to increase mammal captures and can be paired with camera traps or more traditional, passive or active capture techniques. When used appropriately, baits can enhance trap success, but the efficacy of baiting is understudied. The type of bait needed to increase detections is often unknown, and the rate of decay of the bait can influence success.
The list of methods below is not meant to be comprehensive but to serve as a guide to methods that are currently used for monitoring mammal SGCN in Louisiana.

## Mark-Recapture

Mark-recapture is a traditional method of gathering data on population dynamics, movement, behavior, and density. Surveyors can use a variety of methods to mark mammals, including ear tags, Passive Integrated Transponder (PIT) tags, collars, tissue removal, tattoos, branding, claw clipping, fur removal, and fur dye. Researchers must consider the length of time the mark will last and if the tag will cause discomfort or changes in behavior or survivorship 
(Silvy et al. 2020b
). In addition, genetic data can be gathered from marking methods that utilize tissue removal.

## Monitoring Devices

Wearable VHF or GPS remote monitoring devices, often affixed to collars, are also integral for monitoring movement and behavior of wildlife 
(Silvy and Catanach 2020)
. In addition to determining home range size and travel corridors, collars can provide data on mortality and survivorship. The main limitation of these collars was battery life and transmitter size. However, advances in cellular, battery, and GPS technologies have resulted in a longer battery life with transmitter options that have a wide array of applications for nearly every species of mammal. The main limitation now lies in equipment costs ranging from several hundreds of dollars to several thousands of dollars per device.

## Traps

Cage traps, box traps, pitfall traps, foothold traps, and cable restraints are necessary tools for researchers conducting markrecapture studies or affixing VHF or GPS devices to individuals. Baited culvert traps, a cylindrical cage trap with a door that closes when triggered, can be used to capture and translocate Louisiana Black Bears (USFWS 1995). Small treadle traps are excellent for sampling small mammals like rodents; however, these small box traps often under-sample some small mammal species, specifically shrews and voles 
(Torre et al. 2010)
. For under-sampled species and semi-fossorial species, a pitfall trap, a simple container buried to its lip in the substrate, is effective 
(Umetsu et al. 2006)
. Foothold traps and cable restraints are useful for capturing larger mammals and come in a variety of sizes and modifications to best sample target species 
(Silvy et al. 2020a
, Turnbull et al. 2011)
. Researchers interested in using foothold traps or cable restraints should attend a training workshop and consult additional literature for BMPs in an effort to reduce the chance of injury to animals (AFWA 2022).
Regardless of the technique used, surveyors should take care to reduce stress on captured animals by minimizing exposure to hazardous conditions and by checking traps frequently. When using pitfall traps, containers can become flooded with water and drown individuals; closing containers during periods of inundation or perforating the bottom to allow draining can help avoid this issue 
(Silvy et al. 2020a
). Invasive fire ants will often prey upon small mammals caught in traps. Ant insecticide reduces mortality resulting from ant predation, and its use does not affect capture yield of small mammals; users should make sure to follow label instructions 
(Mitchell et al 1996)
. Surveyors should clean and decontaminate traps between uses to reduce presence of scent, ensure moving parts are clean and functioning properly, and minimize disease transfer 
(Silvy et al. 2020a)
.

## Bat Capture Methods

Mist nets, harp traps, and hand nets can be used to capture bats in flight near roosts, flyways, or foraging grounds. Mist nets are fine nylon or polyester mesh netting extended between two poles. As bats fly, they are unable to detect the netting, subsequently flying into it and falling into a baggy portion below for collection. When selecting mist nets, researchers need to consider mist net height, mesh size, and denier/ply size. 
Ferreira and colleagues (2021)
 found that traditional mist nets (length: 12 m, height: 2.5 m, mesh: 16 mm, deniers/ply: 75/2) had a 1.5 times greater capture rate of bats than more modern, ultrathin mist nets (length: 12 m, height: 3.2 m, mesh: 20 mm, denier/ply: 45/1). Harp traps are A-framed stands with vertical strings and a collection area at the bottom. As bats turn vertically in flight to avoid the strings, they lose lift and fall into the collection area. These methods are particularly useful when researchers want to collect data on morphometric characteristics and genetics.
Harp traps can be more effective at capturing bats than mist nets but are smaller, which restricts their use to roost entrances and natural flyways 
(Ferreira et al. 2021)
. Researchers should check mist nets and harp traps at least once every 10 minutes but should remove individuals immediately when captured, if possible (USFWS 2024, 
Kurta and Kunz 1988)
. Users must exercise extra precautions while removing bats from nets as their narrow wing bones are easily broken 
(Kurta and Kunz 1988)
.

## Disease Monitoring

Although many diseases pose threats to mammal SGCN, few have had as widespread an impact as White-nose Syndrome (WNS), the disease caused by the fungus Pseudogymnoascus destructans (Pd). This fungus is a predominant threat to many bat species, and as such, monitoring for its presence is a priority; 

## Citizen Science

The wide use of hand held cameras, particularly cell phones, offers the opportunity for outreach through citizen science projects. Members of the public can report sightings on platforms such as iNaturalist (www.inaturalist.org). In addition, notices on social media platforms can alert members of the public of a researcher's interest in cryptic mammal sightings. For example, informational posters were created for Eastern Spotted Skunk and distributed through various media outlets in an effort to educate members of the public and to provide information on how to report sightings. Such reports from the public are an important part of monitoring marine mammals, as members of the public working or recreating along the coast may be more likely to spot these species.

## Point-count Surveys

Point-count surveys, which involve researchers counting each individual animal in an area, are useful in monitoring easily detectible species. For example, surveyors can easily count muskrat colonies in coastal marsh which can provide an index of densities and distribution 
(Sadowski and
Bowman 2021, Mullin and
Hoggard 2000)
. Researchers can count individual bats in roosts or as they emerge 
(Anderson and LaCour 2022)
. Ideally, such data would be gathered consistently over long periods of time so that fluctuations can be discerned.

## Detection Dogs

Although 

## Environmental DNA

Organisms shed DNA into their environment and researchers can use assays to detect this eDNA in samples. A key limitation of eDNA studies is that they merely detect the presence of DNA and not the actual organism. However, this technique is particularly useful in informing researchers on where to target on the ground surveys to confirm the existence of the organisms. The two more commonly used methods are Quantitative PCR assays and metabarcoding. Quantitative PCR is best for targeting a single species, while metabarcoding is traditionally used for sampling a broad range of taxa groups 
(Wood et al. 2019)
. When using eDNA techniques, users should consider the various factors that can degrade eDNA or affect abundance in the environment such as ultraviolet radiation, temperature, and microbial activity, eDNA shed rate, and water chemistries 
(Eichmiller et al. 2016
, Jo et al. 2020
, Seymour et al. 2018)
.

## PLANTS

Inventory and monitoring of rare plants are essential actions required for protecting and furthering our knowledge of plant SGCN. By consistently tracking population trends, distribution, and habitat conditions, we can identify threats, assess conservation status, determine priorities, and implement effective management strategies for conserving sensitive species. Systematically sampling a population for metrics such as frequency, density, and percent cover can provide insights into the viability of a particular species. Analyzing these data over time can allow us to identify threats and ameliorate their impacts on plant SGCN.
The inventory process often begins with review of herbarium specimens, biodiversity databases (e.g., NatureServe), and citizen science applications (e.g., iNaturalist). Detailed information such as exact location, population size, and habitat metrics can provide insight into the status and desired conditions for a particular species. Ideally, utilizing habitat data is most effective when research definitively links specific habitat conditions to target plant species. Since this information is limited for many rare plant species, hypothesized habitat associations derived from population data and known ecological relationships can often guide survey efforts for discovering new occurrences.
Chapter 9: Monitoring
An LDWF botanist conducts vegetation surveys.

## NATURAL COMMUNITIES

Tracking and consistent monitoring of natural communities allows LDWF to determine the status, distribution, and overall ecological integrity of these communities and to identify changes in these attributes over time. These data are crucial for developing effective management strategies to conserve and restore these critical ecosystems.
Utilizing remote sensing technology can be an efficient and cost-effective way to conduct preliminary surveys and to further refine or constrain survey areas when resources are limited. Remotely sensed data are useful for locating and monitoring natural communities that have distinct associations with characteristic soil types, elevation and topography, specific landscape positions, or other natural patterns. Once a survey area has been constrained to a manageable size, field surveys can become more focused and frequently become more productive. Remote sensing methods commonly used include:

## Light Detection And Ranging (LiDAR)

LiDAR sensors provide visualization of small elevation changes through the use of light in the form of a pulsed laser. This method is useful for differentiating areas that still retain natural surface topography (e.g., distinguishing Coastal Prairie remnants with pimple mounds and potholes from land-leveled agricultural lands and pastures). LiDAR data can provide important hydrologic information that can be used to identify existing, former, and restored hydrologically driven communities such as wetlands. These data can also be used to determine structure and species composition in several community types including forests with high canopy cover and marsh communities.

## True Color Aerial Imagery

This method uses images captured via aerial photography to make preliminary observations of an area remotely and assess large-scale changes in structural features over time. For example, tracking changes to woody cover in prairie ecosystems can be used to evaluate whether a habitat is moving toward favorable or unfavorable conditions and if desired management goals are being achieved.

## Infrared Imagery

This method uses instruments and cameras that detect and translate electromagnetic radiation beyond the visible light spectrum. Infrared imagery can allow for a better understanding of plant species, biomass, soil moisture, and water turbidity that true color aerial imagery may not be able to provide. For example, Field surveys that track and monitor species populations are essential to keep the status of plant SGCN up-to-date. These surveys can either be conducted as standardized routine assessments or incidental assessments. Common methods for tracking and monitoring rare plants are included below:

## Meander Survey

This method is often used for detecting plants with known or hypothesized habitat associations. Surveys are conducted at sites with ideal habitat for a target species during the most optimal time of year for detection and typically focus on habitat features that are commonly associated with these species (e.g., surveying along a stream for obligate riparian species). Upon locating the species, data are collected on various metrics including number of individuals and their life stage, areal extent, plant and habitat conditions, associated species, soil, elevation, and other pertinent details. By employing this method, larger areas can be efficiently surveyed.

## FIREMON Rare Species

In this method, developed as part of the Fire Effects Monitoring and Inventory System, individual plants are studied over time to quantify temporal changes in survivorship, growth, and reproduction for rare, perennial species 
(Lutes et al. 2006)
.
Plants are spatially located using distance relative to a physical baseline and marked with permanent tags. Over time, data are collected on the status, life stage, size, and reproductive effort for individual plants and can be tailored to the species of interest.
The response of rare plants cannot always be directly tied to management intervention. Along with habitat conditions, a matrix of factors must be considered including presence of invasive species, disease, herbivory/trampling, and changes to climatic conditions. In addition, certain species are more difficult to monitor such as plants with an annual life cycle, as their populations can fluctuate significantly from year to year, compared to more stable perennial species. Molecular methods such as utilizing eDNA for detecting and monitoring more cryptic species are in development and may prove useful (see 
Hartvig et al. 2021)
. Analyzing topographic maps, soils maps, and reviewing archived field data (e.g., historical land surveys, herbarium specimens, field notes) are also used to locate potential occurrences of target natural communities. Remote sensing may be followed up with ground-truthing to verify findings and collect more detailed data.
Natural community field monitoring may involve quantitative or qualitative assessments performed by a botanist or plant ecologist, who inspects a site prior to and during implementation of management activities. The biologist then determines if the natural community is responding to management interventions or if additional action is necessary. For example, a Flatwoods Pond may become overrun with invasive plant species. Through visual inspection, the extent of the infestation can be determined, and biologists can recommend adaptive management actions as needed.
More intensive monitoring for hypothesis-driven research can involve quantitative sampling. In order to obtain a comprehensive ecological assessment of a natural community, it is prudent to collect and test soil samples and, in some cases, to measure elevation and other abiotic factors such as slope percent and aspect. Because natural communities are typically defined by vegetation, evaluation of factors such as floristic quality, plant functional groups, species richness, and diversity is also essential to understanding community quality and condition. This can be achieved by conducting vegetation surveys recording metrics such as species frequency, percent cover, basal area, and density. Many vegetation sampling protocols are available, and potential modifications can be made based on the specific site and questions being addressed. Examples and brief descriptions of a few of these methods include:

## Quadrat

Quadrat sampling is a highly variable method that should be customized appropriately to the plant community being sampled and the information that is being collected. A quadrat is a frame, varying in size and shape, placed on the area to be sampled. Within the quadrat, metrics of abundance including species frequency, percent cover, density, and basal area may be quantified and used to calculate species richness and diversity.
To adequately sample a community, quadrat samples must be performed repeatedly in areas chosen through random, systematic, or other standardized methods.
Chapter 9: Monitoring 

## Profile Board

Employing a profile board or other tool (e.g., Robel Pole, forage stick, density board, cover board) is a simple, efficient, and common technique used for quantifying vegetation vertical height and horizontal cover and structure. Through this method, horizontal cover is measured by placing a board (vertically, with measurement markings) a set distance or distances from the observer. Through ocular estimations, the observer determines the height of vegetation and the percent of the board obstructed by vegetation. Quantitative data gathered with this method can be used to better assess changes in vegetative structure and response to management action or environmental factors over extended periods of time. This method is often coupled with photo-point monitoring.

## Photo-Point

Photo-point monitoring is also an effective and simple standardized method to capture changes in vegetative structure and landscape conditions over time. This method consists of taking baseline photographs of the location(s) of interest and returning at specified time intervals to take additional, follow-up photographs. These follow-up photographs must be taken from the same location(s) as the baseline photograph(s) and within the same field of view as the baseline photograph(s). With strict adherence to protocol and appropriate documentation, photopoint monitoring can be precisely replicated by different people many years apart, allowing for quantitative and qualitative assessments.

## Bitterlich

This method uses a sighting device to measure basal area and canopy cover. From randomly located sampling points, the observer views shrubs or trees using a sighting device (e.g., densiometer) with a fixed viewing angle and a wedge prism to calculate basal area. Only shrubs and trees with diameters larger than the specified angle are to be included in the count. The Bitterlich method is a time-efficient way to quantify stand density and overstory structure. These gains, generated through active collaboration between engineers and ecologists (see detailed example below), have the capacity to inform future avian habitat restoration and enhancement, adaptive management, and performance monitoring. Further, when integrated within CPRA's ecological review process, these and future activities will provide realworld means by which CPRA and its partners can easily convey resource benefits realized to resource stakeholders with emphasis placed on the general public.
In 2004, the Coastwide Reference Monitoring System-Wetlands (CRMS) was implemented to improve the program's capability to evaluate the effectiveness of individual restoration projects as well as the combined effects of multiple projects by providing a network of reference sites to compare to project sites 
(Steyer et al. 2003)
. CRMS was also designed to determine the ecological condition of all Louisiana wetlands, not just those affected by projects. As a result, CRMS provides the user an opportunity to evaluate restoration efforts on the ecosystems as a whole. Thus, the effectiveness of landscape-scale restoration strategies can also be determined.
Detailed Example: Suitability of habitats for marsh-nesting birds can be influenced by elevation heterogeneity, land and water interspersion, hydrologic connectivity, vegetation characteristics, and proximity to human activity. The incorporation of these target habitat characteristics into existing project design, construction, maintenance, and related items in CPRA Marsh Creation Design Guidelines (CPRA 2017) has the potential to create high value habitat for wetland-dependent wildlife species including marsh-nesting birds.
Integration of the CRMS network plays a critical role in LDWF's and CPRA's capacity to understand how coastal processes influence biological outcomes. In one case, the Secretive Marsh Bird Habitat Relationships and Distributions in Selected Coastal Louisiana Marshes Project (2018-2025) designated all sampling stations be co-located to established CRMS stations throughout the project's geographic extent (Pontchartrain to Terrebonne coastal basins). In doing so, resource agencies are moving towards a more comprehensive (quantitative versus qualitative) means by which future coastal marsh projects can be designed, constructed, and adaptively managed to generate real-world benefits for marsh-nesting birds.

## SCIENTIFIC RESEARCH AND COLLECTING PERMITS

LDWF issues Scientific Research and Collecting Permits to individuals conducting valid scientific studies on wildlife, including SGCN, in Louisiana. Each permit holder is required to submit an annual report detailing the outcome of field efforts and the numbers and types of organisms impacted. Those permitted to work with SGCN must also submit additional data such as precise location information (i.e. GPS coordinates). These reports are submitted to the Wildlife Diversity Program Data Manager and relevant taxa expert. In instances where a federally listed species is encountered, USFWS staff are also notified per reporting requirements of LDWF's Section 6 Cooperative Agreement. SGCN data from permit reports are entered into the Biotics 5 database, which can be used to inform S-ranks, SGCN status, species distribution, and more. LDWF also uses a variety of other methods to ensure the effectiveness of conservation actions and the efficacy of projects. h SWG interim annual and final reports are reviewed by LDWF taxa experts who provide feedback and request any needed edits or additional information. This level of review ensures that data are collected at appropriate times and locations, that conservation measures are taken to ensure incidental take is minimized or avoided, and that all actions are informed by the best available information. h Pilot studies are conducted by LDWF biologists to test new approaches or tools for species detections. These studies are a fraction of the cost and require less time than undertaking a full study. h Prioritization of monitoring projects is based off staff biologist's participation on regional, national, and international working groups; USFWS listing workplans; species petitioned for federal listing; funding opportunities; data gaps; and additional consultation with academic experts. h Collaboration with counterparts in other states allows LDWF biologists to receive information on successful, novel survey methods. Frecklebelly Madtom, for example, was a species that benefitted from a partnership between LDWF and the Mississippi Department of Wildlife, Fisheries, and Parks. This collaboration resulted in a listing not warranted determination by the USFWS after novel survey techniques were implemented by both states leading to successful survey efforts and increased detections of the species. h Reassessment of methods during an open grant allows staff to request changes if the original approach is not working (e.g., traps or lures are not catching the intended targets despite the species' likely presence). h Grants that support data analysis provide a mechanism to evaluate standardized monitoring protocols and determine if the protocol provides the necessary data needed to conserve species and ensure their persistence. h Data, particularly those from SWG-funded inventory projects, are used in the NatureServe ranking calculator to update S-ranks, which assist resource managers in designing and prioritizing projects for SGCN and their associated habitats.

## EFFECTIVENESS OF CONSERVATION ACTIONS

## ADAPTIVE MANAGEMENT

If conservation actions are found to be inadequate, alternative strategies may be needed to achieve desired results under an adaptive management framework. Because the conservation of SGCN and their habitats is not a straightforward process, the SWAP is designed to be a living document. Conservation priorities are dynamic and have changed since the 2005 and 2015 versions were published, and it is expected that changes will occur before the 2035 SWAP. As such, LDWF understands the importance of adapting to changing conditions, priorities, and new information. Wind and solar energy, which were not mentioned with any urgency 10 years ago, are currently topics of immediate concern. In addition, climate change will act in combination with other threats and may prompt biologists to adapt near term and future conservation strategies to address SGCN and their habitats.
Adaptive management is a four-phase cycle, in which each phase leads into the next, and is a continual process 
(Stankey et al. 2005)
.
The four phases are as follows, adapted from 
Stankey and colleagues (2005)
:
h Phase 1 -planning (either at the project or the WAP level) h Phase 2 -on-the ground conservation action h Phase 3 -the results of the conservation actions are monitored h Phase 4 -the results are evaluated, leading back to Phase 1 This is a continually evolving process, with lessons learned from each project and action feeding back into the loop, improving the outcomes of future conservation actions.
LDWF can also address changing conditions through the use of emerging issues letters. These letters provide USFWS a mechanism to fund SWG projects for high priority conservation issues, new threats (e.g., new disease outbreaks), and species that are outside the scope of the current SWAP. This allows LDWF the flexibility to decide which methods are best for each situation to better fulfill the needs of the SWAP. To that end, it will be important to periodically evaluate the effectiveness of monitoring and determine if the monitoring protocols in place adequately document the impacts, trends, and status of SGCN and their habitats.
Chapter 10: Louisiana:

## A Regional Perspective

In 2018, the Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (AFWA) adopted a resolution that recognized "the importance of collaborating at landscape scales to help fish and wildlife agencies meet their statutory and regulatory responsibilities to conserve fish and wildlife and their habitats." In response to the resolution, AFWA established a President's Task Force on Shared Science & Landscape Conservation Priorities in 2020, which recommended that State Wildlife Action Plans (SWAPs) serve as frameworks for regional coordination and collaboration.
Within this SWAP, Louisiana has identified Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and outlined strategies, including conservation actions, to promote species recovery and prevent federal listings under the Endangered Species Act. However, to sustain the species that are the responsibility of the state, Louisiana must consider its role and the influences of the larger Southeast landscape.
This chapter serves to examine regional, landscape-scale considerations and a means for the state to find potential collaborations across the Southeast to best support Louisiana SGCN and Regional Species of Greatest Conservation Need (RSGCN). In addition, some of the threats that impact SGCN (e.g., climate change), as well as the residents and local economies of the state, are at a regional level. Addressing these threats effectively requires aligning conservation strategies across state boundaries. By using consistent regional information shared by other states to inform their SWAPs, Louisiana may be able to better address its contributions to regional conservation priorities, identify potential landscape level threats, and help connect the lands and waters of the Southeast.

## LOUISIANA'S CONSERVATION PORTFOLIO: CONNECTING THE REGION'S LANDS AND WATERS

Louisiana supports a diversity of habitats, culturally and historically significant landscapes, and ecosystems that provide benefits to the state as well as the broader Southeast region. It plays an integral role in connecting the lands and waters of the Southeast region as well as hosting some regionally important ecosystems like Salt Marsh and Longleaf Pine savanna and woodland. Many regional and local partners are working with the Louisiana Department of Wildlife and Fisheries (LDWF) to help conserve iconic and significant landscapes in the state. According to the Protected Areas Database of the United States (PAD-US, v4.0; USGS 2024), approximately 4.29 million acres (13%) in Louisiana are considered protected 
(Figure 10.1)
. This includes landscapes like the Kisatchie National Forest, national wildlife refuges, and state conservation areas, refuges, and wildlife management areas.
In addition to contributing to the conservation landscape of the Southeast, Louisiana lands and waters also benefit the state's economy. The Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA) provides Outdoor Recreation Satellite Accounts for each state. In 2022, the BEA estimated that the outdoor recreation economy in Louisiana generated $7.9 billion in value for Gross Domestic Product and another $2.7 billion in related wages and salaries.
In addition to recreational value, natural landscapes support working lands such as agriculture and timber.

## LOUISIANA AND THE SOUTHEAST CONSERVATION ADAPTATION STRATEGY (SECAS)

The Southeast Conservation Adaptation Strategy (SECAS) is a regional conservation initiative that spans the southeastern United States and the Caribbean. SECAS brings together diverse partners around an ambitious goal: a 10% or greater improvement in the health, function, and connectivity of southeastern ecosystems by 2060. The Southeast Conservation Blueprint (v2024; Blueprint) is the primary product of SECAS and is a living, spatial plan that identifies priority areas where conservation action would make an impact, based on a suite of natural and cultural resource indicators, towards creating a connected network of lands and waters across the Southeast. Over 1,700 people from more than 500 organizations have been actively involved in developing this Blueprint.
The Blueprint recognizes almost 22 million acres, or roughly 66% of the state, as a priority for connecting the region's lands and waters (Table 
10
.1, Figure 
10
.2). About 10 million acres (31%) are rated as high or highest priority, meaning that these are areas where conservation action would make the biggest impact based on a suite of natural and cultural resource indicators. An additional 10.2 million acres (30%) is rated as medium priority. These are areas where conservation action would make an above-average impact based on a suite of natural and cultural resource indicators. There are also 1.5 million acres (5%) that are considered priority connections, or areas that can help facilitate the flow of species and ecological processes within the state while also considering connectivity with the broader region.
The Blueprint also includes a connectivity analysis that identifies corridors that link coastal and inland areas and span climate gradients. Based on a least-cost path analysis, hubs provide anchors to link corridors across the shortest distance possible, while also routing through Blueprint priority areas. Inland hubs are large patches (approximately 5,000 acres) of the highest priority Blueprint areas and protected lands, while inland corridors connect those hubs within broad areas of established conservation interest. About 13 million acres (39%) within Louisiana are considered hubs or corridors, providing many conservation opportunities to support species movement and migration, an important consideration for supporting wildlife adaptation to landscape-level changes (Figure 
10
.3).
Considering landscape connectivity across jurisdictional borders is becoming increasingly important for species management, as changes in land-use, climate, and weather patterns can shift LOUISIANA'S CONSERVATION LANDS Evaluating ecoregions that Louisiana shares with its neighbors can provide a potential framework to assess and compare ecosystems and ecosystem components as these areas are similar in their respective mosaics of biotic, abiotic, terrestrial, and aquatic ecosystems.

## LOUISIANA AND THE SECAS GOAL

Through SECAS, partners work together to design and achieve a connected network for the benefit of ecosystems, species, and people. The long-term goal for SECAS is a 10% or greater improvement in the health, function, and connectivity of southeastern ecosystems by 2060. To stay on track for achieving that goal, a 1% improvement will be needed every four years.
Although the primary product of SECAS is the Blueprint, which serves as the framework to achieve this goal, there is also an ongoing effort to track and report on regional ecosystem metrics.

## SECAS regularly releases a report -Recent Trends in Southeastern

Ecosystems -that assesses progress toward the SECAS goal using the most current available data. The report is intended to facilitate discussion around conservation actions needed to meet the goal. This assessment of shared measures of progress toward the SECAS goal provides an essential evaluation that helps to focus conservation actions and tracks resulting impacts ( The National Wildlife Federation, as part of the Vital Futures project funded by the U.S. Geological Survey, initiated a process for identifying a set of regional species listed as priority species in the 15 southeastern SWAPs. The Southeastern Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (SEAFWA) Wildlife Diversity Committee collaborated with the National Wildlife Federation and other partners to evaluate these species and produced a list of regional priority species, or RSGCN. Overall, in the Southeast, 960 species were identified as RSGCN. Freshwater fishes, crayfishes, and mussels represent nearly two-thirds of regional priority species.

## Shared Conservation

Priority Areas SE Conservation Blueprint 2024
Identifying RSGCN drew upon a collaborative process among southeastern state fish and wildlife agencies and partners that involved more than 100 experts and used a set of consistent criteria to review current scientific information and evaluate stateidentified SGCN. Species were evaluated based on several primary factors, including the level of conservation concern (i.e. extinction risk), regional stewardship responsibility (i.e. importance of the Southeast in the conservation of the species), and biological or ecological significance (e.g., unique evolutionary lineages).
The regional assessment focused on species in key groups, including vertebrates (mammals, birds, reptiles, amphibians, and fishes) as well as several better-known groups of invertebrate animals (freshwater mussels, crayfishes, and bumblebees; Table 
10
.4). Subject matter experts in each of these taxonomic groups convened to evaluate and identify those species that warranted identification as a regional priority. Additionally, the science teams characterized the level of conservation concern for each regional priority, ranging from moderate to very high concern. Through this established process, the RSGCN list can be updated as the states' SGCN lists change over time.
The number of RSGCN that are considered the stewardship responsibilities of each state varies widely (Figure 
10
.6). These patterns reflect the underlying diversity of species in each state, particularly the number of at-risk or limited range species, which is also influenced by a state's size and diversity of habitats. These patterns also influenced the "regional stewardship responsibility" criterion in the assessment of priority species.
By taxa, fishes and crayfishes represent 50% of all RSGCN within the region. Many aquatic habitats and ecosystems within the Southeast are highly fragmented, resulting in severe limitations to ecosystem integrity and species persistence regionwide. Habitat fragmentation in rivers and streams is a determining factor in the decline in abundance of numerous species particularly freshwater mussels and amphibians.
Nearly 70% of regional priority species are endemic to the 15-state SEAFWA region. The majority of those shared RSGCN are categorized as having a "High Level of Concern," with birds (12), reptiles (10), and fishes (10) representing taxa with the highest number of "Very High Concern" RSGCN shared by these states (Figure 
10
.8).
Louisiana is also home to multiple species that are not only endemic to the SEAFWA region, but are also found in only one other state. Louisiana and Texas support one species of fish, two crayfishes, and two reptiles that are RSGCN only found within these two states. There are also five species of crayfishes, one mollusk, and one amphibian found only in Louisiana and Arkansas. Additionally, there are two species of fishes, one crayfish, and two reptiles found only within Louisiana and Mississippi.
By identifying shared RSGCN, Louisiana can prioritize conservation actions that not only provide local benefits but also support a regional landscape. For example, Louisiana can identify conservation strategies aimed at improving aquatic connectivity that also improve water quality and flow in watersheds that cross into neighboring states.

## IDENTIFYING SHARED PRIORITIES ACROSS THE LANDSCAPE TO SUPPORT REGIONAL SPECIES OF GREATEST CONSERVATION NEED

There are several regional datasets available that can help Louisiana identify lands and waters that support RSGCN and where collaborations with other states may be appropriate. For example, habitat maps from the USGS Gap Analysis Project (GAP) are available for approximately 290 of the RSGCN at the species level. Although GAP does not include habitat maps for invertebrates or plants, it does include a high percentage of vertebrate RSGCN (92% of amphibians [Figure 
10
.9], 88% of birds [Figure 
10
.10], 87% of mammals [Figure 
10
.11], and 78% of reptiles [Figure 
10
.12]). There are an additional 90 subspecies on the RSGCN list that have GAP habitat maps at the species level. In addition, using GAP habitat maps along with other regional conservation plans like the Blueprint can help identify areas where conservation actions are likely to support co-benefits.
Species' predicted habitat maps were built using a national wildlife habitat relational database based on habitat associations described in published literature. The maps are created at 30-meter resolution using core datasets, including detailed land cover, elevation, and hydrological characteristics (e.g., salinity, water type, and velocity). While the actual distributions of many species are likely to be habitat-limited, suitable habitat will not always be occupied because of population dynamics and species interactions. Furthermore, these maps correspond to mid-scale characterizations of landscapes, but individual animals may deem areas to be unsuitable because of the presence or absence of fine-scale features and characteristics that are not represented in the GAP models (e.g., snags, vernal pools, and shrubby undergrowth). All data, including model inputs, are available from the USGS Gap Analysis Project Species' Habitat Maps on ScienceBase.
Using GAP species' habitat maps, species richness of reptile RSGCN that fall within the high concern category occur predominantly within the East Gulf Coastal Plain and Mississippi River Alluvial Plain ecoregions (Figure 
10
.13). Similarly, the GAP species' habitat maps show high species richness for bird RSGCNs listed in the high or very high concern category in the Gulf Coast Prairies and Marshes and the Mississippi River Alluvial Plain ecoregions (Figure 
10
.10).
Using available habitat maps from GAP for specific RSGCN, along with regional conservation plans like the Blueprint, can also help to identify not only a species' habitat across its range, but areas where conservation action can achieve multiple cobenefits. Using the Blueprint with the modeled habitat of species that occur in multiple states can help visualize and highlight important areas for species conservation across multiple jurisdictions. As recommended by AWFA, cross-jurisdictional or regional strategies can enhance ecosystem resiliency, function, and connectivity, especially in the face of climate change.
