## CLIMATE RESILIENCY

Many factors impacting the Southeast regional landscape like sea level rise, changing weather patterns, and habitat loss are often too pervasive for any one agency to address independently. While Louisiana must evaluate the impact of landscape level stressors and threats like climate change and land use change within its own boundaries, management efforts must also be coordinated on a regional scale. The AFWA 2022 (2nd Edition) Voluntary Guidance for States to Incorporate Climate Adaptation into State Wildlife Action Plans and Other Management Plans provides recommended steps for developing and implementing adaptation strategies. Much of the guidance includes taking a broader, regional approach to incorporating climate adaptation into SWAPs.
Projections of Earth's future surface temperatures depend primarily on future emissions and how sensitive the climate system will be to these emissions. Projections of climate change can be examined using two approaches: (1) global warming levels (GWLs) and (2) scenarios. GWLs are defined levels of warming for Earth's average temperature that correspond to increases of 2.7°F (1.5°C), 3.6°F (2°C), 5.4°F (3°C), and 7.2°F (4°C) degrees above pre-industrial levels (IPCC 2018, 
Arias et al. 2021)
. A scenario-based approach can be used to explore plausible climate outcomes under a coherent future trajectory of greenhouse gases and other anthropogenic forcings 
(Leung et al. 2023)
. The Fourth National Climate Assessment (NCA4; USGCRP 2018) used projections created with Representative Concentration Pathways (RCPs) to predict future climate in the United States (USGCRP 2018). The Fifth National Climate Assessment (NCA5; USGCRP 2023) used newly developed climate projections created with Shared Socioeconomic Pathways (SSPs) that represent plausible trajectories of GDP and population growth and trends of economic and technological progress globally for the 21st century (USGCRP 2023). The climate projections used in the NCA5 were created using global climate models from the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 6 (CMIP6) that were statistically downscaled to translate large scale changes in the climate under a given SSP or GWL to local scales for impact assessment and planning 
(Basile et al. 2023)
. Three of the SSPs used with the new climate models from the CMIP6 correspond to three of the RCPs used with the past global climate models from the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 5 (CMIP5) in their overall forcing levels (SSP1-2.6 corresponds with RCP2.6, SSP2-4.5 with RCP4.5, and SSP5-8.5 with RCP8.5) 
(Leung et al. 2023)
. Scenarios can predict when the planet will reach a certain global warming level. Hereafter, throughout this section, a mix of the SSPs and GWLs will be used to show climate impacts (Figure 
10
.13).
Using available climate change data and information that assume a specific GWL-or SSP-based scenario can allow land managers to consider possible future conditions. Exactly when and if we reach a particular GWL depends on the trends of global emissions and how sensitive the climate system is to these emissions. For example, in a very high scenario such as SSP5-8.5, the Earth is very likely to exceed GWL 2 (3.6°F) between 2033 and 2054, depending on the climate sensitivity to greenhouse gas emissions from each global climate model 
(Marvel et al. 2023;
Figure 10.13)
. By contrast, in a low scenario such as SSP1-2.6, the Earth is very likely not to cross GWL 2 at all 
(Marvel et al. 2023;
Figure 10.13)
. However, the rate of global warming varies across these scenarios, as well, with high and very high scenarios assuming the Earth warms faster 
(Marvel et al. 2023)
. Since 1850, the Earth's temperature has risen by an average of 0.11°F per decade and, as of 2023, has risen about 2°F in total (NOAA 2025b). However, since 1982, the rate of warming increased over three times as fast to about 0.36°F per decade. Currently, the planet is estimated to warm by nearly 5.4°F by the end of this century, meaning crossing a GWL 3 threshold, despite current global emission reduction pledges, according to the most recent United Nation's 
Annual 2023
Emissions Gap Report (UN 2023)
. Based on these trends, it is likely that the habitats, ecosystems, and conditions of Louisiana will also change as faster rates of climate change also increases the challenge of adaptation for natural systems and wildlife.
Climate projections use the best available physical science to predict how the climate system will respond to changes in human activities, but not all climate projections are developed in the same manner 
(IPCC 2023)
. Climate experts across the world have produced more than 50 global climate models under multiple greenhouse gas emissions and socioeconomic scenarios, each with unique variations and nuances. Some have undergone transformations like downscaling to improve spatial resolutions for smaller areas in order to make management decisions at a local scale. The most recent climate projections used in the NCA5 and associated climate parameters can be viewed on the National Climate Change Viewer. This tool, developed by USGS, allows for visualizations of climate change impacts such as days of extreme heat under projections of degree warming days, under SSP-based scenarios, and GWLs 
(Alder and Hostetler 2013)
. Additionally, these data are available as interactive maps and layers as part of the Fifth National Climate Assessment Interactive Atlas (National Climate Assessment Interactive Atlas [globalchange. gov]) and is where spatial climate data for this report were derived. Information pertaining to sea level rise, as well as easyto-understand state-level summaries on climate change impacts, are available from the NOAA via their Sea Level Rise Technical Reports, Sea Level Rise Viewer Tool, and State Climate Summaries website 
(Kunkel et al. 2022
, Sweet et al. 2022
).
In the Southeast, climate change will have pervasive impacts on natural habitats, ecological processes, and ecosystem services, and regional efforts to mitigate or adapt to these impacts will be extremely important. According to NCA4 and NCA5, conditions in the Southeast may change dramatically 
(USGCRP 2018
(USGCRP , 2023))
. Both assessments indicate that current trends will continue: increasing frequency, intensity, and duration of heatwaves; increasing wildfire risk; and accelerating rising sea levels. The resulting projected changes include extreme heat, extreme precipitation events, increased drought, accelerated sea level rise, and stronger tropical cyclones as well as decreases in the intensity and frequency of cold-season events. Despite the significant threat climate change poses to the landscape, understanding the tools and resources available to address climate change and develop effective strategies is difficult. As part of the survey administered to SWAP coordinators in 2021, the majority of SEAFWA states and territories reported integrating climate change as one of the most challenging aspects of the upcoming SWAP revision. Anecdotally, some SWAP coordinators noted that it is difficult to discern what tools are available, decide which tool is the right one to use, and choose the appropriate scale and timeframe when using tools. Some also reported it challenging to determine what climate-change associated threats to include and how to spatially represent the impacts.
As such, the Southeast and South Central Climate Adaptation Science Centers (SE CASC and SC CASC) have provided data and information for this chapter to help translate the actionable implications of future climate projections.
FIGURE 10.13. When (or if) the world reaches 2°C of warming depends on future greenhouse gas emissions and the sensitivity of the climate system to those emissions. Shown are the IPCC AR6 assessed warming projections for four future scenarios, with projected years at which the 2°C (3.6°F) global warming level would be reached. For example, under a very high scenario (SSP5-8.5), models project reaching 2°C between 2033 and 2054, with an average estimate of 2042. Under a low scenario (SSP1-2.6), the 5% CI (confidence interval) range begins at 2041, but the average projection shows that warming would actually stay below 2°C. Taken from the Fifth National Climate Assessment Figure 
2
.14 
(Marvel et al. 2023)
.

## PROJECTED GLOBAL SURFACE TEMPERATURE CHANGE

## LOUISIANA AND CLIMATE HAZARDS

Like the rest of the Southeast, Louisiana is predicted to experience a number of climate impacts, although the magnitude of change often varies by ecoregion, emissions scenario, and future timeframe. Generally, these impacts include hotter summers, warmer winters, more frequent extreme precipitation events, and rising sea levels 
(USGCRP 2018
(USGCRP , 2023))
.
Hotter Summers, Warmer Winters, and Flash Droughts Temperatures in Louisiana have risen by 0.5°F since the beginning of the 20th century with the warmest consecutive 5-year interval for the state occurring from 2019-2024 
(Kunkel et al. 2013
, Franskon et al. 2022
, NASA 2024)
. Since 1995, the number of extremely hot days (i.e. days with high temperatures ≥95°F) in Louisiana has begun to increase but has generally remained below average 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
. By contrast, the number of very warm nights (i.e. days with above average nighttime temperatures) has increased steadily since 2000, reaching a record-high level during 2015-2020 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
. Additionally, heatwaves in the United States have become hotter, more frequent, larger, and longer lasting in recent decades. These trends are projected to continue, which has implications for drought in the region 
(Hoffman et al. 2023)
.
Higher temperatures throughout the year in Louisiana will increase the rate of soil moisture loss (i.e. evapotranspiration) during dry spells, which could lead to more intense droughts 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
. Any increases in temperature will cause more rapid loss of soil moisture during consecutive dry days (i. Another way to look at future climate projections is through the lens of GWLs. If the Earth surpasses GWL 1.5 (2.7°F) or GWL 2 (3.6°F) for a sustained period of time, the number of days where the temperature reaches 100°F or higher will increase across the state, compared to the past 30 years (1991-2020) (Figure 
10
.15). Estimates of when these GWL might be reached depend on the emission scenario. 

## Extreme Storms and Precipitation Events, Warmer Waters, and Altered Flows

Climate change projections suggest that the Southeast will get wetter overall, meaning some seasonal, annual average, and extreme precipitation amounts will increase across the region, driven mainly by more extreme events (e.g., precipitation of 3 or more inches in 24 hours) at higher levels of global warming 
(Hoffman et al. 2023)
. Little change in total annual precipitation is projected over this century, although the timing and intensity of precipitation events will change 
(Hoffman et al. 2023)
. However, total annual precipitation has generally been above average in Louisiana since 1970 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
. While 2010 and 2011 were very dry, the majority of the years between 2011 and 2020 have been above average years for precipitation 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
. Chapter 10: Louisiana: A Regional Perspective Extreme precipitation events are expected to increase in the future and often manifest as extreme weather and increased flooding 
(Wong et al. 2014)
. In Louisiana, the number of extreme precipitation events with 4 or more inches of rain has been generally above average since 1980 and was well above average during 2015-2020 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
. After Florida, Louisiana has, on average, the second-highest annual number of thunderstorms in the contiguous United States, with a typical location in the state averaging more than 60 thunderstorms per year 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
. Models indicate that extreme rainfall events like the mid-August 2016 downpour in Louisiana have decreased from an average of once every 50 years to once every 30 years and have increased in magnitude; a typical 30year event in 1900 would have had 10% less rain than a similar event today (e.g., approximately 23 inches instead of 25 inches) ( 
Van der Wiel et al. 2016)
. Extreme rainfall that occurs while ocean water inundates and affects low-lying areas in the coastal plain (e.g., because of high tides or storm surges) creates compound flooding events 
(Hoffman et al. 2023)
.
In the Southeast region, extreme weather events are generally characterized by hurricanes and tropical storms, which will likely increase in frequency and magnitude (USGCRP 2018).
Hurricanes and tropical storms have been responsible for some of the Southeast's biggest and most damaging flooding events since 2018 
(Hoffman et al. 2023)
. The likelihood of hurricanes slowing down or stalling near the Southeast coastline has increased, which exacerbates the rainfall-related flooding threats from these storm systems 
(Hoffman et al. 2023)
. Climate change is expected to strengthen North Atlantic hurricanes to at least Category 4 intensity and cause rapid intensification as well as potentially increasing the likelihood of storms making landfall 
(Hoffman et al. 2023)
. More severe weather, combined with subsequent wave action, high winds, and storm surge, can severely erode beaches and dunes, eliminate nests of sea turtles and beach-nesting birds, and degrade or erode maritime forests and salt marshes (USGCRP 2018). Hurricanes strike Louisiana an average of once every three years, but as the climate continues to warm, hurricane-associated rainfall rates are projected to increase. The resulting flooding is a particular concern for the state 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
.
Figure 
10
.17 shows the projected future percent change in the number of days per year with extreme precipitation (e.g., days with the top 1% of rainfall) in Louisiana at GWL 1.5 (2.7°F warming) and GWL 2 (3.6°F warming) compared to the last 30 years. Estimates of when these GWL might be reached depend on the emission scenario. Although all ecoregions within Louisiana will experience changes in days with extreme precipitation, the central and northern parts of the state in the Mississippi Alluvial Plain Ecoregion and South Central Plains Ecoregions are projected to experience the greatest percent change across GWLs (Figure 
10
.17).
The Southeast has the highest aquatic diversity of any temperate system; however, the ecological relationships and life histories of many of the endemic species are not well understood within the constraints of climate change 
(Ingram et al. 2013)
. Freshwater ecosystems (e.g., streams, rivers, lakes, and wetlands) of the Southeast are highly vulnerable to warming. Impacts on rare species of fishes and mussels are of particular concern in a changing climate. The combined effects of warming and changes in precipitation will likely alter overall hydrology including increased evapotranspiration and reduced stream base flow. Currently, many species declines are associated with widespread alteration of flow regimes and are expected to be exacerbated by climate change. Impacts of climate change on riverine and wetland ecosystems are predicted to also include increased water temperatures, which dictate many species ranges and exacerbates low dissolved oxygen conditions 
(Ingram et al. 2013)
. In general, temperature regimes of freshwater ecosystems are projected to increase in parallel with shifts in air temperatures, which, in turn, may alter plant and animal communities 
(Princé and Zuckerberg 2015)
.
Sea level rise scenarios for the Southeast, described in more detail in the following section, project a higher frequency of compound flooding along the coast by midcentury 
(Hoffman et al. 2023)
. Extreme rainfall events also contribute to increases in river and stream flooding across the landscape, which have different effects in different ecoregions and topography. For example, in the Western Gulf Coastal Plain, Southern Coastal Plain, and lower Mississippi Alluvial Plain ecoregions, where elevation is low, there is growing concern for river levels and future water management in the Mississippi Basin 
(Van der Wiel et al. 2016
, Miller et al. 2023)
. As climate change continues, understanding the interplay between increased precipitation and extreme rainfall events, evaporation (with increased temperatures), and river flow will be vital for protecting natural and water resources and managing flooding in Louisiana's coastal habitats 
(Van der Wiel et al. 2016
, Miller et al. 2023)
.

## Sea Level Rise

Beyond the changes for temperature and precipitation, sea level rise will also impact Louisiana. Coastal and low-lying areas are highly likely to experience a myriad of changes due to sea level rise 
(Wong et al. 2014)
. Sea level rise along the United States' coast is increasing faster than the global average with the highest rates currently observed along the Gulf and Mid-Atlantic coasts of the Southeast region 
(Hoffman et al. 2023)
.
Due to the magnitude of sea level rise projected throughout Projected reduction in days where temperatures drop below 32oF Chapter 10: Louisiana: A Regional Perspective the 21st century and beyond, coupled with subsidence, coastal systems and low-lying areas will increasingly experience adverse impacts such as submergence, flooding, and erosion 
(Wong et al. 2014)
. Historically, coastal ecosystems in the Southeast region have adjusted to sea level rise by vertical (accretion) and horizontal movement across the landscape. As sea levels continue to rise, some coastal ecosystems will be submerged and convert to open water, and saltwater intrusion will allow salt-tolerant coastal ecosystems to move inland at the expense of upslope and upriver ecosystems. Louisiana's coastline is extremely vulnerable to sea level rise due to coastal subsidence, wetland loss, and low elevation in the southern portion of the state. Projected sea level rise poses widespread and continuing threats to the state's coastal ecological communities 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
.
The NOAA 2022 Sea Level Rise Technical Report, Global and Regional Sea Level Rise Scenarios for the United States, provides the most recent projections available to all United States coastal areas out to the year 2150 
(Sweet et al. 2022)
. The following tables show the predicted extent of flooding for coastal Louisiana at the average highest daily tide due to sea level rise (Table 
10
.5) and the projected amount of sea level rise by decade under various emissions scenarios (Table 
10
.6). Figure 
10
.17 also provides a statewide map of areas likely to experience flooding at high tide based on each foot of sea level rise above current levels.

## SPECIES' RESPONSE TO CHANGES IN CLIMATE

As the climate, regional landscape, and weather patterns change, there is value in identifying regional strategies and conservation actions to support the SGCN and RSGCN that are most vulnerable to the impacts. A species is considered vulnerable to climate change if it is both sensitive and exposed to climate change impacts 
(Williams et al. 2008)
. For example, a species may be exposed to climate change, because it experiences an impact like warmer stream temperatures; but if it is resilient to that change or able to disperse into new habitats, it may, ultimately, have low vulnerability. Conversely, a species that would be sensitive to climate change but is unlikely to experience any impacts and is, therefore, not exposed may also be less vulnerable. Understanding if a species is sensitive and will be exposed to climate change can help managers develop strategies to support the species most likely to be impacted. 
10
.5 and 10.6. NOAA calculates the inundation footprint at "mean higher high water," or the average highest daily tide. The area covered in each SLR scenario includes areas projected to be inundated at lower levels. For example, the area inundated by 4 ft of SLR also includes areas inundated by 3 ft, 2 ft, 1 ft, and 0 ft of SLR (where 0 ft represents current levels).
the resulting climate change vulnerability score is distinct from NatureServe's national vulnerability ranking system that considers other threats. A species could score as not particularly vulnerable in terms of its Global or State rank (G-or S-rank), but could still rate as vulnerable to climate change. CCVI is based on an evaluation of the species' direct and indirect exposure to climate change and its sensitivity to climate change. To date, several SEAFWA states 
(West Virginia DNR 2011;
Tennessee SWAP Team 2015;
Florida FWC 2011;
and Louisiana, Holcomb et al. 2015
) have used the CCVI tool to conduct assessments of their SGCN in order to inform previous SWAPs, management priorities, and research and conservation planning. Previous CCVI assessments can be accessed from those states' 2015 SWAPs or from an archived online integrated dataset collated by the Armsworth lab at the University of Tennessee-Knoxville 
(Armsworth et al. 2025)
. Those CCVI assessments can serve as references for what has been done in the past and to shape future planning and conservation efforts.
An ecological niche model (also called a species distribution model) is a common approach for evaluating a species' vulnerability to climate change. These models, typically created by the academic research community, start by identifying shared climate conditions at sites where a species is known to have occurred and then predict where similar climate conditions may be found on the landscape in the future. Some models also take into account a species' ability to disperse to new areas that may become climatically suitable. The resulting predictions from these models allow researchers to create spatially explicit estimates of whether suitable climate conditions for a species are likely to remain stable, become more widespread in the landscape (i.e. increase), or become more geographically rare in the future (i.e. decrease).
For example, if suitable climate conditions for a species were projected to decrease greatly in an area in the future, then the species would be considered more vulnerable to climate change than a species for which climate conditions were predicted to remain stable. It is important to note that raw data from niche models (e.g., spatial raster data) are not usually readily available to the public due to the large size of the files but instead are available in summary form in the research literature (i.e. academic journal articles). Therefore, more translational effort is needed with niche model results, as the form of the data might not be readily interpretable by users. A collated and readily interpretable dataset of niche models conducted for Southeast RSGCN has been compiled 
(Armsworth et al. 2025)
.
Niche model results can be used in tandem with CCVI estimates to enhance the understanding of a given species' vulnerability, as CCVIs and niche models have complementary strengths that can help land managers make informed conservation decisions. Niche models offer more refined and spatially explicit predictions (e.g., changes in suitable habitat) about the direct exposure of a species to climate change but are only available for a limited set of species, in part, because the models require extensive data on a species before they can be applied. CCVI estimates use an expert judgment-based approach that allows integration of different types of information about species (e.g., their natural history) and what might make them vulnerable to climate change. It is important to compare CCVI estimates with available ecological niche model predictions for SGCN to fully understand a given species' vulnerability to climate change while keeping in mind the geographic (e.g., region or habitat evaluated) and biological (e.g., estimated dispersal distance) contexts of these assessments.

## Summary of Available Climate Change Vulnerability Indices

The USGS Southeast Climate Adaptation Science Center (SE CASC) and the University of Tennessee-Knoxville launched a research effort to support the SEAFWA states in integrating climate change considerations into their 2025 SWAP revisions. This effort collated CCVI scores from 10 data sources within the eastern United States 
(Armsworth et al. 2025)
. It draws from CCVI assessments published in four SWAPs in the Southeast 
(West Virginia DNR 2011;
Tennessee SWAP Team 2015;
Florida FWC 2011;
and Louisiana, Holcomb et al. 2015)
 as well as the Illinois SWAP (Illinois DNR 2011), Natural Heritage Programs in the Northeast 
(Schlesinger et al. 2011
, Furedi et al. 2011)
, the Appalachian and North Atlantic Landscape Conservation Cooperatives 
(Sneddon and
Galbraith 2015, Sneddon and
Hammerson 2014)
, and the National Park Service Cumberland Piedmont Network 
(Bruno et al. 2012
), all of which were informed by state conservation practitioners. This work compiles 1,600 CCVI estimates for 795 species (note: some species have multiple CCVI estimates from different states). Many of these species are on the RSGCN list for the Southeast. For Louisiana, 176 SGCN have been scored by 400 CCVI estimates to date by other institutions 
(Armsworth et al. 2025)
.
Most CCVI assessments of Louisiana's SGCN have focused on birds. Many of these CCVI assessments indicate that although some bird species may be vulnerable, a larger proportion may remain stable or likely increase under changing climatic conditions. Although more assessments are needed for other taxa, especially those that are not as well studied (e.g., plants), current CCVI estimates show that amphibians and reptiles, fishes, invertebrates, and mussels have the largest proportions of Louisiana SGCN that are vulnerable to climate change; mammals have the smallest proportion. In addition, while about half of the assessed amphibian and reptile SGCN in Louisiana are vulnerable to climate change, the other half are presumed stable.

## Summary of Available Ecological Niche Models

In addition to the collation and summary of CCVI estimates, the SE CASC and the University of Tennessee-Knoxville summarized complementary information on available ecological niche models. For Louisiana, 173 SGCN have been evaluated, which consists of ~450 vulnerability scores (note: some species have multiple scores for different future climate scenarios) across 12 academic research studies 
(Mckenny et al. 2007
, Iverson et al. 2008
, Matthews et al. 2011
, Sutton et al. 2014
, Bucklin et al. 2015
, Struecker and Milanovich 2017
, Pandey and Papeş 2018
, Bateman et al. 2020
, Lawler et al. 2020
, Zellmer et al. 2020
, Zhu et al. 2021
, Lyon and Papeş 2022)
. Similar to the CCVI assessments, most niche models have focused on birds and have found that many species of birds will experience increases in their climatically suitable landscape within the Southeast region as a result of climate change.

## Comparing CCVI and Available Ecological Niche Models Results

Comparing the results of the ecological niche models to the CCVI estimates for SGCN within Louisiana reveals similar trends for taxa that were evaluated using both methods. All assessed taxa (including plants) will experience some vulnerability and some decreases in suitable range due to climate change. However, besides birds, these results suggest that large proportions of Louisiana amphibian, fish, insect, and mussel SGCN will be vulnerable to climate change. Amphibians have the fewest species with predicted stable populations across both methods and show virtually no SGCN to be presumed stable or increasing in the CCVI estimates. Birds have the most species identified as vulnerable to climate change but also the most species with predicted stable or increasing populations across both approaches, partially due to the number of bird SGCN evaluated as compared to other taxa.
However, there are currently few overlaps between the species assessed by both the CCVI and ecological niche model datasets, limiting the scope of comparisons until more assessments and niche models are completed. In particular, niche model estimates are not yet available for many aquatic species such as fishes and mussels, which the scientific literature suggests may be some of the most vulnerable to climate change in Louisiana and the Southeast region 
(Halpern and Kappel 2012
, Poff et al. 2012
, Ingram et al. 2013)
. In fact, the available CCVI for these aquatic taxa suggest that more fish and mussel SGCN of Louisiana are predicted to be vulnerable to climate change than not.
The comparison of these two methods supports focusing climate adaptation strategies on the conservation of amphibian and reptile species. SARP already considers identification and remediation of anthropogenic barriers to aquatic organism passage a priority conservation action, and this can also be an important climate adaptation strategy to support the resilience of fishes and other aquatic species that rely on aquatic connectivity.
Additionally, more research is needed on the vulnerability of invertebrates like insects that had the greatest number of highly vulnerable SGCN from compiled CCVI estimates.

## CASE STUDY

A Closer Look -Climate Impacts on Louisiana Wetlands and Their Amphibian Species of Greatest Conservation Need Isolated inland wetlands, particularly those ephemeral in nature such as prairies of northwestern Louisiana, Macon Ridge Green Ash Ponds in northeastern Louisiana, and flatwoods ponds throughout the state, are vital for successful amphibian reproduction. These habitats, combined with other types of wetlands throughout Louisiana such as the swamps and bottomland hardwood forests, are critical to amphibian biodiversity and rely on precipitation patterns that are increasingly variable 
(Walls et al. 2013
, Ansley et al. 2022)
.
Many amphibians that inhabit these wetlands are consistently listed in the IUCN Red List as vulnerable, endangered, or critically endangered 
(Adams et al. 2013
, Li et al. 2013
, Halsted et al. 2022)
. Several studies have shown that climatic changes affecting temperature and rainfall patterns can result in negative consequences to amphibians that rely on wetlands, leading to population and occurrence declines 
(Walls et al. 2011
, Adams et al. 2013
, Li et al. 2013
, Salas et al. 2017)
. Amphibians are useful as ecological indicators of system conditions, and their decline may be more widespread than currently documented 
(Adams et al. 2013
, Halsted et al. 2022)
. Future climate projections forecast more extreme temperatures and tropicalization of these habitats, which will further constrict the viable range of amphibian species distribution in the Southeast 
(Salas et al. 2017
, Ansley et al. 2022)
.
Future climate projections also predict increases in the frequency of extreme precipitation events in Louisiana, which have negative effects on wetlands such as stream flooding, high-velocity water flow, high levels of sediment being deposited, and subsequent increases in water turbidity 
(Henley et al. 2000
, Walls et al. 2013
). Flooding, with its associated high-water flow and transport of debris, has been linked to declines and extirpations of a variety of stream and riverdwelling amphibians 
(Walls et al. 2013)
. For example, Gulf Coast Waterdog (Necturus beyeri) populations seem robust in streams with preferred microhabitats and adequate depth, but sedimentation from erosion and flooding within occupied watersheds may impact the species' habitat and ability to thrive 
(Glorioso et al. 2021)
. Additionally, extreme flooding events can increase opportunities for predators of amphibians; a recent monitoring study of the Coastal Plain of the southeastern United States found that the most prominent effects of flooding on the amphibian community were caused by changes in the fish community that led to increased predation of amphibians and resource competition 
(Davis et al. 2022)
. Avoidance of fish predation is a key benefit to ephemeral ponds, which periodically dry out, preventing usage by fish.
To varying extents, all amphibians rely on the availability of fresh water in their reproductive cycles. This is true whether they directly deposit their eggs in aquatic habitats or on land 
(Wells 2007)
. The success of amphibian breeding is closely tied to the length of time and portion of the year that wetlands hold water, called hydroperiods. Shifting hydroperiods and variations in water availability due to climate change can influence amphibian breeding seasons, larval development, and body size, and have profound impacts on extinction rates 
(Walls et al. 2013
, Davis et al. 2017
, LeSage et al. 2022)
. Periods of drought, increased temperatures, and excessive rainfall can dramatically alter the hydroperiod of freshwater wetlands 
(Walls et al. 2013)
. For example, higher temperatures in the driest quarter of the year increase the rate at which ponds dry out, leading to shorter hydroperiods. In a recent study by 
Boycott and colleagues (2024)
, hydroperiod was the most important variable associated with the presence of the Crawfish Frog (Lithobates areolatus) on the landscape.
However, species-specific traits and varying adaptive capacity of amphibians lead to species responding differently to changes in climatic conditions 
(Davis et al. 2017)
. Table 
10
.7 lists a selection of Louisiana's updated amphibian SGCN, the results from NatureServe conservation status and Climate Change Vulnerability Index (CCVI) assessments, and results from ecological niche modeling. Results for some species like Four-Toed Salamander, Louisiana Slimy Salamander, Gulf Coast Mud Salamander, and Eastern Spadefoot show moderate to extremely vulnerable CCVI scores and show large decreases of their climatically suitable habitat (Table 
10
.7). When regional niche model results disagree with CCVI assessments in the table, it is due to the niche models representing the projected trend for the region or for eastern North America, whereas CCVI assessments were conducted at a state level. However, regional niche models are still useful as they show the projected regional trends for the species (Table 
10
.7). Additionally, to date, many species still need to be assessed using the NatureServe CCVI tool, and many have not been the subject of regional ecological niche modeling efforts. Both instances are indicated by the "Not Assessed" boxes in Table 
10
.7. Data collected in long-term monitoring programs will be necessary to address many of these questions related to amphibian success in a changing climate 
(Salas et al. 2017
, Carter et al. 2021
, Ansley et al. 2022)
. Observation periods of less than 10 years or periods with long multiyear gaps are likely to be too short or incomplete to determine if a species is experiencing long-term declines or increases in abundance or occupancy 
(Carter et al. 2021)
. Future research needs to include the potential effects of variable precipitation patterns, including more extreme precipitation events, which have largely been overlooked in studies with amphibians 
(Walls et al. 2013)
.
Most amphibians rely on the presence of water for reproduction and development, and precipitation patterns likely have impacts to their occupancy of a site 
(Adams et al. 2013)
. Therefore a need exists to test impacts of the pattern and timing of rainfall events on amphibians, rather than the total amount of rainfall, especially in relation to seasonal reproduction 
(Walls et al. 2013)
. The Adaptation Workbook is broken down into sections, one of which is Adaptation Strategies and Approaches. In this section, climate adaptation strategies are listed in menus, categorized by focal areas, with the goal of providing conservationists with specific, actionable adaptation options from which to select. The menus are not all-encompassing, nor are they intended to serve as guidance for management decisions. They simply serve as a starting point for those interested in climate adaptation.

## ADAPTATION STRATEGIES

## WORKING IN RESILIENT LANDSCAPES

Regardless of the projected impacts of climate hazards, some portions of the state are predicted to be more resilient than others. Focusing conservation actions within resilient places is a climate-smart investment.

## Terrestrial Resilience

Much of Louisiana's natural landscape is considered a resilient terrestrial site. The Nature Conservancy (TNC) developed a data set called the Resilient Land Data and identifies areas with different landscape characteristics estimated to be the most resilient to climate change (i.e. the areas most likely to maintain species diversity and ecosystem function in the face of climate change). It measures two factors that influence resilience. The first, landscape diversity, reflects the number of microhabitats and climatic gradients created by topography, elevation, and hydrology. The second, local connectedness, reflects the degree of habitat fragmentation and strength of barriers to species movement. Highly resilient sites contain many different habitat niches that support biodiversity and allow species to move freely through the landscape to find suitable microclimates as the climate changes. Resilience emphasizes diverse landscapes where species are likely to be able to move and adjust to changing conditions. While the species composition may change, these areas will likely to be able to host a continued diversity of species into the future.

## Coastal Resilience

Louisiana's coastal and estuarine environments are key areas to build and sustain resiliency in the face of climate change. In particular, tidal marshes and other tidal habitats provide vital ecosystem services like shoreline stabilization, water filtration, food production, and recreational opportunities. Identifying places where tidal marshes and other tidal habitats provide resiliency to climate hazards currently and in the future is a key strategy in mitigating climate change impacts. 
(Sneddon and Hammerson 2014
, Glick et al. 2015
, Holcomb et al. 2015)
 are included and summarized by 
Armsworth and colleagues (2025)
. Ecological niche model vulnerability scores from eastern North America geographic areas 
(Struecker and Milanovich 2017
, Sutton et al. 2014
, Zellmer et al. 2020
) are included and summarized by 
Armsworth and colleagues (2025)
. The U.S. Geological Survey (USGS) used remote sensing to calculate the unvegetated-vegetated ratio of tidal wetlands, which compares the percentage of wetland covered by sediment, rocks, and open water to the percentage covered by plants. Marshes that maintain a higher proportion of vegetation tend to be more stable and resilient to threats like sea level rise, erosion, and coastal development. These data offer a method to identify marshes that may be good candidates for restoration to help them persist in the face of changing conditions (USGS 2018). Recent research shows that a high unvegetatedvegetated ratio correlates with elevation measurements that detect vertical changes due to accretion, subsidence, or compaction -meaning that stable marshes with high vegetative cover tend to better keep pace with sea level rise.

## n/a

In addition to resilient terrestrial sites, TNC also developed Resilient Coastal Sites, which provides a way to map and visualize coastal habitats with the capacity to migrate to adjacent lowlands in order to sustain biodiversity and natural services under increasing inundation from sea level rise. Scientists from TNC evaluated more than 1,500 coastal sites in the Gulf of America region that contained tidal marsh and tidal habitats to identify places more likely to continue to support biological diversity and ecological functions under rising sea levels 
(Anderson and Barnett 2019)
. The sites are based on the characteristics of current tidal complexes, their predicted migration space, and surrounding buffer areas. These characteristics include marsh complex size, shared edge with migration space, sediment balance, water quality, natural land cover, landform diversity, and many others. 

## Short-eared

## WORKING WITH PARTNERS ACROSS THE REGION

There are multiple regional partnerships that include the state of Louisiana within their conservation planning. Below, we describe opportunities for Louisiana to rally conservation partners to shared areas of interest and describe on-going collaborations.

## USFWS BIRDS OF CONSERVATION CONCERN

The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) Birds of Conservation Concern (BCC) are identified as migratory and non-migratory species that are not listed as federally threatened or endangered but still represent high conservation priority. Birds considered for this list do not have sport hunting seasons or have a sport hunting season where very little harvest occurs. Louisiana falls within the boundaries of the Mississippi Flyway and has multiple BCC recognized by USFWS that are also identified by LDWF as a SGCN. Louisiana recognizes 38 of these species as SGCN; all but 14 of these species are also considered RSGCN (Table 
10
.8).

## LOWER MISSISSIPPI VALLEY JOINT VENTURE

Operating within the Lower Mississippi River Valley, West Gulf Coastal Plain, and Ouachita regions, the Lower Mississippi Valley Joint Venture (LMVJV) aims to function as a forum in which the private, state, and federal conservation communities develop a shared vision of bird conservation. This geographic footprint includes not only portions of Louisiana but also spans into all neighboring states and partners with a variety of organizations to develop research and analysis supporting conservation actions and decision support. This expanded network can provide additional opportunities for collaboration in landscape conservation efforts. Several BCC within the LMVJV footprint are also listed as shared RSGCN between multiple states. These species are Prothonotary Warbler, Swallow-tailed Kite, Prairie Warbler, Bachman's Sparrow, Wood Thrush, King Rail, Southeastern American Kestrel, Brown-headed Nuthatch, Henslow's Sparrow, LeConte's Sparrow, Red-cockaded Woodpecker, Kentucky Warbler, Cerulean Warbler, Rusty Blackbird, Wood Thrush, and Little Blue Heron. Additional focus on the habitat supporting these species could prove to be beneficial to not only these species but additional associated species as well.

## GULF COAST JOINT VENTURE

The Gulf Coast Joint Venture (GCJV) is separated into six initiative area. The Mississippi River Coastal Wetlands and Louisiana Chenier Plain Initiative areas are both located in South Louisiana and provide critical habitat for bird species. Like the LMVJV, the GCJV is a collaborative partnership including private landowners, non-governmental organizations, and state and federal agencies focused on priority bird habitat and conservation. The Northern Bobwhite, Loggerhead Shrike, and Seaside Sparrow are recognized on Louisiana's shared RSGCN and GCJV lists of priority species. Aside from an additional expanded network of conservation partners, the GCJV actively pursues advancement of conservation research, design, evaluation, and monitoring. Collaboration on conservation projects could help ensure preservation of critical habitat in these areas.
There are four military bases located in Louisiana: Fort Polk, Barksdale Air Force Base, Camp Beauregard, and the Naval Air Station Joint Reserve Base. The largest of these facilities is Fort Polk. Beyond the facilities and bases themselves, the military also conducts training exercises in a larger physical footprint across the state. There are several main tools that Louisiana can use to look for opportunities to manage and support SGCN through the DoD. These tools include Integrated Natural Resource Management Plans, the Army Compatible Use Buffer, and the Readiness and Environmental Protection Integration program.

## Integrated Natural Resource Management Plans (INRMPs)

There is already an existing avenue of collaboration with the DoD through Integrated Natural Resource Management Plans (INRMPs). INRMPs serve as management plans for the highly diverse habitats for which the DoD is responsible. These plans are installation-specific and designed to balance conservation and mission activities in order to satisfy environmental legal requirements without compromising the capability of installation lands to support the military mission. As part of a comprehensive revision of the Sikes Act in 1997 (the statute governing natural resources management on military lands; 16 U.S.C. §670 et seq.), INRMPs are mandatory for all installations including state-owned National Guard installations with significant natural resources. INRMPs must be prepared in cooperation with the USFWS and appropriate state fish and wildlife agencies and are meant to reflect the mutual agreement of all parties concerning the conservation, protection, and management of fish and wildlife resources.

## The Readiness and Environmental Protection Integration (REPI) Program

While INRMPs focus primarily on those lands and resources within DoD installation boundaries, many installations' species and habitats are more effectively and efficiently managed on an ecosystem or landscape scale. The DoD has legal authority to spend appropriated funds for conservation beyond its installation fence lines through the Readiness and Environmental Protection Integration (REPI) Program.
The REPI program addresses land use conflicts that restrict military activities, and a key component of the REPI Program is the use of encroachment management partnerships, referred to as REPI projects, authorized by Congress within 10 USC 2684a. The REPI enables DoD to enter into cost-shared agreements with specified parties (including state and local governments) to acquire interests in real property in order to, among other purposes, preserve habitat or improve installation resilience. Properties targeted for acquisition using this authority need only be ecologically related to a military installation in order to be eligible. These cost-sharing partnerships between the military services, state and local governments, and private conservation organizations allow for the acquisitions of easements or other interests in land from willing sellers that preserve critical buffer areas and habitat near military installations. Importantly, REPI funding may be used to facilitate off-installation natural resources management when a real property may not be necessary nor available.
In the Fiscal Year 2019 National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA), Congress expanded the REPI authority in 10 USC 2684a to extend the traditional REPI real property authorities to address military installation resilience. Specifically, the language now includes a provision that allows the Secretary of Defense or the Secretary of a military department to "enter into an agreement to address the use or development of real property in the vicinity of, or ecologically related to, a military installation… for the purposes of… preserving off base habitat on the property in a manner that… maintains or improves military installation resilience." "Military installation resilience" carries a specific definition in the law: "the capability of a military installation to avoid, prepare for, minimize the effect of, adapt to, and recover from extreme weather events, or from anticipated or unanticipated changes in environmental conditions, that do, or have the potential to, adversely affect the military installation or essential transportation, logistical, or other necessary resources outside of the military installation that are necessary in order to maintain, improve, or rapidly reestablish installation mission assurance and mission-essential functions." The terms "anticipated or unanticipated changes in environmental conditions" encompass climate change. By incorporating resilience into 10 USC 2684a, a statute focused on limiting encroachment impacts on military testing, training, and operations, Congress has cast climate change as an encroachment concern. REPI will absorb this responsibility by maintaining its focus on readiness and expanding its encroachment mission to incorporate climate change as a threat.
Because Louisiana already has a statutory seat at the table during the preparation and annual reviews of DoD installation INRMPs, the state has an opportunity to bring off-installation conservation opportunities to the installation's attention. Conversely, inviting installation natural resources managers to be more engaged in the SWAP process could reveal to the states previously unrecognized opportunities to collaborate on cost-shared conservation projects to the mutual benefit of DoD and the states as well as the species and habitats addressed by the effort.

## WORKING WITH TRIBAL NATIONS

Meaningful engagement with tribes requires demonstrated respect of their individual tribal sovereignty as well as their history, culture, and governmental structure.
Currently, only four tribes are federally recognized in Louisiana: The Jena Choctaw Band of Indians, the Tunica-Biloxi Tribe of Indians, the Coushatta Tribe of Louisiana, and the Chitimacha Tribe of Louisiana. An additional 11 tribes are recognized by the state.
The Native American Fish and Wildlife Society (NAFWS) is a national Native American non-profit organization that serves as an informative communication network between tribal, federal, and state fish and wildlife management entities. NAFWS maintains a database of Tribal Natural Resource Departments staff.

[FIGURE: and modified by the Louisiana Wildlife Diversity Program (WDP; Figure 2.2). Below are summaries of each ecoregion. See Figure 2.3 for a map of conservation lands within each ecoregion and Appendix H for a list of all managed areas within each ecoregion. East Gulf Coastal Plain The East Gulf Coastal Plan (EGCP) ecoregion extends from southwestern Georgia across western Florida, southern Alabama, and Mississippi, and into the Florida Parishes of Louisiana (Figure 2.4). The western Florida parishes of East Baton Rouge, Livingston, and Ascension feature flat topography and fertile soils, historically supporting primarily Spruce Pine-Hardwood Flatwoods and Small Stream Forest. [FIGURE: following are three associations recognized by the Wildlife Diversity Program in Bottomland Hardwood Forests of Louisiana (LNHP 2009): 1. Hackberry-American Elm-Green Ash Bottomland Hardwood Forest This community occurs in floodplains of major rivers on low ridges, flats, and sloughs in first bottoms (portions of floodplains nearest to rivers, immediately behind natural levees). Soils are seasonally inundated or saturated periodically for one to two months during the growing season. 2. Overcup Oak-Water Hickory Bottomland Hardwood Forest Overcup Oak (Quercus lyrata) and Water Hickory (Carya aquatica) are codominants of this floodplain forest which occurs on low-lying poorly drained flats, sloughs in backwater basins, and on low ridges with clay soils that are subject to inundation. Inundated or saturated soils are generally present for a major portion of the growing season. This community type has long successional stages.]

[FIGURE: SAVANNAS AND WOODLANDSWESTERN GULF COASTAL PLAIN SAVANNAS AND WOODLANDSOUACHITA SAND PRAIRIE AND WOODLANDRarity Rank: GNR/S1 Synonyms: West Gulf Coastal Plain Lowland Sand Prairie, Coastal Plain Sandy Terrace Grassland Ouachita Sand Prairie and Woodland is a unique ecological assemblage imbedded in Bottomland Hardwood Forest and Hardwood Flatwoods floodplains restricted to the Ouachita River watershed spanning northern Louisiana and southern Arkansas. Historically, this community formed as dunes and splay delta ridges on the periphery of Paleolake Monroe during the Pleistocene Epoch(Washington 2001). This community may be inundated under 10 to 30 feet of water for prolonged periods (several months). The substrate consists largely of silty sands with high metal concentrations including copper, zinc, and, particularly, aluminum. Due to the community's unique edaphic and hydrological profile, it supports a diverse variety of wetland and upland plant species. Some of the common plants observed at Louisiana occurrences are Switchgrass (Panicum virgatum), Little Bluestem (Schizachyrium scoparium), Poorjoe (Hexasepalum teres), Sandyland Goat's-rue (Tephrosia onobrychoides), Plumegrass (Erianthus sp.), Thin-paspalum (Paspalum setaceum), Sparkleberry (Vaccinium arboreum), Glade Rushfoil (Croton willdenowii), Woolly Croton (Croton lindheimerii), Redtop Panic Grass (Coleataenia rigidula), Water Dawnflower (Stylisma aquatica), Yaupon Holly (Ilex vomitoria), Wax-myrtle (Morella cerifera), and Black Tupelo (Nyssa sylvatica) (LDWF 2025)]

[FIGURE: G2G3/S1Synonyms: Oak-Farkleberry Sandy Land Xeric Sandhill Woodland develops on deep sandy soils on Tertiary uplands and Pleistocene stream terraces. Most occurrences are in the latter setting. Soils are nutrient-poor, excessively welldrained loamy fine sands. Fire may be an important process maintaining some examples of this community. However, some Xeric Sandhill Woodlands may be isolated by landscape features such as stream bottoms, which naturally protect them from fire, or may have sparse fine fuels, which will not carry fire well. Drought-related tree and shrub mortality may play a role in creating canopy gaps that allow light-loving herbaceous plants to persist. The vegetation composition of Xeric Sandhill Woodland overlaps considerably with that of upland Longleaf Pine savanna and woodland communities that occur on deep, xeric sandy soils. However, Xeric Sandhill Woodlands tend to have more oaks, such as Bluejack Oak (Quercus incana), Blackjack Oak (Quercus marilandica), and Post Oak (Quercus stellata), present in the overstory and midstory. Vegetation structure often differs between these two habitats; Xeric Sandhill Woodland features plants that often have severely stunted growth and sometimes have a sandhill scrub-shrub structural appearance. Xeric Sandhill Woodland tends to be small-scale, inclusional habitats, while the xeric phase of upland Longleaf Pine savanna and woodland is typically more expansive.]

[FIGURE: Marsh and Shrublands are estuarine communities dominated by Black Mangrove (Avicennia germinans). Although sometimes termed a swamp, the physiognomy of the community in Louisiana more closely resembles a shrub thicket or marsh scrub-shrub matrix. The coastal region of Louisiana delimits the northern range of this community due to mangrove's inability to tolerate temperatures below freezing. The top-kill caused by winter freezes also limits mangroves to a shrub-like form (10 feet or less in height). Mangrove habitats are an integral part of the Louisiana Barrier Island system. The mangrove shrubland hasNON-FORESTED GRASSLANDS AND OPEN TERRAINCoastal Mangrove-Marsh and Shrubland, Lafourche Parish]

[FIGURE: Besides freezing weather, other factors affecting mangrove extent are erosion and land subsidence. The mangrove's importance in erosion control was clearly documented by the extreme erosion of Queen Bess Island following the 1983/1984 dieback, and today mangrove is often used for marsh stabilization in coastal restoration projects. Large expanses of mangrove can be viewed near the southern terminus of Louisiana Hwy. 1 on the eastside of Timbalier Bay near Port Fourchon, with patchy occurrences continuing along the highway to Grand Isle. This community can also be found on Elmer's Island Wildlife Refuge, Isles Dernieres Barrier Islands Refuge, and Breton NWR.]

[FIGURE: The major barrier islands include the Chandeleur Islands chain, Grand Isle, the Grand Terre Islands, Timbalier Island, and the Isle Dernieres. Much of the Chandeleur Islands chain is captured by Breton NWR, and the remainder is owned by State Lands and managed by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS). Isle Dernieres Barrier Islands Refuge, managed by LDWF, includes Wine (almost completely submerged), Whiskey, Trinity/East, and Raccoon islands. Grand Isle is the only inhabited Barrier Island, and, as a result, much of the natural habitat has been destroyed; however, extremely valuable habitat remains and should be conserved.]

Louisiana Beach forms along the Gulf-facing shoreline and is low in elevation. Louisiana Beach is usually composed of fine sands and shell hash and is generally less well-developed than beaches along other parts of the Gulf Coast. Louisiana Beach composed of shell fragments is found primarily along the lowenergy shorelines in the western portion of the Louisiana coast and the Barrier Islands in eastern Louisiana. A distinct feature of Louisiana Beach is the shift in prevalence of Sea Oats (Uniola paniculata) to Marshhay Cord Grass (Spartina patens;Barbour et al. 1987).Extent and StatusWell-developed Louisiana Beach with sandy substrate occurs on the Chenier Plain in Cameron Parish in the Oak Grove area west nearly to Sabine Pass. Shell beaches are found mainly on the central part of the coast in Cameron, Vermilion, and Iberia parishes. On the Deltaic Plain, well-developed Louisiana Beach is present on Barrier Islands, and portions of the mainland in Lafourche Parish. Several artificial and natural islands at the mouth of the Mississippi River are developing Louisiana Beach habitat. Exemplary Louisiana Beach habitat occurs on Trinity/ East Island in the Isle Dernieres Barrier Islands Refuge, Timbalier Island, West Grand Terre Island, along the coast from Elmer's Island Wildlife Refuge through Port Fourchon and in the vicinity of Johnson Bayou in Cameron Parish where Louisiana Beach is situated seaward from Coastal Dune Grassland. Louisiana Beach habitat can also be found on Breton NWR. Louisiana Beach Restoration, Lafourche Parish Threats Many Louisiana Beach occurrences are impacted by vehicle traffic and trash accumulation. The maintenance of Louisiana Beach is dependent upon sand supply, which is lacking in most cases due to reduced coarse sediment in the Mississippi River and impediments to longshore sand movement by features such as jetties. Invasive species pose a threat. Potential increased frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones associated with climate change may also threaten this habitat, in concert with inundation resulting from SLR.]

[FIGURE: Rarity Rank: G4G5/S4 Synonyms: River Sandbar Sandbar is a sand/gravel deposit in or adjacent to permanently flowing freshwater contained within a natural channel. Sandbar is composed of coarse to fine-grained alluvial deposits. The community structure is dependent on the mix and stability of substrate, severity and depth of flooding, and permanence of the particular site. The hydrologic regime ranges from intermittently exposed to intermittently flooded. If present, vegetation is dominated by sparse to dense growth of herbaceous plants, with woody plants such as willows (Salix spp.) becoming established when Sandbar is not scoured and reworked. Due to the early successional nature of this community type, it can be invaded by invasive plant species (NatureServe 2015). Sandbar is a critical nesting area for Interior Least Tern (Sternulla antillarum athalassos) and many riverine turtles.EXTENT OF SANDBARNON-FORESTED GRASSLANDS AND OPEN TERRAINExtent and StatusSandbar habitat occurs primarily along the major rivers such as the Mississippi, Red, Pearl, Sabine, and Ouachita rivers. This habitat found within the Mississippi River has shown a general decline over the past 60 years; the USACE reported a 33% decrease in Sandbar habitat in the lower Mississippi River between Memphis, Tennessee, and Baton Rouge, Louisiana, from 1948 to 1994 (USFWS 2015).ThreatsSandbars are subject to frequent human disturbance. Gravel and sand mining pose significant threats to this natural community type. Large rivers are often engineered waterways; the operation of locks and dams, barge traffic, and levees interrupt the natural development and maintenance of Sandbars. Invasive plants and animals threaten this habitat as well, with Feral Hogs being of particular concern.Research Needs and Conservation Actions1. Work with USACE to develop a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) regarding Sandbars management. 2. Work with landowners to develop limits on ORV use and to protect Sandbars from negative impacts of cattle. 3. Support and commission a study examining the need for and economic impact of existing dams on Louisiana rivers. 4. Remove low-impact (unnecessary) structures, particularly on the Red and Ouachita rivers, to restore natural flow. 5. Implement control of invasive species on Sandbars. 6. Work with regulatory agencies to minimize impacts to this habitat from gravel and sand mining operations.]

[FIGURE: Intermediate Terraces (Pleistocene) associated with old Red River deposits in northwestern Louisiana (Morse Clay Prairies), (2) the Fleming Formation (Tertiary-Miocene) in central-western Louisiana, (3) the Jackson Group (Tertiary-Eocene) in central Louisiana, and (4) the Cook Mountain Formation (Tertiary-Eocene) in central and western Louisiana. Although these prairies are grouped together, each site possesses distinct characteristics, sometimes serving as the sole habitat for specific plant SGCN. Soils are stiff calcareous clays with high shrink-swell characteristics and range in color from red to olive-tan to gray-black. The surface soil pH of these systems varies from slightly acidic to alkaline (pH 6.5 to 8.0); however, they typically have an alkaline horizon at or within the upper 30 inches of the soil profile(Kelley and Bordelon 2024,  Smith et al. 1989). Various soil inclusions occur (depending on geology) and may include calcareous concretions (limestone nodules), marine mollusk shells, shark teeth, and gypsum crystals. The herbaceous flora is very diverse and dominated by grasses, composites, and legumes. Regularly occurring fire, alkaline soil, extreme physical soil properties, and drought stress to woody plants are postulated to have acted in concert to generate and perpetuate these upland clay prairies.]

RED BASIN The headwaters of the Red River begin in Curry County, New Mexico, and the river ends 1,360 miles downstream at the Mississippi River (Figure 5.63). The Red River watershed is 69,200 square miles (44,287,823 acres) (Ken Guidry, personal communication) and receives drainage from five states including New Mexico, Texas, Oklahoma, Arkansas, and Louisiana. The Red River drains approximately 7,760 square miles within Louisiana (USACE 1998).The Red River enters Louisiana from Arkansas in the northwest corner of the state and follows a southeasterly course, passing through or forming the boundary of 10 parishes, until it reaches its terminus at the Mississippi River. Shreveport and Alexandria are the principle cities located along the river. The Red River received its name from the high concentration of red soil present in the river following periods of flooding. Much of the basin is wooded, and significant agricultural lands are located within the Red River's historical floodplain.Navigational improvements on the Red River began in the early part of the 19th century. The most recent improvements, part of the $1.9 billion Red River Waterway Project (RRWP) authorized by Congress with the Rivers and Harbors Act of 1968, consisted of dredging a channel 9 feet deep and 200 feet wide and adding a series of five lock and dam complexes to improve navigation from the Mississippi River to Shreveport. Other improvements within the RRWP consisted of developing a comprehensive plan for bank stabilization from the Denison Dam on the Texas-Oklahoma border to the Mississippi River.]

[FIGURE: Sabine River starts in north-central Texas and flows in an easterly direction to the Texas-Louisiana state line near Logansport, Louisiana(Figure 5.64). The Sabine River flows as boundary waters between the two states for some 270 river miles to the Gulf of America, and drains an area of approximately 9,700 square miles, 7,190 square miles of which are above the Toledo Bend Reservoir (A.I.D.Associates 1981). Roughly 2,510 square miles of drainage are situated below the dam, which is located at River Mile 200. The entire basin drains 3,257 square miles within the state. The Toledo Bend Reservoir was constructed in the 1960s and became operational in 1969. Operation of a hydroelectric plant on the Sabine River has affected water flows on the lower portions of the river since that time. However, as part of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission relicensing process in 2013, several new requirements were incorporated into plant operations. Intended to benefit fish and wildlife in the system, the new operating license requirements included the elimination of hypolimnetic releases (i.e. releases of a mixture of upper and lower water strata) and changes to generating schedules that will reduce impacts to fishes. The elimination of hypolimnetic releases will enable passage of the migratory American Eel past the dam.The northern and central portions of the basin are primarily wooded with scattered agricultural lands throughout. Most of the basin supports cultivated and ruderal pinelands, with the majority of hardwoods located along principle drainages. Along the coastal zone, almost all Freshwater Marsh was converted to Intermediate and Brackish Marsh by the late 1970s as a result of saltwater intrusion and increased tidal influence (LaCoast 2005). Within lower Sabine Lake, one of the largest unharvested oyster reefs in the world exists, estimated at 10 square kilometers(Moore 2008, Nevins et al. 2014). This reef habitat has received extensive interest in recent years as the state of Texas and coastal protection and restoration advocates in Louisiana have pushed strongly for the continued prohibition of commercial harvest. In 2018, harvest of oysters was prohibited in Sabine Lake by act of the Louisiana Legislature (SB451).]

[FIGURE: .65). It includes all of Terrebonne Parish and parts of Lafourche, Assumption, St. Martin, St. Mary, Iberville, and Ascension parishes.The extreme northern portion of the basin is primarily agricultural lands, which continue south along its eastern edge within the historical floodplains of the Mississippi River and Bayou Lafourche. The western half of the basin consists of Bottomland Hardwood Forest and Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest. The coastal zone consists of Freshwater, Freshwater Floating, and Intermediate marshes inland and Brackish and Salt marshes near the bays and Gulf (LaCoast 2005). Approximately 729,000 acres of the Terrebonne Basin are wetlands, which consist of about 21% freshwater swamp and 79% marsh (LaCoast 2005). The two primary water sources that enter this system are rain water and flood water from the Atchafalaya River containing nutrient-rich sediments which inundate the southwestern coastal marshes (LaCoast 2005). As is the case in other basins, however, coastal land loss is a significant threat, and numerous projects have been implemented to address the issue (CPRA 2023). The lower Terrebonne estuary is separated from the open Gulf by the Isles Dernieres and Timbalier barrier island chains. Water exchange with the Gulf of America is accomplished through numerous tidal inlets and passes. The barrier islands of the Terrebonne Basin are considered some of the most rapidly deteriorating barrier shorelines in the United States. These islands, including the Isle Dernieres Barrier Islands Refuge, are critically important for multiple bird SGCN, which makes maintenance and restoration of these areas of the utmost importance. Many of these islands, including Raccoon, Whiskey, Trinity/East, and Timbalier, have received restoration and nourishment through state and federal projects but will continue to need attention to remain subaerial.]

[FIGURE: Figure 8.6 depicts a map of terrestrial resiliency in Louisiana using the Southeast Conservation Blueprint's Resilient Terrestrial Sites indicator. The indicator originates from The Nature Conservancy's (TNC) Resilient Land data and measures two factors that influence resilience. The first, landscape diversity, reflects the number of microhabitats and climatic gradients created by topography, elevation, and hydrology. The second, local connectedness, reflects the degree of habitat fragmentation and strength of barriers to species movement. Highly resilient sites contain many different habitat niches that support biodiversity and allow species to move freely through the landscape to find suitable microclimates as the climate changes.]

[FIGURE: FIGURE 8.7. Map of the Southeast Conservation Blueprint's Resilient Coastal Sites indicator.]

[FIGURE: This type of survey is most useful in clear, shallow creeks (<1 meter in depth). Monitoring agencies have utilized this tool to survey for Louisiana Pearlshell since the 1980s. This labor-intensive technique involves the surveyor traversing the length of the clear, headwater streams where this mussel occurs, scanning the entire creek bottom through a transparent-bottomed bucket, and counting the individual Louisiana Pearlshells observed. Due to observed statistical limitations and the substantial time commitment required, monitoring agencies developed a statistically rigorous protocol in 2014. This protocol focuses solely on delineating and counting mussels in the dense aggregations formed by this species, thereby eliminating the need to travel the entire length of the creek. For more detailed information, see Quantitative Ecological Services, Inc. (2014).]

[FIGURE: e. consecutive days without rainfall), increasing the intensity of naturally occurring droughts in the future(Runkel et al. 2022). Although, hydrological droughts have become less frequent in eastern regions of the country since the 19th century, higher increases in evapotranspiration (the processes by which water is transferred to the atmosphere from Earth's surface) have generally made the Southeast more drought-prone than the Northeast(Hoffman et al. 2023). However, future changes in the annual number of consecutive extreme dry days in Louisiana are fortunately not projected to increase substantially -by 2-4% by 2025-2049 and by 1-6% by 2050-2074 depending on ecoregions and emission scenario(Alder 2024).]

[FIGURE: Figure10.15 shows the change in the projected number of extremely hot days in Louisiana at GWL 1.5 (2.7°F warmer than the 1991-2020 time period) and GWL 2 (3.6°F warmer than the 1991-2020 time period). Overall, the projections used in the NCA5 indicate that the number of extremely hot days will increase across Louisiana, with the most pronounced increases occurring in the northern, inland portions of the state, although the magnitude of change often varies greatly by GWL or SSP scenario and location within the state (Figure10.15).]

[FIGURE: FIGURE 10.14. EPA level III Ecoregions of Louisiana.]

[FIGURE: FIGURE 10.15. Projected future changes in the number of days of Extreme Maximum Temperatures (threshold temperature of 100°F) compared to the last 30 years (1991-2020) based on GWL 1.5 (global temperature increase of 2.7°F) and on GWL 2 (global temperature increase of 3.6°F). Figure created from data in the "Atlas of the 5th National Climate Assessment" web tool (https:// atlas.globalchange.gov/) accessed on 1 February 2024 (USGCRP et al. 2024).]

[FIGURE: FIGURE 10.16. Change in the number of days when minimum temperatures reach or drop below freezing (32°F) compared to the previous 30 years (1991-2020) based on GWL 1.5 (global temperature increase of 2.7°F) and on GWL 2 (global temperature increase of 3.6°F. Figure created from data in the "Atlas of the 5th National Climate Assessment" web tool (USGCRP et al. 2024).]

[TABLE: SCENARIOS AND DRIVERS Sea level rise and subsidence, processes further exacerbated by human actions, have been identified as the principal drivers influencing coastal land loss. Fifty- year model predictions of two scenarios: low (moderate future environmental conditions) and high (more severe environmental conditions) estimate significant land loss (1,100 to 3,000 sq. miles). These data emphasize the importance of implementation of the Coastal Master Plan projects (CPRA 2023). ]

*In 2019, the economic impact of wildlife watching (e.g., bird watching) in Louisiana was associated with $702 million in retail sales with a total economic impact of $1.29 billion. Wildlife watching supported more than 16,000 jobs and contributed $474.8 million in salaries and wages. It generated $79.7 million in federal tax revenues and $87.1 million in state and local tax revenues (Southwick Associates 2021). *

*Life History (Reproduction, Movement, Survivorship) Determine Habitat Needs Population Monitoring Develop and Promote BMPs (Addressing Bycatch) Promote Quality Habitat Outreach and Increase Awareness displace or outcompete native species, can alter vegetation through herbivory, and can consume the eggs, larvae, and adults of native aquatic organisms. Efforts to minimize the introduction of non-native species is paramount, as is the development of effective measures to minimize, mitigate, or eliminate the impacts of established non-natives. Surveillance monitoring, increased awareness, and enforcement must act in concert to address the threat of non-natives. Monitor habitat changes (e.g., loss of grass, rocking/bulkhead installation/dredging etc.) near historical sampling sites, possibly change or add sites with more natural shorelines. h Incorporate findings regarding water quality changes such as PFAs, industrial spills, algae blooms, pharmaceuticals and other detrimental contaminants found in inshore environments. Partner with the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers (USACE) to encourage the beneficial use of dredge material. h Work with partners to incorporate strategies for Marine SGCN into future coastal restoration efforts. h Continue efforts to characterize bycatch within Louisiana commercial fisheries.*

[TABLE:  All marine turtles occurring in Louisiana are federally and state listed: three of the five are endangered (Kemp's Ridley Sea Turtle, Hawksbill Sea Turtle, and Leatherback Sea Turtle); and the Loggerhead Sea Turtle and Green Sea Turtle are threatened. U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service recovery plans have been developed for each species. Other federally and state listed species include the Dusky Gopher Frog, Gopher Tortoise, Ringed Map Turtle, Eastern Pinesnake, and Louisiana Pinesnake. In August 2024, the Pearl River Map Turtle became the species most recently added to the federally threatened list.]

[TABLE]
*TABLE 4 .11. cont. BIRD THREATS *

| Threats to Louisiana birds include those widely recognized |
| --- |
| as significant at the continental scale, with habitat loss and |
| degradation being the greatest of these threats. Migratory |
| species may be threatened by habitat loss within either or |
| both their breeding and nonbreeding ranges, including loss of |
| migratory stopover sites between breeding and nonbreeding |
| sites. Primary drivers of habitat loss and degradation (including |
| fragmentation) include industrial, commercial, and residential |
| development; conversion of natural areas to agriculture; |
| intensification of agricultural practices; and changing forest |
| conditions (e.g., due to fire suppression). Coastal disturbance |
| and land loss represent particularly important threats in |
| Louisiana, given the regional, and in some cases continental or |
| global, importance of the state's coastal habitats as breeding |
| sites for many state and regional bird SGCN. Indeed, among |
| Louisiana's most imperiled bird habitats are Barrier Islands and |
| coastal forests (e.g., Coastal Live Oak-Hackberry Forest, Barrier |
| Island Live Oak Forest, etc.), all of which are threatened primarily |
| by natural system modification, sea level rise, subsidence, |
| tropical cyclones, and development. The high mobility of birds |
| may mitigate impacts to some species, but the risk to coastal |
| obligates is exacerbated by effects of climate change. |
| In addition to loss of habitat or habitat function, birds are |
| threatened by direct anthropogenic mortality in the form |
| of predation by outdoor cats (Felis catus), collisions with |
| infrastructure (e.g., buildings, communication towers, power |
| lines, wind turbines) and vehicles, ingestion of plastics, |
| electrocutions from power lines, fisheries' bycatch (e.g., |
| entanglement in fishing gear), and other factors. Predation |
| by cats (including outdoor, owned cats) is the most significant |
| source of anthropogenic mortality, likely exceeding 2 billion |
| (and up to 4 billion) bird deaths per year in the United States |

[/TABLE]

* Acidic Bay Forest, Schoolhouse Springs Preserve, Jackson Parish on steep slopes and in stream valleys often having a closed canopy. Calcareous Forest on upper slopes and ridge tops was likely woodland, where dry site conditions and fire maintained a more open canopy. Soils are stiff calcareous clays, not quite as alkaline as in the prairies (surface pH ~ 6.5-7.5+), with high shrink-swell characteristics and, typically, have an alkaline horizon at or within the upper 30 inches of the soil profile*

* Pondcypress Flooded WoodlandPondcypress-Swamp Tupelo Forest is strongly associated with blackwater streams and floodplains. It occupies the backwater portions of larger swamplands in places well removed from active stream channels. This habitat can also occupy isolated depressions in East Gulf Coastal Plain flatwoods embedded within a matrix of Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna. This swamp type may grade into Baldcypress-Tupelo-Swamp Forest which is more influenced by river flooding. Pondcypress-Swamp Tupelo Forest is acidic and nutrient poor. Floristic diversity is significantly higher than that of Baldcypress-Tupelo Swamp Forest. Fire may play a role in the stand density and species distribution of Pondcypress-Swamp Tupelo Forest, which can have a fairly open to dense canopy.*

* ThreatsEastern Hillside Seepage Bog is a very rare natural community in Louisiana. Most existing occurrences are degraded by woody encroachment due to inadequate fire. Residential development is also a serious threat as such development is occurring at rapid rates, replacing bogs or is occurring in close proximity to bogs, impeding proper management. Invasive species, especially Feral Hogs, pose serious threats to the remaining bogs in this region. Continue to encourage landowners to implement BMPs and adopt Sustainable Forestry Initiative standards in the management of this habitat type. 3. Provide cost-share funds for landowners to reduce or eliminate costs associated with conducting prescribed burns on their property.Eastern Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna are herb-dominated wetlands that are naturally sparsely stocked with Longleaf Pine. This community is most often dominated by numerous grasses and sedges in the understory and is noted for very high plant diversity, including insectivorous plants, showy orchids, lilies, and many other types of wildflowers. Pine savannas historically dominated the Gulf Coastal Plain flatwoods regions of southeast and southwest Louisiana(Smith 1996).*
