[TABLE]
* Historically, the eastern Florida Parishes of Louisiana were dominated by extensive stands of Longleaf Pine. Now only 1% to 5% of the original estimated 1 million to 2 million acres of Eastern Upland Longleaf Pine Savanna and Woodland remains(Smith 1993(Smith , 1999)). Habitat conversion, development, and fire exclusion are factors in this habitat loss. Today, there are a few thousand acres in small blocks scattered across this area. LDWF owns and manages Sandy Hollow WMA which is the largest tract of Eastern Upland Longleaf Pine Savanna and Woodland remaining in Louisiana. Louisiana State University's (LSU) Lee Memorial Forest located in Washington Parish also contains high-quality Eastern Upland Longleaf Pine Savanna and Woodland. There are several tracts of restorable habitat on private lands in the eastern Florida Parishes, some of which are enrolled in the NRCS Longleaf Pine Initiative, which provides funding for habitat restoration; and some properties have and may continue to receive assistance with prescribed fire through LDWF programs.*

[TABLE]
* Sparta Sand Ponds may be ancient inter-dune depressions that formed in dry shifting sands during historical dry climate intervals. Known examples are mostly wooded, but it is possible this is an artifact of fire exclusion. Black-fruited Spikesedge is a sand pond specialist, and its presence at one Louisiana Sparta Sand Pond is strong evidence that it is a natural feature. Louisiana muck bogs are hypothesized to be younger than some of the muck bogs in the Post Oak Savanna of Texas. However, the formation of West Gulf Coastal Plain Muck Bog seems similar in Louisiana as in Texas. Surrounding, deep sandy soils efficiently capture and transmit precipitation as groundwater, which converges on stream valleys. Small streams become impeded by Beaver (Castor canadensis), and constant saturation from seepage leads to peat accumulation.*

[TABLE:  Western Hillside Seepage Bog is underlain by an impervious clay or sandstone layer that, when conditions are right, causes groundwater to constantly seep to the soil surface. The herbaceous ground cover is dense, continuous, and floristically rich. It is dominated by sedges, grasses and grass-like plants, and many kinds of unusual forbs, including Yellow Trumpet Pitcher Plant and a variety of orchid species. Many species are restricted to this habitat and the closely allied Western Longleaf Pine Flatwoods Savanna. Patches of shrubs are often present within bogs and can become more prevalent, possibly degrading the habitat, if fire is excluded from the system. Western Hillside Seepage Bog is embedded in what is now, or historically was, Western Upland Longleaf Pine Savanna and Woodland, which is a fire-driven system. This bog type, therefore, evolved with frequent growing-season fire events. Among other things, frequent fire deters invasion by shrubs and trees and stimulates growth, flowering, and seed production by indigenous bog herbs]

[TABLE]
* Sandhill Pine Forest, Clayhill Pine ForestThis habitat occurs in the hilly uplands in western and central Louisiana. It occurs on acidic sandy loams to acidic clays associated with Pleistocene or Tertiary formations. Soil moisture regimes range from dry-mesic to xeric. The community is characteristically dissected by small to large creek bottoms. Longleaf Pine is the dominant overstory species, and in locations where fire has frequently occurred, it is often the only canopy species. Where fire is less frequent or suppressed, a number of overstory species may occur. The herbaceous flora may be exceedingly diverse if fire has frequently occurred. Grasses, composites, and legumes are predominant in the ground layer.*

* 55 plant species in Intermediate Marsh versus only 17 species in Salt Marsh. Intermediate Marsh is often dominated by Marshhay Cord Grass, bulltongues (Sagittaria spp.) and bullsedges (Schoenoplectus spp.). Two other major autotrophic groups in Intermediate Marsh are epiphytic and benthic algae. Intermediate Marsh occupies the smallest acreage of any of the four marsh types. This marsh type is important to many bird species including large numbers of wintering waterfowl. Intermediate Marsh is also critical nursery habitat for larval marine organisms. Gradual changes in salinity conditions can cause this habitat to shift towards Brackish Marsh.Hartley et al. 2000). In the Chenier Plain of southwestern Louisiana, Rockefeller Wildlife Refuge and Sabine NWR contain Intermediate Marsh. In the Deltaic Plain, Intermediate Marsh can be found on Pointe-aux-Chenes, Pass-a-Loutre, Pearl River, Biloxi, and Manchac WMAs as well as Bayou Sauvage and Big Branch Marsh NWRs, and Jean Lafitte National Historical Park and Preserve. Research Needs and Conservation Actions 1. Support efforts by the NRCS Plant Materials Center and other growers to produce a greater variety of plant species for the restoration of coastal habitats and for mitigation. 2. Work with USACE and state agencies to ensure water control structures provide the maximum benefit to Intermediate Marsh. 3. Work with landowners and partners to develop BMPs for livestock production in this habitat.*

*FIGURE 5.41 Research Needs and Conservation Actions 1. Work with partners to implement Louisiana's Comprehensive Master Plan for a Sustainable Coast and to promote the protection and restoration of Barrier Islands (including Isle Dernieres Barrier Islands Refuge and the Chandeleur Islands) to benefit SGCN. 2. Work with local governing boards to recommend limits on vehicle use on undeveloped portions of Barrier Islands. 3. Support efforts by partners for shoreline stabilization and habitat restoration, prioritizing inclusion of habitat features important for SGCN. In addition, limited nutrient availability and substrate instability also affect coastal dune vegetation. The vegetative cover ranges from sparse to fairly dense and is dominated by salt spray tolerant plants. Dune swales may be extensive and are considered as inclusions in this natural community. Dunes and ridges may be shifted or eroded by storm floods, destroying vegetation. Hypothetically, if dunes remain stable, allowing natural succession to progress, then Coastal Dune Shrub Thicket is formed. This community occurs on established sand dunes and beach ridges on Barrier Islands and the mainland coast. Coastal Dune Shrub Thicket is of very limited extent in Louisiana due to relatively poor development of coastal dunes. The sites are typically xeric and moderately exposed to salt spray. This community normally appears as a relatively dense stand of shrubs, often covered with a dense growth of lichens and various vine species. This habitat type may be destroyed by sand dune migration or erosion and may be replaced by Coastal Dune Grassland. Threats Both Coastal Dune Grassland and Coastal Dune Shrub Thicket habitats are threatened by several sources of disturbance and may face increased tropical cyclone frequency and intensity associated with climate change. Inadequate sand supply is a possible long term problem, especially for Coastal Dune Grassland. Sand supply is limited by the relatively sedimentimpoverished Mississippi River and impediments to longshore deposition of sediments.*

* ThreatsRemaining occurrences of this very rare habitat are threatened by inadequate fire, incompatible grazing management, improper mitigation banking practices, and disturbance from human activities as well as invasive species. Lack of fire is particularly acute in railroad prairie remnants, which are being severely encroached upon by brush. Invasive species such as Chinese Tallow Tree and Feral Hogs threaten prairie remnants. Feral Hogs are particularly destructive in wetter prairies. More than half of the Coastal Prairie remnants, including the six largest Coastal Prairie remnants, and over 75% of the remaining Coastal Prairie acreage in Louisiana are restricted to Cameron and Calcasieu parishes(Early 2025).A rising threat to Coastal Prairie in Louisiana comes from unsound mitigation practices and permitting. The largest known, contemporary, single loss of Coastal Prairie acreage (approximately 31 acres) resulted from an improperly permitted mitigation bank, which allowed the conversion of a wetland Coastal Prairie remnant to a forested mitigation bank(Early 2025). In recent years, approximately 5,770 acres on Coastal Prairie-classified soils have been converted to "Bottomland Hardwood Forest" mitigation types, while only 0.11 acres of this area was actually classified as potential Bottomland Hardwood Forest soil(Early 2025). In addition, three coastal prairie remnants have been completely surrounded by several "Bottomland Hardwood Forest" mitigation banks, further fragmenting and isolating these Coastal Prairie remnants from other grassland types and potential reconnections or expansions via restoration efforts(Early 2025).*

Another major issue is hypoxia, which makes the lower water column less suitable for fishes and invertebrates(Rabalais et al. 2002). In addition, dredging, fishing activities, pollution, and invasive species further threaten the health of this habitat(Galloway et al. 2000).*

[TABLE:  The source of the river is in the Ouachita Mountains of west-central Arkansas near the Oklahoma border. The river flows south through northeast Louisiana and joins the Tensas River north of the town of Jonesville to form the Black River, which empties into the Red River. The total length of the river is 542 miles. In Louisiana, the Ouachita Basin covers 10,000 square miles of drainage area(LDEQ 1993)  which mostly consists of rich alluvial plains cultivated in soybeans, cotton, and corn. The northwest corner of the basin is cultivated in Loblolly Pine plantations. Bayou Bartholomew and Bayou D'Arbonne are the major tributaries of the Ouachita River with Bayou Bartholomew being home to one of the most diverse assemblages of freshwater mussels and fishes in the United States.There are two sets of locks and dams on the Ouachita River in Louisiana. The Jonesville and Columbia locks and dams were constructed by the USACE and opened to navigation in 1972. Each structure impounds a slack-water pool approximately 100 miles long. The Catahoula Diversion Channel and Control Structure and the Little River Closure Dam are located in the Jonesville Lock and Dam pool southwest of Jonesville. The diversion channel diverts flows from Catahoula Lake into Black]

[TABLE]
* Pearl River Map Turtle (Graptemys pearlensis) from the Pearl Basin Many rivers drain the Florida Parishes, introducing fresh water into Lakes Maurepas, Pontchartrain, and Borgne. The largest of these, the Pearl and Amite rivers, have headwaters in Mississippi. The rivers of this basin have eroded and incised the uplands to form distinct river valleys. Lakes Maurepas, Pontchartrain, and Borgne form a shallow brackish receiving basin for freshwater from the Amite, Tickfaw, Blind, Tangipahoa, Tchefuncte, and Pearl rivers as well as Bayou Lacombe and Bayou Bonfouca. Freshwater is also introduced through regional drainage and diversion canals, whereas salt water enters these lakes from the Gulf of America via the Mississippi Sound, Mississippi River Gulf Outlet (MRGO), Chef Menteur Pass, and Rigolets Pass. The Mississippi River Deltaic Plain lies to the south of these lakes. The extreme eastern edge of the basin is bordered by thin ribbons of sand and marsh known as the Chandeleur Islands. These islands are the headland remnants of the St. Bernard Delta of the Mississippi River but have undergone extensive erosion through the years, most recently due to strong hurricanes such as Katrina, Gustav, Isaac, and Ida. These islands are of critical importance to a number of SGCN, including sea turtles and many species of shorebirds and colonial nesting waterbirds.*

The techniques outlined in this plan are not exhaustive but include commonly used and widely accepted practices for each taxa group.In Chapter 4: Species of Greatest Conservation Need, each taxa group has an action table that includes research and monitoring needs for each species. Column headings include research and monitoring efforts most commonly required for that taxon and are categorized in the table as determine abundance, implement population monitoring, study life history, determine habitat needs, and others. For Crustaceans and Non-crustacean Arthropods, the data gaps and research needs are so extensive that a table would not have been informative; instead, the needs are listed within the General and Species-specific Research Actions and Needs section. Although many of the needs are similar among the taxa groups, monitoring approaches may vary among species. In some instances, a standardized monitoring protocol does not exist, and research into appropriate and effective techniques or gear types may be necessary. Below is a description of the monitoring techniques used by LDWF.]

[TABLE:  Using nested containers allows the collector to more easily remove the inner container, in which specimens are trapped, while leaving the outer container in the substrate for easier redeployment of the trap. Usually each trap is covered with a roof to prevent filling from rain and disturbance by curious animals. Pitfall traps may be deployed dry or partly filled with alcohol or other killing/preserving agents; trap-checking intervals can vary among types. These traps are effective for capturing ground-dwelling arthropods in many different habitats, but, typically, require a settling in period to avoid disturbance effects from trap installation(Montogomery  et al. 2021).Hohbein and Conway (2018) provide a thorough review of pitfall trap-related studies, and Brown and Matthews]
[TABLE:  Common practice includes utilizing multiple active sampling techniques within a single survey effort to increase detection rates. It is important to record abiotic factors (e.g., temperature, time, relative humidity) during survey efforts to evaluate how these parameters influence species' presence and aid in comparisons among survey results.]
[TABLE: -Recapture and Mark-Resight Surveys Mark-recapture surveys involve uniquely marking captured individuals with the intent to recapture those marked individuals at a later time. Biologists can gather information on species' life history data such as growth rates, maturity rates, and age from recaptured individuals. This method, which may be paired with other survey techniques, can provide relative population abundance and population density as well as estimates of population size and structure(Winne 2013). Commonly used marking methods include but are not limited to, scute drilling and notching, paint marking, scale clipping and cauterizing, Passive Integrated Transponder (PIT) tags, and Visible Implant Elastomer (VIE) tags.]

*Avian Productivity and Survivorship (MAPS) and Monitoring Overwinter Survival (MoSI) Rosenberg et al. 2019). The success of the BBS hinges on contributions of volunteers proficient with vocal identification of all of a region's bird species. LDWF highly values the BBS and continues its commitment to ensure that as many BBS routes as possible are surveyed every year through staff effort and recruitment of qualified volunteers. See: www.pwrc. usgs.gov/bbs/. protocols (e.g., BBS) fail to represent adequately. Coordinated marsh bird monitoring has developed since 2010, both within Louisiana, spurred by response to and assessment of the 2010 Deepwater Horizon oil spill, and continentally with the publication of a standardized marsh bird monitoring protocol(Conway 2011). The Conway protocol consists of three surveys per season, encompassing the breeding phenology of all focal species, conducted at a number of permanent survey points located at least 400 m apart. Individual surveys are essentially point counts that consist of alternating periods of silence and periods of focal species call-broadcast (to elicit vocal response), and utilize distance sampling to estimate detection rates. North American marsh bird monitoring data are housed on the Avian Knowledge Network. Initiated in 1989 and coordinated by the Institute for Bird Populations (IBP), the MAPS program represents the standard for the collection of breeding bird demographic data utilizing LDWF Waterfowl Surveys LDWF conducts several different aerial surveys to develop population indices for monitoring relative changes in the abundance and distribution of different guilds and species of waterfowl. Annual surveys of the coastal zone between the Gulf of America and U.S. Highway 90 are conducted from September through January along north-south transects that are one-quarter mile in width and range from eight to 48 miles in length. These transects are spaced at 7.5-mile intervals in southwestern Louisiana and at 15-mile intervals in southeastern Louisiana, resulting in 3% and 1.5% sampling rates in the two areas, respectively. Observers conduct these transect surveys using a fixed-wing aircraft flying at an altitude of 125 feet, at approximately 100 mph, estimating counts of waterfowl by species and habitat type. Separate aerial, transect surveys are also conducted to estimate the number of scaup (Lesser Scaup and Greater Scaup, combined) on Lakes Pontchartrain and Borgne in December and January each year and to estimate the number of breeding Mottled Ducks in the West Gulf Coast population in April. The Mottled Duck survey, which occurs simultaneously with a comparable survey in Texas, consists of 42 north-south transect lines surveyed using a low-flying, zigzagging helicopter; randomly selected five-mile segments are re-surveyed to generate a visibility correction factor.*

[TABLE]
* dogs have assisted with wildlife research and management for centuries, there is renewed interest in the use of detection dogs, especially for cryptic mammals. When a variety of scent samples are available, trainers can teach dogs to locate signs of scent in the wild. When dogs detect scat, researchers can analyze it for genetics, physiological characteristics, diet, and parasites. Some southeastern states have used this methodology in an effort to locate Long-tailed Weasels and other mustelids(Jachowski et al. 2024, Hassler et al. 2024). Limitations to this technique include length of time to train, lifespan of the dog, personality of the dog, ability to secure an appropriate number of scent samples for training, lack of standardized protocols, and lack of understanding regarding degradation of scent in the wild(Jachowski et al. 2024). Despite the difficulties of training detection dogs, this technique can be a more cost effective means of detecting species than many common practices such as camera traps and hair snares(Long et al. 2018).*

[TABLE:  Gauging the effectiveness of conservation actions outlined in the SWAP is critically important to ensure that conservation goals are met. LDWF monitors the effectiveness of State Wildlife Grants projects through the use of the USFWS Office of Conservation Investment Tracking and Reporting Actions for the Conservation of Species (TRACS) reporting module. TRACS provides a standardized grant reporting tool that allows users to create, manage, review, and approve grant proposals and performance reports. This enables the USFWS and state agencies to highlight program accountability and adherence to federal reporting requirements, demonstrate sound grant management, and provide transparency on the use of grant funds. TRACS also documents grant performance and standardizes reporting of accomplishments through numerical metrics identified by objectives that are specific, measureable, achievable, relevant, and time bound (SMART). SMART objectives provide an easy way to demonstrate achievement of goals outlined in the project. A list of past and current SWG projects can be found on the LDWF website (www.wlf.louisiana.gov/page/completed-and-ongoing-louisiana-swg-projects).]

[TABLE:  Table 10.2). States can use the goal report to assess both progress in their state and overall trends for the broader Southeast region. The goal report identifies where states may be able to facilitate discussion around conservation actions for shared priorities or ways to contribute to monitoring programs that inform the annual report. The Recent Trends in Southeastern Ecosystems (2024) goal report is based on a synthesis of 14 different assessments to evaluate indicator trends. Assessments ranged from remotely sensed data like the National Land Cover Database to long-term volunteer-driven monitoring programs like the USGS Breeding Bird Survey. Additional assessments used included America's Longleaf Restoration Initiative Range-wide Accomplishments reports, Forest Inventory and Analysis, Gopher Tortoise Candidate Conservation Agreement reports, USDA Soil and Water Resources Conservation Act reports, Blueprint, International Shorebird Survey, EPA 303(d) state The Southeast Blueprint (v2024) connectivity analysis showing hubs and corridors in the state of Louisiana and select surrounding watersheds shared with Mississippi, Arkansas, and Texas.]

Fish taxa have the most species categorized as "very high concern" and comprise 30% of all RSGCN within the Southeast region. Louisiana identified 345 animal SGCN in the 2015 SWAP and has stewardship responsibility of 172 RSGCN. The most common RSGCN taxa types represented in Louisiana are birds (53), reptiles (31), and fishes (30) (Figure 10.7). Louisiana has shared stewardship of its SGCN and RSGCN with multiple other states, it has the most in common with adjacent states. Louisiana has the greatest number of RSGCN in common with Mississippi (130) (Figure 10.8). Other states which with Louisiana shares a high number of RSGCN include Texas (n=82) and South Carolina (N=67) (Figure 10.8).*

Two common tools that have been used to determine climate vulnerability of species are(1) the Climate Change Vulnerability Index (CCVIs)(Young et al. 2016) developed by NatureServe and (2) environmental niche modeling-based vulnerability estimates produced by the academic research community. The CCVI is a worksheet-based tool where users apply expert opinions, as well as readily available information about a species' natural history, distribution, and landscape circumstances, to predict whether the species will likely suffer a range contraction and/ or population reduction due to climate change. [TABLE:  Identifying adaptation strategies to address impacts and vulnerability is a step towards climate resiliency. Climate adaptation and mitigation come in various forms, and multiple frameworks have been built to support planning and implementation of climate adaptation. Most climate adaptation frameworks include: 1. Recognizing limitations to adaptation such as the degree of natural resource management, availability of funding, or staffing capacity; and 2. Deciding which type(s) of adaptation approach(es) meet specific conservation goals. Two well-known examples of frameworks include the Resist-Accept-Direct (RAD) Framework developed by the U.S. National Park Service and the Climate Adaptation in Practice: Resistance, Resilience and Transition (RRT) Framework developed by Colorado State University. There are also multiple sources for adaptation and mitigation strategies. The Adaptation Workbook (Swanston et al. 2016), a compilation of resources developed by the Northern Institute of Applied Climate Science and its partners, provides an excellent overview of strategies.]

University of Illinois h University of Louisiana at Lafayette h University of Lousiana Monroe h University of Tennessee h University of Texas at Tyler Range Rank -range used to indicate uncertainty about the exact status of a taxon or ecosystem type (e.g., G2G3) GU: Unrankable -Species may be imperiled, but lack of information or substantially conflicting information about status or trends makes ranking impossible. Critically Imperiled -at very high risk of extirpation³ in Louisiana due to very restricted range, very few populations or occurrences [5 or fewer known populations], very steep declines, severe threats, or other factors S2: Imperiled -at high risk of extirpation in Louisiana due to restricted range, few populations or occurrences [6 to 20 extant populations], steep declines, severe threats, or other factors S3: Vulnerable -at moderate risk of extirpation in Louisiana due to a fairly restricted range, relatively few populations or occurrences [21 to 100 extant populations], recent and widespread declines, threats, or other factors S4:Apparently Secure -at a fairly low risk of extirpation in Louisiana due to an extensive range and/or many populations or occurrences [100 to 1000 known extant populations], but with possible cause for some concern as a result of local recent declines, threats, or other factors S5: Secure -at very low or no risk of extirpation in Louisiana due to a very extensive range, abundant populations or occurrences [1000+ extant populations], with little to no concern from declines or threats S#S#: Range Rank -range used to indicate uncertainty about the exact status of a taxon or community type (e.g., S1S2) SU: Unrankable -Species may be imperiled, but lack of information or substantially conflicting information about status or trends makes ranking impossible.State Rank Qualifiers: B, N, and M -If a species' occurrence or abundance in Louisiana is seasonal or otherwise temporally dependent, State Ranks may contain qualifiers to designate separate ranks; this is most commonly found in birds or other migratory elements. Resident populations of species that are not augmented by migrant populations and migrant species in which abundance remains the same for both breeding and nonbreeding will not have qualifiers; instead, those species receive a single rank applicable the entire year (e.g., Northern Bobwhite, a resident species not augmented by migrants, is ranked S3 in Louisiana). S#B: Breeding -Numerical rank refers to the breeding population of the species; if not followed by "S#N," then the species is only found in Louisiana during its breeding season (e.g., Louisiana Waterthrush breeds in Louisiana but is absent from the state in nonbreeding season; its State Rank is S3B). S#N: Non-breeding -Numerical rank refers to the nonbreeding population of the species; if not preceded by "S#B," then the species is only found in Louisiana during its nonbreeding season. S#B, S#N: Breeding, Non-breeding -Numerical ranks refer to breeding and non-breeding populations, respectively (e.g., Grasshopper Sparrow is a critically imperiled breeding species, but the species occurs in greater abundance in non-breeding season; its State Rank is S1B, S3N). S#M: Migrant -migrant species occurring regularly on migration at particular staging areas or concentration spots where the species might warrant conservation attention; rank refers to the aggregating transient population of the species in Louisiana.Endangered -at risk of extinction; take or harassment of this species is a violation of State and federal laws. Threatened -at risk of becoming endangered; take or harassment of this species is a violation of State and federal laws. Prohibited -possession of this species is prohibited; no legal harvest or legal possession is allowed without valid Scientific Research and Collecting Permit issued by Department. Restricted -harvest restrictions regarding the take and possession of this species exist; see current seasons and regulations at www.wlf.louisiana.gov. Delisted -no longer listed as State threatened or endangered Field blank (i.e. null) -usually indicates that the taxon does not have any state status; note that this does not preclude protection under other possible applicable federal protections (e.g., Bald and Golden Eagle Protection Act, Migratory Bird Treaty Act, etc.) or municipal or other local protections (e.g., bird sanctuary designations).*

## CHANDELEURS

The Chandeleurs COA was delineated in 2015 to capture priority areas for protection and restoration actions of seagrass and barrier island ecosystems (Table 
7
.12). This COA was included in the 2025 set without further analysis. However, the 2023 Blueprint Report (see supplemental materials) identified only 18% of seagrass and barrier island ecosystems in this part of the state that scored in fair to good or better coastal condition. Further, the assessment found 2,211 acres with seagrasses present and 97% of the acreage ranked above the 70th percentile for sea turtle habitat. The area also showed about 15% being above the 70th percentile for marine mammals. However, NOAA sea level rise inundation data indicates nearly all the island acres evaluated would be affected by a 2' sea level rise by 2060. 

## BIOME

## DEPARTMENT OF DEFENSE PRIORITIES AND LOUISIANA

The Department of Defense (DoD) manages nearly 29 million acres within the United States. While one of the primary functions of these lands and waters are to support vital military readiness testing, training, and operations, they also encompass some of the most important and biodiverse lands under federal control. DoD managed lands provide habitat for nearly 500 federally listed plant and animal species and for over 550 additional at-risk species. Sixty federally listed species and 74 at-risk-species occur only on DoD lands. Protecting species and managing natural resources helps DoD sustain the use of their lands for realistic and mission-essential testing, training, and operations. Given the frequent alignment between military and conservation goals, working with the DoD bases offers opportunities to promote conservation actions and collaborate with the DoD to support both regional and state SGCN.
